Advancing Global Academic Medicine through Innovation and Collaboration: proceedings of the International Congress on Academic Medicine 2026

The International Congress on Academic Medicine (ICAM) 2026, held from April 16–19 in Ottawa, Ontario, united a diverse global community engaged in academic medicine. Organized by the Association of Faculties of Medicine of Canada (AFMC), the congress aimed to foster collaboration, innovation, and progress in medical education, health research and social accountability. It provided a platform for over 1,900 delegates, including medical students, residents, graduate students, faculty, researchers, and patient partners—to meet, network, and develop new relationships and collaborations with colleagues globally.

ICAM featured innovative keynotes and accredited sessions exploring global themes in academic medicine, including health misinformation, compassion in healthcare, nutrition and child health, food prescription, and planetary health. Additionally, ICAM hosted international presentations and symposiums, such as the Gairdner Symposium, workshops, and oral and poster presentations.

ICAM 2026 was recognized for its commitment to inclusivity, achieving "Patient Included Status," ensuring that the experiences of patients as experts in living with their conditions were incorporated into all aspects of the congress. This year, ICAM also achieved platinum status as a certified sustainable event through uOttawa’s Sustainable Events program.

Opening keynote: health lies: manufactured doubt – health and science misinformation and the fight for trust

The Opening Keynote at ICAM 2026, explored the growing impact of health misinformation on public trust, health outcomes, and society. Featuring speakers Stanley Kutcher, Marcus Kolga, Timothy Caulfield, and patient partner Lydia Greene, the session examined how misinformation is shaped by political identity, amplified through foreign influence, and spread within today’s information environment. Discussions emphasized the urgent need for coordinated and trusted responses to combat health disinformation and rebuild confidence in science and healthcare institutions.

At the end of the session, participants were able to: To outline the decline in general scientific knowledge in our communities and the processes used by those aiming to share misinformation.Explain different roles that government and civil society have in addressing health disinformation and outline gaps in the work that needs to be done.Discuss practical and impactful healthcare lead initiatives that could be created to effectively address health disinformation.

To outline the decline in general scientific knowledge in our communities and the processes used by those aiming to share misinformation.

Explain different roles that government and civil society have in addressing health disinformation and outline gaps in the work that needs to be done.

Keynote: The case for compassion: reframing compassion as a source of strength

The compassion-themed keynote explored the role of compassion in healthcare and challenged the perception of compassion as a “soft skill” that contributes to burnout. Featuring speakers Shireen Mansouri and Darren Lauscher, the session examined the evidence-based benefits of compassionate care for patients, providers, and health systems, while highlighting the neurophysiologic foundations of compassion as a source of resilience and strength. Participants also explored practical strategies for integrating compassion into clinical practice, teaching, supervision, and assessment.

At the end of the session, participants were able to: Define compassion in healthcare and distinguish it from related concepts such as empathy, sympathy, and patient-centered care.Describe the evidence-based benefits of compassionate care for patients, healthcare providers, and health systems, including impacts on patient outcomes, and provider wellness.Identify common barriers to practicing compassion in healthcare and explore strategies to overcome these challenges at individual and institutional levels.Develop strategies for integrating compassion into clinical teaching, supervision, and assessment.

Define compassion in healthcare and distinguish it from related concepts such as empathy, sympathy, and patient-centered care.

Session: Planetary Health as a medical education and health system priority

The session explored how planetary health principles can be integrated into healthcare education, institutional practice, and broader health system transformation. Featuring speakers Nicole Simms, Owen Luo, and Nicole Redvers, the session highlighted both the progress and challenges of advancing climate-conscious healthcare education and policy in Canada. Through discussion and shared perspectives, participants explored practical approaches to advocacy, curriculum development, collaboration, and community action in support of sustainable and climate-resilient health systems. his engaging panel brought together global experts to explore the intersection of planetary advocacy efforts and the integration of planetary health principles into curricula, highlighting impactful initiatives and lessons learned in overcoming common challenges.

At the end of the session, participants were able to: Advocacy and Curriculum: Focus on advocacy efforts and the integration of planetary health principles into medical curricula and research.Highlight Successes and Challenges: Showcase successful actions and initiatives across institutions alongside lessons learned from encountering barriers.Practical Guidance: Offer actionable steps for individuals and institutions that have not yet started their journey towards incorporating planetary health into their frameworks.Community and Collaboration: Foster a sense of community and enhance collaborative efforts to drive change and move from community ecoanxiety to action.

Keynote: Gairdner Lectures: progress and challenges in the management of severe acute malnutrition & human milk: the ultimate superfood and personalized medicine

The Gairdner Award Lectures at ICAM 2026 highlighted major advances and ongoing challenges in global child health and early life nutrition. André Briend examined the evolution of community-based treatment for severe acute malnutrition through the development of ready-to-use therapeutic foods, while also addressing persistent barriers related to prevention, access, and global funding challenges. Meghan Azad explored the biological complexity of human milk and its role in supporting infant development, lifelong health, and health equity through breastfeeding and public health policy.

At the end of the session, participants were able to: Summarize recent progress in the treatment of severe acute malnutrition and the move to mass treatmentAccess remaining challenges, in particular problems met for funding programmes, limited access to treatment and need to improve treatment protocolsJustify the importance of human milk as both food and medicineExplain how breastfeeding shapes the infant microbiome and lifelong health

Keynote: Gairdner Panel

The Gairdner Panel brought together André Briend and Meghan Azad to explore the connections between nutrition, the microbiome, and child health across diverse global settings. Building on the themes of the preceding lectures, the discussion examined how approaches to severe acute malnutrition, infection prevention, growth, and long-term health outcomes can be strengthened through more integrated and equitable nutrition strategies.

At the end of the session, participants were able to: Recognize how the microbiome provides a common lens linking malnutrition treatment, early feeding, and long-term health.Reflect on similarities and differences in nutrition challenges and opportunities across diverse global and income settings.Identify broad gaps and opportunities for better alignment between treatment-focused and prevention-focused nutrition approaches.

Session: Learner perspectives: navigating systemic challenges and educational design

This Learner Forum brought together learner organizations from across Canada and around the world to share their priorities, challenges, and proposed solutions for the future of medical education and healthcare training. Representatives from national and international learner organizations highlighted diverse perspectives from the learner community and emphasized the importance of collaboration, advocacy, and learner-driven change within health professions education.

At the end of the session, participants were able to: Identify priority areas of action needed to support learner success.Recognize and mitigate the unique challenges faced by systemically disadvantaged learners.Integrate tools and strategies to support learner wellbeing.

Session: AFMC Board Invitational on social accountability

Annually, ICAM has hosted a Board Invitational event dedicated to a topic aligned with one of its three pillars – medical education, health research, and social accountability. This year, the Invitational centered on social accountability.

This session brought together more than 120 participants from across Canada, including medical education leaders, learners, patient partners, researchers, community representatives, and experts in equity, anti-racism, Indigenous health, accessibility, and planetary health. Centered on the theme Driving Systems Change through Collective Accountability: Charting the Next Chapter of Social Accountability Throughout Canadian Medical Education, the session fostered dialogue on the future of social accountability in academic medicine and health systems. Through collaborative discussions and reflection activities, participants explored institutional accountability, national priorities, and practical opportunities to strengthen social accountability across medical education, research, and healthcare systems. Insights from the session will inform the development of a shared Social Accountability Framework and national priority roadmap for Canada.

Closing Keynote: Food prescription

The Closing Keynote examined the growing impact of food insecurity and limited access to nutritious food on individual and community health. Featuring speakers Donald Warne and Mair Greenfield, the session explored how structural and social determinants shape food access, particularly within underserved communities. Through discussions on food deserts, food prescribing, and community-based solutions, participants considered strategies to address food insecurity and support prevention, wellbeing, and equitable healthcare outcomes.

At the end of the session, participants were able to: Link structural and social determinants of health to food accessibility and disproportionately impact specific populationsRecognize and integrate the concept of 'food deserts' and their impact on many communitiesSynthesize action plans for individuals and communities that can and will reduce food insecurity in both research and clinical care

ICAM Patient Included Status

The Patients Included™ Status provides conference organizers with a means of demonstrating that their events are committed to incorporating the experience of patients as experts in living with their condition while ensuring they are neither excluded nor exploited. To achieve this status, a conference must successfully meet all five of the Patients Included™ conference charter’s clauses: 1) patients or caregivers with experience actively participate in the design and planning and delivery of the event and appear in its physical audience, 2) travel/accommodation expenses for patients or carers participating in the advertised program are paid in full, in advance, 3) scholarships are sourced by the conference organizers, 4) the disability requirements of participants are accommodated, and 5) free access for virtual patients/carers.

We continue to actively engage with patient partners, recognizing their vital role in shaping the future of medical education and research. This collaboration is steadily growing, reflecting our commitment to embedding the patient voice at the heart of academic medicine. A recent highlight of this engagement was our highly successful Patient Partner Luncheon, which served as a meaningful opportunity to gather insights directly from patient partners, faculty and learners attending the conference.

ICAM commitment to sustainability

ICAM 2026 demonstrated a strong commitment to sustainability through a series of impactful initiatives that not only minimized environmental impact but also supported local communities across Ottawa, Ontario.

The conference earned a platinum certification of sustainability from uOttawa’s Sustainability Office, recognizing its dedication to sustainable event planning practices. This underscores ICAM's proactive approach to integrating sustainability into every facet of the event, from logistics to waste management.

In collaboration with the Rogers Centre Ottawa, ICAM 2026 ensured that approximately 325 pounds of surplus food from the event was redirected to families in need across Ottawa, helping to address food insecurity while reducing food waste. Remaining furniture from the conference was also donated to Matthew House Ottawa, ensuring these items were repurposed to support individuals and families within the community.

ICAM 2026 also proudly partnered with Forêt Capitale Forest, an Ottawa-based registered charity dedicated to mitigating the effects of climate change through tree planting and promoting awareness of the importance of forests and biodiversity. A mandatory $15 carbon offset fee was included in each delegate registration. In total, more than $26,000 was donated in support of Forêt Capitale Forest’s community tree planting initiatives and environmental sustainability efforts.

Lastly, ICAM 2026 collaborated with Buy Social Canada, a social enterprise that connects purpose-driven purchasers with social value suppliers. This partnership facilitated the inclusion of social enterprises in the conference's supply chain, promoting economic and social benefits within local communities.

We are privileged to share the work of Canadians in academic medicine both nationally and internationally. Looking ahead, we are excited to announce that in 2027, ICAM will be hosted by Montréal, Québec, where we are preparing an exceptional and enriching program for all participants. We look forward to continuing this journey of growth and collaboration together.

Christine De Maria (Lead Author), Evelyn Constantin (Co Author), Cheryl Hunchak (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Alors que les outils d'intelligence artificielle (IA) s'intègrent rapidement dans les soins de santé canadiens, les éducateurs médicaux doivent préparer les apprenants à naviguer des risques médico-légaux complexes. L'Enquête nationale 2024 auprès des médecins canadiens (1) révèle que 7 % des médecins utilisent l'IA pour soutenir les soins aux patients - une augmentation de 250 % depuis 2021. De plus, 77 % identifient la formation comme une priorité pour soutenir l'utilisation de l'IA. Des preuves récentes démontrent des risques significatifs : erreurs de documentation générées par l'IA, violations de confidentialité dues à une gouvernance inadéquate des données, et cadres de responsabilité peu clairs lors de résultats défavorables (2). Les programmes médicaux actuels n'abordent pas suffisamment ces normes légales et professionnelles évolutives, laissant les étudiants mal préparés pour les défis reliés à l'IA en pratique clinique.

Instructional Methods: Les activités interactives et l'apprentissage par cas représenteront environ deux tiers de cet atelier. Les participants s'engageront dans des sondages, des exercices de cartographie du continuum de risque, et des analyses étape par étape des scribes utilisant l'IA. Les interactions porteront sur le consentement et la responsabilité de l'IA, tandis que les participants développeront des réponses pédagogiques à des scénarios réels. Les activités seront basées sur des approches éducatives fondées sur les données probantes pour enseigner des concepts médico-légaux complexes. Les séances interactives permettront de clarifier les stratégies d'implémentation dans leur milieu. Du matériel éducatif sera fourni. Cet atelier sera animé par des médecins-conseils éducateurs expérimentés de l'ACPM.

Target Audience: Éducateurs médicaux, directeurs de programme, corps professoral, professionnels de la santé et résidents responsables d'enseigner la responsabilité professionnelle, la gestion des risques, et les considérations médico-légales dans les environnements de soins améliorés par l'IA.

Claire Kendall (Lead Author), Erin Cameron (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Medical schools have social accountability mandates to reduce health inequities in the communities they serve. Many schools are looking to establish innovative partnership models to identify and respond to the priority health needs of their regions. Medical schools at the University of Ottawa and Northern Ontario School of Medicine University are implementing a community-campus engagement model called CityStudio to address the priorities articulated through their local Community Safety and Well-Being Plans. CityStudio is a transferable model of community-campus engagement that builds trust-based relationships between students, city staff, faculty, and community. CityStudio Ottawa and CityStudio Thunder Bay have collectively conducted 209 projects involving 2,210 students across faculties, 87 city staff, and 28 community partners.

Instructional Methods: The interactive workshop will begin with an introduction to the CityStudio model from change idea to action project. We will share how this process has been implemented as a mechanism to engage students in addressing priority community needs in two unique contexts. Participants will move into smaller breakout groups. In the first activity, participants will map out how the CityStudio model could be applied in different medical school curricular domains (i.e., curriculum, community service and experiential learning, and extracurricular opportunities). In the second activity, participants will select one change idea and work through the CityStudio project cycle to identify concrete next steps for initiating such a community-campus partnership in their region.

Target Audience: Faculty members, medical educators

Katrina Hurley (Lead Author), Susan Snyder (Co Author), Alex McLean (Co Author), Roetka Gradstein (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Improvisation, often referred to as improv, is a style of spontaneous storytelling where participants collaboratively and reciprocally create a narrative. The best improv comes when participants are present (listening to verbal and emotional cues rather than planning what's next), embrace uncertainty, accept contributions of others without judgment, and accept mistakes as part of the process. As a curricular tool in medical education and healthcare, improv offers a type of simulation learning which includes debriefing, participant time-outs and reflection. Improv has been shown to impact most CanMEDS roles, enhancing communication (active listening), empathy (perspective taking), psychological safety, teamwork, and wellness. Healthcare interactions with patients and on interprofessional teams, although somewhat structured, are, in essence, unscripted, imperfect, complex and vulnerable. Improv makes explicit these dynamics and is a tool for the real world of healthcare.

Instructional Methods: This workshop is highly interactive, using techniques rooted in the 'le jeu' tradition of theatre practice, most famously developed by pedagogues Jacques Lecoq and Philippe Gaulier and central to the creative practice of Halifax's Zuppa Theatre. After a brief introduction of the concepts and evidence, attendees will be engaged through a series of approachable improvisation games and exercises. These activities emphasize a spirit of playness, close listening, responding 'in the moment,' and collaborative storytelling. Following these experiential activities, attendees will debrief with a focus on learning outcomes, and how to integrate key learnings and tools into their work.

Target Audience: This workshop is for everyone - medical educators, leaders, staff, and both undergraduate and postgraduate learners.

Tania Riendeau (Lead Author), Véronique Castonguay (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Le phénomène de «failure to fail» (échec à l'échec) a été décrit en médecine universitaire depuis les années 1990 et est encore présent dans les facultés de médecine à ce jour. Ce phénomène se définit comme l'incapacité en éducation médicale à faire échouer des stagiaires malgré le fait que leur performance est jugée inadéquate. Cela a pour impact principal la graduation de médecins avec des difficultés parfois majeures dans leur pratique amenant des conséquences néfastes sur leur communauté. Malgré un changement de paradigme en matière d'évaluation pour un passage en approche des compétences, ce phénomène est encore bien présent. Les superviseurs rapportent des difficultés à donner une rétroaction non favorable et sont parfois angoissés d'aborder ce sujet avec les apprenants.

Instructional Methods: Au cours de l'atelier, les participants seront invités à partager leurs expériences personnelles comme enseignant, à discuter de cas fictifs et à identifier les causes de du phénomène d'échec à l'échec ainsi que leurs conséquences. À l'aide d'exercices dirigés, ils pourront identifier leurs propres biais comme superviseur clinique ains que leurs freins à donner des rétroactions défavorables. Enfin, des pistes de solution seront discutées et élaborées.

Target Audience: Cliniciens, enseignants et superviseurs impliqués dans la formation et l'évaluation des étudiants en médecine ou en sciences de la santé, directeurs de programme

Stephen Russell (Lead Author), Mélanie Houle (Lead Author), Sonia Chernov (Lead Author), Gaëlle Rioual (Co Author), Chris He (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: How can we teach new material while simultaneously not substantially increasing the amount of information that students need to memorize? This is a continual problem for instructors of all disciplines, and it is especially true for those of us who teach the language of medicine and anatomy. This bilingual workshop demonstrates how we are using games, and the games we use, to teach medical language. Our particular challenge lies in the fact that some things, such as anatomical names, must to some extent be memorized - but our goal is to reduce reliance on rote memorization while artfully introducing a type of learning that is memorization-adjacent. Some games have somewhat simple nature, such as recognizing which phrases are real and which ones are impossible, while others involve more steps, such as moving from the Latin of an anatomical phrases to the Greek-based phrases of clinical terms. Some games involve competition with others, while some can be "played" alone. But all of them have two rules: (1) they must have pedagogical value and (2) they must be fun. This second rule is important to keep learners motivated. We start with the traditional kinds of games - with paper, cards, and personal interaction - but these can be branched out into the world of smartphones and internet gaming culture if the opportunity and the technology allow.

Instructional Methods: We will ask the audience to participate in playing some of the games we use in the classroom, to demonstrate how we, as language instructors (and former students), are turning the seemingly dry topic of terminology into an area that all learners can benefit from and enjoy. We will also invite feedback regarding improvements that can be made and will welcome dialogue regarding collaborations in developing new concepts and approaches that can be brought into the learning environments.

Target Audience: Medical professionals and instructors at all levels.

Strategic Network of AFMC Communicators SNAC (Lead Author) Jason Clement (Co Author), Jordanna Heller (Co Author), Virginia Middleton (Co Author), Gillian Earle (Co Author), Kimberly Larkin

Rationale/Background: In today's fast-paced, online, media-driven world, those in the academic medicine community often find themselves at the centre of health-related discussions and debates in the public eye. Effectively engaging with traditional and social media is a critical skill for cultivating trust, dispelling misinformation, and advocating for evidence-based medicine. The first 45 minutes of this workshop will focus on media relations and will equip participants with the tools to navigate interviews, manage challenging questions, and communicate complex health information with clarity and confidence. Participants will learn to prepare for interviews with journalists, develop key messages, avoid common pitfalls, and build constructive relationships with media representatives. The second 45 minutes will explore strategies for handling social media. Attendees will leave with a deeper understanding of how to represent the medical profession effectively in the public eye while safeguarding their own reputation, and an awareness of principles and guidelines in effect at the university level, with licensing bodies, and with health authorities. Both sections of this workshop will be led by seasoned communications professionals from medical schools across the country (members of the Strategic Network of AFMC Communicators). This workshop is ideal for anyone seeking to integrate media and social media best practices into their work, research, medical education or professional development. Together, we will bridge the gap between medicine and media to foster informed, impactful communication. Delegates are welcome to attend one or both parts of this session, according to their interest and availability.

Instructional Methods: One 90 minutes workshop, outlining best practices, discussing case studies and application to real-time examples.

Target Audience: All delegates, including learners, faculty, researchers, patients and other members of the medical community.

Robert Sternszus (Lead Author), Beth-Ann Cummings (Co Author), Merril Pauls (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: How do we navigate our roles as health advocates while maintaining a professional stance and avoiding harm to patients and colleagues with differing views? How does one determine appropriate 'self-disclosure' in their online presence? Or when working in a small rural community? These are only a few examples. To address these challenges, professionalism education must move beyond the non-negotiable and support learners in 'playing in the mud.' This workshop will draw on the literature on professionalism, professional identity, role boundaries, and communities of practice to assist educators in making that leap.

Instructional Methods: In this workshop, participants will be provided with a framework to explore non-negotiable and contextual aspects of professionalism. Subsequently, they will work through cases in small groups, examining professionalism concerns while considering diverse contexts and perspectives. Finally, participants will consider how they can adapt professionalism education at their institution to better meet the real-world needs of learners.

Target Audience: This workshop is designed for those involved in professionalism curricula or remediation across the spectrum of health professions education

Arun Radhakrishnan (Lead Author), Douglas Archibald (Co Author), Jerry Maniate (Co Author), Zack Van Allen (Lead Author), Joshua Rash (Co Author), Jayne Beselt (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: AI and large language models (LLMs) can support qualitative and quantitative analysis in research, and the evaluation of educational innovations in healthcare. Our team is applying AI to analyze de-identified interview transcripts, and program documents from Adaptive Mentoring Networks (AMNs) using implementation science frameworks (e.g., Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research, CFIR; Active Implementation Framework - Implementation Stages). These AMNs are clinical mentorship networks for care providers and are an important source of Continuing Medical Education for practicing clinicians. In practice, LLMs can expedite coding, promote cross-site consistency, and surface linkages to behavior-change strategies; yet they also introduce risks (e.g., privacy, bias, reproducibility, and overreliance). This workshop equips participants to integrate AI responsibly by pairing human expertise with transparent prompts, de-identification, and structured validation against established frameworks. We foreground ethical decision-making and participant safety while demonstrating concrete workflows that improve rigor and efficiency.

Instructional Methods: After a brief primer on LLM capabilities/limits, participants will engage in small group activities, including: (1)manually code anonymized excerpts; (2) run an AI-assisted coding pass with a prompt template; (3) compare outputs, adjudicate disagreements, and document changes; (4) map codes to CFIR constructs; (5) co-create an ethics/participant-safety checklist (de-identification, consent language, secure data handling, bias monitoring, prompt/version logging, and human-in-the-loop verification). We close with a whole-group synthesis and take-home resources (e.g., prompt scaffolds, decision tree, one-page checklist).

Target Audience: Medical education researchers, clinician educators, evaluators, trainees

Ursin Bayisenge (Lead Author) Maria Albin (Co Author) Phaedra Henley (Co Author) Kelsey Ripp (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Amid global health threats such as pandemics, antimicrobial resistance, and climate-driven disease emergence, the need for health professionals with systems thinking and intersectoral collaboration skills is urgent. One Health, an approach similar to Planetary Health, recognizes the interdependence of human, animal, and environmental health, and offers a framework to prepare future leaders. Yet, most medical curricula lack structured approaches to integrate One Health competencies. The University of Global Health Equity (UGHE) in Rwanda is one of the first institutions worldwide to embed One Health longitudinally across its undergraduate medical curriculum. Its collaboration with the Northern Ontario School of Medicine (NOSM) illustrates the broader impact of this approach: a UGHE presentation on One Health integration at NOSM inspired the creation of a One Health Alliance to strengthen similar efforts in Canada. Building on these experiences, this workshop will equip participants with practical strategies to adapt and implement One Health training in medical education programs globally.

Instructional Methods: The 90-minute session will combine brief presentations, case scenarios, and interactive small-group work. 1. Introduction (10 min): Overview of UGHE's approach and lessons learned from integrating One Health into medical education. 2. Case-based Breakouts (30 min): Participants analyze curriculum scenarios, map integration strategies, and identify barriers and enablers in their own contexts. 3. Action Planning (30 min): Groups develop draft action plans for introducing or strengthening One Health teaching in their institutions. 4. Group Reflection & Next Steps (20 min): Sharing key insights and building a collaborative network for continued exchange.

Target Audience: Academic deans, health professions educators, medical students, residents, and global/One Health practitioners.

Andrea Brose (Lead Author), Erin Weir (Co Author), Aleksandra Mineyko (Co Author), Ciorstaidh Reid (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Supporting medical residents in difficulty is a common yet challenging responsibility for clinical faculty. Preceptors may notice early warning signs of distress but feel uncertain or ill-prepared to respond effectively while balancing their dual roles as educators and evaluators. Key challenges include addressing learner wellbeing, facilitating access to resources, and establishing appropriate boundaries. Evidence highlights the importance of faculty development in equipping preceptors with practical tools to recognize distress, initiate supportive conversations, and offer options for support including academic resources, mental health resources, and accommodations. Forum theatre offers a dynamic approach for engaging faculty in the experience of supporting students by becoming "spect-actors", an audience that participates in the performance. Participants will witness scenarios, intervene, and practice strategies and alternate solutions in real time.

Instructional Methods: This 90-minute workshop employs a scripted simulation delivered in forum theatre style. A three-act case study portrays a resident experiencing escalating distress and a preceptor navigating how to respond. Audience members will be invited to pause the action, discuss possible approaches, and even step into the role of the preceptor to try new strategies. Facilitators will guide structured debriefs after each act, drawing attention to effective practices, available institutional supports, and the challenges of setting healthy boundaries. The majority of the session will involve active participation and reflection.

Target Audience: Clinical faculty, program directors, and others involved in postgraduate medical education who supervise and support residents.

Evelyn Constantin (Lead Author), Heather Murray (Co Author), Cheryl Hunchak (Co Author), Elisabeth Boileau (Co Author), Lisa Thurgur (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: The Canadian Institute for Health Information (CIHI) reported that 1 in 17 hospitalizations involved at least one harmful event (CIHI. Hospital Harm Results, 2014-2015 to 2023-2024. Ottawa, ON: CIHI; 2024). Iatrogenic harm and patient safety incidents (PSIs) are not uncommon. Meaningful ongoing support post PSI, a genuine apology, and assurance of informed quality improvements are some needs identified by patients and families. At the Canadian Medical Protective Association (CMPA), experienced physician educators provide CPD based on fundamental medicolegal principles related to PSIs, with the goal of helping promote patient safety. Key themes are communication challenges including informed consent and disclosure discussions and documentation, and challenging patient interactions. This workshop will help expand participants' familiarity and awareness of these core patient safety fundamentals and help identify opportunities to reinforce these through integrated learning opportunities tailored to the unique contexts of their interdisciplinary clinical environments. In the current Canadian healthcare environment of limited resources, embedding patient safety practices and teaching effective strategies to promote patient safety and reduce medico-legal risk can help foster learner confidence in practice, and build trust and connections between health professions educators/supervisors and learners.

Instructional Methods: This 90-minute highly interactive workshop will be facilitated by experienced CMPA physician educators. Participants will develop an approach to incorporating patient safety fundamentals and practices into clinical teaching and embed patient safety practices into day-to-day interdisciplinary clinical settings. Using interactive case-based scenarios, patient-centred approaches for communication skills (related to informed consent and disclosure discussions and challenging patient encounters) and potential medicolegal implications and practical implementation strategies to mitigate medico-legal risk will be discussed and applied. Active participation and interactive exercises will encompass about two-thirds of this workshop. Educational materials will be provided, including specific checklists and written materials.

Target Audience: Health professions educators, leaders, and learners.

Nandini Nandeesha (Lead Author), Bronwyn Thomson (Lead Author), Marijana Jovanovic (Co Author), Lissa Johnston (Co Author), Marguerite Beaudoin (Co Author), Sayed Mohammed Behbehani (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Providing feedback on sensitive topics such as professionalism, clinical competence, communication skills, or critical incidents is a significant challenge in medical education. These emotionally charged conversations occur across all levels of supervision, from attending-to-attending interactions to attending-resident and resident-student relationships. Poorly delivered feedback can harm relationships, evoke defensiveness, and impede growth, while skillful feedback can transform these moments into opportunities for development and trust-building. This workshop equips participants with tools and strategies to navigate feedback conversations that go beyond the scope of conventional models like the "sandwich" approach.

Instructional Methods: Participants will begin with a needs assessment and guided reflection, analyzing personal experiences with challenging feedback to explore what made these moments effective or ineffective. The didactic portion will draw on key literature, including fostering psychological safety (e.g., creating a safe learning environment for feedback), addressing emotional dynamics in feedback (e.g., validating emotions and managing defensiveness), and emphasizing behavior-focused feedback over personal traits. This workshop will include group work focused on case-based scenarios about giving feedback in different settings. Small group discussions will normalize shared challenges and help participants collaboratively identify actionable strategies. These strategies will be shared by each group and synthesized in a large group discussion and connected to evidence-based tools. The workshop will end with action planning where participants can identity one strategy to implement in the future. The workshop concludes with action planning, enabling participants to translate these strategies into practical changes they can implement in their work settings. By combining reflection, discussion, and evidence-based tools, this session empowers participants to confidently approach even the most challenging feedback conversations, fostering connection and professional growth at all levels.

Target Audience: Medical student learners, supervisors, and practitioners

Victor Do (Lead Author), Lyn Sonnenberg (Co Author), Ming-Ka Chan (Co Author), Jerry Maniate (Co Author), Robert Sternszus (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Professional Identity Formation (PIF) is a central pillar in health professions education, shaping how learners integrate knowledge, values, and roles into a coherent sense of self. As Artificial Intelligence (AI) becomes increasingly embedded in clinical practice, education, and decision-making, its implications for identity and competence are profound, but remain underexplored. This interactive workshop invites participants to engage critically with the evolving relationship between AI, professional roles, and core values. Drawing on early insights from a national dialogue among clinician-educators, we examine how AI is reshaping what professionals do (roles) while challenging, but potentially strengthening, who they are (values). Participants will reflect on whether AI threatens or enhances identity formation and clinical competence, and how deliberate, values-driven identity development may serve as a protective factor against challenges such as imposter phenomenon and epistemic disruption. We will explore three core provocations: 1) How should approaches to supporting professional identity adapt to AI-mediated environments? 2) How do current conceptualization of competence stand up as reasoning and decision-making becoming increasingly AI-assisted? 3) How can equity be embedded (not merely foregrounded) in the design of future-facing learning environments?

Instructional Methods: This session will use our Critical Dialogues for Action (CDFA) approach to guide structured small- and large- group discussions. After a brief overview, participants will engage in a role-versus-values identity mapping exercise to surface their own assumptions. We'll then move into three rounds of dialogue, each focused on a key provocation. Participants will be invited to name tensions, explore emerging questions, and consider implications for their own teaching and leadership.

Target Audience: This session is designed for clinician-educators, curriculum developers, faculty development leaders, program directors, educational researchers, and others who are responsible for shaping professional identity and competence in learning environments increasingly influenced by AI.

Holly Harris (Lead Author), Amy Hsieh (Co Author), David Wiljer (Co Author), Laura Williams (Co Author), Sophie Soklaridis (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Patient and family engagement in health education research is gaining traction because it enhances the relevance and impact of research. However, engagement rarely transcends the level of consultation on specific projects or discrete aspects of research. Barriers include epistemic bias, which privileges academic knowledge over other ways of knowing, and a lack of practical guidance on how to move beyond consultation toward co-production. Co-production involves patients, families, professionals, and those with multiple perspectives working as equal partners throughout the research process. Our workshop will highlight the different types of knowledge that patients and families bring to health education research, identify common barriers to equitable engagement, and suggest ways to foster co-production across the research cycle. These include strategies for relationship building, multi-directional learning, and inclusive knowledge translation. Drawing on concepts such as epistemic humility, critical reflexivity, and standpoint theory, we will emphasize the value of integrating both lived and learned expertise into the research process.

Instructional Methods: Participants will introduce themselves through an interactive, reflexive activity. Background (10 minutes): Facilitators will provide an overview of patient and family engagement in health education research, highlight forms of patient and family knowledge, and introduce key concepts. Roles and Barriers (20 minutes): Participants will brainstorm barriers to engagement and the roles that patients and families can play in research in large groups. Themes will be developed collaboratively, setting the stage for discussions on practical strategies. Practical Strategies (40 minutes): Participants will rotate through tables to explore co-production strategies at various research stages using activities and case examples (e.g. the co-production of an AI patient bill of rights and a strategic research agenda). Reflection (15 minutes): Facilitators will identify key themes and actionable takeaways. Participants will close the workshop with key insights and collective reflection.

Target Audience: Health educators, health education researchers, and leaders.

Carlyn Seguin (Co Author), Cassandra Opikokew Wajuntah (Lead Author), Cari McIlduff (Co Author), Jacob Alhassan (Co Author), Anne Lise (Co Author), Sylvia Abonyi (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Global and planetary health education risk perpetuating harm when they fail to critically examine colonial legacies, systemic power imbalances, and the structural determinants that shape human and ecological well-being. Without this lens, curricula can unintentionally reinforce inequities and overlook the interconnectedness of human, animal, and environmental health. Global and planetary health educators have a pivotal role in shaping learners' values and advocacy skills, although there is a paucity of resources to support educators to facilitate dialogue about decolonization, Indigenous knowledge systems, cultural safety, climate justice, and planetary health. The Making the Links Global Health Certificate at the University of Saskatchewan has recently undergone a major restructuring to embed decolonizing and planetary health frameworks across coursework and community-based practicums. Central to this process has been a commitment to sustained, reciprocal partnerships with local, Indigenous, and global communities. Lessons from this work provide practical strategies for redesigning curricula, creating safer and more inclusive learning spaces, and preparing learners to advance equity centered, community driven solutions to complex health challenges.

Instructional Methods: This workshop uses a multifaceted approach to exploring the principles of decolonizing global and planetary health. Facilitators will deliver short, focused presentations to ground participant understanding of key concepts and historical contexts. Through interactive case-based learning, featuring structured group discussions, reflective exercises, participants will work collaboratively in small groups to apply decolonizing frameworks, critically analyze real-world scenarios, and strengthen their ability to advance equity-centered solutions in global and planetary health.

Target Audience: Medical education leaders, faculty members, course directors

Meeta Patel (Co Author), Reena Pattani (Lead Author)

Rationale/Background: Microaggressions-subtle, often unintentional acts of discrimination based on protected grounds within most Human Rights frameworks-are pervasive in academic medicine and undermine the well-being, performance, and career advancement of learners, faculty, and staff (Poorsattar, Blake, & Manuel, 2021). They also erode trust in institutions, and drive attrition-impacts that are particularly pronounced for individuals from structurally marginalized groups-maintaining a persistent representation gap in medicine (Sukhera et al., 2020). Environments where such behaviours go unaddressed foster a culture of incivility and inequity. Raising awareness alone is insufficient. Faculty, staff, and learners require practical strategies to recognize and respond to microaggressions. Frameworks such as "Open the Front Door" (Souza, 2021) offer structured communication approaches that support dialogue while reducing harm. This workshop draws on initiatives at the University of Toronto's Temerty Faculty of Medicine to shift from awareness to action, equipping participants with skills to become better communicators, collaborators, and advocates, including through the practice of allyship at interpersonal and systemic levels.

Instructional Methods: Analyze video-based case scenarios in small groups to identify microaggressions. Practice real-time responses using structured frameworks ("Open the Front Door," "Calling In vs. Calling Out"). Engage in a pre-briefing exercise to foster psychological safety. Participate in a large group debrief to consolidate strategies and explore possible systems-level actions.

Target Audience: Medical educators, program directors, faculty, learners, and other health professions educators seeking to foster inclusive and safe learning environments.

Jamaica Cass (Lead Author), Alexandra Nychuk (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Canadian medical schools face a pressing responsibility to integrate Indigenous health, cultural safety, and anti-racism throughout undergraduate curricula. A major barrier to meeting this responsibility is a lack of capacity within institutions to transform curricula meaningfully. The Truth and Reconciliation Commission's Calls to Action #23 and #24, the National Circle on Indigenous Medical Education (NCIME) Core Competencies, accreditation standards (CACMS), the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP) and national licensing exam requirements all set clear expectations. This workshop aims to support undergraduate curricular leaders in the integration of these concepts into existing medical curricula.

Instructional Methods: Analyze case vignettes illustrating effective application of NCIME's core competencies. Engage in facilitated discussions to identify barriers and enablers to curricular change within their programs. Use structured worksheets to align current curricular elements with accreditation requirements, NCIME core competencies, and TRC Calls to Action. Develop a framework for advancing integration of Indigenous health, cultural safety, and anti-racism into their undergraduate MD programs.

Target Audience: Undergraduate curriculum and assessment leads, medical school deans and associate deans, course directors, and faculty leaders responsible for program design, accreditation readiness, and equity initiatives.

Renate Kahlke (Lead Author), Sarah Burm (Co Author), Lindsay Cowley (Co Author), Emiko Blalock (Co Author), Kori LaDonna (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Research interviews are a key tool used to produce data in many qualitative health professions education studies. It seems intuitive that if we want to find out how participants are thinking about a topic, we should ask them. But designing interviews is not as simple as asking participants what they think - the number and structure of our questions can deeply impact participants' responses, and interview guides that are misaligned with our research questions and methodologies often fail to support interviewers in working with participants to produce rich and relevant data. Drawing on our recent scholarship on qualitative interviewing,1,2 in this workshop we aim to provide concrete strategies and coaching to support researchers and graduate students new to designing interview studies. Specifically, we focus on key decisions about how to structure interview guides to facilitate rich conversation and produce data that align with a study's research question(s) and other elements of study design. We operate from the assumption that researcher bias is not a threat to be minimized; rather, researcher, interviewer, and participant perspectives are critical resources to draw on when interviews are thoughtfully designed and conducted.3 Participants can work through their own interview based studies, or with exemplars provided by instructors.

Instructional Methods: • 20 min didactic - Introductions, session overview, and presentation (How can paradigm, methodology, and research questions impact interview guide development?) • 10 min small group - Discuss how your positionality might impact your approach to interviewing • 10 in large group debrief • 10 min didactic - Structuring the interview guide (question type and flow) • 30 min small group - Draft or revise an interview guide with feedback from instructors • 10 min large group debrief and closing

Target Audience: Researchers and grad students new to designing qualitative interview studies

Ramy Azzouz (Lead Author), Nicolas Doudeau (Lead Author), Marine Gourguechon-Lacaille (Co Author), Arnaud Delehaye (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Clinical placements are a pivotal component of health-professions training, yet their quality remains heterogeneous. Learners and institutions alike need trustworthy tools to capture feedback, strengthen supervision, and detect educational, organizational, and psychosocial risks early. GELULES (Guide en Ligne Unifiant les Evaluations de Stage-Online Guide Unifying Clinical Placement Evaluations) is an association-run web platform managed by student representatives. It enables anonymous, standardized evaluations; student-led moderation under an ethics charter; real-time incident alerts; and data dashboards tailored to learners, placement supervisors, educators and institutions. This workshop will elaborate on the rationale for a decade-long initiative, share local and national experiences, and facilitate a collective discussion on improving tools and procedures for placement evaluation, incident reporting, and incident management. Since 2014, GELULES has collected over 280,000 evaluations across more than 50 faculties and schools of medicine, midwifery, and pharmacy. At the University of Lille (France), GELULES evaluations determine placement selection and directly inform accreditation reviews. Incident reports have led to the creation of a joint Ethics and Professional Conduct Commission with the Regional Health Agency (ARS) and professional regulatory authorities. GELULES illustrates students' capacity to co-construct a system of continuous improvement for workplace-based learning in health professions. Future directions include automated detection of adverse events, algorithmic support for placement choice, and faculty development. The workshop aims to share this experience and to foster an interprofessional, community-engaged dialogue about learner-driven placement evaluation and the management of incidents arising during training.

Instructional Methods: Case analysis and guided simulation: Teams analyze student-reported evaluations; Practice incident triage; draft a local action plan. Large-group synthesis: Comparative discussion of processes and governance models; sharing experiences in handling incident reports. The workshop will be bilingual in English and French. L'atelier sera bilingue en Français et en Anglais.

Target Audience: Educators, leaders and learners of all levels are welcome.

Eleftherios Soleas (Lead Author), Ashley Waddington (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: The rapid rise of Artificial Intelligence (AI) has transformed society, yet the education of healthcare providers in our field lags behind.1,2 Indeed, AI's potential is wholly predicated on having a health professional workforce that understands its fundamentals and its implications. In healthcare, where AI promises accurate diagnoses and personalized treatment, bridging the knowledge gap becomes vital.3 This workshop explores the challenges of AI education2, such as mitigating the emergence of self-proclaimed experts, who have reach but not grasp of AI; and the need for comprehensive training in AI ethics. Providing this training would ensure a health professions workforce that is able to ask insightful questions, draw key inferences, and ultimately successfully augment clinical practice with AI. Learners will apply their learning and insights at their tables and with the whole group. Whole and small group discussions will be facilitated. We will close with a whole group discussion of implications and the future of AI integration in healthcare and what the potential ethical pitfalls, promise, and emergent reliance on AI, Machine Learning, and Augmented Reality means for our world.

Instructional Methods: Our workshop will begin with a stage-setting micro-lecture (15 minutes) on dimensions of ethical considerations for successfully integrating AI into clinical practice and then embark on a series of case studies (60 minutes; across 4 case studies) of realistic scenarios that demonstrate some of the potential ethical challenges with AI implementation.

Target Audience: Healthcare professionals, faculty preceptors, policymakers, educationalists, and trainees in the health professions

Stephanie Moore-Lotridge (Lead Author)

Rationale/Background: Gamified learning-incorporating games, points, badges, or playful mechanics into education-has been shown to increase learner motivation, engagement, and acquisition of knowledge and skills in health professions education. Yet its adoption remains limited due to barriers such as time, resources, and lack of instructional design expertise. These challenges create a high "activation energy" that prevents many faculty from experimenting with game-based strategies. The rapid emergence of generative AI tools (e.g., ChatGPT, Copilot, ect) provides a timely solution, enabling educators to ideate, prototype, and implement gamified approaches with reduced creative and technical burden. This workshop leverages AI as a catalyst to democratize gamified learning design, ensuring that educators at all levels can harness these strategies in their own curricula.

Instructional Methods: This session will include an immersive game play among participants to foster collaboration and creation, with the goal of participants leaving the session with new skills and developed products. We will begin with a brief overview of gamified learning/terminology, its educational benefits, and examples of gamified interventions ranging in complexity. Facilitators will then demonstrate how AI can "lower the activation energy" by brainstorming game ideas, generating educational content, designing visuals, and organizing logistics. Using case examples ranging in design complexity and a library of example prompts provided by facilitators, participants will work in small groups to practice designing and implementing gamified learning with the help of AI. Concluding large-group discussions will present created products, discuss challenges with design, and review the importance of faculty oversight in ensuring pedagogical value and content accuracy.

Target Audience: Health professions educators interested in using gamified learning, regardless of prior game design experience.

Lisa St. Amant (Lead Author), Julie Johnstone (Lead Author), Melissa Kennedy Hynes (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Conflicts of interest (COI) and bias-whether conscious or unconscious-pose significant challenges to fair and equitable decision-making in medical assessment. These risks arise when an individual's or group's judgment regarding a learner is influenced by personal, professional, or academic relationships. Within Competence and Promotion Committees (CCs), mitigating COI and bias is particularly complex due to the diversity of faculty roles, interpersonal dynamics, and the variability of program structures across specialties and institutions. Without robust safeguards, COI and bias can compromise the integrity of assessment processes and perpetuate inequities in learner progression. Institutions must therefore establish clear, actionable procedures to identify, disclose, and manage both COI and bias, while ensuring that these guidelines remain feasible and adaptable across diverse training environments. This workshop addresses the need for structured, scalable policies by drawing on a literature review of best practices and an environmental scan of residency programs at the University of Toronto. The scan examined the types and frequency of COI encountered, mitigation strategies employed, and implementation challenges faced. Findings underscore the importance of ethical decision-making frameworks, annual declarations of COI and bias, and regular training in equity, diversity, inclusion, and anti-discrimination practices. Participants will engage with these themes through interactive exercises designed to promote fair, transparent, and accountable committee practices.

Instructional Methods: This workshop will incorporate a blend of brief didactic presentations and interactive, experiential learning. Presentations will provide theoretical grounding in ethical decision-making, conflict of interest (COI), and bias mitigation within medical education governance. Participants will engage in small-group discussions to analyze real-world COI and bias scenarios, identify risks, and propose context-sensitive mitigation strategies. Real-time polling will be used to explore participant responses to ethical dilemmas, followed by facilitated reflection to deepen understanding. Teams will collaborate to design adaptable policy elements-including disclosure protocols, bias recognition tools, and equity-focused training components-tailored to the structural and cultural contexts of their respective programs.

Target Audience: This workshop is designed for medical education leaders, administrators, program directors, and faculty members who participate in admissions, assessment, or advancement decisions.

Priya Chopra (Lead Author), Suzanne Cook (Co Author), Dorothy Bakker (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: The development of a new undergraduate medical education (UGME) curriculum at Toronto Metropolitan University School of Medicine (TMU SoM) provided a unique opportunity to embed the CanMEDS Health Advocate Role directly into our program. To achieve this, TMU SoM created the Community Advocacy Project (CAP). Anchored in a mission of social accountability, the CAP is an innovative service-learning initiative that integrates health equity, cultural competence, and advocacy skills. The CAP combines foundational lessons on social determinants of health, design-thinking, allyship, community needs assessment, and project management with extensive, meaningful community engagement. This workshop will share our experience in designing and implementing the CAP. We will guide participants through the key steps of developing a similar project, empowering them to translate theory into action. We encourage faculty teams to attend together to collaboratively design a project template for their specific context.

Instructional Methods: This interactive session is designed to be a practical, hands-on experience. We will begin with an overview of our framework, touching upon the application of the Health Advocate role, relevant accreditation standards, and core concepts like design-thinking and community needs assessment. Participants will then break into small groups for an immersive simulation activity, where they will use a guided template to design a community advocacy project tailored to their home institution. This activity will be followed by a structured debrief, incorporating think-pair-share and large-group reflection to share insights, provide feedback, and reinforce the integration of key concepts. The workshop will conclude with an action-oriented discussion focused on practical next steps, evaluation strategies, and adapting the roadmap to diverse learning environments.

Target Audience: This workshop is designed for faculty members, course directors, curriculum developers, and medical educators who are interested in creating or enhancing an advocacy-based service-learning project within their undergraduate medical education (UGME) curriculum.

Pierre-Luc Dazé (Lead Author), Marie-Hélène Girouard (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Depuis 20 ans, le Canada a vu se multiplier le nombre de campus décentralisés de ses facultés de médecine. Ces campus jouent un rôle important dans la capacité à accueillir un nombre grandissant d'étudiant et servent de moyens d'attraction pour les régions moins bien desservies médicalement. L'arrivée d'un campus décentralisé est souvent accueillie avec beaucoup d'enthousiasme et d'énergie. Avec les années, cette énergie diminue. Plusieurs facteurs influencent l'implication des cliniciens aux tâches professorale et viennent moduler l'engagement professoral. Cet atelier permettra d'explorer les facettes liées à l'implication pédagogique propres aux campus décentralisés, identifier les barrières ainsi que de partager ou créer des pistes de solutions à ceux-ci.

Instructional Methods: Cet atelier se déroulera principalement sous forme de jeux de rôle et de discussion en petits groupes suivis de plénières. L'animation se fera de façon bilingue mais les discussions de groupe et jeux de rôle se feront dans la langue de choix des participants. Les jeux de rôle permettront d'identifier les raisons freinant l'implication professorale des cliniciens suivi d'un retour en petits groupes, puis en plénière pour partager les constats. Une courte présentation magistrale, en français avec un support visuel bilingue permettra de clarifier les particularités des campus décentralisés et de dévoiler les résultats d'un sondage sur les marques de reconnaissance aux cliniciens-enseignants. Des discussions en petits groupes, suivis de plénières permettront d'adresser les freins à l'implication en partageant des solutions déjà existantes ou innovantes.

Target Audience: Toute personne impliquée de près ou de loin auprès de l'enseignement en campus décentralisé pré ou post doctoral ou auprès des affaires professorales.

Jean-Luc Dumas (Lead Author), Patrick Cossette (Co Author), Nathalie Caire Fon (Co Author), Zoe Chabot (Co Author), Julie Langlois (Co Author), Ahmed Maherzi (Lead Author)

Rationale/Background: La formation en santé a pour finalité de préparer des professionnels capables de répondre aux besoins réels des populations qu'ils desserviront. Or, l'évolution rapide des connaissances, la complexité croissante des compétences attendues et les transformations sociétales imposent de repenser les modèles de formation. Les approches traditionnelles, centrées sur l'équilibre entre savoirs théoriques et pratiques cliniques, montrent leurs limites face aux nouveaux enjeux : accès numérique aux connaissances, attentes accrues en matière d'inclusion et de bien-être étudiant, nécessité d'une vision globale de la santé et d'une performance sociale et sociétale tangible.
