Sean Tackett (Lead Author), Scott Wright (Co Author), Yvonne Steinert (Co Author), Cynthia Whitehead (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Canada embraces the concept of social accountability in medical education, but its medical school admissions processes result in disparities, which may cause misalignment with society's needs [Acad Med. 2025;100(3):388-99]. An alternative lens through which to consider the fundamental purpose of medical education may be useful to stimulate change. More than two millennia ago, Aristotle described "eudaimonia" as a striving to realize one's full potential - the ultimate goal of a good life. This concept has been revived as "human flourishing," most notably through the work of Harvard's Human Flourishing Program led by Tyler VanderWeele. The U.S.-based Kern National Network has also specifically proposed that flourishing serves as the "north star" in medical education [Med Teach. 2024 Dec;46(12):1539-43]. Flourishing encompasses all aspects of individual and collective well-being. It is relational and holistic, unlike traditional medical school admissions, which are generally individualistic and incentivize competition. This workshop will prompt participants to consider what it could mean for medical education to be guided by the concept of human flourishing. It will use medical school admissions as a concrete example and starting point for change.

Instructional Methods: Presenters will first introduce the concept of human flourishing and contemporary efforts to measure and study flourishing in medical education and more broadly. Participants will discuss in small groups what it means to flourish in medical education and practice. How can we know when students, educators, and health workers are flourishing? How might that relate to the flourishing of patients, communities, and broader society? Small groups will share highlights with the full group. Presenters will give a short overview on current medical school admissions in Canada. Participants will then discuss to what degree admissions are promoting flourishing throughout their processes and how admissions might improve. Small groups will share their most promising ideas with the large group and discuss next steps.

Target Audience: Flourishing is relevant to everyone; we welcome all participants.

Russell Franco D'Souza (Lead Author), Mary Mathew (Co Author), Krishna Mohan Surapaneni (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Artificial intelligence (AI) is rapidly transforming the landscape of medical education. From admissions and assessment to curriculum design and simulation-based learning, AI tools promise to increase efficiency, personalize learning, and broaden access. However, these innovations also raise pressing ethical questions related to equity, bias, transparency, accountability, and professionalism. For academic medicine, the challenge is not simply whether AI can be used effectively, but how it can be integrated responsibly in ways that uphold integrity, fairness, and social accountability in education. This workshop will provide participants with an opportunity to critically examine the ethical dimensions of AI in medical education and to co-develop guiding principles for its responsible use.

Instructional Methods: 1. Case analysis: Small groups will analyze scenarios such as algorithmic bias in admissions, automated grading, and AI-driven learning analytics, identifying key ethical concerns. 2. Framework application: Participants will apply ethical frameworks (e.g., justice, fairness, professionalism) to evaluate the impact of AI on learners and institutions. 3. Consensus-building exercise: Groups will collaboratively draft a set of principles for ethical and socially responsible use of AI in medical education. Two-thirds of the session will be devoted to active discussion and participant-driven activities.

Target Audience: Medical educators, faculty developers, curriculum leaders, students, and education researchers with an interest in AI, ethics, and innovation in medical education.

Judy McCormick (Lead Author), Marieke Kalkhove (Co Author), Christine Séguin (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Key features are defined as critical or essential steps in the resolution of a problem; steps in which candidates are most likely to make mistakes, and/or a difficult or challenging aspect in the identification and management of the problem in practice. Yudkowsky et al (2014) concluded that limiting checklist items to those affecting diagnostic decisions improved discrimination as well as internal consistency and generalizability. In addition, "thoroughness" checklist items do not reflect clinical reasoning ability. Since 2018, The Medical Council of Canada (MCC) has been using the "Key Features" scoring methodology in its performance-based assessments. After an iterative series of pilots, refinements, and data analyses, MCC has found that using Key Features checklists results in more reliable results, improved item discrimination, less cognitive load on examiners and less "noise" in scores. In an effort to retain expensively developed content, MCC will often "retrofit" long checklists to this shorter format.

Instructional Methods: Led by content developers from the Medical Council of Canada, this workshop presents a hands-on approach to refining your OSCE checklists. After a short didactic session on the background and approach to Key Features scoring, participants will work individually and in small groups to review and revise checklists, with coaching and feedback from MCC's development team.

Target Audience: Assessment leads, medical educators, OSCE examiners, OSCE content developers, and others interested in OSCEs

Meredith Young (Lead Author), Tim Dubé (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Much of the research generated in Health Professions Education (HPE) is the result of the interdisciplinary and interprofessional research teams. Despite the prominence of research teams in HPE scholarship, and continued calls for an increase in interdisciplinary and transdisciplinary research in the broader scientific field, little guidance is available for how to build, support, and nurture research teams. This workshop is designed to fill this gap, and provide participants with: 1) a grounding in the many types of research teams possible in HPE, 2) strategies for how to build and support effective research teams. Participants will leave with practical strategies, lessons learned, and resources to support the research teams in their scholarly practice.

Instructional Methods: This workshop will be interactive and problem-based with a balance of didactic and hands-on activities. Didactic lecture (10 min): Brief overview of the types of research teams in HPE scholarship. Think, pair, share (10 min): participants will identify the type(s) of research team(s) they participate in, and the strengths and challenges of these types of teams. Didactic lecture (10 min): General description of stressors and strategies identified through study of interprofessional and interdisciplinary research teams in HPE. Shared experience (10 min): Facilitated discussion of the stressors and strategies of team based research in HPE Small group discussion (30 min): When is it 'worth it' to work in a research team? What strategies could be put in place to better support your research teams? Panel (10 min): Our team experience and lessons learned from in research on research teams in HPE. Large group discussion (10 min): Group discussion focused on practical considerations for why, when, and how to effectively mobilize research teams in HPE scholarship.

Target Audience: Novice and intermediate level scholars interested in reflecting on how to best build, support, and nurture research teams.

Marcel D'Eon (Lead Author), Sean Polreis (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Delivering feedback or debriefing (henceforth to be called just "debriefing") is one of the most common teaching activities in medical education settings at all levels with great potential to influence learning for the better. Debriefing is also one of the most stressful activities for both teachers and learners. Coaching has been identified as an effective approach to implementing Competency Based Medical Education. Adopting a coaching approach (non-judgemental inquiry and learning-centred) can make debriefing sessions more effective and less stressful. Twice the University of Saskatchewan has successfully run a course of about 20 hours over five months to form and educate faculty and staff to use a coaching approach to debriefing. This workshop will engage participants in learning about the design principles used and in critiquing the course and specific modules.

Instructional Methods: Methods underpinning workshop design: • Cooperative learning: Analysis and critique tasks to be accomplished in small groups • Experiential learning: participants will experience one of the learning activities used in the U of S course. • A developmental approach (scaffolding and the Zone of Proximal Development) Outline and flow of the workshop: • Facilitators will provide a written overview of the U of S course with an explanation and some evaluation data. (20 min) • Small groups of workshop participants will explore one or more of the modules to analyze and critique. (30 min) • In the whole group, small groups will share their insights including strengths and suggestions for overall course design and delivery with discussion (30 min)

Target Audience: Faculty developers, learners at all levels, and leaders

Peter de Jong (Lead Author), Kimberly Dahlman (Co Author), Anna Karwowska (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: The role of the educator is continuously shifting, caused by the introduction of new educational methodologies and technologies, and society's changing expectations of future health care providers. The educator of today is more of a process facilitator and personal coach rather than a knowledge provider, creating a dynamic learning environment, mentoring students and peers, and contributing to educational scholarship. The educator of the future must therefore develop sufficient competencies and skills to engage effectively with current generations of students. This includes not only pedagogical expertise but also the development of leadership skills essential for navigating the evolving landscape of higher education. In this symposium, the presenters will share examples of faculty development opportunities designed to support faculty in their ongoing professional growth as educators and leaders.

Instructional Methods: In this symposium 3 presenters will address different aspects of faculty development. The presentations are followed by a moderated Q&A discussion with the audience.

Target Audience: All those involved in teaching health professions students at any levels

Meredith Young (Lead Author), Maia Siedlikowski (Co Author), Aliki Thomas (Co Author), Andrea Quaiattiini (Co Author), Sayra Cristancho (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Mapping a gap in the literature is more than an academic exercise; it helps frame the importance and value of a given research and scholarship endeavour. Yet, despite how often "gaps" are invoked in scientific publications, researchers are given little guidance on how to find, frame, and convincingly document them. This workshop addresses that challenge by introducing two complementary approaches: 1) how to rhetorically frame a gap using the problem-gap-hook heuristic to build a compelling rationale for a study, and 2) how to systematically document a gap through scoping review methodologies. Participants will leave with practical strategies, examples, and lessons learned from our efforts to map gaps in the HPE literature.

Instructional Methods: This workshop will be interactive and problem-based with a balance of didactic and hands-on activities. Didactic lecture (10 min): Brief overview of the problem-gap-hook heuristic and how to articulate the need for a given study. Think, pair, share (10 min): participants will identify and articulate the rhetorical gap that their work is designed to fill. Didactic lecture (10 min): General description of the purposes of scoping reviews to systematically document gaps in the literature. Shared experience (10 min): Description of methodological approaches developed to evidence and document a gap in the HPE literature Small group discussion (30 min): When is it 'worth it' to map a gap? What gaps should be addressed in HPE literature? Panel (10 min): Our team experience and lessons learned from engaging with gaps in the HPE literature. Large group discussion (10 min): Group discussion focused on practical considerations for why, when, and how to engage with gaps in the HPE literature.

Target Audience: Novice and intermediate level scholars interested in identifying, framing, and documenting a gap in the HPE literature.

Neil Osheroff (Lead Author), Yvonne Steinert (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Impostor syndrome is a psychological phenomenon in which individuals doubt their accomplishments and fear being exposed as frauds, despite clear evidence of competence. It is especially common among health professions educators, as they balance teaching, research, clinical practice, and leadership in environments that place high value on expertise. Psychologists describe several types of impostor syndrome: the Perfectionist, who sets impossibly high standards; the Expert, who feels they must know everything; the Natural Genius, who expects effortless success; the Soloist, who avoids asking for help; and the Superperson, who strives to excel in every role simultaneously. For educators, these patterns may appear as overpreparation, reluctance to admit uncertainty, or fear of being questioned by learners or peers. If left unchecked, impostor syndrome can lead to burnout, anxiety, and diminished performance. While impostor syndrome is often managed through positive self-talk and acknowledgement of achievements, this workshop will explore how it can also be embraced and leveraged for professional growth and career development. Self-doubt can foster humility, empathy, and a commitment to growth, qualities that enhance teaching, leadership, and mentoring. Practical strategies include normalizing vulnerability, engaging in reflective practice, seeking peer support, and reframing feedback as a tool for learning.

Instructional Methods: This workshop will examine the impact of impostor syndrome on the professional growth and career development of health professions educators. The session will begin with an interactive large-group session that introduces impostor syndrome, its different manifestations, strategies for mitigation, and the concept of embracing it to enhance professional growth and career development. (20-min) Participants will then engage in three small-group activities, followed by large-group discussions, to: Self-assess level and type of impostor syndrome. (20-min) Describe their experiences of feeling like an imposter and identify strategies used to deal with them (20-min) Discuss potential strategies to enhance professional career growth by embracing one's impostor syndrome. (20-min) The session will conclude with a summary and reflection. (10-min)

Target Audience: This workshop will benefit health professions educators at all career stages.

Jerry Maniate (Lead Author), Brandon Tang (Co Author), Babar Haroon (Co Author), Muhammad Mumdani (Co Author), Victor Do (Co Author), Lyn Sonnenberg (Co Author), David Wiljer (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Artificial intelligence (AI) is rapidly transforming health professions education, research, and care. While enthusiasm is high, many educators and leaders struggle with how to implement AI responsibly and equitably. Existing ethical frameworks often remain abstract and difficult to apply in the day-to-day realities of academic medicine. To address this challenge, we developed and validated the Health CARE-AI Framework, a set of nine principles for responsible AI in health contexts, alongside a practical toolkit that supports their integration into teaching, clinical decision-making, research oversight, and administrative leadership. This workshop provides participants with a structured opportunity to engage with these principles, explore case-based applications, and consider how to adapt the toolkit to their own institutional and professional settings.

Instructional Methods: This 90-minute session will combine focused presentation with active application. The first segment introduces the CARE-AI principles and the evidence supporting their development, grounding participants in a shared language for responsible AI. Building on this foundation, participants will work through structured case scenarios illustrating real-world opportunities and challenges in AI adoption. In facilitated small groups, they will analyze scenarios through the lens of CARE-AI principles, identifying both strengths and shortcomings. The final segment introduces the CARE-AI Toolkit, offering participants concrete strategies and resources to embed responsible AI practices in their own contexts. Two-thirds of the workshop will involve interactive engagement through case-based discussion and toolkit adaptation, with large-group synthesis to consolidate learning.

Target Audience: This session is designed for faculty developers, clinical educators, researchers, administrators, and health professionals who are navigating the integration of AI into academic medicine and health systems. Participants with varying levels of AI knowledge will benefit from the combination of conceptual grounding and hands-on application.

Maryam Amirrad (Lead Author)

Rationale/Background: A robust and reliable assessment of clinical skills is crucial to ensure that medical students possess the necessary competencies for optimal patient safety. Traditional clinical assessments lack standardization, are subjective, and cannot reliably evaluate a comprehensive range of skills. The Objective Structured Clinical Examination (OSCE) is a structured, standardized, and objective way of assessing clinical skills. By developing distinct, measurable stations, OSCEs can evaluate essential competencies, including history taking, physical examination, procedural skills, and professionalism. Although OSCE is a practical assessment tool, many clinical educators lack the knowledge and skills to develop an OSCE station. This workshop is planned to fill this knowledge and skills gap. Through this hands-on workshop, participants will acquire the expertise necessary to develop high-quality OSCEs tailored to their specific curricula and learning objectives.

Instructional Methods: 1. Interactive, concise presentations (10 minutes) with real-world examples while posing questions to the participants. 2. Small group discussions and brainstorming (15 minutes) by providing a concept and asking the small groups to discuss their diverse experiences and share their challenges. 3. Hand-on OSCE development exercises (60 minutes) by providing templates and clear instructions, the participants will • Develop an OSCE blueprint. • Write an OSCE station scenario for a clinical skill. • Design a scoring checklist related to their scenarios. • Draft examiner instructions for their station. 4. Summary (5 minutes).

Target Audience: Faculty members, clinical educators, curriculum directors, and SIM lab staff.

Melanie Henry (Lead Author), Batya Grundland (Co Author), Anna Loi (Co Author), Sandra Fournier (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: In alignment with national curriculum priorities set by the College of Family Physicians of Canada, this workshop will explore how collaborative, stakeholder-engaged approaches can be used to co-create competencies related to social accountability in family medicine education. Participants will learn iterative co-design processes that can shape meaningful clinical learning outcomes that go on to reflect the values of underserved communities.

Instructional Methods: Using a case study from the Department of Family and Community Medicine at the University of Toronto, this session will walk through the process of developing competencies for a community-based, socially accountable clinical rotation, co-created with patients, residents, and faculty. Participants will engage in hands-on, participatory methods used in the actual development process-including value mapping, consensus building, and stakeholder prioritization.

Target Audience: Medical educators, curriculum designers, program directors, faculty development leaders, and others involved in undergraduate and postgraduate health professions education who are committed to advancing health equity and social accountability.

Kristine Gibson (Co Author), Marc Zucker (Lead Author), Regina Macatangay (Co Author), Nicole Johnson (Co Author), Jonathan Gold (Co Author), Amal Khidir (Co Author), Lawrence Ma (Co Author), Jeremy Middleton (Co Author), Jeff Nosek (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Research over the past 10 years has noted that patient safety, interprofessional team functioning and physician well-being are all improved with the establishment of psychologically safe working environments.2 More recent studies have focused on the need to extend psychological safety to our medical education settings. 2 Unlike many areas in academic medicine where we can roll out change to both student and faculty simultaneously, creating psychologically safe clinical learning environments requires the development of faculty knowledge, skills and attitudes first. 3 Leadership from faculty is needed to set expectations for learning and model inclusiveness, intellectual curiosity and cultural humility rather than shaming and humiliating learners as a means to motivate learning.1 We cannot first coach our students to commit vulnerable acts if they will be met by reproach. This will only harm our learners and decrease their capacity to learn.3 This train the trainer workshop will assist participants in increasing their competence and confidence in the foundations of psychological safety and implementing changes in their home institutions. 1. Clark TR. The 4 Stages of Psychological Safety: Defining the Path to Inclusion and Innovation. Berrett-Koehler Publishers,; 2020. 2. Edmondson AC, Higgins M, Singer S, Weiner J. Understanding Psychological Safety in Health Care and Education Organizations: A Comparative Perspective. Res Hum Dev. 2016;13(1):65-83. doi:10.1080/15427609.2016.1141280 3. McClintock AH, Fainstad T, Blau K, Jauregui J. Psychological safety in medical education: A scoping review and synthesis of the literature. Med Teach. 2023;45(11):1290-1299. doi:10.1080/0142159X.2023.2216863

Instructional Methods: After an initial icebreaker, our workshop will begin with a brief large group didactic on theoretical models of, and the need for, psychological safety in medical education. Using Clark's 4 Stages of Psychological Safety Model, we will then work with participants to analyze two scenarios - one that is student focused and one that is faculty focused to identify concerning behaviours. Working in small groups, participants will develop psychologically safe behavioural solutions to each scenario. We will wrap up with a large group discussion on implementation of psychological safety initiatives and provide resources that can be used in faculty and staff development.

Target Audience: This workshop is relevant to all conference attendees who work in teaching environments and wish to improve their confidence in facilitating psychological safety in their home institutions

Christina St-Onge (Lead Author), Marcel D'Eon (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: The Canadian Medical Education Journal publishes in-depth explorations of critical issues surrounding the formation, education, and training of health care professionals in Canada and internationally, covering both pre- and post-licensure stages. Each year, the journal highlights trending topics and influential authors shaping the field. Since 2024, in collaboration with ICAM, the journal has been spotlighting select articles that demonstrate significant impact or hold strong potential to influence practice and research in health professions education.

Instructional Methods: This session will highlight published articles in the Canadian Medical Education Journal from 2023-2025. While we considered article reach (abstract views and downloads), we wanted to highlight articles in different sections, languages, methodologies, and institutions to showcase the wide variety of papers published in CMEJ. During this session, authors will present their work, outlining the key implications and potential impact their papers may have on medical education. The session offers a valuable opportunity for interactive discussion with leading authors at the forefront of health professions education. We will feature four articles selected to highlight a variety of sections, represent both official languages, and reflect diverse institutions. Each author will do a 10-minute presentation, followed by a 5-minute question-and-answer period. We will wrap up the session with a 20-minute open panel discussion, encouraging broader conversation among presenters and attendees. Session structure • Welcome/Introduction (5min) • Presentation 1 (15min) • Presentation 2 (15min) • Presentation 3 (15min) • Presentation 4 (15min) • Panel Discussion (20min) • Thank you (5min)

Target Audience: The target audience includes clinician teachers, medical education researchers, practitioners and professionals, administrators, decision-makers, medical schools, universities, and their trainees who are interested in discovering current trends and influential authors.

Heather Murray (Lead Author), Cheryl Hunchak (Co Author), Lisa Thurgur (Co Author), Evelyn Constantin (Co Author), Elisabeth Boileau (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Unprofessional behaviour in the health care workplace undermines working relationships and team dynamics. The current climate of health care resource scarcity, access to care challenges and provider stress and burnout contribute to workplace incivility. Physicians who exhibit incivility may have concurrent challenges with stress and mental health. Learners experiencing it report a wide range of negative impacts on their mental health and ability to learn and practice medicine, with downstream impacts on their career plans. Educational institutions with structures in place to identify these events can compassionately debrief events with involved learners, offer faculty with concerning behaviours insight into its impact, and connect faculty to supports and strategies for positive behaviour change. The Canadian Medical Protective Association (CMPA) offers a course for physician leaders, entitled "Strategies for Managing Unprofessional Behaviour." The course equips leaders with tools and skills to address and prevent incivility in the health care workplace.

Instructional Methods: This 90-minute case-based workshop excerpts key content elements from the above named course, facilitated by experienced physician educators from the CMPA and modified to explicitly support educational leaders. Participants attending this workshop will apply insights into the causes, contributing factors and impacts of unprofessional behaviour in two selected scenarios involving trainees. They will evaluate their skills by practicing conversations addressing this behaviour with an informal intervention (known as the "cup of coffee" conversation) based on the "Professionalism Accountability Pyramid" developed by Hickson et al. Workshop participants will have access to selected materials from the CMPA course during and following the session.

Target Audience: medical educators, residents, program directors

Joanne Hamilton (Lead Author), Heather Buckley (Lead Author), Latika Nirula (Lead Author), Katherine Wisener (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Despite widespread recognition that high-quality feedback drives learning, both learners and educators frequently describe fear or apprehension when giving or receiving it. We are hearing from increasing numbers of faculty who are feeling vulnerable and are expressing a reluctance to provide feedback to learners that could potentially lead to negative consequences, including damaged relationships, losing face, negative emotions, retribution, or feeling judged. While these can be valid concerns for both faculty and learners alike, vague or absent feedback delivered in response to these concerns can limit both the quality and quantity of feedback conversations and growth.

Instructional Methods: We will use a combination of small- and large- group discussions interspersed with brief theory bursts. Using case-based learning, participants will explore real-world scenarios to surface what might be needed to support psychological safety when providing and receiving feedback in their context. Participants will have opportunities to discuss and share experiences, strategies and approaches that they have applied at both individual and institutional levels. Participants will leave with practical tools to foster a culture where feedback is anticipated rather than feared, supporting competency-based medical education and learner and faculty well-being.

Target Audience: Medical educators, faculty developers, clinical preceptors, residents, and health-professions faculty seeking to improve feedback culture in their programs.

Nikhita Singhal (Lead Author), Chetana Kulkarni (Lead Author), Albina veltman (Co Author), Marijana Jovanovic (Co Author), Phillippe Hwang (Co Author), Raj Rasasingham (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Although equity-focused initiatives are increasingly being prioritized in health professions education, the question remains of how to most effectively build curricula with sustainable impact that move beyond tokenism into meaningful engagement of those with lived/living expertise. Despite recognition that work involving marginalized communities should not be completed without their input and recognition, educators continue to grapple with challenges including facing institutional barriers and resource limitations, experiencing discomfort navigating sensitive topics, and ensuring collaborations are mutually beneficial while authentically representing the perspectives of those involved. This interactive workshop thus aims to provide participants with practical, evidence-based strategies to engage individuals with lived/living expertise in building equity-focused curricula for health care providers.

Instructional Methods: The workshop will begin with introductions using a reflective think-pair-share exercise on positionality, followed by a group needs assessment using interactive polling to better understand participants' backgrounds and goals. A case study will be shared to help illustrate best practices in co-designing EDI curricula, including an overview of the youth engagement process for the project described; this will include reflections from the youth advisors involved in the project. Participants will then divide into breakout groups to share their own experiences with curricular co-design and to discuss unique considerations in developing curricula focused on Indigenous health, 2SLGBTQ+ populations, and newcomer/refugee health through a small group activity, followed by a facilitated large-group discussion focused on application of key learnings to participants' own contexts.

Target Audience: This workshop is designed for educators, clinicians, and trainees of all levels interested in effectively engaging individuals with lived/living expertise in EDI-focused educational design. It is suitable for both those new to curriculum development and those seeking to deepen their practice.

Beth-Ann Cummings (Lead Author), Jamiu Busari (Co Author), Jerry M. Maniate (Co Author), Kuper Ayelet (Co Author), Yvonne Steinert (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: The importance of inclusion - and the potential harm of exclusion - are being discussed in health professions education. For example, the AFMC's Culture of Academic Medicine Initiative (https://cami-icmu.ca/) and the CMA's Physical, Psychological, and Cultural (PPC) Safety in healthcare and medical education initiative (https://www.cma.ca/physician-wellness-hub/physical-psychological-and-cultural-safety) are two national efforts to promote respect and meaningful inclusion in our learning and working environments. Traumatic invalidation - when one's lived experience is denied or dismissed - is a threat not only to individuals, impacting self-worth, identity, and performance, but also to the collective. Learners, teachers, and healthcare practitioners work with patients and colleagues with differing worldviews and lived experiences; they also need to experience a sense of inclusion and belonging in order to be effective and fulfilled. The goal of this case-based workshop is to describe the notion of traumatic invalidation and explore strategies to overcome this negative experience for learners and faculty alike.

Instructional Methods: In a brief large group plenary and discussion, participants will be introduced to the framework of traumatic invalidation from the psychology literature (e.g. Linehan 2015). Subsequently, they will work through several cases in small groups, examining different scenarios while considering diverse contexts and perspectives. Returning to dyads and a large group discussion, participants will consider how the language of traumatic invalidation can help them understand the experiences of others and develop strategies to better meet the real-world needs of learners and faculty. More than 2/3 of the workshop will be interactive.

Target Audience: This workshop is designed for those involved in health professions education.

Natasha Ross (Lead Author), Aman Sium (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Equity is often addressed in academic medicine and clinical research as an isolated priority, rather than woven into the fabric of how institutions teach, conduct research, and lead change. This fragmented approach risks reinforcing systemic barriers rather than dismantling them. Project Chrysalis was launched to re-vision equity as a central and actionable principle in academic medicine. Through co-design with faculty, scientists, and educators, the initiative is developing a curriculum that integrates equity into research practices, teaching strategies, and institutional decision-making. Grounded in implementation and education science, this work highlights equity not as an outcome alone, but as a method, mindset, and practice essential to the future of academic medicine.

Instructional Methods: This 90-minute workshop will engage participants in experiential activities adapted from the Chrysalis curriculum. After a brief framing of key concepts, participants will take part in: • Equity-mapping exercise to surface gaps and opportunities in research and training systems. • Case study analysis of common equity challenges in academic and clinical contexts, where participants identify structural barriers and propose systemic solutions. • Peer consultation rounds to collaboratively problem-solve real-world equity challenges. Facilitators will intersperse mini-lectures, reflection, and consensus-building. Two-thirds of the session will focus on active participation, ensuring applied learning.

Target Audience: Faculty, scientists, educators, and institutional leaders in academic medicine and clinical research who seek practical and systemic approaches to embedding equity into their work.

Mohamed Hefny (Lead Author)

Rationale/Background: The rapid integration of artificial intelligence (AI) into medical education creates exciting opportunities and substantial ethical, pedagogical, and implementation challenges. Empowering educators, fellows, residents, and students to critically evaluate, adopt, and supervise AI-powered educational and clinical tools is essential for effective, humanistic, and safe learning environments. Simulation-based training (SBT) is highly effective in preparing medical professionals for technological innovation and complex decision-making, making it an ideal modality for developing AI literacy and responsible practice.

Instructional Methods: This 90-minute workshop uses explicit simulation framing: participants engage in immersive, role-play simulations that closely mirror real-world training environments to design, test, and critique AI-supported educational scenarios. Small groups collaboratively create mock AI interventions (like adaptive feedback models) and then participate in simulation-based role-play, revealing challenges such as algorithmic bias and data privacy risks. Facilitated debriefs draw out actionable insights on capacity building, ethical considerations, and practical strategies for implementation. At least two-thirds of workshop time is devoted to these active, hands-on exercises.

Target Audience: Medical educators, fellows, residents, and students interested in developing practical skills for evaluating and integrating AI responsibly within educational and clinical settings.

Erin Cameron (Lead Author), Paul GrandMaison (Lead Author), Christine Colin (Lead Author), Lisa Graves (Co Author), Sean Tacket (Co Author), Nicholas Torres (Co Author), Somaya Hosny (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Social Accountability (SA) represents a worldwide movement among medical schools to better respond to societies' actual and future health needs. SA is a beacon guiding their missions of education, research and service to better respond to the needs of communities they serve. Performance milestones linked to SA have been identified and schools around the world struggle to achieve them. Recognizing that accreditation is a powerful lever for change and improvement, standards derived from such milestones must be included in their accreditation processes. The ISAASC (International Social Accountability in Accreditation Steering committee) has been in place since 2022. Its goal is to "create a momentum towards a global initiative to ensure accreditation systems of medical schools are designed and used to better respond to priority health needs and challenges of societies today and in the future". Concrete actions have been and are currently implemented and lessons derived.

Instructional Methods: formal presentations, small and large group exchanges, summarizing sessions.

Target Audience: medical schools' leaders and faculty, people involved in accreditation, people interested and committed to SA.

Julianah Oguntala (Lead Author), Casey Schapel (Co Author), Seema Marwaha (Co Author), Meeta Patel (Co Author), Reena Pattani (Co Author), Justin Lam (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Despite increasing awareness of learner mistreatment, many faculty lack training on how to recognize and effectively respond to such scenarios. Competing responsibilities and time pressures further limit opportunities for faculty development. Video-based education, grounded in experiential and narrative learning theory, offers a flexible and efficient approach to modeling supportive faculty behaviors, helping faculty learn how to recognize and address learner mistreatment. Videos are also a dynamic and flexible pedagogical tool that can be modified for a variety of educational sessions. During this workshop, we will describe our Office of Learner Affairs' approach to creating four videos that illustrate learner mistreatment and model strategies for supporting learners. Participants will then actively engage in the process of brainstorming and storyboarding video concepts tailored to their own institutional context.

Instructional Methods: The workshop will begin with a 15-minute overview of our development process, including brainstorming, script generation, and storyboarding of our four videos. Then, for approximately 45 minutes, participants will work in small groups to develop scenes and storyboards based on provided cases or their own institutional examples of learner mistreatment. Groups will share their drafts in a 15-minute large-group discussion and receive structured peer feedback using interactive polling tools. The session will conclude with a 15-minute synthesis, including lessons learned from pre- to post-production, and showcase a sample of our completed institutional videos.

Target Audience: Faculty developers, medical educators, learner affairs leaders, and learners of all levels are encouraged to attend.

Kimberly Dahlman (Lead Author), Stephanie Moore-Lotridge (Co Author), Neil Osheroff (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Due to changes in health professions curricula and institutional expectations over the past decade, there has been a shift away from the traditional health professions teacher, whose duties were often limited to the role of lecturer or course organizer, to a professional educator with a multidimensional skill set. As a result, it is important for health professions educators to develop modern educator skills and qualities such as educational leadership, curriculum design/implementation, mentorship and coaching, competency-based assessment, evidence-based teaching/facilitation, and educational scholarship. This workshop will explore how the expansion of educator roles has had a profound effect on the professional identity of health professions educators and how personal, social, and structural factors related to professional identity formation impact the ability of faculty to thrive as resilient and adaptive health professions educators.

Instructional Methods: 1. Identify factors that supported their career pathways (20 min) 2. Discuss challenges and strategies to thrive as educators (20 min) 3. Document strategies to enhance their excellence as health professions educators (20 min) The session will then close with a summary and commitment to change exercise (10 min).

Target Audience: This workshop will benefit health professions educators at all career stages.

Heather MacNeill (Lead Author), Ken Masters (Co Author), Sofia Valanci (Co Author), Neil Mehta (Co Author), Jennifer Benjamin (Co Author), Anoop Agrawal (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: GenAI has the potential to increase efficiency and effectiveness in HPES, however issues such as copyright, privacy, critical reasoning, and bias need to be carefully considered. Framed within Boyer's model of Scholarship, this workship will cover practical and hands-on application of GenAI for HPER while debating ethical considerations and potential pitfalls.

Instructional Methods: This interactive workshop will provide opportunities for hands on use of GenAI in a scholarship context, as well as interactive discussion around ethical issues, through scholarship of discovery, integration, application and teaching. Participants will use tools such as research rabbit to refine their literature searches (Discovery). Next they will see how GenAI tools can help summarize large volumes of literature to understand and share key scholarship ideas through multimedia such as infographics, videos and podcasts (Integration). Next, participants will use tools such as Open Evidence to help aggregate and synthesize clinically relevant evidence to support HPE decision-making (Application). Lastly, participant will discuss and debate issues such as copyright. privacy and critical reasoning, and how both trainees should be prepared for GenAI use in HPES as well as how faculty should model use. (Teaching)

Target Audience: Health Professions Faculty and Learners interested in using GenAI in HPES

Jamie Read (Lead Author), Lisa-Jayne Edwards (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: This interactive workshop, run by the UK Academy of Medical Educators, will explore the importance of developing the next generation of medical educators for a changing global education landscape. Based on the Academy's Professional Standards, the session will explore how early career educators can develop their learning and teaching experience through professional recognition. Attendees will be encouraged to share their own routes into medical education and to consider how they can support the next generation of educators. Topics discussed in groups will include near-peer education, the value of postgraduate medical education qualifications and the chance to share personal experiences relating to education career journeys.

Instructional Methods: Following brief introductions by senior members of the Academy of Medical Educators, participants will be asked to work in small groups to explore their vision for the future of medical education and discuss ways in which the next generation of medical educators can be supported. Mentoring relationships between more senior and more junior educators will be developed and participants will map their own experiences against the professional standards of the Academy as part of professional recognition and the award of post-nominals.

Target Audience: Individuals of all backgrounds are welcome, but the workshop will be especially relevant for early-career educators wanting to develop their experience and networks and established educators who want to support the next generation of medical educators.

Michael Quon (Lead Author), Stephanie Quon (Co Author), Pamela Liao (Co Author), Erin Norris (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Improving physician well-being has become an important system-based priority in addressing Canada's shortage of health professionals. However, accessibility and other disability-related needs are not always included within the scope of Wellness or Equity, Diversity and Inclusion offices. In absence of institutional and cultural leadership focusing on disability inclusion in medicine, medical learners, faculty and leaders with disabilities continue to experience structural ableism, leading to discrimination and worsened health outcomes. The academic medical community plays a crucial role in co-creating and promoting strategies to ensure accessibility and inclusion by incorporating principles of universal design and developing readily available accommodation processes and supports in both work and learning environments. As the first points of contact for medical learners, undergraduate and postgraduate medical education programs are well-situated to lead efforts in advancing disability inclusion in medicine.

Instructional Methods: In this problem-based learning workshop, participants will be divided into groups, with each group receiving a different clinical vignette that illustrates an accessibility barrier in medical education. Discussion questions designed to promote reflection will also be circulated. Participants will work in groups to develop institutional strategies to advance inclusion for their particular medical learner. Groups will then come together to discuss their ideas to advance accessibility and disability inclusion in academic medicine.

Target Audience: This workshop is geared towards faculty and learners at all career stages who are interested in advancing leadership to improved accessibility, disability inclusion, and wellness in medicine and medical education.

Benoit-Pierre Stock (Lead Author), Annie Descoteaux (Lead Author), Mélanie Lussier (Co Author), Louis Lochhead (Co Author), Benoit Hurtel (Co Author), Zahra Jolibois (Co Author), Émilie Leblanc (Co Author), Marie-Pierre Codsi (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Since 2010, the Faculty of Medicine's Office of Patient Partners (OPP) has played a central role in embedding patient partnership into medical education. One major achievement has been the creation of the OPP Academy, a training and learning space supporting the development of patient partners' skills. While patient partnership has gained international recognition, few institutions have created systematic training programs tailored to patient partners. This workshop addresses that gap by presenting a structured approach grounded in instructional design, co-construction, and sustained engagement. It offers strategies for faculties, healthcare organizations, and educators seeking to advance patient partnership in health education. The Académie du BPP was established to strengthen the capacity of patient partners to contribute as educators and collaborators. Its mission is twofold: to sustain the motivation of experienced patient partners, and to cultivate new competencies aligned with curricular requirements. Over the years, it has become a living laboratory testing pedagogical methods such as thematic discussions, role-playing, and collaborative planning. Drawing on these experiences, the workshop will provide participants with tools to structure training while respecting reciprocity, inclusivity, and co-construction.

Instructional Methods: The workshop will begin with examples of training sessions, illustrating both effective practices and pitfalls identified through the PPO's experience. Participants will then work in small groups to analyze the specificities of their contexts, exploring real and perceived training needs. Supported by facilitators, they will design the outline of a session adapted to their settings. A plenary session will close the workshop, enabling participants to share insights, compare approaches, and consolidate reflections on implementing patient-partner training in medical education.

Target Audience: educators, decision-makers, students and patient partners interested in advancing patient partnership in medical education

Diane Bouchard-Lamothe (Lead Author), Maude Hébert-Lalonde (Co Author), Véronique Lapierre (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Après des décennies de formation destinée aux superviseurs et mettant de l'avant l'alliance thérapeutique et le climat de bienveillance en rétroaction, les étudiants ont maintenant accès à des formations sur le processus interactionnel de la rétroaction. Ce processus interactionnel requiert toutefois que l'étudiant soit réceptif aux échanges, afin d'assimiler et d'appliquer l'information partagée. Peu d'auteurs se sont attardés aux stratégies de niveau intrapersonnel comme celles proposées et fondées sur le modèle conceptuel H.O.T.E (humilité, ouverture, ténacité et explicitation). Les étudiants sont invités à adopter une posture d'apprentissage fondée sur un état d'esprit de croissance personnelle, nourrie par le désir de s'améliorer plutôt que de performer et d'utiliser le dialogue intérieur pour y parvenir. Qu'en pensent les précepteurs ? Maintenant qu'une telle formation existe pour les étudiants, doit-on informer ou former les précepteurs ?

Instructional Methods: Une séance interactive s'impose puisque cet atelier tente de préciser l'avis des précepteurs sur la faisabilité d'encadrer les étudiants désireux d'adopter un esprit de croissance personnel. Cette séance se déroule en quatre temps : • Courtes présentations du modèle conceptuel H.O.T.E. • Échanges en grand groupe : Points forts et points faibles du modèle, réalisme d'appliquer le modèle H.O.T.E.; répercussions possibles sur les précepteurs au plan personnel et de formateur. • Activités en petit groupe : élaboration d'un plan d'action réaliste pour les précepteurs face aux étudiants voulant développer un état d'esprit de croissance personnelle • Retour en grand groupe, plénière : partage des plans d'action

Target Audience: Précepteurs de niveau novice à expert et en devenir

Marc Zucker (Lead Author), Lynn Ashdown (Co Author), Darren Lauscher (Co Author), Melanie Clement (Co Author), Hosanna Au (Co Author), Julian Midgley (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Active involvement of patients ie. Patient/Caregiver Partners, in medical education recognizes that patients are experts in their lived experiences.1 Patient Partnerships enhance learner empathy, understanding of illness as experienced by patients, communication with patients and understanding of patient-centered care.1,2,3 Patient partnerships are important in enriching educational programs and fostering interprofessional relationships.1,3 There have been calls for the formal inclusion of Patient Partners in all levels of the medical education system, including institutional involvement.1,4 This exciting, interactive workshop will allow the participant to learn about patient partnerships while working directly with Patient Partners. This format recognizes and addresses the barriers that Patient Partners experience when attempting to work with educational organizations. 1. Lynn Ashdown & Linda Jones (2024) The time for patient partnership in medical education has arrived: Critical reflection through autoethnography from a physician turned patient, Medical Teacher, 46:10, 1322-1327, DOI: 10.1080/0142159X.2024.2308065 2. Morris Gordon, Simon Gupta, Debra Thornton, Michael Reid, Ernie Mallen, and Angela Melling. Instructional Methods: Teaching and learning modalities will include small and large group work, reflective activities, panel discussions and group brainstorming to engage the participant. The workshop will start with a short introduction to the history and principles of patient partnerships. These principles will then be used in active small and large group work to analyze teaching materials, demonstrating how patient partnerships can lead to better learning materials. Patient Partner facilitators will then lead a session on different models of patient partnerships that can directly enhance medical education programs and improve student learning. Enas El Gouhary (Lead Author), Salhab el Helou (Co Author), Amneet Sidhu (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Burnout is a multifaceted phenomenon with profound implications for individual's health, patient safety, retention, engagement and quality of care. Residents occupy a unique position as both learners and frontline healthcare providers, often experiencing heightened vulnerability due to limited autonomy, variable self-efficacy, and exposure to mistreatment. Conventional interventions for wellbeing frequently emphasize individual resilience or mindfulness strategies, without adequately addressing system-level contributors to burnout. The Joy-in-Work (JIW) Framework, developed by the Institute for Healthcare Improvement (IHI), shifts the paradigm by focusing on the presence of joy rather than the mere absence of burnout and by targeting system-level changes. Grounded in Maslow's hierarchy of needs, the framework emphasizes psychological safety as the foundation for engagement and utilizes appreciative inquiry, co-design, and quality improvement (QI) methodology. Leaders are positioned as facilitators of psychological safety and shared purpose, empowering frontline providers as "change makers" who can identify and address systemic barriers "the pebbles in their shoes" in a participatory management model. Our experience demonstrates that the JIW framework is adaptable to residency programs, within large clinical units, and within interprofessional teams, restoring agency and fostering team-based solutions.

Instructional Methods: This 90-minute interactive workshop will combine didactic and experiential learning. The session will begin with a brief overview of the definition, drivers and consequences of burnout, an introduction to the four steps of the IHI JIW Framework, the role of leadership in fostering psychological safety, and how to apply QI approach to wellbeing (30 min). Participants will engage in small-group activities to practice leading "What matters to you?" conversations, identify barriers to joy in their own contexts, and explore practical applications of JIW within residency and clinical settings (25 min). Using role-play and group discussion, participants will apply QI tools including defining a SMART aim, cause-and-effect diagrams, and the plan-do-study-act (PDSA) cycle to analyze barriers and design potential interventions (30 min).

Target Audience: Education and health system leaders, program directors, administrators

Cynthia Whitehead (Lead Author) Lucy Vorobej (Co Author) Robert Paul (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: History is gaining visibility in health professions education (HPE) as researchers recognize ways past practices and debates shape contemporary issues. Yet there is a risk that history may be treated merely as stories about "how things used to be," rather than as a rigorous method of inquiry. Historical research methods provide frameworks for interrogating evidence, situating practices within broader contexts, and testing narratives of progress. This workshop introduces participants to core historical methods, demonstrating their relevance to HPE. Participants will engage with historical sources and explore how historical inquiry can complement and expand existing approaches to HPE research.

Instructional Methods: This 90-minute workshop involves small group work examining three case studies paired with an interactive activity that practices a core element of historical reasoning. The first case study shares historical evidence about hospital labour to explore discourse of compensation in healthcare. This exercise highlights how historians begin with fragments and questions. Next is an activity on evaluating what counts as historical evidence. The second case discusses a historical study of a Toronto COVID-19 vaccine initiative and the use of traditional and non-traditional data sources (social media). The third case examines the history of education addressing physician workforce shortages. After a short presentation, participants compare earlier debates with today's concerns about the family medicine crisis, identifying continuities and changes. Participants will construct a context matters map, identifying conditions that shaped education planning. The discussion will focus on how historical studies can challenge assumptions of innovative solutions. The workshop concludes with a whole-group discussion, bringing together insights from the three cases, followed by an exercise where participants reframe one of their own research or teaching questions through a historical lens. The session ends with key takeaways and a resource handout.

Target Audience: Researchers, educators, and trainees in HPE who wish to expand their methodological repertoire. No prior training in history is required.

Muriel Slim (Lead Author), Kana Halić Kordić (Lead Author)

Rationale/Background: Effective feedback is essential to ensure better performance in HPECP (Lim et al., 2024), yet challenges persist. Learners are not always provided with regular feedback (Shafian et al., 2024), limiting its utility in fostering improvement. Additionally, educators often feel inadequately trained in delivering effective feedback and consider that learners don't receive it properly (Yaris et al., 2009), underscoring a significant gap in professional development. These issues hinder student learning, educator effectiveness, and patient outcomes, highlighting the need for more structured and impactful feedback strategies in HPEP. The International Federation of Medical Students' Associations (IFMSA) bridges that gap through capacity-building opportunities (e.g., Training Medical Education Trainers workshop). So far, over 500 students worldwide have participated as trainers, with workshop results showing an average increase in feedback skills, ranging from 3.82 to 4.88 on a 5-point scale.

Instructional Methods: Participants give and receive feedback through 5 pre-prepared scenarios set in various environments and circumstances. After each role-play, a peer reflection follows, where the other participants provide feedback. - Introduce IFMSA efforts mentioned in the background as examples of good practice. - Activity: Participants will receive case studies to analyse and discuss feedback techniques (provider and receiver), feedback settings perceptions, and challenges. - Share examples of successful practices and explore available resources for implementation. - Present IFMSA research findings based on literature review and take-home messages.

Target Audience: Healthcare students, early-career healthcare professionals, novice educators, and everyone committed to advancing a culture of constructive feedback in medical education.

Clara Dallaire (Lead Author), Antoine Payot (Co Author), Nathalie Orr Gaucher (Co Author), Carole Lambert (Co Author), Marisol Sanchez (Co Author), Vincent Jobin (Co Author)

Rationale/Background: Les instances gouvernementales, professionnelles, institutionnelles, ainsi que les établissements d'enseignement, sont préoccupés par les enjeux du curriculum caché (CC) et ses effets sur les apprenants, les enseignants et à plus large échelle, sur les professionnels œuvrant dans les milieux cliniques ainsi que sur les patients. La gestion du CC et ses effets sur les différents acteurs des milieux d'apprentissage présentent un défi de taille pour les établissements d'enseignement. Le présent atelier offre une façon constructive d'aborder le CC sur le terrain, dans une vision de démarche réflexive rejoignant les différentes réalités cliniques et s'adaptant aux singularités des nombreux milieux et des diverses personnes qui y contribuent.

Instructional Methods: Accueil et introduction (5 minutes) Partie 1 : Présentation conceptuelle (10 minutes) Objectif 1 : Reconnaître le curriculum caché en éducation médicale Partie 2 : Pratique en groupe (60 minutes) Objectifs 2 : Appliquer le concept de démarche réflexive pour analyser un cas vécu Objectif 3 : Utiliser l'outil réflexif proposé pour réfléchir au curriculum caché en éducation médicale Partie 3 : Discussion (10 minutes) Réflexions en groupe sur les possibilités d'utilisation de l'outil dans différents contextes Conclusion et messages clé (5 minutes)

Target Audience: Toute personne impliquée en éducation médicale du prégradué aux études postdoctorales et DPC

Michael Quon (Lead Author), Andrew Neitzel (Co Author), Erin Norris (Co Author), Daniel Myran (Co Author), Ananya Banerjee (Co Author), Stephanie Quon (Co Author), Dinesh Palipana (Co Author), Emily Cleveland Manchanda (Co Author)

Methods: We performed a cross-sectional study analyzing the content of all national, provincial, and territorial strategic plans to determine whether well-being, EDI and accessibility were identified as organizational priorities. Each document was examined by two researchers and discrepancies verified by a third. We contacted each association by email to inquire about any existing formal disability leadership roles with mandates to improve accessibility.

Results: 11 of 15 medical associations had strategic plans available for review. All plans prioritize physician well-being (11/11) and most address EDI (9/11). Accessibility is not referenced in any strategic plan (0/11). Email inquiries to 13 of 15 associations received responses. 0 of 13 associations' organizational structure includes a leadership position or office with a defined mandate to advance accessibility.

Discussion: Despite growing attention to physician health, disability inclusion continues to be overlooked by medical associations. Several organizations offer examples of how disability leadership can be mobilized, including the Royal College of Physicians and Surgeons of Canada, the British, American and Australian Medical Associations. All medical associations can learn from organizations that serve as models for effectively enacting accessibility initiatives, including the development of disability leadership and equitable policies.

Antonia Bellefleur (Lead Author), Jane Gair (Co Author)

Methods: A comprehensive qualitative search of the literature is underway across MedLine, Embase, and CINAHL databases. From an initial pool of 1,467 articles focusing on healthcare providers in North America, 370 duplicates were removed, resulting in 1,097 articles to be reviewed using Covidence. The review process is currently ongoing using thematic analysis.

Results: Preliminary findings suggest that burnout is a nuanced, multifactorial issue. Teaching was described as both a protective factor, and a risk factor, depending on the publication. This variation appears to stem from external factors such as (1) overall workload and institutional support, (2) appreciation and recognition of the educator role, and (3) the working alliance between educator and student.

Discussion: These findings will have implications for healthcare providers, educators, and administrators. Given the lifelong learning commitment in healthcare, all practitioners will engage in the educator-student working alliance throughout their careers. As a result, practitioners can influence burnout, both personally and among their colleagues. By examining the impact of clinical teaching on burnout, this review aims to inform strategies that reduce burnout within this unique population.

Amanda Roze des Ordons (Lead Author), Aliya Kassam (Co Author), Rachel Ellaway (Co Author)

Methods: An environmental scan of public-facing websites of Canadian medical schools (n=18) and partner organizations involved in the governance of resident training (n=42) was performed (December 2024 - June 2025). The initial focus on policy was expanded to include strategic planning, standards, guidelines, reports, educational documents and support resources.

Results: We found no RF-TIME specific policies at any Canadian medical school or partner organization. Thirteen schools briefly mentioned RF-TIME approaches within strategic planning (n = 3 schools), policies not focused on trauma (n = 9), guidelines (n = 1), reports (n = 3), educational resources (n = 3), and/or support resources (n = 8). Seventeen partner organizations included RF-TIME content in a limited way within strategic planning (n = 2 organizations), standards (n = 2), guidelines (n = 1), reports (n = 9), educational resources (n = 2), and/or support resources (n = 4).

Discussion: Canadian institutions involved in resident training demonstrate limited engagement with psychological trauma among residents. Policies and procedures are needed to address both the needs of residents with psychological traumatic injury and the many factors in residency education that can exacerbate the negative impacts of their injuries.

Tiffany Yu (Co Author), Athena Ko (Lead Author), David Martin (Co Author)

Methods: Nine psychiatry residents voluntarily enrolled in four weekly 90-minute group sessions facilitated by an attending psychiatrist and psychiatry resident using narrative medicine with various art modalities. Five interviews were conducted following completion of the group. Qualitative data was analysed thematically using mixed iterative and deductive themes. Quantitative data was gathered by questionnaires including the Maslach Burnout Inventory (MBI) administered at baseline, week 4 and week 12 after start of group. Results were analysed descriptively.

Results: Themes included (1) individual factors: reduced burnout, decreased isolation, increased fulfillment in work, (2) collective factors: deepened resident relationships, peer mentorship, resident cohort connections, and (3) patient care factors: increased capacity for listening to patient stories and intentionality in patient care. Eight out of nine (88.9%) participants completed baseline and week 4 questionnaires; six (66.7%) completed week 12 questionnaires. MBI scores demonstrated improvement in burnout (19.88 to 12.50) and personal achievement (34.13 to 39.33), with improvements evident at 4 weeks and sustained at 12 weeks.

Discussion: This group had a meaningful, positive, and sustainable impact on participating residents' sense of burnout, community, and fulfilment in patient care. Further iterations of the group and additional data can support more residents and systemic changes to incorporate such groups into medical curricula.

Adam Bizios (Lead Author), Rushil Dutta (Lead Author), Benjamin Kwan (Co Author), Jap Singh (Co Author)

Methods: Following PRISMA reporting guidelines, six databases were searched for primary research published after 2015, restricted to studies with Canadian residents, with no limitations placed on intervention type. Studies were excluded if they did not describe a specific intervention, used an inappropriate design, or were ongoing trials. The TIDieR checklist was applied to guide reporting and ensure consistent intervention descriptions.

Results: Out of 210 studies screened, seven met the inclusion criteria, with four studies containing 40 or more participants. Of these, three used an official burnout assessment tool to evaluate intervention impact, with two reporting reductions in burnout symptoms post-intervention. Interventions included duty hour and scheduling changes (n = 3), group or individual counseling (n = 2), mentorship (n = 1), and physical activity (n = 1).

Discussion: Studies on duty hours and scheduling report conflicting results, underscoring the need for further research on their effectiveness. Physical wellness programs, such as yoga, offer a potentially valuable avenue for promoting physician wellness given their feasibility and positive outcomes. Future studies should assess interventions with validated burnout scales and comparative designs to generate more transferable and generalizable findings.

Jyotsna Needamangalam Balaji (Lead Author), Krishna Mohan Surapaneni (Co Author)

Methods: A qualitative study using Grounded Theory methodology was conducted to explore perceptions of AI in PME. Thirty-five educators across clinical and preclinical specialties and forty-two medical students from first through final year were purposively sampled. Data collection involved seven focus group discussions with educators and six with students, complemented by semi-structured individual interviews. Transcripts were thematically analyzed to identify convergent and divergent perspectives. Strategies to ensure rigor included triangulation, systematic coding by multiple investigators, participant validation, and linkage to existing literature.

Results: Educators consistently emphasized that while AI supports adaptive learning and efficient feedback, it cannot replicate mentorship, clinical judgment, or professional identity formation. Students echoed the value of human educators, particularly in areas of ethical reasoning, empathy, and role modeling. Three cross-cutting themes emerged. First, Mentorship and Connection: educators and students agreed that human guidance is central to professional growth. Second, Ethical and Contextual Learning: both groups highlighted that AI lacks the ability to foster ethical decision-making or sensitivity to cultural and interpersonal context. Third, A Balanced Future: participants envisioned a hybrid PME model, in which AI enhances technical learning but educators remain responsible for fostering critical thinking, communication, and professionalism.

Discussion: Findings from both groups underline that AI should be viewed as a powerful complement rather than a replacement for educators. While AI offers precision and scalability, educators shape the human dimensions of medicine, including mentorship, ethical judgment, and learner identity formation. A hybrid approach that integrates AI capabilities while preserving faculty and learner interaction is essential for preparing physicians who are both technically proficient and humanistically grounded. OA-2-2 Facilitating the Implementation of Equity, Diversity and Inclusion Principles in Continuing Professional Development Using Artificial Intelligence

Branka Agic (Lead Author), Zhengbang Yao (Co Author), Mu He (Co Author), Kayle Donner (Co Author), Asha Maharaj (Co Author), Holly Harris (Co Author), Sanjeev Sockalingam (Co Author), Petal Abdool (Co Author), Eshana Ghuman (Co Author)

Methods: This project builds an AI agent grounded in the Health Equity and Inclusion Framework for Education and Training, an evidence-based approach for embedding an equity lens into the planning, development and delivery of CPD. An initial prototype was developed on the DIFY platform using OpenAI GPT models and refined by EDI, CPD and AI experts through an iterative prompt-engineering process using XML tags. The prototype will be tested by 10-15 CPD educators and evaluated using mixed-methods design. Data will be analyzed using descriptive and thematic approaches.

Results: Preliminary findings suggest that the AI agent can identify potential gaps and generate actionable recommendations to integrate EDI considerations into CPD. The prototype is currently being iteratively refined with formal evaluation beginning in November.

Discussion: By offering an innovative and replicable approach to addressing equity gaps in CPD using AI, this project will make a significant contribution to the field.

Amrit Brar (Lead Author), Ekambir Saran (Co Author), Diana Toubassi (Co Author)

Methods: We analyzed 1,000 images generated by ChatGPT-5 using the prompt "family doctor". Data generation occurred in August 2025. Images that lacked a clearly identifiable human face or exhibited visual distortions were excluded. Two reviewers independently classified each image by gender and race using the Chicago Face Database. Interrater reliability was assessed with Cohen's kappa. Distributions were compared with 2023 Association of American Medical Colleges data using chi-square tests.

Results: Of 1,000 images generated, 81.3% depicted males and 18.7% females, compared with workforce data showing 56.2% male and 43.8% female (χ2=255.9, P<0.0001). Racial distributions also differed (χ2=774.0, P<0.0001): White (66.1% generated by ChatGPT vs. 57.9% in the workforce), Black (24.6% vs. 6.2%), Asian (3.0% vs. 15.3%), Hispanic/Latino (5.2% vs. 7.4%), and Other (1.1% vs. 13.2%). Interrater reliability was high (κ=1.00 for gender; κ=0.87 for race).

Discussion: AI-generated imagery of family physicians fails to capture the true workforce diversity, with consistent overrepresentation of White, Black, and male physicians. These skewed portrayals risk reinforcing stereotypes, influencing patient trust and shaping trainees' sense of belonging in the field. Ongoing audits and deliberate bias-mitigation strategies are needed to ensure AI supports, rather than undermines, diversity in family medicine.

Mathilde Gallant (Lead Author), Mathieu Gauvin (Co Author), Avi Wallerstein (Co Author)

Methods: A randomized set of 225 multiple-choice questions from the cataract and refractive surgery section of the BCSC Self-Assessment Program was administered to seven LLM-based systems: ChatGPT-4o, ChatGPT-5o, Claude, Copilot, Gemini, Grok, and OpenEvidence. Accuracy was defined as the proportion of correct responses. Performance from 1,983 BCSC Self-Assessment Program users served as a benchmark.

Results: OpenEvidence achieved the lowest accuracy (76%, 95% CI: 70.4-81.6%). Claude achieved the highest accuracy (92.0%, 95% CI: 88.4-95.6%), followed by Grok (89.8%, 95% CI: 85.8-93.8%). ChatGPT-5o, Copilot, and Gemini each scored an accuracy of 88.0% (95% CI: 83.7-92.3%), while ChatGPT-4o scored 86.2% (95% CI: 81.7-90.8%). No statistically significant differences were detected among the general-purpose LLMs, whereas five models significantly outperformed OpenEvidence (P < 0.05). All general-purpose LLMs significantly outperformed BCSC Self-Assessment Program users, whose mean accuracy was 77.0% (95% CI: 75.1-78.9%), while OpenEvidence demonstrated comparable performance.

Discussion: OpenEvidence did not outperform general-purpose LLMs or exceed the performance of BCSC Self-Assessment Program users on refractive surgery multiple-choice questions. Contemporary general-purpose LLMs matched or exceeded user-benchmark accuracy. These findings suggest that alignment between system architecture and task structure plays a critical role in examination-style performance, and that domain-specific, retrieval-oriented design alone does not guarantee superior accuracy on multiple-choice assessments in ophthalmology.

Rashaad Bhyat (Lead Author), Sunand Kannappan (Co Author), Waldo Beausejour (Co Author), Owen Adams (Co Author)
