Jinelle Ramlackhansingh (Lead Author), Philip Chan (Lead Author), Hector Earnshaw (Co Author), Sony Tene (Co Author)

Methods: A mixed-methods study will be conducted during scheduled MMIs in December 2025 for the 2026/27 intake. Participants will be invited to complete a survey and participate in a focus group discussion about their experience with the group interview. The theoretical frameworks of Goffman and Bourdieu will be used to explore influences of performance and symbolic capital. Group station scores will be compared with other MMI stations by logistic regression.

Results: We can conclude whether scores achieved on the group station correlated to any of the individual stations, or whether it contributes a unique element to the MMI. Focus group findings will concentrate on aspects of applicant acceptability, particularly if the group station was regarded as fair, different or relevant to their selection.

Discussion: The research evaluates whether the group station adds value to the MMI selection by offering unique assessments such as teamwork, listening, reflection, and the ability to respond to other viewpoints. These qualities are essential for a good doctor but are not tested in the standard MMI. Applicants' views will inform the influence of background and education on their performance, and ultimately on the validity of the group station in medical selection.

Sierra Land (Lead Author), Jordyn Linders (Co Author), Kady Carr (Co Author), Bradley MacCosham (Co Author), Geneviève Lemay (Co Author), Claire Kendall (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a cohort study of all 183 first-year MD students admitted in 2023. Student postal codes were linked with neighborhood socioeconomic indicators from the Ottawa Neighbourhood Study. Sociodemographic variables (race, language, parental education, income, and occupation) were drawn from the Ontario Medical Student Applicant Service Equity, Diversity, and Inclusion survey. Descriptive comparisons were made with Ottawa regional and Canadian census data.

Results: Among students, 74.9% were from Ontario, yet only 22.4% were from our priority region. 28.2% grew up in the two most disadvantaged quintiles versus 51.2% in the most advantaged. Students reported greater diversity in non-official first languages (19.7% vs. 1.1%), second-generation status (43.7% vs. 17.9%), and non-White racial identities. However, Black students were underrepresented (4.4% vs. 6.4%) and Indigenous students likely underrepresented due to Census underreporting (4.4% vs. 3.4%). Students disproportionately came from higher-income households (32.8% vs. 13.2%) and parents with higher educational attainment and professional occupations, particularly in education (30.1% vs. 16.5%) and health (15.8% vs. 7.5%).

Discussion: Our findings highlight disparities in equity and regional representation, despite gains in linguistic and cultural diversity. Linking admissions and community data offers a model for socially accountable admissions and monitoring to ensure physician workforce alignment with population needs.

Sierra Land (Lead Author), Jordyn Linders (Co Author), Hailey Land (Co Author), Kady Carr (Co Author), Geneviève Lemay (Co Author), Claire Kendall (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a scoping review using Arksey and O'Malley's methodological framework and Joanna Briggs Institute guidance. MEDLINE, Embase, Web of Science, ERIC, and Education Source were searched from inception to August 2024. Eligible studies examined MD admissions policies explicitly incorporating social mission objectives and reported selection or enrollment outcomes. Two reviewers independently completed screening and data extraction. Results were synthesized descriptively, categorized inductively, and reported according to PRISMA-ScR guidelines.

Results: Seventeen studies published between 1994 and 2022 were included, spanning North America, Australia, Oceania, Europe, Africa, and the Caribbean. Three major categories of social accountability strategies emerged: (1) geographic and practice location policies targeting rural and underserved applicants, often improving local retention; (2) sociodemographic equity pathways for applicants from Indigenous, low-income, racialized, and marginalized groups, with evidence of increased representation; and (3) workforce composition initiatives focused on recruiting future primary care and generalist physicians. Implementation challenges included inconsistent alignment with institutional missions and limited tracking of long-term outcomes.

Discussion: Social mission-driven admissions can enhance physician diversity, rural representation, and alignment with community needs. Their success requires sustained investment, institutional commitment, and integration across the medical education continuum.

Jessica Bennett (Lead Author), Dr. Laura Stovel (Co Author)

Methods: Two Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) were identified for the MD Ambassador Program: number of prospective medical students engaged and number of volunteers recruited. Outreach was categorized into three data points: urban high school presentations, rural high school presentations, and on-campus events. Within each category, the demographic and socioeconomic backgrounds of program participants and volunteers were assessed.

Results: There was a marked increase in rural and urban high school presentations completed over time corresponding to an increase in monetary support for the program from the University of Alberta. Since receiving funding, MD Ambassadors has been able to improve student and volunteer participation across all three outreach modalities, and increase the percentage of students from underrepresented backgrounds receiving outreach annually.

Discussion: Faculty support of high school and undergraduate outreach programs is essential in providing students with tools to make medicine a career. Over the past 2 years, the MD Ambassador Program has improved outreach to rural students and increased volunteer engagement, increasing the number of students reached annually and the number of volunteers engaged both by factors of almost 10-fold.

Peggy DeJong (Lead Author), Kelly Moore (Co Author), Chanmeet Kaur (Co Author), Eugenia Piliotis (Co Author)

Methods: Thresholds are set annually for academic metrics at levels that align with the potential for success in medical school. All applicants who meet thresholds are placed in an early phase lottery (Qualified Applicant Randomization Selection - QARS). QARS is used to randomly select qualified applicants to proceed to the Mini Medical Interview (MMI). Based on performance on the MMI, top-ranked applicants proceed to the panel interview and file review.

Results: QARS allows us to set more reasonable thresholds for academic metrics. Progression from QARS to an offer of admission requires excellent performance on the MMI, panel interview and file review. The class of 2029 is the first class to go through this renewed admissions process. 35/138 students in the class of 2029 would have been removed in previous years by artificially elevated thresholds. However, the average GPA and average MCAT score of the class of 2029 are essentially the same as the class of 2028.

Discussion: QARS allows us to consider a larger number of qualified applicants for MMI. Since applicants still require strong academic abilities, and must perform well on MMI, panel interview and file review, it is not surprising that the metrics of the class of 2029 are comparable to the previous year. However, the new admissions process provided an opportunity to qualified applicants who previously would have been screened out early in the process.

Michelle Pham (Lead Author), Armeen Nabavi (Co Author), Alison Darnley (Co Author), Katarina Padilla (Co Author), Rachel Ellaway (Co Author), Aliya Kassam (Co Author), Eric Chan (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a content analysis of all Canadian residency programs in family medicine, pediatrics, and emergency medicine. Programs were contacted by email for information on suicide-related curricular content, teaching methods, and duration. Responses were analyzed descriptively.

Results: Among 24 program respondents, 12 (50%) reported providing formal suicide intervention training, 11 (46%) reported no such training, and 1 (4%) declined participation. Of programs reporting formal training, content most frequently addressed suicide risk assessment (n=7), patient management (n=6), and epidemiology (n=5). Other themes included comorbidities (n=5), safety planning (n=4), definitions and concepts (n=4), risk factors and warning signs (n=4), documentation (n=3), and Mental Health Act (n=3). Training was typically limited to a single lecture or day of teaching. All programs used didactic instruction (n=12), with two also including simulation training.

Discussion: Half of Canadian residency programs surveyed offered some form of formal suicide intervention training in addition to clinical bedside training; however, teaching was generally brief, all didactic, and varied across specialties. These findings highlight opportunities to incorporate experiential, evidence-based approaches and standardize suicide intervention curricula to better prepare residents to support at-risk patients.

Joanne Hamilton (Lead Author), Madison Peeler (Co Author), Vanessa Benson (Co Author), Christen Rachul (Co Author), Maury Pinsk (Co Author), James Butler (Co Author)

Methods: A two-stage multi-methods review was performed: Gap Analysis: Our curriculum was evaluated against national standards and local priorities. Curriculum Audit: All 2024/2025 curricular materials were reviewed for instructional design, inclusive pedagogy, and generalist competencies. Two reviewers coded data; discrepancies were resolved by consensus. Comments on materials reviewed were grouped thematically.

Results: Our gap analysis revealed deficiencies including in areas such as clinical skills and chronic care. Gaps arose more frequently in areas of existing bias (e.g. women's health, black health, anti-oppression) whereas redundancies were more often seen in areas of clinical medicine or areas of instructor interest. Our audit flagged criteria scoring below 80% on an aggregate score. Common instructional design deficiencies related to content density and accessible design. Inclusive pedagogy was generally present but fell short in representing clinical relevance and population diversity (75.4%). Where generalist competencies were relevant, there was moderate alignment with criteria, however, materials generally lacked emphasis on doctor-patient relationships (77.3%) and team-based care (59.1%).

Discussion: The needs assessment identified strengths and innovations but exposed gaps in inclusivity and generalist training. These findings are informing faculty development, integrated curricular threads, and curriculum revision-offering a replicable model for socially accountable curriculum renewal

Methods: Parallel student and administrator surveys, aligned with Health Quality Ontario Gender-Affirming Care Quality Standard, were disseminated via CFMS networks and direct outreach. Methods: Residents who graduated in the last three years (i.e., ECPs) were invited to participate in our semi-structured interviews. Participants were presented with the list of Royal College TTP competencies and asked to broadly reflect on their most/least valuable competencies, and to share their perceived gaps in current TTP competencies (if any). Taylor Orchard (Lead Author), Alexa Hryniuk (Co Author), Jason Peeler (Co Author)

Methods: The curriculum was developed for second year medical students at a Canadian accredited medical program. Fitts and Posner's model of motor skill acquisition guided the development, targeting the cognitive stage of learning. Cognitive load theory principles were applied to reduce extraneous load and enhance schema development.

Results: The CLC consisted of three asynchronous modules on the shoulder, knee and spine - common sites for MSK injury. Each module used a multimodal approach integrating didactic lectures, computer-based resources and standardized notes packages. Relevant anatomical structures were reviewed and physical examination skills were chunked by region and common injury types to promote progressive schema development. Case-based formative and summative assessments were included to evaluate student knowledge and provide feedback.

Discussion: This innovative, evidence-based CLC addressed critical gaps in MSK training, and incorporated previously identified curricular elements that enhance MSK knowledge retention. Future work will implement the curriculum and conduct a repeated measures mixed-method interventional study to evaluate the efficacy of the curriculum on students' MSK knowledge and confidence.

Darshana Seeburruth (Lead Author), Eunice Aluko (Lead Author), Serena Dienes (Co Author), Muskaan Sachdeva (Co Author), Mehnaz Munir (Co Author), Samantha Bestavros (Co Author), Sameh Hanna (Co Author), Marissa Joseph (Co Author)

Methods: We audited the University of Toronto's undergraduate dermatology curriculum from 2019 to 2025, with two independent reviewers categorizing images as light skin (LS, Fitzpatrick I-III) or SOC (Fitzpatrick IV-VI). First-year students completed surveys after a one-week dermatology block, assessing diagnostic accuracy and confidence in diagnosing common conditions in LS and SOC.

Results: SOC images increased from 3.7% (19/513) in 2019 to 26.4% (221/836) in 2025 (p<0.0001). SOC diagnostic accuracy improved from 84.0% to 93.5% (p<0.0001). Confidence in SOC diagnoses rose from 57.0% to 76.8% (p<0.0001). By 2025, 91.4% felt SOC was adequately represented, 85.7% felt comfortable diagnosing across diverse tones, and 94.4% reported SOC content sparked meaningful discussions.

Discussion: Increasing SOC representation enhances diagnostic accuracy, confidence, and satisfaction among learners, emphasizing the need to diversify curricula to advance equitable care.

Kieran Chalmers (Lead Author), Andy Deprato (Co Author), Mindy Baroody (Co Author), Justin Gilbert (Co Author), Phuong Huynh (Co Author), David Kanter Eivin (Co Author)

Methods: Two evening events were held in the 2024/2025 academic year with two more events scheduled for 2025/2026. Clinical Talks invited 5 to 10 minute reflections on clinical experiences, while Research Talks featured oral presentations and poster displays of ongoing or completed projects. Events were student organized, used existing campus space, and were promoted through simple advertising. Scheduling and brief abstract submissions supported visibility and accountability.

Results: Participants included students from all years, with more than half of the cohort transitioning from pre-clerkship to clerkship participating. The structured format enabled follow up, fostered cross year research connections, and consistently strengthened campus community.

Discussion: Peer-led Clinical and Research Talks are feasible, low cost, and easy to replicate. They prepare students for higher stakes venues, improve communication skills, and strengthen community. By sharing our approach and educational insights, we aim to enable other regional campuses to adopt this model.

Dominique Elien Massenat (Lead Author) Viriginie Rouhana (Co Author) Laura Muldoon (Co Author) Sophie De Roock (Co Author)

Methods: During the winter semester, 177 second-year students across French and English streams completed formative quizzes linked by QR codes for each lecture. Each quiz consisted of 3 questions and lasted five minutes. Completion of both the pre and post quiz indicated class attendance. At semester's end, students who completed all quizzes received a 2% participation grade toward their overall unit mark, independent of quiz scores.

Results: Attendance, averaged 89.0% in the French stream and 89.5% in the English stream, with minimal unauthorized absences. Educational gains were evident: English stream scores increased from 85% to 96% (+10%), while French stream scores rose from 81% to 94% (+13%).

Discussion: This pilot demonstrates that low-stakes, formative quizzes are both feasible and effective for supporting attendance and comprehension in social medicine curricula. The observed gains suggest that such assessments foster engagement and provide measurable learning benefits. By reinforcing socio-cultural competencies essential to patient care, this approach offers a scalable, evidence-based strategy for integration into pre-clerkship medical education.

Melissa Bouhraoua (Lead Author), Laura M. Drudi (Co Author), Joudy Charbaji (Co Author)

Methods: A PubMed search was conducted from inception to February 1, 2025 using MeSH terms related to gender equity, leadership, and diversity among female surgeons in vascular surgery. Articles in English and French, including original research, reviews, and commentaries, were included. Articles addressing gender equity, leadership, mentorship, and representation in vascular surgery were included. Findings were categorized across training, workforce, pay parity, leadership, authorship, performance, and mentorship.

Results: A total of 57 relevant articles were identified. Barriers identified include inflexible training pathways, underrepresentation in leadership, unequal access to mentorship, pay disparities, and a professional culture that often prioritizes work over personal responsibilities-factors that disproportionately impact women and contribute to burnout and attrition. Intersectional gaps, particularly for women of color, remain underexplored. Barriers include inflexible training pathways, limited mentorship opportunities, harassment, pay disparities, and underrepresentation in senior authorship and leadership positions. Burnout and attrition rates were higher among women, with intersectional gaps for women of color largely unexplored. Encouragingly, initiatives such as integrated residencies, diversity task forces, mentorship programs, and editorial leadership reforms have begun to improve recruitment and visibility, though retention challenges persist.

Discussion: Achieving gender equity in vascular surgery requires targeted interventions, including structural reforms, dedicated mentorship programs, and inclusive leadership initiatives. Promoting female representation is not only an equity issue but a strategic imperative to foster innovation, enhance patient care, and strengthen the future surgical workforce.

Daniela Keren (Lead Author), Martina Kelly (Co Author), Taryn Taylor (Co Author), Lorelei Lingard (Co Author), Pim Teunissen (Co Author)

Methods: We employed narrative methodology, guided by Catherine Riessman's narrative inquiry framework. Physicians across Canada who had taken illness-based leave during medical school or residency were recruited via professional networks and social media. Seven participants completed in-depth, semi-structured interviews. Interviews served as co-constructed narrative spaces, where storytelling unfolded dynamically between researcher and participant. They were transcribed verbatim and analyzed inductively for both content and structure through iterative team discussions.

Results: Three participants took leave during medical school and four during residency, for illnesses including malignancy, mental illness, rheumatological conditions, traumatic brain injury, and movement disorders. Across varied contexts, common narrative arcs emerged: (1) Identity disruption, as illness fractured trainees' sense of competence and resilience; (2) Shifting protagonists, where education leadership assumed control of their narratives; (3) Isolation, stemming from disrupted cohorts and the need to justify their illnesses; (4) Individual needs versus cultural expectations, with reliance on informal workarounds highlighting systemic opacity; and (5) Finding purpose, but not peace, where illness reshaped values and fostered empathy, but coexisted with disillusionment.

Discussion: Illness-based leaves expose tensions between medical culture's ideals of resilience and grit, and the realities of vulnerability. Transparent policies and cultural change are needed to support ill trainees better, and to recognize vulnerability as a legitimate dimension of professional identity.

Colleen Davison (Lead Author), Heather Braund (Co Author), Giselle Valarezo (Co Author), Ashley Waddington (Co Author), Josh Li (Co Author), Sarah Funnell (Co Author), Wiley Chung (Co Author), Monakshi Sawhney (Co Author), Stephanie Nixon (Co Author), Afolasade Fakolade (Co Author), Eleftherios Soleas (Co Author)

Methods: Members from QHS developed the upstander simulation session and accompanying guide. The committee hosted a pilot session and collected feedback from participants using a survey. The guide includes information on conducting pre-brief and debrief; multiple simulation scenarios, reflection questions, and additional resources on how to be an upstander.

Results: 14 people completed the survey. All participants reported that they were able to identify specific strategies for being an upstander when discrimination occurs and how to listen with empathy to learners/trainees with different life experiences. Some indicated they were less comfortable debriefing learners/trainees after a traumatic encounter with a patient which suggests an area for ongoing work. Many participants shared that the programming was a valuable learning experience, and they would use the examples. The committee used this feedback to revise the guide and adapt the cases for use in other programs.

Discussion: The program continues to be offered across QHS with the aim of responding to discrimination and proactively shaping the future of healthcare education that centres on compassion, accountability, and justice. By sharing our experience, we will inspire educators and institutions to expand efforts that prioritize justice in health professions education.

Krishna Mohan Surapaneni (Lead Author)

Methods: Final and pre-final year medical students participated in a 90-minute interactive session introducing the concept of microaggressions. Instruction included short modules, case-based scenarios, and small group discussions to explore recognition, response strategies, and preventive approaches. Students then engaged in a four-week structured reflection exercise, documenting experiences, learning points, and planned future actions. Reflection notes and in-depth interviews were thematically analyzed to identify patterns in awareness, barriers, and strategies.

Results: Three major themes emerged. First, Expanding Awareness: students reported clearer recognition of the diverse forms and impacts of microaggressions. Second, Navigating Barriers: hierarchical dynamics, lack of confidence, and fear of negative consequences limited their willingness to intervene. Third, Developing Responses: students identified inclusive language, non-confrontational interventions, and peer support strategies to address incidents while maintaining professional relationships. Across reflections, 17 actionable approaches were described and categorized.

Discussion: Structured teaching on microaggressions, supported by case-based discussion and guided reflection, enabled students to critically examine their experiences, acknowledge barriers, and generate practical strategies for intervention. These findings highlight that dedicated sessions can foster upstander readiness, empower learners to challenge exclusionary behavior, and contribute to more inclusive clinical learning environments.

Rebecca Gill (Lead Author), Dr. Preston Smith (Co Author)

Phillip Staibano (Co Author), Akanksha Guleria (Lead Author), Alex Thabane (Co Author), Moad Alsefaou (Co Author), Tyler Mckechnie (Co Author), Ashlyn Chou (Co Author), Goran Calic (Co Author), Mohit Bhandari (Co Author)

Methods: We systematically searched MEDLINE, Embase, and Web of Science for articles describing surgical innovation frameworks. Screening, full-text review, and data extraction were performed independently and in duplicate, with a third-party reviewer resolving discrepancies. We evaluated each framework on the following domains derived from two existing innovation models: ideation, risk assessment, implementation strategy, training and education, monitoring and evaluation, stakeholder involvement, and scalability and sustainability.

Results: We identified 13 surgical innovation frameworks. Many were developed through consensus-based methods (n=6/13, 46%) and five were iterations of a single framework (IDEAL). The domains most adequately addressed were: risk assessment (n=10/13, 77%), stakeholder involvement (n=10/13, 77%), implementation strategy (n=8/13, 62%), and monitoring and evaluation (n=8/13, 62%). The domains with the least-sufficient guidance include: ideation (n=7/13, 54%), training and education (n=7/13, 54%), and scalability and sustainability (n=6/13, 46%). No framework provided adequate guidance on every domain of the surgical innovation process.

Discussion: The current surgical innovation frameworks are inadequate, particularly with respect to the ideation, training, and long-term scalability aspects of the innovation process. More comprehensive frameworks, or adaptations of existing ones, are needed to support innovation from conception to sustainable implementation.

Stacie Carey (Lead Author), Ryan Brydges (Co Author), Ninon Crestois (Co Author), Sanjeev Sockalingam (Co Author)

Methods: Three initial program theories (IPTs) were developed with key stakeholders. We completed iterative data collection and analysis using document review and interviews with program architects, education and clinical leaders, and frontline staff (n=22). We tested and refined the original IPTs, using realist techniques of configurational analysis and retroductive reasoning.

Results: Our analysis produced three refined and four new program theories. Refined theories explained that: (1) organizational policies set expectations for knowledge application; (2) regular opportunities to manage patient escalations enable practice of learned skills; and (3) embedded facilitators tailor knowledge to clinical contexts. Emerging theories indicated that: (4) perceived alignment with professional role increases likelihood of applying knowledge in practice; (5) unit leaders shape which skills are applied; (6) organizational tools support transfer; and (7) clear accountability structures promote consistent application in practice settings.

Discussion: Our study demonstrates how educational interventions and organizational structures jointly and uniquely enable knowledge transfer in naturalistic practice settings. Realist evaluations show how educators benefit from unveiling and understanding how multiple 'program theories' cause variation in which mechanisms support or hinder KT interventions.

Nivetha Chandran (Lead Author), Andrew Petrosoniak (Co Author), Ryan Brydges (Lead Author), Aradhana Tewari (Co Author), Lindsay Beavers (Co Author), Kristen Sampson (Co Author), Christine Léger (Co Author), Nazanin Khodadoust (Co Author)

Methods: We applied a logic model evaluation framework to assess four years of activity in Unity Health Toronto's established Translational Simulation Program (TSIM). Researchers from outside the program interviewed 10 internal team members and 14 healthcare partners to explore project selection, implementation, and outcomes. We used a team-based consensus approach to develop an integrative logic model framework describing the impacts and challenges of the program.

Results: Our analysis reflected experiences from 11 completed projects. Early collaboration during project intake was critical, but occasionally slowed progress. Establishing shared conceptual understanding and clear roles at the outset was essential. Mutual adaptability between the simulation team and partners emerged as a key factor in successful implementation. Program outputs primarily consisted of simulation reports, which generally met expectations; however, several partners sought more detailed, actionable analyses. Mid- to long-term outcomes varied by project goals, including workflow changes, policy updates, and enhanced team performance.

Discussion: Grounded in foundational documents that defined concepts and linked them to actions, our evaluation generated data-driven recommendations for implementing and sustaining translational simulation programs. Findings informed both local refinements and generalizable principles: 1) flexible engagement tailored to partner context, 2) clear definitions of roles and objectives, 3) structured communication across all phases, and 4) consistent follow-up to support sustained system change.

Asiana Elma (Lead Author), Vyshnave Jeyabalan (Co Author), Tavis Apramian (Co Author), Jeff Myers (Co Author), Ilana Halperin (Co Author), Sarah Wright (Co Author), Giovanna Sirianni (Co Author)

Methods: Using a qualitative descriptive design, three family medicine residents and faculty members, and seven patients participated in semi-structured interviews to date. Data were analyzed via reflexive thematic analysis, with iterative coding and comparison across groups to identify recurring patterns and construct broader themes.

Results: Preliminary findings revealed divergent views on incorporating narrative PGHD into the EHR. Patients largely welcomed the opportunity to share their stories and values, viewing it as enhancing ownership and ensuring their perspectives were formally documented. However, they raised concerns about privacy, information overload, and whether physicians would meaningfully engage with these narratives. Family physicians questioned the added value of such narratives, perceiving this information as already being known and embedded in the patient-physician relationship, while raising concerns about the workload of keeping them updated and the risk of them being lost in the EHR.

Discussion: Considering the conflicting views on the value of narrative PGHD, there is a need to reconcile patient interest in documenting these with physician concerns about feasibility and usability. Future efforts should focus on developing approaches that preserve patient voice while ensuring the information is accessible and usable by physicians.

Angela Huang (Lead Author), Caroline Caswell (Co Author), Stacey Ritz (Co Author), Lawrence Grierson (Co Author)

Methods: Arksey and O'Malley's scoping review methodology was used to summarize results of research studies published between 1980-2024 written in English with empirical evidence of medical school applicants' behaviours being driven by admissions policies. Data extraction was guided by a central tenet of Tummers (2019) policy theory - that policies are important institutional influencers of the behaviour of target populations.

Results: Of 2349 studies initially screened, 15 were included in this review. These revealed three types of policy-mediated admissions practices that influence aspirant behaviour: high weightings of grade point averages (GPA) and standardized tests; value placed on extracurricular activities; and prioritization of professional and interpersonal skills assessed via essays, personal statements, interviews, and letters of recommendation. These policies influenced variously how aspirants made academic choices, sought supports, and marshalled their time and resources. Notably, influences were seemingly mediated by aspirants' interpretation of selection policies, rather than by the explicit articulation of selection policies.

Discussion: Admissions policies that incentivize behaviours misaligned with the values of medical practice may have harmful downstream effects on both applicants and the profession. Medical schools should consider the unintended consequences of their selection practices and establish policies that protect aspirant wellness and that promote behaviours aligned with the values of the profession.

Geneviève Lemay (Lead Author), Jordan Buxton (Lead Author), Melissa Washbrook (Lead Author), Claire Kendall (Co Author), Alan Chaput (Co Author), Máire Kerrin (Co Author)

Methods: This project was split into two phases. Meredith Vanstone (Lead Author), Rebekah Eatmon (Co Author), Maria Hubinette (Co Author), Tracy Yiu (Co Author), Justin Boehringer (Co Author), Angela Wolfe (Co Author), Steve Birnie (Co Author), Gary Tithecott (Co Author), Lawrence Grierson (Co Author)

Methods: Our goal was to create an admissions process that is evidence-informed, rigorous, merit-based, and inclusive. A small working group conducted literature reviews and analyzed health and workforce data to identify innovations. We consulted widely within SFU, the School of Medicine, and the communities of the Fraser Health region, integrating priorities through iterative refinement. Ongoing data monitoring will support continual improvement and responsiveness to evolving community needs.

Results: Applicants are invited to apply through the Open or Indigenous Admissions stream. Both streams share the same academic eligibility thresholds which may be demonstrated through three options (GPA-only, MCAT-only, or GPA+MCAT). GPA and MCAT scores are used as a threshold, with no additional advantage accrued for higher scores. Eligible applicants are assessed for alignment with eight institutional priority attributes and the school's social accountability mandate. Keith Wycliffe-Jones (Lead Author), Alain Papineau (Co Author), Michelle Morros (Co Author), Sarah Kinzie (Co Author), Jason Sutherland (Co Author), Jonathan Gerber (Co Author), Emma Morley (Co Author), Melissa Washbrook (Co Author), Maire Kerrin (Co Author), Fiona Patterson (Co Author), Tania Riendeau (Co Author), Lindsay Jantzie (Co Author), Ed Seale (Co Author), Kim Curtin (Co Author), Joshua Greggain (Co Author), Heather Waters (Co Author), Sheila Smith (Co Author), Daniel Grushka (Co Author), Russell Dawe (Co Author), Karina Prucnal (Co Author),Sasha Sealy (Co Author)

Methods: All 2023 FM residency applicants (n=1159) across 13 programs completed the FM-specific SJT. Supervisors assessed residents' performance on the four SJT attributes at mid- and endpoints of training. Additional selection data (e.g., file review, interview scores) and remediation data were also collected.Non-parametric correlations and independent t-tests were used to examine the relationship between SJT and interview scores, and the outcome variables.

Results: Supervisors' assessment data and other selection assessment data was collected for 422 residents (mid-training) and for 380 residents (end-of-training). Initial analysis has revealed a statistically significant, positive relationship between residents' SJT scores, overall supervisor ratings and their empathy/compassion ratings. Residents who required remediation scored significantly lower on the SJT than those who did not. There was also positive correlation between interview scores and supervisors' overall ratings.

Discussion: This study provides good initial evidence for the validity of a new FM-specific SJT in predicting FM Residents' in-training performance overall and in relation to their empathy and compassion, as well as the requirement for remediation. Findings also highlight the value of using a combination of selection methods, which has relevance across diverse international contexts.

Xavier Quan-Nguyen (Lead Author), Arnaud Robitaille (Co Author), Jean-Pierre Martineau (Co Author), Jean-Michel Leduc (Co Author)

Methods: Language requirements were extracted from the Canadian Resident Matching Service (CaRMS) website in late 2024. Program directors were then surveyed regarding language-related challenges among applicants and residents, the language proficiency criteria used in the selection process, and the minimum verbal and written French proficiency level expected from residents.

Results: According to CaRMS, 57/62 (91.9%) UdeM residency programs require application materials in French. All 5/5 (100%) Family medicine/Enhanced skills programs have standardized prerequisites, such as prior French education or language proficiency exams. Only 3/26 (11.5%) R-1 programs explicitly list language criteria, although 18/26 (69.2%) require French documentation or assess proficiency during the process. Few subspecialty programs in medicine (4/15, 26.7%) and pediatrics (3/13, 23.1%) explicitly specify any minimum French proficiency. Among program directors, 48/128 (37.5%) completed the survey, and 24/48 (50%) reported challenges with applicants, primarily during interviews; and 14/48 (41.1%) with residents, mostly in oral communication. All 4/4 fellowship directors reported language issues with applicants, and 3/4 (75%) with admitted fellows. The average expected minimum proficiency for trainees was between B2 (upper intermediate) and C1 (advanced) on the Common European Framework of Reference for Languages (CEFR).

Discussion: These findings highlight a gap between French proficiency expectations and screening criteria. Implementing minimum requirements, such as CEFR-based thresholds, could improve communication and support success in francophone clinical settings.

Latifah Kamal (Lead Author), Allison Paquette (Co Author), Bensun Fong (Co Author), Sandy Tse (Co Author), Claudia Malic (Co Author)

Methods: An API-driven dashboard consolidated 5 years of data from faculty evaluations across undergraduate medical education and 88 postgraduate medical education programs, allowing analysis of performance ratings by individual, program, and department. Evaluation criteria include teaching effectiveness, professionalism, and contributions to a safe learning environment. The dashboard is automated to upload monthly data and employs row-level security to ensure role-based access and confidentiality. Key performance indicators categorize faculty performance as "Needing Improvement," "Meeting Expectations," or "Exemplary," with "Exemplary" being a rated as 80% or more effectiveness, in alignment with service excellence standards. Iterative feedback from end-users is used to continuously refine the dashboard's functionality and usability.

Results: The implementation of the dashboard significantly streamlines the processes for teacher assessment, support and recognition, and informs faculty development.

Discussion: This innovation highlights the value of integrated data visualization in medical education. By centralizing and automating faculty evaluation data, it enhances transparency, promotes accountability, supports data-driven decisions related to faculty development and promotion, and reduces administrative burden. The dashboard also aids accreditation efforts by offering real-time access to longitudinal data and enabling cross-program comparisons of teaching performance.

Armita Dabirzadeh (Lead Author), Alireza Jalali (Lead Author), Salomon Fotsing (Co Author), Sarah Mann (Co Author), Nassima Marouf (Co Author), Meghan Stevens (Co Author), Mathilde Tardif (Co Author)

Methods: This work was planned and conducted according to the steps described by Arksey and O'Malley and carried out in accordance with PRISMA-ScR guidelines. The main sources searched included MEDLINE, EMBASE, CINAHL, PsycINFO, Eric, and Education Source. Studies were included if they examined AI applications in HEI marketing, communications, alumni engagement, or fundraising. The articles were processed and evaluated using COVIDENCE, then synthesized narratively. Data extraction focused on publication characteristics, AI tools, functional domains, reported outcomes, and ethical/privacy considerations.

Results: Preliminary findings from a review of 20 peer-reviewed articles published between 2023 and 2025 reveal that artificial intelligence applications in Marketing, Communications, and Fundraising predominantly include chatbots, predictive analytics, generative AI for constituent relationship management, and hybrid recommendation algorithms. Reported benefits included personalization, efficiency, predictive insight, and enhanced engagement. Challenges noted were privacy and security risks, algorithmic bias, unclear return on investment, and institutional readiness barriers.

Discussion: AI is beginning to transform marketing, alumni engagement, and fundraising in HEIs, with promising outcomes for personalization and donor relations. However, current evidence is fragmented, with limited empirical evaluation and insufficient consideration of ethical and equity issues.

Alexa Moschella (Lead Author), Edgar Akuffo-Addo (Co Author), Shakira Brathwaite (Co Author), Tatiana Lapa (Co Author), Miranda Waugh (Co Author), Marissa Joseph (Co Author)

Methods: Sixteen images representing eight common dermatologic conditions - one with a lighter and one with a darker skin phenotype per condition - were randomly selected from the Fitzpatrick 17k dataset. ChatGPT-4o was asked to describe primary and secondary morphology, with a new session started per image. Responses were compared to senior dermatology resident responses. Qualitative and quantitative analyses were used to assess consistency.

Results: ChatGPT demonstrated greater consistency with senior residents in identifying primary (31%) versus secondary morphology (28%). Morphology descriptions were more frequently completely inconsistent (no common terms with residents) in SOC (20%) versus non-SOC images (7%). ChatGPT provided partially consistent descriptions in most cases (53%), reporting at least one term used by senior residents with other terms not used by senior residents. This commonly occurred due to reporting secondary as primary morphology or reporting features that were absent (although consistent with the diagnosis).

Discussion: ChatGPT's ability to discern primary and secondary morphology is limited, with increased limitation in SOC images. Morphological features consistent with a presumed diagnosis may be reported without presence in the image. Although ChatGPT shows some ability to describe morphology, its accuracy is limited, and morphology teaching should be guided by clinician expertise.

David L. Nguyen (Lead Author), Andrew Ganea (Co Author), Minh Hoang Ton (Co Author), Matthew Sibbald (Co Author)

Methods: We evaluated Medevi using 52 peer-reviewed medical education studies with existing Medical Education Research Study Quality Instrument (MERSQI) and Newcastle-Ottawa Scale-Education (NOS-E) scores from a prior systematic review. Medevi outputs were compared against human-rater ground truths. Agreement was quantified using intraclass correlation coefficients (ICC) and mean absolute error (MAE). Efficiency was benchmarked by comparing time and cost per study against conventional human data extraction.

Results: Medevi achieved substantial agreement with human raters for MERSQI (ICC = 0.617, MAE = 0.904) and fair agreement for NOS-E (ICC = 0.365, MAE = 0.962). Domain-level agreement ranged from 46.2%-98.1% (MERSQI) and 32.7%-86.5% (NOS-E), with lower performance in more subjective areas. Compared to estimates for manual extraction (65-130 minutes, $21-43 per study), Medevi reduced appraisal time to <1 minute and cost to $0.10-0.57, yielding over 97% in cost savings.

Discussion: Medevi demonstrates promise as a transparent, open-source tool for accelerating systematic reviews in medical education. While best suited to objective criteria, it highlights the value of human oversight for subjective judgments. By reducing cost and time barriers, Medevi may help democratize high-quality evidence synthesis in medical education.

Urmi Sheth (Lead Author), David Nguyen (Co Author), Yar Al Dabagh (Co Author), David Kanter Eivin (Co Author), Ryan Siroosi (Co Author)

Methods: In 2024 and 2025, we delivered four student-led 60-minute interprofessional workshops at McMaster University, encompassing students from medicine, physician assistant, nursing, and other allied health programs. Workshop content covered AI fundamentals (supervised vs. unsupervised learning, deep learning, generative AI), clinical applications, limitations (biased data, overfitting, lack of explainability, confabulations), and appraisal of AI studies using the APPRAISE-AI tool. Case-based small group discussions supported active learning. Pre- and post-workshop quality improvement surveys assessed students' knowledge and attitudes towards AI, assessed by paired analyses.

Results: Of 109 attendees, 81 (74%) completed both surveys. Correct responses for "supervised learning" increased from 24.7% to 90.1% (p<.001). Recognition of convolutional networks increased from 21.2% to 81.2% (p<.001). Moreover, participants on average doubled the number of AI applications they could identify. Confidence in AI understanding increased by +1.31 points (2.19 to 3.49, p<.001), and familiarity with AI ethics by +0.91 (3.17 to 4.09, p<.001) on a 5-point Likert scale. After the workshop, 70.4% agreed that more formal AI teaching is needed.

Discussion: A student-led workshop significantly improved AI knowledge and confidence among interprofessional trainees. While not a substitute for formal curricula, student-led initiatives can bridge educational gaps, and prepare students for practice in an AI-enabled future.

Kameela Alibhai (Lead Author), Heather McFadgen (Co Author), Savtaj Brar (Co Author), Michael Ott (Co Author), Stanley Hamstra (Co Author), Marisa Louridas (Co Author)

Methods: A focus group was held during the RC's general surgery specialty committee meeting in September 2024. Participants, recruited via purposive convenience sampling, included PDs and faculty CBD leads with experience assessing residents. The deidentified transcript was analyzed using interpretative phenomenological analysis (IPA) in NVIVO15. Two reviewers conducted line-by-line reading and inductively identified emergent themes, which were iteratively clustered into superordinate themes.

Results: Twenty-five PDs and faculty leads representing all 14 English-speaking, CGP participated. Three superordinate themes were identified. First, PDs believed CBD was forced to fit the surgical context; it was inflexible to program-specific needs, did not capture intraoperative feedback, and was implemented without sufficient resources. Second, participants described tension between the theoretical goals of CBD and the realities of implementation, including the persistence of time-based progression and limited data to inform promotion. Finally, unintended system-wide consequences were reported, including increased faculty burden, resident wellness concerns, and siloed training pathways.

Discussion: This study highlights concerning national themes from PDs regarding CBD in CGP and can inform the RC's rollout of CBD 2.0. Potential improvements include additional financial and infrastructural resources as well as specialty-specific adaptations.

Cecilia Alves (Lead Author), Andrea Gotzmann (Co Author)

Methods: Physicians from across Canada were invited to fill out a demographic survey expressing interest in participating in the standard-setting process. Twenty-three physicians attended the standard-setting meeting, representing demographics across Canada (e.g., work location, specialty, years of experience, language, ethnicity, etc.) and divided into two subgroups that matched the full group demographics. The Bookmark Method was used to set the cut score, validated by the Hofstee Method for policy reasonableness checks. Panelists provided anonymous feedback via a post-session survey.

Results: After two rounds of applying the Bookmark and Hofstee methods, the pass score was set at 439. In the post-session survey, panelists expressed high confidence in the process, training, and the final score. The training, types of validity evidence gathered, and post-session survey results will be presented.

Discussion: The findings support the reliability and validity of the standard-setting process, resulting in a recommended pass score that is defensible from both psychometric and holistic perspectives. This approach continues to strengthen the exam's credibility among educators, employers, and the public by ensuring that only competent candidates pass.

Mélanie Marceau (Lead Author), Kathleen Ouellet (Co Author), Tim Dubé (Co Author), Jean-Sébastien Renaud (Co Author), Aliki Thomas (Lead Author), Christina St-Onge (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a study using a descriptive interpretive approach. Online focus groups were held with health sciences faculty members from eight Quebec universities and with experts in measurement and evaluation. Data were analyzed through inductive thematic analysis.

Results: We conducted six focus groups with faculty members, one with experts, and conducted an individual interview with another expert. Participants proposed solutions at multiple levels. At the individual level, they emphasized the need to strengthen understanding of validity, foster engagement, and manage workload. At the collegial level, they suggested formalizing peer support. At the organizational and program level, they mentioned considering AI and student involvement in validation processes, creating spaces for dialogue on assessment and validation, and providing access to tools and examples of best practices.

Discussion: Although faculty and experts have identified actionable solutions, their implementation remains constrained by organizational structures and professional regulations. Still, adapting these solutions is critical. Strengthening classroom assessment is not only about improving learning measures; it ensures fairer judgments of competence, supports student success, and contributes to safer patient care and public trust in health professionals.

Bonita Sawatzky (Lead Author), Cathy Kline (Co Author)

Methods: We used a case-based study of fourth-year medical students to evaluate the impact of learning from patients in the optional Health Mentors Programme (HMP). Students reviewed a video of a person with cerebral palsy who fell at home. We gave them 30 minutes to write a care plan for this patient in the emergency ward. We compared students who participated in the HMP (N=23) with those who did not (N=80), counting how often they considered the patient's and caregiver's perspectives, the number of diagnostic tests ordered, and referrals to other professionals and community services.

Results: T-tests showed that HMP students significantly prioritized the patient's and caregiver's voices (p = .014, Cohen's d = 0.6) and ordered fewer diagnostic tests than non-HMP students (p = .001, Cohen's d = 3.3). However, there were no significant differences in medical consults, referrals to allied health professionals or community services.

Discussion: Case-based assessments could be a valuable tool for measuring the long-term impact of patient-led learning. Designing new assessments to explore longer term impact of medical education initiatives should be considered as we seek new approaches in medical education in a constantly changing world.

Maxim Morin (Lead Author), Jason Frank (Co Author)

Methods: The NATP includes multiple levels of training. The main course, designed for frontline assessors, equips physicians in PRA with WBA fundamentals such as direct observation and feedback. It is delivered through a multimodal approach, balancing self-directed learning with interactive components, both online and in person. A refresher module is planned to sustain ongoing development. Completion is recognized with a credential, serving as a trusted signal for regulators and institutions.

Results: Although still under development, the NATP has been piloted with PRA programs and physicians across Canada. Post-training surveys highlight the program's value and identify opportunities for refinement.

Discussion: The NATP demonstrates how structured assessor development can enhance alternative routes to licensure in Canada, complementing postgraduate education. Beyond PRA, it offers a scalable model to build assessor expertise and confidence, with potential applications for post-licensure assessment or specialist contexts.

Jamie Harshman (Lead Author), Sheila Holmes (Co Author), Dahlia Benedikt (Co Author), Sidrah Noor (Co Author), Dorothy Bakker (Co Author), Clare Hutchinson (Co Author)

Methods: We prospectively designed patient narratives for the Year 1 CBL curriculum to align with the demographics of the Peel Region, derived from publicly available data from Peel Health Data Zone. TMUSoM engaged community members through surveys, town halls, advisory councils and consultative meetings that informed the CBL curriculum development. Aimun Q. Shah (Lead Author), Nicole Fu (Co Author), Helen Neighbour (Co Author), Mathew Sibbald (Co Author), Sharon Bal (Co Author), Sarah Kinzie (Co Author), Cathy Risdon (Co Author), Meredith Vanstone (Co Author), Stacey A. Ritz (Co Author), Lawrence Grierson (Co Author), Keyna Bracken (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a discourse analysis of all formal UGME curriculum documents, including strategic plans, mission statements, learning objectives, tutorial case problems, and elective content. We also analyzed the clinical professions of instructors and the broader relative program structure. Our approach was guided by core FM principles of comprehensiveness, prevention, relational care, and longitudinal care. Each document was reviewed by at least two independent researchers, with regular team discussions to ensure analytic rigour and consistency.

Results: Strategic documents affirm the value of FM, but this is reflected inconsistently within curricular components. Medical Foundations curriculum emphasizes hospital-based, disease-oriented care, while Professional Competencies foregrounds person-centred approaches. Tutorial cases often position emergency physicians as central problem-solvers, with FM roles limited to referral or diagnostic functions. FM's broader scope in managing complexity, coordinating care, and innovating in resource-limited settings is rarely depicted. Educators come from diverse backgrounds, but FM educators typically represent focused rather than comprehensive practice.

Discussion: Limited integration of FM across curricular content, educator roles, and tutorial case design may contribute to a fragmented understanding of its importance. A more explicit focus on FM principles will help future physicians recognize its essential role in an actively interdependent healthcare system.

K. Jean Chen (Co Author), Lyric Oblin-Moses (Lead Author), Darlene Kitty (Co Author), Noah Bennell (Co Author), Mia Otten (Co Author), Hannah Mckay (Co Author), Alexandre Kokoko-Petiquan (Co Author), Laura Muldoon (Co Author), Bradley Mac Cosham (Co Author), Luc Brisebois (Co Author), Meghan Cornett (Co Author), Amy Nakajima (Co Author)

Areeba Chaudhry (Lead Author), Nidhi Mehta (Lead Author), Kirin Dhillon (Co Author), Alice Kam (Co Author), Elizabeth Birchard (Co Author), Fowad Daud (Co Author), April Christiansen (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a meta-narrative review of 20 articles on CCC and interprofessional care, guided by Cultural Historical Activity Theory (CHAT). Articles were mapped to CHAT's activity system components, including subject, tools, rules, community, division of labour, and outcomes, with contradictions between these components identified as analytic entry points for understanding system dynamics.

Results: Four recurring contradiction types were observed, including misalignments between institutional rules and available tools, clashes between patient/caregiver goals and institutional constraints, and uneven divisions of labour across teams. Documented adaptations included role flexibility, psychosocial supports, patient education tools, and coordinated interprofessional communication.

Discussion: CHAT reframes narrative reviews from descriptive summaries to systemic analyses that expose contradictions and adaptations shaping care. By surfacing these tensions, CHAT offers a pedagogical method that enables learners to understand structural barriers, develop relational agency, and integrate compassion into their professional identity. This approach informs curriculum co-design for interprofessional education, equipping future clinicians to navigate complexity and advance equity in patient care.

Olivia Chung (Lead Author), Zia Bismilla (Co Author), Chris Gilchrist (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a structured scoping review of physical examination maneuvers listed in the online AFMC clinical skills recommendations. Literature searches through PubMed, UpToDate, and other clinical resources were conducted for each maneuver or physical exam. Each maneuver was then categorized as "Yes," "Maybe," or "No" for curricular inclusion based on their diagnostic accuracy, inter-observer reliability, and clinical relevance.

Results: Maneuvers within general, head and neck, respiratory, cardiovascular, gastrointestinal, genitourinary, musculoskeletal, and neurological examinations were reviewed. Core techniques like inspection, palpation, percussion, and auscultation showed strong evidence for inclusion, while some traditional signs (e.g., sinus transillumination, puddle sign, rectovaginal exam) lacked reliability and were recommended for removal. Some maneuvers were found to have inconsistent evidence or utility only in specific contexts (e.g., Kernig's test, Brudzinski's test, jolt accentuation).

Discussion: This review underscores the need for an evidence-based approach to clinical skills education. By removing low-value maneuvers and emphasizing reliable, high-yield techniques, curricula can better prepare learners and improve patient care. As an initial step, this work will be followed by consultation with clinical skills experts to ensure that future AFMC recommendations remain both evidence-informed and clinically relevant.

Heidi Sinclair (Lead Author), Gisselle Gallego (Co Author)

Methods: This was a cross-sectional study, via an online national survey of Australian medical students with disabilities between 2022-2023. The survey included both multiple-choice, and open-ended questions. Responses were analysed through both quantitative and qualitative methods, employing descriptive statistics and content analysis.

Results: 145 responses qualified for final analysis. While the majority of respondents disclosed their disability to their university (77%), only 37% received adequate accommodations, and 27% described their university as very supportive. 80% experienced discrimination or negative treatment. Barriers identified include stigma, administrative burden, bureaucratic complexity, generic accommodations, institutional resistance, ableism, inflexible assessment policies, and inflexible academic design.

Discussion: The study revealed critical barriers faced by Australian medical students with disabilities. Despite some level of institutional support, the findings underscore the need for systemic change to foster inclusivity in medical education. The results highlight the importance of accommodating diverse learning needs, as well as moving towards an outcomes-based approach that recognizes the capabilities of students with disabilities. Focus should be placed on a re-evaluation of policies and practices to ensure equitable access and representation within the medical profession.

Teanna Rowe (Lead Author), Saakethiya Sriranjan (Co Author), Suzanne Archie (Co Author), Albina Veltman (Co Author), Ashley Johnson (Co Author)

Methods: Recordings and learning objectives from the 2023-24 Academic Half-Days from the Psychiatric Residency curriculum were obtained. These resources were reviewed & mapped against the proposed modified EDI and social justice-related CanMEDS objectives. A comparative analysis quantifying the percentages of these objectives met in teaching sessions during PGME years 1-5 was represented in table and graphical formats.

Results: Of the 44 proposed objectives, only one was not addressed within the sessions. The majority were covered in ≤15% of sessions, although two were covered in at least half. Coverage was strongest overall in the Medical Expert category (mean = 27%) and weakest in the Leader domain (mean = 6%). Discussion: The McMaster Psychiatry Residency program addresses most of the proposed modified EDI and social justice-related CanMEDS objectives throughout the curriculum. This information can be utilized by curriculum leads and individual educators to optimize coverage of EDI and social justice-related topics.

Meghan McConnell (Lead Author), Tim Wood (Co Author), Nancy Dudek (Co Author), Neera Jain (Co Author), Victoria Luong (Co Author), Kori LaDonna (Co Author), Lindsay Cowley (Co Author)

Methods: 577 Canadian medical trainees and physicians completed an online questionnaire that consisted of: demographic questions (gender, ethnicity, training location, sexuality, disability), the McCroskey Introversion Scale, the Clance Imposter Phenomenon Scale, self-identification as an introvert or an extrovert, and perceived impact of introversion/extroversion on participation in educational, clinical, and professional activities.

Results: Introversion scores were positively correlated with impostor phenomenon. Compared to self-identified introverts, extroverts were more likely to believe that their extroversion influenced their participation in educational and clinical activities. However, self-identified introverts believed their introversion impacted their engagement in professional settings than extroverts. The backward selection linear regression found that introversion, gender, and disability status predicted imposter syndrome scores.

Discussion: We found that participants who were introverted were more likely to have imposter syndrome and believed their introversion affected their participation in professional activities. Advancing equity in medical education depends on tackling the invisible challenges faced by trainees and faculty physicians. Educators and researchers alike should consider whether introversion can have detrimental effects on educational, clinical, and professional opportunities.

Breanna Chen (Lead Author), Éolie Delisle (Co Author), Jessica Forcillo (Co Author)

Methods: Female medical students at Université de Montréal organized a research conference for women in surgery to evaluate its effect on students' confidence, surgical interest, and perceptions of gender issues. Two literature reviews were conducted to identify student surgical research conferences and surgical initiatives for female medical students, and compare them with our results. Non-validated, non-standardized pre- and post-conference surveys were administered to participants. The Wilcoxon signed-rank test was performed to compare pre- and post-conference responses.

Results: Sixty-five students attended. Fifty-five female medical students from all years and Quebec medical schools completed both surveys. Confidence in approaching surgeons increased from 47% to 71% (p < 0.001). Participants who disagreed that women in surgery lack support rose from 7% to 24% (p = 0.001), and those who disagreed that gender is an obstacle rose from 22% to 33% (p = 0.041). The conference was rated more friendly and conducive to mentoring than prior experiences (p = 0.009). Surgical interest showed no significant change.

Discussion: Two studies on student surgical research conferences reported increased surgical interest in 85% and 98% of participants, whereas our conference showed no change. Some initiatives for female medical students reduced perceived gender barriers, consistent with our findings, though one study found no effect. Our student research conference for women in surgery, the first described in the literature, demonstrated that such initiatives meaningfully foster mentorship and support for female medical students pursuing surgery.

Aaron Johnston (Lead Author), Kaia Thauberger (Co Author), Grace Perez (Co Author)

Methods: We interviewed 19 out of 29 human books, and transcripts were analyzed thematically.

Jacqueline Torti (Lead Author), Nadia Khan (Co Author), Deep Kaur Sandhu (Co Author), Abdulla Ahmed Al-Najjar (Co Author), Kristen Bishop (Co Author)

Methods: Fifteen physicians from across North America participated in this narrative study exploring experiences of courage in medical practice. Guided by a constructivist perspective, semi-structured interviews elicited participants' stories of witnessing and enacting courage. Transcripts were analyzed using reflexive thematic analysis, identifying patterns in how courage is conceptualized, enacted, and experienced, including associated decision-making processes and perceived outcomes.

Results: Participants' narratives revealed the complex, morally grounded nature of courage in medicine. They described courage not as heroic spectacle but as ethically anchored, everyday acts that reflect integrity and justice. Stories highlighted courage enacted through action, restraint, or collective decision-making, shaped by both innate disposition and learned experience. Moments of choice-"thresholds" for action-were influenced by personal values, organizational pressures, and prior experiences. Witnessing acts of courage, whether recognized or penalized, further shaped participants' willingness to act, with outcomes varying across contexts.

Discussion: These findings highlight that courage in medicine is not only a personal trait but an ethically grounded practice shaped by context, experience, and organizational culture. By revealing how courage is conceptualized, enacted, and experienced, this study provides insights for educators, clinical leaders, and healthcare teams seeking to cultivate moral and professional courage in practice.

Stephen Russell (Lead Author), Chris He (Co Author)

Methods: This talk focuses on examples of contranyms that might arise within a healthcare environment, both from medical professionals and patients. For example, "presently" means "now" but it also means some ambiguous time in the future. And to feel "sanguine" can mean both "hopeful/optimistic" and "out for vengeance".

Results: There are numerous words and phrases that should be avoided when communicating in healthcare settings, because they can potentially lead to needless confusion.

Discussion: Medicine isn't poetry, where words are often used to play with possible meanings. In all aspects of medicine, the best route is to pick the most unambiguous phrase, even if the term may not sound as elegant.

Stephen Russell, (Lead Author) Mélanie Houle, (Co Author) Chris He (Co Author)

Methods: The Latin of anatomy is surprisingly quite straightforward, and our master class videos emphasize some basic patterns, forms, and paradigms that are relevant to those who need to use the language but have never formally learned how to read Latin - especially to people from areas of the world that do not teach Latin.

Farah Qaiser (Lead Author), Ariel Gershon (Co Author), Dan Petrescu (Co Author)

Methods: We utilised the Library of Parliament's Parliamentarians database to identify physician decision-makers, and obtained additional biographical information through newspaper archives and authorized biographies. Findings were tabulated and summarised, using descriptive statistics and graphical assessment.

Results: Since 1867, we found that 220 of 4,695 (4.69%) MPs, and 64 of 1,006 (6.4%) Senators have been physicians. There has been a steep decline in political participation over time. In the current Parliament, out of 343, only seven (2.04%) physicians serve as MPs - but notably, physicians remain over-represented in Parliament, compared to the general population (0.24%). We found that most physician decision-makers are men (96.8%), represent a riding in Ontario (38.6%) or Quebec (27.7%), and tend (90%) to run for office more than once.

Discussion: Our study is the first to characterise, and present a complete list of Canadian physicians elected to serve as MPs, and/or appointed to serve as Senators. We found that there has always been a physician in the House of Commons and the Senate. We also note a notable decline in physician political participation over time, likely due to the increased rigour of standards to qualify as a physician, and the challenges faced in political participation.
