Téa Christopoulos (Lead Author), Michael Atkinson (Co Author)

Methods: A narrative ethnographic approach was adopted. Five ethnographic observations were conducted for an 11-week, second-year undergraduate medical school course focused on caring for vulnerable patient populations with complex presentations. Twenty second-year and third-year students and three course instructors (n=23) participated in semi-structured narrative interviews which were iteratively guided by preceding ethnographic observations and a feminist bioethical lens.

Results: Data are currently being analyzed using a critical narrative analysis (Souto-Manning, 2014). Early findings suggest that invisible disability is not explicitly taught as a concept in medical curricula. Students provide various conceptualizations for invisible disability and medical uncertainty and narrate their educational experiences with curiosity, empathy, and an openness toward learning from patient narratives. Students assume a personal responsibility toward learning more about these topics and recognize the importance of their management in clinical contexts.

Discussion: This study will help illustrate what and how students are currently learning about invisible disability to co-create a framework that can be integrated into undergraduate medical curricula to enhance their education on these topics.

Shauna Hacker (Lead Author), Alain Scalabrini (Co Author), Hailey Sutherland (Co Author)

Methods: Between 04/01/2024 and 03/31/2025, NP trainees were integrated into our FM procedures clinic. Data collected included the number of clinics, participating trainees (MD and NP), and details of subsequent referrals to skin-related specialists. Informal feedback was gathered through exit interviews of graduating residents.

Results: Over 49 clinics, 40 FM residents, four NP students, three medical students, and one dermatology resident participated, totaling 128 learner clinic opportunities (mean 2.6 learners/clinic). Among 274 patients seen, only 28 (10%) required skin specialist referral, primarily for definitive excision (61%), cosmesis (6%), or e-consultation (14%). Learner feedback was overwhelmingly positive, with most requesting increased exposure.

Discussion: The interprofessional clinic reduced specialist referrals through high-value, in-house dermatologic care, while offering robust educational opportunities. Future directions include formal evaluation of trainee competence and a focused assessment of interprofessional education outcomes.

Ruby Shanker (Lead Author)

Methods: Grounded in a macro-sociological Foucauldian analysis of power/resistance and a micro-sociological application of Iris Marion Young's communicative democracy, the intervention invites small interprofessional groups to participate in facilitated "tracing exercises." Sessions are intentionally commensal, combining shared meals with guided dialogue to encourage inclusion, narrative exchange, and mutual recognition. Participants surface dominant organizational discourses, explore how they are positioned to reinforce or resist these narratives, and identify everyday acts of productive resistance. A qualitative feedback survey will be administered six months after launch; selected participants may be invited to semi-structured interviews pending Research Ethics Board approval.

Results: Pilot implementation is planned within a regional rehabilitation network. Early observations focus on feasibility, participant engagement, and the richness of dialogic exchange during shared meals.

Discussion: Integrating Foucauldian insights on systemic power with Young's principles of communicative democracy, this commensal approach aims to release feelings of helplessness associated with moral injury, strengthen interprofessional bonds, and help teams renegotiate professional identities. Findings will inform scalable strategies for ethics-oriented team development in resource-constrained health systems.

Eleftherios Soleas (Lead Author), Wiley Chung (Lead Author), Heather Braund (Co Author)

Methods: Guided by Carless and Boud's Feedback Literacy Framework, a 40-minute workshop replaced traditional lectures and incorporated two custom AI chatbots simulating distinct feedback personalities. The intervention integrated foundational feedback teaching, dynamic group interaction with chatbots, and peer role-play exercises. Pre- and post-session surveys (n=112) measured changes in confidence, comfort, and understanding related to feedback.

Results: Students showed statistically significant improvements in comfort giving and receiving feedback, confidence in delivering constructive feedback, and understanding effective feedback components (all p < 0.05). Qualitative feedback highlighted the value of chatbot interactions for practicing challenging feedback dialogues in a safe environment, fostering engagement, curiosity, and skill development. Most participants welcomed further AI-assisted sessions, although some noted limitations including chatbot realism and the artificiality of the experience.

Discussion: The AI-augmented workshop increased feedback literacy and communication skills while mitigating anxiety associated with feedback exchange. Chatbots acted as both educational tools and engagement facilitators, especially useful prior to clinical immersion. Limitations include self-reported data, single-site design, and lack of longitudinal assessment. Future work should refine chatbot realism, explore broader application across curricula, and measure long-term impacts on clinical performance. Thoughtful integration of AI chatbots in medical education offers promising opportunities to enhance feedback skills, engagement, and learner confidence, supporting essential competencies for professional growth.

Allison Paquette (Lead Author), Latifah Kamal (Co Author), Bensun Fong (Co Author), Sandy Tse (Co Author), Claudia Malic (Co Author)

Methods: An API-driven dashboard was developed to aggregate learning environment for professionalism (LEP) evaluations across programs, departments, and affiliates. The dashboard allows users to filter data by site, rotation, and learner program whilst protecting learner confidentiality. Evaluation criteria include observations of effective doctor-patient relationships, collaboration with healthcare workers, and observations of professional behaviour. Key performance indicators categorize LEP observations as "Needing Improvement," "Meeting Expectations," or "Exemplary" with "Exemplary" being a rated as 80% or more satisfaction, in alignment with service excellence standards. Monthly data uploads are automated.

Results: The dashboard has proven effective in identifying patterns and potential areas for improvement (AFI) within the learning environments, enabling affiliates to conduct data-informed, targeted interventions. It complements qualitative data collected through institutional accreditation reviews and site audits, offering a comprehensive, multi-stream view of the learning environment. The system's confidentiality safeguards protect learner identity, encourage honest feedback, and enhance data accuracy.

Discussion: This dashboard improves the ability for Postgraduate Medical Education, sites, and programs to monitor LEP concerns, compare learning environments, track patterns over time, and identify potential AFIs. This dashboard works to support evidence-based decision-making and fosters a culture of CQI across institutions.

Cole Walsh (Lead Author), Gill Sitarenios (Lead Author), Alexander MacIntosh (Co Author), Rodica Ivan (Co Author), Muhammad Zafar Iqbal (Co Author), Colleen Robb (Co Author)

Methods: We piloted a new open-response formative SJT measuring intrapersonal skills, interpersonal skills, social consciousness, and critical thinking and developed a novel system for scoring and generating feedback for this test using LLMs. Using responses collected from 400 students and human ground truth ratings for these responses we constructed LLM-based models to score and generate student feedback.

Results: We evaluated the agreement between AI-generated scores and ratings provided by human experts. We compared this agreement to the agreement between independent human raters rating the same responses and found that our AI models achieved comparable or superior results. We additionally demonstrated that these models are able to produce personalized feedback for each of the four dimensions.

Discussion: Our findings demonstrate that LLMs can effectively score open-response SJTs with reliability comparable to human experts, offering a scalable solution for evaluating critical skills in medical education. The LLM pipeline for generating personalized feedback provides a promising avenue for supporting student development and timely intervention, making robust skills assessment and feedback feasible in a wide array of medical education settings.

Runjie Bill Shi (Lead Author), Chris Gilchrist (Co Author)

Methods: We developed a web-based platform featuring two core AI-driven functionalities created through prompt engineering: a conversational virtual patient for unscripted history taking and an interactive AI tutor for case discussion. A pilot study is planned for Fall 2025 with first-year medical students to evaluate the platform's user experience and learning outcomes.

Results: A functional prototype has been successfully developed. The platform enables students to lead a clinical interview using natural, open-ended language. The AI tutor guides students through questions not by providing direct answers, but with Socratic hints. For example, when discussing pharmacodynamic interactions, the AI tutor automatically prompts students to: "Consider how opioids and sedatives affect the nervous system and what might happen when they are taken together. What do you think?"

Discussion: This AI-driven platform offers a solution to the current CBL crisis. Our approach transforms AI from a simple answer generator into a sophisticated learning partner, making CBL more immersive and effective. Future work will focus on analyzing data from the planned pilot study to assess the platform's educational impact and guide its further development.

Alireza Jalali (Lead Author), Sarah Mann (Lead Author), Salomon Fotsing (Co Author), Mathilde Tardif (Co Author), Nassima Marouf (Co Author), Meghan Stevens (Co Author)

Methods: Après une revue de littérature rigoureuse, Jasper, Claude et ChatGPT ont été retenus. Des évaluateurs ont généré des lettres à partir d'un profil fictif et utilisé un questionnaire avec des questions de type Likert et dichotomiques pour évaluer la difficulté, la pertinence, la personnalisation, l'adaptabilité, la rentabilité et la satisfaction des outils. Des questions ouvertes ont complété l'évaluation.

Results: Les résultats exploratoires montrent une bonne fiabilité interne pour les questionnaires Likert de Jasper (α = 0,949) et ChatGPT (α = 0,727), mais une faible fiabilité interne pour Claude (α = 0,150). ChatGPT a été le plus facile à utiliser (5/5), suivi de Claude (4,6/5) et de Jasper (4,2/5). La pertinence du contenu généré était élevée pour tous les outils, tandis que la personnalisation était plus faible pour ChatGPT (2,6/5) que pour Jasper (4/5) et Claude (3,6/5). En termes de rentabilité perçue, Claude a obtenu le score moyen le plus élevé (4,4/5).

Discussion: L'IA peut soutenir la rédaction de lettres de nomination. Jasper et Claude offrent un contenu plus personnalisé que ChatGPT, qui se distingue par sa simplicité d'utilisation. Des recherches plus larges sont nécessaires pour confirmer ces résultats.

Jeffery Okoroma (Lead Author),Riana Fisher (Co Author), Cari Whyne (Co Author), Portia Kalun (Co Author)

Methods: This study was conducted at a large academic hospital. Intraoperative recordings of hip fracture surgeries were assessed by experts using validated teamwork assessment tools, generating checklist and global rating scores. YE was calculated for all team members, including surgeons, nurses, radiation technologists (RTs), anesthesia teams and associated trainees. Associations were tested using linear regression and ANOVA.

Results: We analyzed 103 surgeries. Surgeon and nurse YE were not significantly associated with checklist or global rating scores. In contrast, RT YE was weakly associated with checklist (slope=0.005, p=0.0013) and moderately with global rating scores (slope=0.52, p=0.0007). At the team level, composite YE was not significantly associated with performance. Beyond YE, having at least one "comfortable/consistent" nurse in the case was significantly associated with higher nurse global ratings (p=0.048) and entire team global ratings (p=0.036).

Discussion: These findings suggest YE alone does not consistently capture expertise across all surgical roles. While RT performance improved with experience, other team members' YE showed no clear relationship. Nurse familiarity enhanced performance, underscoring the importance of role-specific experience and team dynamics in shaping intraoperative teamwork. Future research will explore additional determinants of surgical team performance and consider targeted onboarding or training strategies to support RT development.

Ehab Abd El-Moneim (Lead Author), Ahmed Abdelmagied (Lead Author), Anwar Sayed (Lead Author), Tayseer Mansour (Co Author)

Methods: For five graduating MBBS classes (2019-2023) at Taibah University College of Medicine, Saudi Arabia, we assessed the achievement of seven knowledge-based PLOs. On a scale of 5, whereas 3 was the desired benchmark, each PLO was measured directly (students assessment mapped to PLOs) and indirectly (graduates' perception). Variation coefficient (CV) analysis was utilized to detect any performance fluctuations, classifying a CV <10% as low and >30% as high.

Results: Except for the first year, which had three underachieved PLOs, the class average of all knowledge PLOs surpassed the benchmark (2.8±0.8, 4.1±0.6, 3.7±0.8, 3.2±0.3, 3.8±0.4; 2019-2023, respectively). Cumulative 5-year PLO achievements; PLO1 to PLO7 shows 3.1±0.4, 4±0.5, 3.2±1.2, 3.3±0.3, 3.5± 0.5, 3.7±1.0, 3.8±0.5. From 2019 to 2023, performance stability improved steadily, with CV decreasing from 31% to 11% for direct PLO scores and from 5% to 3.3% for indirect scores. PLO3 (social and behavioral sciences) showed the highest variability (CV 36.5%), while PLO1 (basic biomedical sciences knowledge) exhibited recurrent underachievement.

Discussion: Crucial targets for tracing program performance longitudinally are to identify learning content that's resistant to remediation, spot instability in achievement, and track learning trajectories over time. Our program demonstrated positive development over the academic cycle with increased coherence of knowledge construct delivery. There is evidence suggesting the need for long-term targeted interventions to enhance social sciences and biomedical knowledge.

Russell Franco D'Souza (Lead Author), Krishna Mohan Surapaneni (Co Author), Mary Mathew (Co Author)

Methods: This study was conducted in two phases. In the first phase, focus groups with bioethics and medical education experts and a systematic literature review were conducted to identify key ethical competencies in medical education, drawing primarily from UNESCO's Universal Declaration on Bioethics and Human Rights (UDBHR), competency-based frameworks, and international guidelines. Using these insights, an initial set of 34 competencies was drafted and categorized into seven domains. These were then refined into ten core EPEAs, with a measurable two-tier milestone system differentiating between pre-entrustable and entrustable behaviors. In the second phase, the EPEAs were validated via an e-Delphi study involving 35 international experts using a 47-item questionnaire, a 4-point Likert scale, and open-ended feedback. Consensus was defined as ≥80% agreement on clarity, relevance, and feasibility. Statistical measures included content validity indices (CVI), Intraclass Correlation Coefficient (ICC), and Kendall's W to determine reliability and agreement levels.

Results: Validation demonstrated 86.49% agreement on relevance, 82.86% agreement on milestone differentiation, and 81.76% agreement on measurability. The final set comprised ten validated EPEAs, covering key ethical actions in informed consent, privacy, equity, sustainability, and emerging technologies. Reliability testing (ICC: 0.87) and content validity indices (S-CVI/AVE: 0.80, I-CVI: 0.85, S-CVI/UA: 0.92) confirmed the robustness of the EPEAs.

Discussion: EPEAs provide a structured, competency-based approach for integrating and assessing bioethical decision-making in medical education. By establishing global consensus, this framework enhances the ability of medical educators to train and assess ethical competence in future physicians. Future research should explore implementation strategies and longitudinal assessment of EPEAs in various medical training environments. EPEAs offer a validated, practical framework for assessing bioethical competence in medical trainees and residents, ensuring they are prepared to navigate complex ethical dilemmas with confidence, professionalism, and accountability.

Ingrid de Vries (Lead Author)

Methods: This quantitative observational study analyzed video recordings (n=55) of paramedic teams performing simulated clinical scenarios. Communication and coordination behaviours were coded by two independent researchers. Sequential analysis was conducted to identify significant behavioural transitions with standardized residuals (z-scores), Odds Ratios (OR) and Yule's Q. Group differences for high and low-experience teams were tested using χ2.

Results: Sequential analysis showed two sequences occurring more often than chance with robust association: the transition from "Gathering information" to "Sharing an interpretation" (z = 18.57, p < .001, OR = 4.835, Yule's Q = 0.66) and from "Interpretation sharing" to "Projection sharing" (z = 13.64, p < .001, OR = 13.19, Yule's Q = 0.86). Overall, there was a significant difference between high- and low-experience teams (χ2(11) = 40.18, p < .001). "Planning" and "Projection sharing" were underused by high-experience teams and overused by low-experience teams. "Implicit information sharing" was underused by low-experience teams.

Discussion: Findings show experience-based differences in team communication and coordination patterns; this will support medical educators to improve team performance through targeted team-level feedback.

Nancy Forget (Lead Author), Carlos Gomez-Garibello (Co Author), Maryam Wagner (Co Author)

Methods: A narrative review was performed using a search strategy that spanned across four databases. The included published literature was analyzed using conventional content analysis [7, 8].

Results: The review provided a comprehensive definition of relational skills tailored to allied health professions. The results also offered a detailed list of relational domains and sub-skills, linked to OT competencies, thus identifying the relevant skills for training and practice.

Discussion: This foundational work clarifies the construct of relational skills and offers a shared terminology to be used across allied health professions. The results inform the development of PREP-OT-RS, which will provide individualized feedback to OT students, supporting diverse educational needs.

Sarah Simkin (Lead Author), Ivy Bourgeault (Co Author), Dax Bourcier (Lead Author)

Methods: We conducted an environmental scan to identify leading practices, principles, standards, guidelines, existing frameworks, and persistent challenges in health workforce data and planning. Using an iterative development process, we created a comprehensive description and visual representation of the framework. We then assessed its applicability to the Canadian context, implementation considerations, potential remediations, and added value to health systems.

Results: The resulting data architecture framework features four fundamental components: data ingestion, integration, storage, and fit-for-purpose use. It addresses major data challenges, including the lack of comprehensiveness, granularity, standardization, interoperability, timeliness, usability, and accessibility. The assessment of the applicability of the framework to the Canadian context revealed that added value stems primarily from its promotion of comprehensiveness, interoperability, compatibility with artificial intelligence and accessibility of data to decision-makers.

Discussion: This data architecture framework addresses persistent challenges in health workforce data by enabling more consistent, timely, and actionable insights. It provides policymakers, planners, and researchers with fit-for-purpose data to improve resource allocation, forecasting, and innovation. Ultimately, it establishes health data ecosystems that support evidence-informed decision-making, workforce optimization, and sustainable health care delivery.

Manav Vyas (Co Author), Umberin Najeeb (Lead Author), Csilla Kolacsai (Co Author), Marwa Douelrachad (Co Author)

Methods: A mixed-methods prospective observational study was conducted, inviting postgraduate trainees (PGY4-6) in the Department of Medicine at the University of Toronto. Information on social position was gathered with a demographic survey. Responses were obtained on a Likert-scale exploring their attitude about academic medicine. Follow-up interviews were also offered. Transcribed interviews were analyzed using qualitative descriptive paradigm, exploring the themes associated with attitudes towards academic medicine.

Results: Fifty-two trainees completed the survey (~90% aged 26 to 35 years, 57.1% women, 71% middle to upper-middle). About one-third intended to work in academic medicine, while 28% were neutral. Over 90% reported negative feelings about academic culture and its impact on mental health. 73% felt that non-academic medicine would allow them to have a work-life balance. Ten trainees participated in the interviews (~90% aged 26-35 years, 60% men, 55.6% middle to lower-income). Emerging themes highlighted how academic medicine was associated with the image of an ideal academic physician which may seem both unattainable and undesirable due to financial considerations, social positioning and culture of academic medicine.

Discussion: While at an aggregate level, social position was not directly associated with career choice in academic medicine, participants with diverse social identities identify structural barriers associated with academic medicine influencing their decision-making. Strategies to address cultural and structural barriers may help reduce the influence of social position in determining post-graduate medical trainees' career choices.

Nicole Bajcar (Lead Author), Hei Ching Kristy Cheung (Co Author), Naomi David-Brumer (Co Author), Nicole Woods (Co Author)

Methods: We used an iterative design approach guided by the principles of adaptive expertise and Kemp's Model of Instructional Design. The curriculum was piloted across three healthcare professions (i.e.,. second-year Occupational Therapy students, Personal Support Workers, and Community Paramedics) across Northern and Southern Ontario. Participants completed post-training knowledge checks and surveys evaluating the training's relevance, confidence, and feasibility. We descriptively analyzed quantitative data (e.g., Likert scale responses), while we conducted a thematic analysis of the open-ended feedback to identify areas for improvement and perceived value.

Results: Knowledge checks revealed a ceiling effect among OT students, suggesting prior familiarity with diabetes foot care. PSWs showed greater learning gains and reported increased confidence. Survey feedback indicated that participants found the curriculum relevant and beneficial to their professional roles. However, virtual training participants expressed discomfort with performing foot screenings due to limited hands-on practice. Thematic analysis highlighted the need for more practical components and clearer post-training integration pathways.

Discussion: This study demonstrates the feasibility of training healthcare workers in diabetes foot screening using an evidence-informed curriculum. Participants valued the training, but challenges remain in translating knowledge into routine practice-particularly in virtual formats and in settings lacking follow-up support. Future phases will include interviews to explore enablers and barriers to integration, and expand training to community paramedics and staff at community centres. Addressing these gaps is essential to improving diabetes foot care equity and reducing preventable amputations in underserved populations.

Wiley Chung (Lead Author), Eleftheria Laios (Co Author), Natalie McGuire (Co Author), Mujeedat Lekuti (Co Author), Julia Field (Co Author), Luca Stoicescu (Co Author), Christian Cataudella (Co Author)

Methods: A study was conducted with data collection through an online survey designed to be completed by each program as a group (program director and members familiar with its ID/IP components). To promote survey completion and engagement with nuanced survey items, we conducted virtual interviews per program.

Results: Sixteen programs within the Schools of Nursing, Medicine, and Rehabilitation Therapy participated (25% undergraduate, 63% graduate, 13% other). Most were interested in expanding ID/IP education through shared curricula within their school (75%), outside their school but within Health Sciences (92%), with other faculties (42%), and through community-integrated education (50%). Priorities for developing new ID/IP included simulations (73%), ID problem-solving (55%), ID course-based research projects (55%), ID teaching teams (64%), and community-based shadowing (64%). Regarding scaling up existing initiatives, participants preferred an ID/IP course on social determinants of health (55%), interprofessional shadowing (60%), clinical skills training (60%), and global health initiatives (60%).

Discussion: Most programs expressed interest in expanding ID/IP education primarily through collaboration with programs within Health Sciences rather than outside, stating barriers such as resources and conflicting timetables. Programs prioritized development of simulations and scaling up existing initiatives such as global health.

Beatriz Alvarado (Lead Author), Nicholas Cofie (Co Author), Oluwatoyosi Kuforiji (Co Author), Nancy Dalgarno (Co Author), Jorge Martinez-Cajas (Co Author)

Methods: This evaluation study is based on data obtained from pre (n = 38) and post training surveys (n = 20) administered to PCPs who completed a 2-hour educational module on PrEP prescription and use via Qualtrics, an online platform for survey development and data collection.

Results: from the surveys demonstrated substantial improvements in PCPs' understanding of PrEP eligibility (64%-80), clinical management knowledge (55%-180%), confidence in medication management (57%-68%), skills in monitoring PrEP clients (47%-84%), and knowledge regarding PrEP discontinuation (84%). Michelle Gibson (Lead Author), Shayna Watson (Co Author), Natasha Aziz (Co Author)

Methods: Curricular leaders collected ongoing formal and informal feedback from faculty and students. This permitted real-time adaptations to existing curriculum and innovations for second year curriculum. Analyses were made of student performance in key assessments to compare results at both campuses.

Results: Positive feedback was received about curricular structure, small group learning, and community placements. Adam Gavarkovs (Lead Author), Wendy Blunt (Co Author), Melissa Majoni (Co Author), Claire Styffe (Co Author), Dawn Gill (Co Author), Rob Petrella (Co Author)

Methods: Interviews were conducted with seven medical students from two Canadian institutions who participated in a six-month elective that involved delivering the HealtheSteps program, an evidence-based chronic disease prevention and management program that entails longitudinal lifestyle counselling. Interviews explored students' motivations, experiences, and outcomes during the elective. Reflexive thematic analysis was employed to identify recurrent patterns of meaning in the data.

Results: The flexibility of the HealtheSteps program supported students' sense of autonomy and allowed them to develop meaningful relationships with program participants, enhancing their sense of relatedness. The longitudinal nature of the elective allowed students to observe participants' progress, enhancing their sense of competence. Need-satisfying interactions with patients supported students' learning, well-being, and reinforced their aspirations to practice family medicine.

Discussion: This study provides evidence that family medicine electives can enhance students' feelings of autonomy, competence, and relatedness, which can support their aspirations to practice family medicine. Future research could explore how these need-satisfying experiences influence career aspirations among students without a pre-existing interest in family medicine.

Jonathan Chainey (Lead Author), Ayelet Kuper (Co Author), Ryan Brydges (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a citation analysis of a recent article claiming the "success at scale" of CBD implementation in Canadian PGME. Among 169 references, we categorized each by type and empirical relevance. We also reviewed Canadian-situated empirical studies on Entrustable Professional Activities (EPAs) and their role in feedback within CBD systems. Additionally, we consulted the Royal College of Physicians and Surgeons of Canada's (RCPSC) 2024 CBD Adaptations Plan to contextualize recent developments.

Results: The article's citations did include empirical studies (49.2%), though just 59.4% of those directly addressed CBME. Notably, we observed many (25.3%) self-citations, suggesting limited diversity of perspectives. Our appraisal of available studies revealed mixed outcomes: although EPAs aimed to enhance feedback, trainees reported increased stress, administrative burden, and inconsistent feedback quality. While the original article did not reflect these findings clearly, the RCPSC's 2024 plan did acknowledge them and proposed reforms to reduce assessment load and improve feedback.

Discussion: Our findings challenge the narrative of CBD's unequivocal success in Canada. We highlight the need for context-sensitive empirical research to guide future reforms. We believe the RCPSC's 2024 plan will stimulate a more balanced understanding of CBD's impact on PGME.

Jacomie Strydom (Lead Author), James Hudson (Co Author), Ashley Clelland (Co Author), Victor Do (Co Author), Cheryl Goldstein (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted three World Café sessions with eighteen medical undergraduate students at the University of Alberta. Café transcripts were analyzed using constructivist grounded theory and organized into a preliminary model of PerMS impact.

Results: We identified four key themes that medical students used to conceptualize the impact of PerMS, including: 1) Stress Management & Coping, 2) Development as Medical Learners, 3) Compassionate Patient Care, and 4) Personal Growth. Students described how PerMS buffer the stressors of medical school from negatively affecting their lives outside of medicine. Learners also reported that PerMS can build confidence, improve thinking capacity, encourage self-directed learning, and prevent poor clinical performance. Students discussed that PerMS are needed to be effective and empathetic helpers for patients, and can be used to support patients in developing their own coping skills. Lastly, PerMS supports personal growth through self-reflection, changing one's perspective, and making the student a well-rounded individual overall.

Discussion: The positive and multifaceted impact of PerMS underscores the importance of supporting medical learners to develop these skills as part of their training. Future research should explore how to best integrate these skills into the medical school and residency curricula.

Dévi Moorjani Houle (Co Author), Isabelle Burnier (Co Author), Diane Bouchard-Lamothe (Co Author), Joseph Zarychta (Co Author), Sophie De Roock (Lead Author)

Methods: Une équipe interdisciplinaire (expert de contenu, tuteur, résident, étudiant de premier cycle, concepteur pédagogique) a collaboré pour analyser les besoins des intervenants impliqués dans les SARC. Erin Norris (Co Author), Sophie Eisen (Lead Author), Michael Quon (Co Author), Anne Kawamura (Co Author), Tina Martimianakis (Co Author)

Methods: This qualitative study used semi-structured interviews to collect data. Fifteen physicians and residents across Canada living with a variety of disability types, and of varying ages and career stages, were enrolled. We classified motivations and barriers to participation as cultural, institutional, and personal/interpersonal. Using Self-Determination Theory, we then identified their drivers of autonomy, competence, and relatedness.

Results: Motivating factors include making connections, navigating institutional systems, and having a shared community identity. Intrinsic motivators include a strengths-based perception of disability which is found across different disability types and career stages. Barriers to participation include stigma, fear of repercussions, and disability imposter syndrome.

Discussion: Identifying motivations and barriers to participation is a crucial first step in building a robust and inclusive CoP for physicians with disabilities. Results inform recruitment and engagement strategies, contribute to equity-focused continuing professional development, and provide a model for other equity-seeking groups in medicine. Supporting physicians with disabilities is not only a matter of equity, but also an important strategy for sustainable healthcare.

Tuana Kant (Lead Author), Darshana Seeburruth (Lead Author), Ike Okafor (Co Author), Lisa Robinson (Co Author), Lisa Richardson (Co Author) Dalal Mahir (Co Author)

Methods: Participants enrolled in COSMOS between January-March, 2025 completed two surveys, before and after the seminars. Both questionnaires utilized a 5-point Likert scale to measure self-assessed confidence and competence during the interview process. Pre- and post-survey responses were compared using ANOVA and unpaired t-tests. Qualitative responses were analyzed thematically.

Results: 110 seminar participants completed both of the surveys. Confidence and competence in responding to ethical and healthcare-related questions increased significantly (p<0.0001). Participants also reported greater confidence using the CanMEDS framework, expressing experiences within time limits, and selecting activities reflecting personal values (all p<0.0001). Participants with personal connections in the medical field reported significantly higher baseline confidence and perceived competence in responding to healthcare-related interview questions (p<0.05). 99.1% of the participants (109/110) recommending the program.

Discussion: The COSMOS seminars were associated with significant improvements in self-reported confidence and perceived competence in medical school interview process among UC applicants. The findings highlight the potential of free, targeted, mentorship-driven interventions to mitigate structural barriers in the medical school admissions process and promote greater equity in access to medical education.

Tuana Kant (Lead Author), Darshana Seeburruth (Lead Author), Ike Okafor (Co Author), Lisa Robinson (Co Author), Lisa Richardson (Co Author) Eunice Aluko (Co Author)

Methods: Applicants from UC who participated in the free Community of Support Medical School Application Online Writing Seminars (COSMOS) were surveyed on self-perceived confidence and competence in preparing autobiographical sketches (ABS), personal essays, and prior interview invitations. Subgroup analyses with R used unpaired t-tests and ANOVA to assess how race/ethnicity, gender, medical connections, paid assistance, and education influenced applicants' confidence and competence.

Results: A total of 369 UC applicants participated. Interview invitations differed significantly across racial/ethnic groups (p<0.0001) but not gender. Personal connections in medicine were linked to higher confidence in ABS (p<0.001) and essays (p=0.04), and greater competence in essays (p=0.01). Applicants using paid assistance showed higher ABS confidence (p=0.003) and more interview invitations (p=0.02). Those with or pursuing graduate degrees reported greater confidence and competence in ABS and essays than undergraduate-level applicants (p<0.05).

Discussion: This study underscores the role of race/ethnicity, personal connections, paid application assistance, and educational background in shaping UC applicants' confidence and competence in the medical school admissions process. These findings highlight the need for targeted interventions to foster a more equitable admissions process.

Jessica Bennett (Lead Author), Dr. Laura Stovel (Co Author)

Methods: AMCFY's efficacy was measured via number of program applicants, and participant and volunteer session-based feedback. Demographic information collected on applicants ensured that campers were among the program's target population. Campers completed an objective knowledge assessment of the MD Admissions process at the program's beginning and end, alongside a subjective post-program survey assessing each session offered on a five-point scale.

Results: Over four years, camper applications increased from 77 to 261, and we increased the camp capacity from 50 to 110. Across Indigenous, Black, Filipino, persons with disability, recent immigrant, rural, and sexual/gender minority populations, our acceptance rate increased from an average of 40% to 60% across all populations at both sites. Clinical skills sessions remained the most popular among campers, specifically suturing, intubation skills, and the cardiopulmonary exam. Student knowledge of the MD Admissions process grew at both sites at the conclusion of the AMCFY.

Discussion: AMCFY serves as an effective means of targeted medical outreach to underrepresented populations. At both sites, we achieved the same learning outcomes for camp participants. Our MMS increased campers' interest in medicine and their knowledge of medical program admissions processes.

Lisa Meeks (Lead Author), Tonya Fancher (Co Author), Mytien Nguyen (Co Author), Shruthi Venkataraman (Co Author), Gabriel Abrams (Co Author), Karina Pereira-Lima (Co Author), Amy Addams (Co Author), Christopher Moreland (Co Author), Dowin Boatright (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a cross-sectional study using deidentified data from the Association of American Medical Colleges Graduation Questionnaire (2020-2022) of U.S. MD-granting schools. Demographic variables included disability status, race/ethnicity, and sex. Outcomes were self-reported experiences of general, gender-based, and race-based discrimination. Modified Poisson regression was used to estimate relative risks of discrimination by identity categories and their intersections.

Results: Among 45,705 graduating students, 3,863 (8.5%) identified as having a disability, and 52.9% identified as female. MSWD were more likely than nondisabled peers to report general (RR, 1.57; 95% CI, 1.50-1.65), gender-based (RR, 1.64; 95% CI, 1.57-1.72), and race-based discrimination (RR, 1.55; 95% CI, 1.44-1.67). Intersectional analyses revealed striking disparities: Asian and Black female MSWD reported the highest risks of general discrimination (RR, 2.40 and 2.58, respectively) and race-based discrimination (RR, 8.53 and 12.48, respectively), while White and Asian female MSWD reported the highest risks of gender-based discrimination (RR, 4.65 and 4.41, respectively). Nearly half of Asian and Black female MSWD reported multiple forms of discrimination.

Discussion: Findings demonstrate that discrimination in medical school disproportionately affects MSWD, particularly women of color, reflecting the compounded impact of ableism, racism, and sexism. Addressing these inequities requires adopting intersectional frameworks for policy and practice, strengthening accommodations, and creating inclusive learning environments that support all trainees.

Nadine Wiper-Bergeron (Lead Author), Alain Stintzi (Co Author), Catherine Dumoulin (Co Author), Geneviève Breton-Harper (Co Author), Emilio Alarcon (Co Author)

Methods: CTM offers MSc (research project or thesis) and PhD streams built on a translational competency framework. Core elements include coursework, interdisciplinary seminars, and independent study plans tailored to each learner's background and goals. A central innovation is the co-learning model, in which MDs and PhDs co-train and co-mentor students, embedding reciprocal understanding of clinical and scientific perspectives into every project. Progression is tracked through EPA-linked assessments, research milestones, and supervisory committee reviews.

Results: The inaugural cohort received 21 applications (16 MSc, 5 PhD), with 16 admissions and 12 enrollments from diverse scientific and clinical backgrounds. All students developed individualized study plans and engaged in EPA-mapped activities. Learner feedback highlights the value of the MD-PhD co-learning environment and the flexibility of individualized pathways. Faculty report feasibility of EPA-based assessment and its utility in aligning training with translational competencies. Demand has increased, with 107 applications, 62 admissions (58%), and 43 enrollments for the 2025 cohort.

Discussion: To our knowledge, CTM is the first competency-based graduate program in translational medicine to operationalize EPAs while bridging clinical and basic sciences. Early outcomes demonstrate feasibility, strong learner engagement, and the promise of MD-PhD co-mentorship to develop effective translational scientists.

Claudio Del Grande (Lead Author), Annie Descôteaux (Co Author), Marie-Pierre Codsi (Co Author), Isabelle Croteau (Co Author), Alexandre Hudon (Co Author), Caroline Bissonnette (Co Author), Louis-Philippe Thibault-Lemyre (Co Author), Maude Perreault (Co Author), Marie-Michèle Dufour (Co Author)

Methods: As part of a mandatory university course on inter-professional collaboration in contexts of relational complexity, 1481 students from 13 health and social service programs were assigned to groups of 10 using an algorithm maximizing professional diversity. Each group participated in a 3-hour workshop co-facilitated by professionals and patient partners during fall 2024. Due to limited space, 78/148 groups (53%) held the workshop virtually. A total of 993 students (67% response rate; 52% virtual) completed an anonymous post-workshop questionnaire assessing group dynamics (primary outcomes) and collaboration skill gains (secondary outcomes), based on the updated Competency Framework of the Canadian Interprofessional Health Collaborative (CIHC 2024). Wilcoxon rank-sum tests and t-tests will be used to evaluate between-group differences on ordinal and continuous outcomes.

Results: Statistical analyses are underway; results will be presented at the conference.

Discussion: This study will provide valuable evidence to guide universities and educators in teaching inter-professional collaboration in a post-pandemic environment where in-person and virtual formats must be rebalanced.

Emma-Lee Rhyno (Lead Author), Anuradha Mishra (Co Author), Loukman Ghouti (Co Author)

Methods: This course was delivered in three 90-minute sessions that included patient experience panels, interactive modules, and hands-on workshops with Vision Loss Rehabilitation Canada. Student experiences and perceptions of the course were assessed through written reflections and a structured Likert scale survey.

Results: In its first semester, this course enrolled 49 students (Medicine 45%, Social Work 35%, Nursing 10%, Clinical Vision Science 8%, Health Promotion 2%) and 65% of participants responded to the evaluation survey. The course was highly rated, with 62.5% of participants strongly agreeing that the sessions were engaging. Most (65.6%) reported increased confidence in approaching the care of patients with vision loss, 84.4% strongly approved of facilitator competence and 93.8% of respondents would recommend this course to others. Student reflections highlighted recurring themes that included: 1) misconceptions and stereotypes around vision impairment can contribute to poor patient experiences; 2) clear communication strengthens patient rapport and understanding; 3) Vision Loss Rehabilitation Canada offers valuable support resources; and 4) the patient perspective adds significant value to the education of health care professionals.

Discussion: Course participants were equipped with strategies to provide accessible and compassionate care while increasing awareness of systemic barriers faced by patients with vision impairment. Positive participant feedback supports the role of this IPE initiative in addressing educational gaps, reinforcing plans for future course development.

Deborah Ocholi (Lead Author), Blair Bigham (Co Author), Susan Bondy (Co Author), Robert Steiner (Co Author), Jesse Kancir (Co Author)

Methods: A 12-week course was developed to employ dialectical inquiry to teach engagement, depolarization and trust- building skills. A metacognition approach was used to layer skills in public communication and journalism into the instructional design. Frequent individual, small group and large group reflections occurred. We evaluated education outcomes by purposefully recruiting graduates to participate in recorded semi-structured interviews. Using Grounded Theory, transcripts were coded by two reviewers using constant comparative methods. Results adhere to the COREQ checklist.

Results: Participants recognized the importance of the curriculum and developed new competence and expertise in dynamics of trust, trust building, depolarization and engagement strategies. Participants valued being encouraged to undertake perspective taking, which they report broadened their perspectives. Participants were eager to apply new competencies to their work. Despite feeling better equipped to communicate, students expressed fear of making missteps and of external factors limiting their ability to build trust.

Discussion: A novel communications curriculum employing dialectical inquiry and skills training developed competence and attitudinal shifts in public health graduate students with respect to engagement, depolarization, and trust building.

Nick Busing (Lead Author) Louise Hanvey (Co Author)

Methods: Compassionate Ottawa, a community-based organization with volunteer facilitators trained for ACP workshops, reached out to a local Family Health Team, an interprofessional group practice in Ottawa. We developed a letter that all the doctors sent out to their patients over the age of 55, asking them to participate in an ACP workshop. We offered workshops to the 150 patients who responded to the letter. 100 participants signed on for the workshops, 79 completed them and 64 completed the workshop evaluation form.

Results: More than 50 percent of respondents indicated that they had increased knowledge about the ACP process and how to start the conversation. Equal numbers learned more about substitute decision makers and were clearer about what they wanted to talk about going forward.

Discussion: This small project demonstrated how a community organization can work with medical professionals and how ACP is something we all need to think about as we age.

Clara Lu (Lead Author), Sarah Elias (Co Author), Sydney Ruller (Co Author), Samantha Halman (Co Author), Aliza Moledina (Co Author), John Graydon Simmons (Co Author), Kori LaDonna (Co Author)

Methods: The social medicine rotation is a 4-week postgraduate elective opportunity offered to core Internal Medicine and subspecialty General Internal Medicine residents. Through experiential learning, residents build competence in the theory and practice of social medicine, gaining confidence in advocating for patients with complex and intersecting social determinants of health. Residents rotate through clinical experiences related to inner city health, substance use, newcomer health, Indigenous health, chronic illness and disability, and 2SLGBTQ+ and sexual health. Scholarly aspects of this rotation include a reflective exercise and a resident-led teaching session on the Clinical Teaching Unit on a topic relevant to health advocacy.

Results: We will perform semi-structured interviews with residents and clinical preceptors on the Social Medicine Rotation to assess its impact on residents'(1) attitudes, behaviors, and career aspirations; (2) knowledge, skills, and competence; and (3) perceived strengths and weaknesses of this rotation in training residents for the Health Advocate role. We will collect aggregate quantitative data from Entrustable Professional Activities (EPAs) and rotation evaluations to characterize residents' experience of this rotation.

Discussion: By combining experiential learning, reflective practice, and teaching exercises, we envision that the Social Medicine Rotation will provide residents with a powerful educational opportunity and momentum for continuous professional development related to the Health Advocate role.

Erin Peebles (Lead Author), Rabia Khan (Co Author)

Methods: Using Adele Clark's Situational Analysis, we constructed an archive of research on "trust" in health professions education. A preliminary situational map was constructed to conceptualize 'trust' depictions, illustrating the complex pathways patients navigate when seeking care.

Results: Our analysis reveals patient "trust" is a multidimensional, complex, and intergenerational decision. Patients process their personal interactions alongside the historical experiences of their population. Marginalized groups, having experienced racism, stigmatization, and oppression, often mistrust providers and the system, fearing active harm.

Discussion: The assumption that patients trust providers forms the basis of medical encounters and physician training. Our findings underscore that patient entrustment decisions are profoundly shaped by historical harms and systemic power dynamics. Appreciating this complexity is essential for healthcare providers to (re)establish trust with historically marginalized groups, which is crucial for advancing equity and combatting misinformation to improve patient outcomes.

Tim Dubé (Lead Author), Matthieu Touchette (Co Author), Linda Bergeron (Co Author), Mariem Fourati (Co Author), Cassandra Barber (Co Author), Christina St-Onge (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a cross-sectional descriptive study using Canadian Post-M.D. Education Registry data for 2,410 residency graduates (2000-2020) from 35 programs at Université de Sherbrooke (UdeS). Specialties were grouped using UdeS's postgraduate classification, aligned with standard specialty classification frameworks. Practice locations were analyzed at two-, five-, and ten-years post-graduation, by specialty, sex, and Quebec's administrative health regions.

Results: Of the 2,410 graduates, 57.8% completed training in family medicine and 42.2% in other specialties. Most (77.7%) practiced in Quebec, with notable concentrations in the Eastern Townships (19.4%), Montérégie (14.6%), and Saguenay-Lac-Saint-Jean (7.6%).

Discussion: The trends observed in our study of graduates practicing in the university's designated health regions reflects long-standing social accountability efforts, including the establishment of distributed training campuses and targeted curricular reforms. This regional alignment underscores the university's role in addressing local health needs, particularly through its contribution to the family medicine workforce. By empirically examining where graduates ultimately practice, this study contributes evidence on how medical education programs can measure the impact of their efforts toward social accountability. The approach is transferable: linking educational outcomes to workforce trajectories offers a valuable framework for other institutions to assess, adapt, and align their training programs with the health priorities of the populations they serve.

Cassandra Barber (Lead Author), Edward Matsumoto (Co Author), Saad Chahine (Co Author)

Methods: A cross-sectional analysis using the 2021 Canadian Census for respondents aged 25-64 with a university degree in medicine and employed in professional health occupations. Immigration status was classified as immigrant (landed/permanent resident) versus non-immigrant (Canadian-born); non-permanent residents were excluded. Location of training was determined using the census "location of highest degree" variable (Canada vs. outside Canada). Racial identity was defined using the Population Group variable. We calculated relative representation ratios (physician population ÷ general population) to compare: (1) physicians vs. general population, (2) Canadian-trained vs. internationally trained physicians, and (3) immigrant vs. Canadian-born physicians.

Results: At first glance, physicians appeared more diverse than the general population. However, diversity was concentrated among internationally trained and immigrant physicians (Diversity Index = 1.94 and 1.98, respectively). Canadian-trained, Canadian-born physicians were markedly less diverse (0.85), below the general population benchmark (1.34), with persistent underrepresentation of Black, Indigenous, Filipino, and Latin American groups.

Discussion: Findings reveal that Canada's physician workforce diversity is sustained largely by immigration and international training pathways. Results underscore systemic barriers within domestic medical education and highlight the need for equity-focused admissions policies and data-driven accountability mechanisms.

Julie Massé (Lead Author), Olivia Gross (Co Author), Marie-Claude Tremblay (Co Author), Raphaël Chaumont (Co Author), Marie-Audrey Brochu-Doucet (Co Author), Isabelle Mercure (Co Author), Chantal Gravel (Co Author), Charles Boissonneault (Co Author), Sandra Criales (Co Author), Alhem Khacha (Co Author), Tim Dubé (Co Author)

Methods: The participatory logic analysis involved: co-developing the program's logic model with key stakeholders; building an integrative theoretical framework based on the results of a rapid review of the literature on the principles, characteristics, and activities used in such programs in medicine; and the re-examination of the logic model in light of the framework, to be carried out during a deliberative workshop involving program stakeholders and multidisciplinary expert.

Results: Preliminary results highlight CRSE program theory strengths and weaknesses in those key areas emphasized in the literature: (1) community partnerships aimed at engaging learners with communities through immersive and interactive activities; (2) interdisciplinary and intersectoral mentoring and role-modeling; (3) reflection on experience; (4) consideration for both learners' needs (e.g., preparation, safety, belonging) AND community partners' needs (e.g., reciprocal benefits); (5) organizational support.

Discussion: These findings identify key elements to consider when developing educational programs and initiatives aimed at learners' social engagement. They can be mobilized both locally to improve the CRSE and to develop a broader pedagogical model that could be transferred to other contexts.

Methods: Guided by a RE-AIM framework, the study assessed program reach (demographics) and effectiveness (self-reported confidence, wellness, connectedness, and goal achievement).

Ashley D'Amour (Lead Author), Clare Cook (Co Author), Mat Litalien (Co Author)

Methods: The CEPD office created a ChatGPT instance to generate and review learning objectives. Training materials included CFPC/RCPSC accreditation standards, the CanMEDS framework, Bloom's taxonomy, and office-created tip sheets. The Learning Objective Specialist (LOS) is free to use and is highlighted and linked on the NOSM U CEPD program development page. Coordinators share the LOS with planning committees, and use it in their own day-to-day work.

Results: The LOS drafts learning objectives at the program and session level. Each learning objective includes an associated Bloom classification, CanMEDs role(s), and key competencies. Drafted content is reviewed and refined by planning committees and/or speakers. More specific prompts (e.g., gaps, target audience) promote further tailoring. The LOS also reviews learning objectives, with a rationale for suggested revisions.

Discussion: To date the LOS has recorded 700+ conversations. Coordinators report timesaving and consistency as the biggest benefits, allowing them to provide additional support and facilitating better adherence to accreditation standards/processes. The LOS eases the administrative burden of drafting objectives, allowing committees to focus on refinement based on subject expertise. Other CPD offices have also adopted the tool. Additional use cases include drafting session descriptions and summarizing accreditation processes.

Rene Wong (Lead Author), Naomi Steenhoff (Co Author)

Methods: Using diabetes as a case study of interphysician collaboration, we analyzed interviews with 16 family physicians and specialists to explore how challenges implementing guidelines stimulate learning. We combined Foucault's concepts of discourse and resistance with AE to examine how: 1. power structures shape physicians' views of challenges and uncertainty as learning opportunities, 2. AE is made possible, 3. collaboration discourses facilitate or constrain adaptive practices.

Results: AE manifests as physicians judiciously deviating from guidelines when justified by context. Family physicians and specialists reflect on their relationships with patients to create context-matched solutions. However, an emphasis on guidelines-oriented performance metrics creates moral pressure on family physicians to refer to specialists, thereby constraining their flexibility and opportunities to navigate uncertainty.

Discussion: Combining Foucault with AE provides insights into how macrosocial factors shape how AE develops and is constrained. AE manifesting as context-specific adaptive deviations from guidelines may be labelled as 'clinical inertia', with implications for professional development. We discuss how integrating these perspectives could advance theoretical insights and pedagogical strategies to cultivate AE in complex health care systems.

Clare Cook (Co Author), Ashley D'Amour (Lead Author)

Methods: A new form bias question was implemented, asking participants to select from a list of possible perceived bias types. A comment box invited elaboration. Second, a bias debrief form was created. Completed by the program coordinator, it documents each type of bias perceived and collates comments. Third, there is dedicated time on the debrief meeting agenda to review reported bias and document response actions on the debrief form.

Results: Bias reporting and mitigation was tracked for 16 conferences (150 30-90 minute large-group sessions, with CME and FD topics). 24.7% of sessions had 1+ report of bias. "Other" was the most frequently option chosen; mention of branding; or speaker's expression of personal opinion were most commonly identified. In two cases, committees made new policies as a result; in another case, the committee is now exploring the perceived impact of bias on participants.

Discussion: The standardized process has increased understanding of bias' role in CPD programs; encouraged better accreditation standards adherence; and supported effective review of applications.

Adam Gavarkovs (Lead Author), Ievgeniia Rozhenko (Co Author), Emily Boardman (Co Author)

Methods: This qualitative study analyzed semi-structured interviews with 22 rural physicians who participated in UBC CPD's Coaching and Mentoring Program (CAMP). Interviews were originally collected for program evaluation and analyzed for the purpose of research using template analysis, guided by contemporary motivational theory.

Results: Four themes emerged: (1) Physicians sought coaching to expand, reinforce, verify, or diversity their skillset in response to three factors characteristic of rural practice - practice transitions, low procedure volume, and professional isolation; (2) Physicians were more engaged when coaches were perceived as credible and invested; (3) Ongoing motivation was sustained through skill-building, feedback, and personal connections; and (4) Time constraints posed a barrier for positive momentum.

Discussion: Rural physicians are motivated to engage in coaching when it addresses gaps in their evolving scope of practice and offers credible, supportive relationships. These findings contribute to a broader understanding of motivation in CPD and offer practical insights for designing coaching programs.
