Elizabeth Wooster (Lead Author), Adaira Landry (Co Author), Teresa Chan (Lead Author)

Methods: A series of semi-structured qualitative interviews were conducted with medical students, residents, and junior academic faculty within medical education and healthcare settings who self-identified as being talented. The transcripts of these one-on-one interviews were transcribed and dual-coded. Interactive thematic analysis was conducted.

Results: Through thematic analysis, it was found that identifying one's own talents is viewed as an iterative, developmental process. Often, a series of smaller, seemingly unimportant, experiences will have a greater impact on the talent development process than one large, "high-stakes" experience. Talents that existed prior to entering into medical training do not always remain once medical training is completed. Persisting talents may have shifted/been adapted by experiences throughout the medical education continuum. Participants emphasized the role of feedback and positive and negative experiences/opportunities in shaping talents throughout the learning process.

Discussion: Talents continue to develop and be shaped throughout the medical education continuum, and this emphasizes the importance of having support systems for individuals throughout the process. Lack of support and opportunities to nurture or develop talent may result in physicians who are disengaged and disinterested in being fully invested in the healthcare system.

Hei Ching Kristy Cheung (Co Author), Emily Guo (Co Author), Ellena Andoniou (Co Author), Nicole Woods (Co Author)

Methods: An experimental study was developed and will be implemented in January 2026 to assess the validity of this rubric using an objective structure clinical examination (OSCE). Learners who completed the program will be assessed at three OSCE stations covering key clinical and communication competencies that are relevant to diabetes foot screening. Each station will include two evaluators, one using a standardized OSCE checklist and the other using the novel rubric. Paired t-test will be used to compare participants' scores across the two evaluation tools, and inter-rater reliability will be calculated.

Results: Construct and content validity will be supported if paired t-tests show no significant difference between tools and if inter-rater reliability achieve 0.75 or above.

Discussion: Validation of the novel rubric will enable community partners to properly evaluate learners competence in providing adequate foot screening. This will in turn support the expansion of virtual training while ensuring its quality is comparable to in-person instruction, where immediate feedback is generally provided to learners.

Quinten Clarke (Lead Author), Douglas Lebo (Co Author), Erene Stergiopoulos (Co Author), Lynn Ashdown (Co Author), Shira Gertsman (Co Author), Julia Hanes (Co Author), Saleem Razack (Co Author), Tal Jarus (Co Author)

Methods: Data was obtained from the AFMC Student Questionnaires' MD Entry Survey (2019 to 2023) and Pre-Clerkship Survey (2021 to 2023); respondents were Canadian medical students. Individuals were identified as disabled if they responded yes to having a mental health concern, physical limitation, or learning disability. Preferred residency speciality, and optimism about a career in medicine ("Yes, a lot"; "Yes, a little"; "No") were analyzed using Cramér's V.

Results: Preliminary results showed that family medicine was the most prevalent first choice residency for all students (16.7%). Preferred residency speciality was loosely correlated with stage of training (Cramér's V = 0.19) and disability status (Cramér's V = 0.12). Career optimism was seen to be loosely correlated with disability status at the beginning of clerkship (Cramér's V = 0.22) and at time of entry to medical school (Cramér's V = 0.13). 1.51% of students living with disabilities noted no optimism for their career compared to 0.58% of non-disabled students at entry to medical school. At entry to clerkship, 5.69% of students with disabilities noted no optimism in their career compared to 1.60% of non-disabled students.

Discussion: Preliminary analysis suggests that residency choice varies between program entry and clerkship for all students. Career optimism appears to meaningfully differ by disability status with prominent reductions noted for students with disabilities.

Jobin Varughese (Lead Author), Elizabeth M Wooster (Co Author), Teresa M Chan (Co Author), Brent Thoma (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a multiphase environmental scan of community primary care clinics within 100 km of TMU which included: identifying potential teaching settings through an online search, assessing site readiness by interviewing physicians during site visits, and seeking feedback on the results from learners, faculty, and staff regarding the availability of teaching resources. Narrative data underwent thematic analysis.

Results: Fifty clinics were assessed. Of these, 46 (92%) expressed willingness to accept family medicine residents. Thematic analysis identified key challenges: limited emphasis on continuity of care, poor integration of teaching between the College of Family Physicians of Canada (CFPC) and Royal College specialties, and inadequate resources to support teaching (e.g., space, time, faculty development).

Discussion: Targeted interventions addressing the identified gaps will be needed to increase the number of primary care teaching clinics. Faculty development and ongoing support may mitigate resistance to teaching, while cross-specialty collaboration could reduce siloing. Phased implementation and additional resources would help overcome structural limitations. This work provides a foundation for building sustainable, community-based training sites to meet Ontario's pressing primary care needs.

Mohamad Hemadi (Lead Author), Susan Lamb (Co Author), Mathew Mercuri (Co Author), Cristian Rangel (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a scoping review using the Mak and Thomas (2022) framework, systematically searching ERIC, MEDLINE, and Scopus. We identified 58 empirical studies on humanities-based interventions (HBIs) and conducted qualitative content analysis of their definitions, disciplinary foundations, pedagogical aims, and methodological approaches.

Results: Our scoping review synthesizes 58 peer-reviewed studies and proposes an operational framework that classifies humanities-based interventions (HBIs) into five conceptual categories: ethical reasoning and moral education, narrative and reflective practice, aesthetic and expressive arts, historical and cultural insight, and critique and power analysis. Most studies used qualitative or mixed methods, with outcomes targeting empathy, reflective capacity, and ethical awareness.

Discussion: Our findings reveal significant conceptual ambiguity in how "the humanities" are defined in medical education. The paper contributes to ongoing discussions within medical humanities by offering a conceptual taxonomy that can support more rigorous, intentional, and epistemologically transparent approaches to integrating the humanities in medical education. It also identifies key patterns, epistemic tensions, and methodological challenges that complicate the design and evaluation of HBIs, providing a critical foundation for future research and curricular development.

Lucy Vorobej (Lead Author), Cynthia Whitehead (Co Author)

Methods: A historical case study approach was used, focusing on document analysis of the Canadian Family Physician (CFP) journal from its inception in 1967 to 2000. We chose the CFP as our case study because it launched during a transformative period for general practice. Its content reflects evolving priorities, identity formation, and professional tensions. An inductive qualitative analysis of CFP articles was conducted with a focus on education.

Results: During FM's foundational period (1967 to 1972), curriculum reform was presented as central to attracting medical students, countering "brainwashing" by specialist training, and legitimizing FM against perceptions of being a "watered-down" discipline. Calls for earlier and more prominent FM training persisted throughout the following three decades. Despite this persistent advocacy, FM recruitment challenges remained entrenched.

Discussion: With growing attention to the crisis in primary care access, history allows us to reconsider how we understand present dilemmas. Decades of curricular innovation have not substantially elevated family medicine's status in the medical hierarchy or attracted greater student interest. Addressing current workforce crises requires a critical re-evaluation of proposed educational solutions and collaborative efforts beyond education alone.

Vivian Uyen Vi Nguyen (Lead Author), J. Cristian Rangel (Co Author), Christopher Ramnanan (Co Author)

Methods: A comparative case study was employed, comparing selected document reviews, on-site observations, and semi-structured interviews with educators and laboratory staff at two major Canadian anatomy programs. Emerging patterns were captured using thematic analysis across all data sources.

Results: Policies and regulatory frameworks guiding body donation and cadaveric education shape the language and possibilities of personhood retention in anatomical education at both selected sites. The analysis revealed three meta-themes from the codes: (1) the conceptualizations of the body, (2) the treatment of the decedent, and (3) the living humans with continued responsibilities towards the deceased. The document review revealed that the body is regulated as hazardous materials, for donation, and as a pedagogical tool. The interviews suggest tensions in retaining donor personhood at various stages of cadaveric education.

Discussion: Regulatory language shapes the retention of personhood of human donors in anatomy education. The findings can serve as a foundation for developing recommendations on the respectful use of cadaveric donors as uniquely human teaching resources, enhancing humanistic principles in both anatomy education and clinical practice.

Samia Cherkaoui (Lead Author)

Methods: Archival research was conducted using the Wilder Penfield Digital Collection, the Osler Collection, and digitized holdings from the Internet Archive. Primary biographical accounts, contemporaneous reports, and modern clinical analyses were reviewed. A comparative case study framework was applied to correlate clinical symptomatology with measurable compositional features and to situate findings within historical and cultural contexts.

Results: Ravel's suspected primary progressive aphasia produced truncated phrasing, reduced thematic development, and incomplete sketches, particularly evident in the Piano Concerto for the Left Hand. His late works show measurable structural simplification aligned with progressive language dysfunction. Schumann's bipolar disorder with psychotic features manifested in cyclical oscillations: manic phases produced dense contrapuntal textures and abrupt modulations, while depressive periods yielded fragmented motifs, culminating in the incoherence of the Gesänge der Frühe. Scriabin's temporal lobe epilepsy generated ictal auras that translated into synesthetic experimentation; Prometheus: The Poem of Fire incorporated light as a notated element, providing evidence of seizure phenomenology directly shaping musical form. Across cases, illness imposed identifiable constraints while simultaneously driving stylistic innovation, leaving reproducible neural signatures in composition.

Discussion: These findings demonstrate how medical humanities can reveal quantifiable traces of disease in cultural production. By bridging neurology and musicology, the study reframes creativity not as the absence of illness but as its transformation. In Osler's spirit, it shows that medicine must study not only pathology in the clinic but also its imprint on human legacy.

Kevin Wyne (Lead Author), Kirsten Brown (Lead Author)

Methods: The purpose of this longitudinal applied qualitative research is to assess the integration of narrative competence-the ability to acknowledge, absorb, interpret, and act on patients' stories-into a PA curriculum. The NM curriculum includes 7 NM touchpoints, including a Pictures of Health activity that is completed by all students at both the start and end of their training. Each student submission was analyzed using a 6-step process to identify themes within a cohort.

Results: Evaluation of the initial submissions shows that students begin their training with a relational idea of what health means to them, often as a static entity. Evaluation of student submissions at the end of their training shows a much more nuanced, dynamic, and multidimensional conception of health often with a focus on empathy, advocacy, and balance.

Discussion: The longitudinal NM curriculum provides a unique window into PA students' evolving conceptions of health as the undergo their training. This knowledge allows educators to better understand professional identity formation among health sciences learners and to develop specific educational activities to foster this growth.

Prey Patel (Lead Author), Ruiyang Lauren Xu (Co Author), Riaz Mahmud (Co Author), David Liu (Co Author), Nooh Kabir (Co Author)

Methods: This project was reviewed by the Queen's University General Research Ethics Board and deemed exempt from formal REB review under TCPS 2 Article 2.5 (quality-improvement/educational-assessment category). We employed a mixed-methods design with a qualitative descriptive approach to capture faculty perspectives in depth. Using purposive sampling, MD faculty involved in teaching or facilitating case-based learning within the Undergraduate Medical Education (UGME) curriculum were invited to participate. Faculty emails were obtained through online searches and an internal registry available to medical students. Of 139 faculty contacted, 24 completed a 20-item Google Forms survey assessing perceived relevance, awareness, curricular experience, barriers, and readiness for integrating planetary health into the Queen's MD curriculum; four open-ended questions were included. Two participants subsequently completed semi-structured interviews consisting of 14 open-ended questions, allowing for follow-up and probing. Interviews were conducted via Zoom between September and November 2025 by one author, audio-recorded, and transcribed using third-party software.

Results: Quantitative Among 24 faculty across diverse departments, significant differences emerged between those with and without prior planetary health training. Faculty with prior training demonstrated higher knowledge scores (M=3.88, SD=0.91) compared to those without training (M=3.29, SD=1.03), though this difference was not statistically significant (W=56.0, p=0.363). Attitudes toward integration differed more substantially. Faculty with prior training reported more positive attitudes (M=4.17, SD=0.78) compared to those without training (M=3.44, SD=1.44), a statistically significant difference (W=32.5, p=0.023). The most pronounced difference was observed in self-efficacy: trained faculty reported higher confidence in teaching planetary health (M=3.33, SD=1.07) compared to untrained faculty (M=2.08, SD=0.73), also statistically significant (W=31.0, p=0.019). Perceptions of institutional readiness were identical between groups (M=3.44), suggesting individual training did not influence perceptions of organizational capacity. Significant differences were also observed for perceived importance of integration (W=25.5, p=0.005) and access to teaching resources (W=25.0, p=0.006), favouring those with prior training. Overall, faculty reported moderate familiarity with planetary health (mean ≈4/5) and agreed that integration is important for preparing future physicians (mean ≈3.8/5). However, confidence in teaching was lower (mean ≈3.1/5), and access to teaching resources was rated poor (mean ≈2.3/5). Commonly cited barriers included curricular overcrowding, unclear placement of content, and lack of expertise. Facilitators included dedicated curricular time, ready-made teaching materials, leadership support, and case-based or clinically integrated approaches. Qualitative Two semi-structured interviews were conducted with faculty in the Department of Family Medicine who held both clinical and UGME teaching roles. Participants emphasized the increasing clinical relevance of planetary health, particularly in relation to patient care. They described observing more health effects of climate change, including worsening respiratory symptoms during wildfire events, reinforcing the need for curricular integration to better contextualize patient presentations. Student interest was described as both a facilitator and a limitation: planetary health could be discussed organically when raised, but responsibility often fell on students to initiate discussion. Key barriers included curricular overcrowding, lack of a clear curricular home, limited institutional leadership or champions, insufficient national-level direction, and lack of accessible teaching resources. Participants also highlighted the need for interdisciplinary collaboration, particularly involving public health, and suggested integration through generalist and preventive health lenses. Facilitators included embedded case-based teaching, structured slide decks or seminars, and clearer institutional guidance on curricular goals.

Discussion: Faculty demonstrated moderate familiarity with planetary health and generally agreed that its integration is important for preparing future physicians; however, confidence in teaching and access to resources were limited. Prior planetary health training was associated with significantly more positive attitudes and greater self-efficacy, though not with differences in perceived institutional readiness. These findings suggest that integration barriers may relate more to confidence and structural support than to knowledge deficits alone. Qualitative findings reinforced the clinical relevance of planetary health, particularly in relation to observable patient impacts of climate change, while highlighting curricular overcrowding, unclear placement of content, and lack of visible leadership as key barriers. Faculty emphasized the need for dedicated curricular time, ready-made teaching materials, and institutional support. Overall, successful integration will likely require coordinated faculty development and structural alignment within the curriculum to ensure sustainable and equitable implementation.

Owen Luo (Lead Author), Rosemarie Vincent (Co Author), Linda Snell (Co Author), Ning-Zi Sun (Co Author)

Methods: Using the theory of planned behaviour as a sensitizing framework, a cross-sectional survey was designed, piloted, and distributed to Canadian core and general IM residents. The survey assessed participants' perceived learning needs, behavioural, normative and control beliefs regarding planetary health, knowledge with 10 multiple-choice questions (MCQs) and answer explanations, and a post-test reassessment of behavioural and control beliefs.

Results: A minority of survey participants (n=145 total) received planetary health education during residency (28%). Most participants reported low or no engagement in planetary health (88%), absent or borderline knowledge of planetary health (79%), and wanting more planetary health teaching (53%). Participants' baseline behavioural beliefs were higher than their normative (p<0.0001) and control beliefs (p<0.0001). Participants only answered 57±20% of the knowledge assessment MCQs correctly. Comparing pre- and post-test results, there was an increased perceived importance of performing environmental risk assessments (p=0.028), providing anticipatory guidance to improve patient climate resilience (p<0.0001), reducing the carbon footprint of their practice (p=0.014) and the healthcare system (p=0.035), and most participants (68%) reported more perceived capacity to integrate planetary health into their practice.

Discussion: Canadian IM residents report receiving limited planetary health education and perceived knowledge gaps that were objectified through knowledge assessment. Exposure to a brief "question-explanation" type educational intervention influenced residents' behavioural and control beliefs, suggesting that planetary health curricula can enhance residents' intention to integrate planetary health knowledge into practice.

Kaitlin Pedley (Lead Author), Mathilde Gaudreau Simard (Co Author), Harry Wang (Co Author)

Methods: A mixed-methods design was used, incorporating a quantitative survey and qualitative interviews. The survey was distributed to all Internal Medicine residents at the University of Ottawa; responses were characterized using descriptive statistics. Interview participants were identified through the survey and the transcripts were analyzed to identify themes related to Planetary Health learning experiences.

Results: A total of 17 eligible respondents completed the survey, and 4 completed in-depth interviews, with representatives from each year of study. The majority believe that climate change is an important health issue (94%), but few agreed that they had adequate training opportunities to develop Planetary Health skills (35%). Barriers including staff expertise, educational priorities, and collective investment were elicited from the interviews. The practical application of skills, longitudinal learning, and facilitation of engagement were determined to be needed.

Discussion: Respondents demonstrated an interest in Planetary Health despite few opportunities to learn about it. This study identifies the perceived and expressed learning needs of Internal Medicine residents concerning climate health and provides actionable recommendations for integrating Planetary Health education into residency training

Isabelle Raiche (Lead Author), Héla Kabani (Co Author), Madeline Amina Leroux (Co Author), Geneviève Gauthier (Co Author)

Methods: Cette étude qualitative de cas multiples, ancrée dans une approche réaliste, analyse la perspective d'acteurs de quatre milieux chirurgicaux canadiens, à la lumière de la théorie du curriculum-making, qui décrit le curriculum vécu comme complexe et difficile à saisir. Douze entretiens semi-dirigés ont été réalisés auprès de directeurs (n=2), de résidents (n=3), de gestionnaires (n=3) et de cliniciens-éducateurs (n=4).

Results: Trois enjeux structurent l'intégration de la santé planétaire : (1) la conceptualisation, entre une approche factuelle (transmission de connaissances) et transformatrice (changement de valeurs et pratiques) ; (2) la priorisation, soutenue par la sensibilisation climatique et la responsabilité sociétale, mais freinée par la primauté des compétences techniques et les contraintes d'agrément ; (3) l'opérationnalisation pédagogique, compliquée par l'absence de consensus, les défis d'évaluation et la nécessité de mobiliser savoir-être et engagement.

Discussion: L'intégration de la santé planétaire révèle les tensions entre innovation curriculaire et culture professionnelle. Elle met en lumière la nécessité de comprendre les processus de négociation dans des programmes saturés et de développer des approches pédagogiques adaptées aux domaines interdisciplinaires.

Stephanie Howe (Lead Author), Allison Chhor (Lead Author), Alex Sy (Co Author), Anthony Goodings (Co Author), Yasmine Zemni (Co Author), Hela Kabani (Co Author), Sydney Ruller (Co Author), Husein Moloo (Co Author)

Methods: A retrospective, mixed-methods approach was used to collect quantitative and qualitative data from the PHRC's published in 2022, 2023, and 2024. We examined outcomes in five domains: Curriculum, Interdisciplinary Research, Community Research and Advocacy, Support for Student-Led Initiatives, and Campus Sustainability.

Results: Over three years, worldwide, there was an 11.1% increase in overall PHRC score. The greatest increase was in the Curriculum domain and the smallest was in the Campus Sustainability domain. The most common facilitators include a dedicated sustainability committee and strong student leadership, while the most common barriers include fragmented communication and lack of institutional support.

Discussion: By comparing three years of PHRC integration and institutional changes, areas of progress and opportunities for further development were identified. Longitudinal implementation of planetary health policies was primarily driven by dedicated sustainability committees, while student leadership provided new initiatives and events. Fragmented communication and lack of institutional support were often associated, and indicated minimal awareness of the few planetary health opportunities available to students at these institutions. This analysis highlights the effectiveness of the PHRC in driving change and informs future efforts to enhance institutional engagement with planetary health.

Glenn Posner (Lead Author), Kaitlin Endres (Co Author), Michael O'Brien (Co Author), Fahad Alam (Co Author), Khyldoon Bajwa (Co Author), Vicki LeBlanc (Co Author)

Methods: Content was created by developing an EM scenario for VR and 360°-video, both piloted for usability. 20 trainees were randomized to each group (n=40 total). Pre- and post-tests and heart rate (HR)/HR variability (HRV) monitoring assessed knowledge acquisition, performance and emotional engagement between groups.

Results: Preliminary data from the first 16 participants showed 360°-video significantly improved knowledge, from 63.3%, SD 16.5 to 72.2%, SD 12 (p=0.24), while VR showed no significant change (69.3%, SD 12 to 79.0%, SD 6.5) (p=0.86). HRV did not differ between groups.

Discussion: Findings suggest that the 360°-video may enhance knowledge acquisition more effectively than VR, possibly because witnessing the perfect management of the case is superior to the trial-and-error required of the VR module. These results can help support decisions regarding which virtual modalities are most educationally effective for distanced simulation. Future directions include completion of full analysis of 40 participants and will include a cost analysis to allow for fact-based estimations of future investments in VR education.

Tara Leary (Lead Author), Clare Cook (Co Author), Adele Bodson (Co Author), Kristen Morin (Co Author)

Methods: Two lo-fi addictions simulation modules were developed and accredited (Mainpro+ 3:1; RCPSC Section 3). One addresses buprenorphine initiation for opioid use disorder; the other addresses managing alcohol withdrawal and cravings. The simulations provide practical experience for practitioners in low-resource areas, focusing on evidence-based treatment and highlighting key non-stigmatizing communication skills. After piloting, sims were offered in three community contexts. Sessions were advertised by email and newsletter.

Results: Despite high interest in the topic, very low registration uptake for 2 of the 3 community offerings led to cancellation. The third offering was provided during an existing CPD conference and run three times; fully booked. In post-session retrospective post-then-pre data, 100% of participants reported more confidence in assessing for opioid withdrawal;78% reported more confidence in initiating buprenorphine. Median positive change was three steps (ten-point scale). At 8-week followup (n=6), 33% had implemented change; 67% were in-progress. 67% reported sharing learning with colleagues.

Discussion: Initial data indicates modules are well-received and leave participants prepared to initiate new, evidence-based approaches in their practice. Multiple participants are setting up new addictions services in their community. Uptake remains a large barrier, prompting further research on motivation and barriers to attending.

Aradhana Tewari (Lead Author), Ryan Brydges (Lead Author), Anni Germann Najbjerg (Co Author), Rune Dall Jensen (Co Author), Kristian Krogh (Co Author), Lindsay Beavers (Co Author), Nazanin Khodadoust (Co Author), Kerry Fraser (Co Author), Natalie Wong (Co Author), Katherine Allan (Co Author), Douglas Campbell (Co Author)

Methods: We gathered data through realist interviews and observations in Canadian and Danish hospitals to explore how, why, for whom, and under what conditions training can be (in)effective. Initial program theories (IPTs) and Context-Mechanism-Outcome (CMO) configurations were developed through stakeholder consultation and review of program materials. Our team-based analysis of the resulting CMOs guided our sampling, data collection and analysis to iteratively refine IPTs.

Results: from Canada (23 Interviews: unit leads, nurse educators, training educators, unit staff; and 13 training observations) and Denmark (data collection ongoing, 5 interviews: unit leads, training educators; and 14 training observations) indicate that educator-led, small-group, hybrid, in-situ training with familiar contexts and equipment improves learning and engagement. Continued leadership support, regular exposure to IHCAs (real and simulated), and outcome-oriented training processes help maintain resuscitation skills.

Discussion: Realist evaluations demonstrate how mapping CMOs enables unit leaders to make informed decisions about training needs, implementation practices, and unit-specific metrics to enhance patient survival. Our list of refined program theories can help other hospitals understand how their organizational context and training designs uniquely impact first-responder performance.

Maria Hussain (Lead Author), Heather Braund (Co Author), Jon Pipitone (Co Author), Simon Obrien (Co Author), Melissa Andrew (Co Author), Leah Nemiroff (Co Author), Emily St Denis (Co Author), Julia Kirkham (Co Author), Matthew Tobin (Co Author)

Methods: A total of 18 Geriatric Psychiatry and PGY 2-4 Psychiatry residents at Queen's University participated in a financial capacity simulation day in June 2025. They completed two simulations, receiving feedback from faculty between cases. They completed a pre-post knowledge test and an exit focus group to discuss their experiences. Focus groups were conducted with the 6 faculty debriefers. Quantitative data were analyzed in R using descriptive analyses and qualitative data were analyzed thematically.

Reza Akbari Asbagh (Lead Author), Roghayeh Gandomkar (Lead Author), Babak Mostafazadeh Davani (Co Author), Sara Mortaz Hejri (Co Author), Seyed Mohsen Ahmadi Tafti (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a social network analysis utilizing a survey-based methodology. The survey was consisted of three domains of procedural skills, clinical assessment, and professionalism. A sample of fifteen surgery residents was asked to report the frequency of feedback-seeking interactions with a predefined list of their peers and supervisors over the preceding month within the three identified domains. Network metrics-including density, centrality, and reciprocity-were computed for each feedback category, and inter-domain comparisons were performed using Jaccard similarity coefficients.

Results: Peer networks for procedural skills and clinical assessment demonstrated moderate density (0.15-0.16) and reciprocity (0.39-0.48), with third-year residents occupying central positions in both weighted and unweighted metrics. In contrast, the professionalism network exhibited lower density (0.095) and reciprocity (0.20), indicating sparser and less mutual interactions. Bipartite networks between residents and supervisors showed higher weighted densities, particularly in procedural skills (0.71), suggesting stronger supervisory engagement in technical guidance. Centralization values varied across domains, with higher indegree centralization in clinical assessment and professionalism, reflecting reliance on select individuals for domain-specific feedback. Jaccard similarity coefficients indicated substantial overlap between procedural skills and clinical assessment networks (binary: 0.72; weighted: 0.86), while professionalism networks remained more distinct.

Discussion: These findings underscore domain-specific variations in feedback-seeking behavior and highlight the influence of hierarchical and peer relationships in shaping learning networks within surgical training.

Andrew D'Elia (Lead Author), Armaan Dhanoa (Co Author), Kien Dang (Co Author), Samantha Inwood (Co Author)

Methods: Fifteen second-year medical students were paired with a 3rd or 4th-year clinical clerk for 2-4 clinical half-day shadowing sessions from Feb-Apr 2025. Semi-structured interviews and anonymous surveys were administered to assess program impact and feasibility.

Results: Seven 4th and eight 3rd year clerks were matched with 15 second year students, and 14 pairs successfully completed the pilot. Surveys and semi-structured interviews were completed by 76% and 43% of participants, respectively. Participants unanimously endorsed program feasibility and recommended adoption into the curriculum. Interview data supports that PreTTC provides valuable insight into the role of the clerk, eases stress regarding the TTC and provides students with opportunities to seek clinical mentorship that is distinct from other curricular/extracurricular offerings.

Discussion: Results suggest this model provides an improved, effective approach to addressing students' needs during TTC. This work lays the foundation for larger pilots and possibly program-wide implementation of longitudinal, near-peer shadowing experiences as a method of facilitating students' TTC.

Abdullah Qureshi (Lead Author), Ibrahim Al-Mouaiad Al-Azem (Co Author), Adam Ali Hussain (Co Author), Xiaoyu Yu (Co Author), Abdu Etagiuri (Co Author), Ayeh Aldulaymi (Co Author), Dr. Rick Jaggi (Co Author)

Methods: Following PRISMA 2020 guidelines, this review extracted literature from MEDLINE, Embase, Scopus, and Web of Science from inception to June 28, 2025. Primary studies of undergraduate medical students participating in SEAD were included. Outcomes were extracted and categorized by Kirkpatrick level. Study quality was appraised across eight domains.

Results: Ten studies (2013-2023) with a total of 376 first and second year students met inclusion criteria. There was high overall participant satisfaction with the SEAD program. Interestingly, workshops and structured mentorship were rated most useful, while virtual operating room observerships were least favored. Surgical knowledge improved in most studies. One cohort doubled test scores (10 out of 25 to 20 out of 25) and one showed a 21.2 percentage point improvement (51.5 to 72.7 percent, p < 0.001). Behavioral outcomes included increased or refined career interest. Level 4 Kirkpatrick outcomes were rarely reported; one underpowered study found 41 percent of SEAD alumni ranked surgical specialties in the CaRMS match (p = 0.47).

Discussion: SEAD programs are consistently well received. The program reproducibly improves short term learner satisfaction, surgical knowledge and career clarity (Kirkpatrick Level 1-3). However, the evidence is not yet strong enough to claim it significantly impacts residency choice or match outcomes (Kirkpatrick Level 4). Future studies should use stronger comparators, validated instruments, with a focus on longitudinal tracking.

Padraig Casey (Lead Author)

Methods: Using interpretive description methodology, semi-structured interviews were conducted with recent graduates and preceptors from the Dalhousie Family Medicine Program. Thematic analysis was applied to transcribed interview data.

Results: Supervision practices vary widely across the program due to contextual and individual factors. These variations can be understood through the lens of communities of practice, particularly in terms of how they facilitate or hinder full participation. A shared desire for continued mentorship beyond residency emerged as a key theme.

Discussion: The different supervision experiences of family medicine residents highlight disparate understandings of concepts such as "independence" and "competence". The CFPC has named practice-readiness as the essential outcome of residency trainig. Attention to the authenticity of clinical work might help residents become ready for practice before the end of training. Steven Montague (Lead Author), Jennifer Turnnidge (Lead Author), Laura Marcotte (Co Author), Oluwatoyosi Kuforiji (Co Author), Heather Braund (Co Author), Sarah Greco (Co Author), Nancy Dalgarno (Co Author), Laura McEwen (Co Author), Adam Szulewski (Co Author)

Methods: We invited program leaders/staff, faculty, and residents of a GIM program at a Canadian tertiary care centre to participate in the study. We conducted interviews and focus groups with program leaders and staff (n = 5), faculty (n = 3), and residents (n = 4), which were transcribed and analyzed abductively.

Results: We identified five themes that best represented program partners' experiences of CBME implementation within the GIM program: (a) navigating the "growing pains" of CBME, (b) balancing responsibilities, (c) re-connecting theory and practice, (d) creating a supportive context, and (e) adopting a learner-centric approach. Tasha Wyatt (Lead Author), Rachel Chamberlin (Co Author), Witzard Seide (Co Author), Emily Scarlett (Co Author), Jessica Bunin (Co Author)

Methods: We used elements of constructivist grounded theory for data collection and analysis. We conducted 15 semi-structured interviews with current and former SPC members at a U.S. medical school. Using constant comparative methods, we identified the salient theme of student 'insight'. We then used a conceptual framework from rehabilitation science to interpret our findings, drawing a connection between the language of medical education and that of the legal system.

Results: Insight was a critical component of the SPC's deliberation and decision-making process. It was viewed both as a strong indicator of a student's potential for growth and as a clear indicator of risk to patient safety when absent. Students demonstrated insight by taking ownership of their issues, using available resources, and collaborating with the committee. Conversely, a lack of insight was identified when students engaged in "magical thinking," repeated problematic actions, or blamed others.

Discussion: The prominence of insight in student presentations suggests that medical education may have unintentionally placed the burden of providing insight on students. This study provides transparency by articulating what SPC members look for in these presentations, which can help improve the remediation process.

Stella Yiu (Lead Author), Samantha Halman (Co Author), Ariel Hendin (Co Author), Marianne Yeung (Co Author), Warren Cheung (Co Author), Fady Balaa (Co Author), Jason Frank (Co Author)

Methods: Using a constructivist stance, we explored handover as a social process inductively. Staff physicians from a tertiary academic urban institution were recruited for interviews. Independent researchers identified codes and resolved discrepancies collaboratively. The team met to discuss codes and generate themes. Data collection continued until thematic saturation. We re-analyzed the data using the Shared Mental Model framework, which includes four dimensions: task-specific knowledge, task-related knowledge, team knowledge, and team attitudes.

Results: Clinical handover encompassed all four dimensions of the Shared Mental Model. Task specific knowledge involved procedures; task-related knowledge encompassed institutional factors; team knowledge included understanding colleagues' personalities; and attitudes captured concepts such as patient ownership. The emphasis on each dimension varied based on factors such as discipline, workload, and coverage. Some dimensions were tacit, and incompatibilities in these areas led to conflict.

Discussion: Clinical handover operates as an elastic framework with multiple dimensions that clinicians dynamically adjust to suit specific contexts. Team knowledge and attitudes in particular, were often tacit and prone to miscommunication. Current checklists typically exclude these tacit dimensions, and lack flexibility to fit specific context. To mitigate miscommunication, physicians should make these dimensions explicit, and tailor their handover framework to their unique clinical settings.

Helen Mullaney, MEd (Lead Author), Roetka Gradstein, MD (Co Author), Stephanie Power, MD (Co Author), Georgina Mckenna, MPH (Co Author), Roberta Preston, PhD (Co Author), Katrina Hurley, MD (Co Author)

Methods: Using a feminist-informed document analysis, we reviewed institutional policies and contacted 36 departments in Nova Scotia and New Brunswick. At least two researchers analyzed each document using the READ approach (readying, extracting, analyzing, distilling), with the CARDA framework guiding reporting.

Results: Along with institutional policies, we reviewed 47 emails and 20 documents. No scientific departments had policies beyond the university collective agreement. One of 13 New Brunswick and four of 16 Nova Scotia clinical departments provided policies. Themes included reliance on informal cultures of support; narrow focus on compensation and leave; deficit framing of parenthood as a disruption to productivity; and gaps for circumstances such as pregnancy loss and surrogacy.

Discussion: The absence of parenthood-related policies may disadvantage women, early-career physicians, and new hires. While informal structures may help, they lack accountability. Our findings underscore the need for transparent policies extending beyond finances and leave.

Nicole Fu (Lead Author), X Catherine Tong (Co Author), Stacey Ritz (Co Author), Keyna Bracken (Co Author), Lawrence Grierson (Co Author)

Methods: A corpus of discussion threads from the r/medschoolcanada subreddit (August 2024-August 2025) was collected using a Python scraper to extract posts tagged with keywords relevant to medical specialties. Threads were then manually screened for relevance. A framework combining Discursive Psychology and Critical Discourse Analysis guided qualitative analysis of how individual and structural discourses shape specialty perceptions.

Results: Analysis of 338 posts and 3900 comments revealed that family medicine and career decisions were frequently framed through competing repertoires of individual agency and structural constraints. Concurrently, there is systematic devaluation of generalist practice through ideological frameworks privileging subspecialty expertise and income. Overall, students' identity work simultaneously reproduced and contested power structures that systematically undervalue family medicine while establishing a hierarchy of medical specialty desirability.

Discussion: These findings highlight how individual and social engagement with dominant ideologies dissuade students from pursuing family medicine. Understanding this discursive construction provides educators and policymakers insight into how students make career decisions, guiding potential interventions to improve recruitment into family medicine.

Anne Mahalik (Lead Author), Roberta Preston (Co Author), Christy Simpson (Co Author), Wendy Stewart (Co Author)

Methods: We gathered stories from 56 staff across Dalhousie University's Faculty of Medicine. We used narrative inquiry, focusing on personal stories to better understand their workplace culture. A four-level socioecological model was applied to the data to identify varying levels of influence over individual experiences, representing the complex interplay between an individual, relationships with their Department/Academic Unit, Faculty, and Dalhousie, the institution in which they work.

Results: Participants noted that a simple "thank you" or acknowledgement of their work made them feel valued. However, many felt undervalued due to hierarchical structures, lack of accountability for poor behaviour, and financial stress, contributing to feelings of hopelessness and futility. Discussion: The well-being of staff has been largely ignored in medical education settings. Despite their essential role, their contribution is often overlooked, or shaped by others, leaving them vulnerable to overwork, burnout, and hopelessness. Our findings highlight the need for greater support and recognition of staff in academic settings to preserve high quality medical education

Oceane Juillet (Lead Author), Jade Bigras (Lead Author), Sophie Plamondon (Co Author), Carolina Lucena Fernandes (Co Author), Marie-Pier Villemure (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a literature review (1990-2025) in English and French, focusing on frameworks that define teaching competencies in medical education (undergraduate, clerkship, residency, fellowship). Inclusion criteria targeted explicit frameworks developed for use by teachers in practice, grounded in rigorous methodology (systematic reviews, Delphi, empirical validation) or produced by recognized organizations. Data were extracted, compared, and synthesized to identify common domains and gaps.

Results: Ten relevant frameworks were identified. While they address essential competencies (communication, professionalism, leadership, scholarship, collaboration, commitment to learning), their uptake remains limited due to complexity, limited accessibility, or insufficient dissemination. Our synthesis highlights six integrative and transdisciplinary domains that capture "what makes a good medical teacher" and provide guidance for physician-educators across diverse learning contexts, based on an existing framework that is well known and widely used.

Discussion: This conceptual framework has the potential to strengthen faculty confidence, clarify expectations, and promote recognition of the educational mission within academic medicine. By offering shared language and concrete guidance, it supports both individual development and institutional consistency in medical teaching.

Heather Braund (Co Author), Eleftherios Soleas (Lead Author), Wiley Chung (Lead Author), Amireza Goli (Co Author)

Methods: We reviewed literature and integrated structural competency and professionalism frameworks to propose "clean charting," the intentional use of respectful, unbiased language as a core competency. We developed a conceptual framework for embedding clean charting into continuing professional development (CPD) and faculty development (FD). Recommended strategies include targeted workshops, institutional policies on documentation language, routine documentation feedback, and leveraging EHR features to flag biased language and suggest alternatives.

Results: This framework outlines practical interventions addressing chart pollution at multiple levels in medical education and clinical practice. Faculty workshops on identifying and removing biased language, combined with institutional guidelines and EHR alerts, increased clinician awareness and improved documentation habits. Pilot programs reported greater sensitivity to documentation bias and stronger engagement with structural competency among faculty and learners.

Discussion: Treating stigmatizing clinical documentation as a site of bias is vital for advancing health equity. Embedding clean charting into FD and CPD curricula can disrupt patterns of prejudice entrenched in medical records, improve patient-clinician trust, and foster a culture of respectful, patient-centered care. Medical education must broaden to include documentation ethics, giving chart language the same critical attention as verbal communication and clinical reasoning.

William McCauley (Lead Author), Adenike Femi-Mese (Co Author)

Methods: Three electronic surveys were administered: (1) Leaders (n=55/236; 23.3% response), including decanal leaders, department chairs, and residency directors; (2) Faculty (n=318/2,806; 11.3%), representing medicine, dentistry, and basic sciences; and (3) Learners (n=146/2,042; 7.2%), spanning undergraduate and postgraduate training. Surveys included Likert scales, multiple choice items, and open-text responses. Descriptive statistics and inductive thematic analysis were applied to identify patterns across teaching, research, leadership, and wellness domains.

Results: Leaders highlighted challenges in mentorship, research funding, and promotion for early-career faculty, and leadership training needs for mid-career faculty. Faculty respondents emphasized effective feedback, structured onboarding, wellness supports, and flexible programming. Learners reinforced these findings, reporting inconsistent feedback and variable mentorship. Across all groups, mentorship and accessible, embedded programming emerged as the most consistent needs. Social accountability topics identified as a need include indigenous health, anti-racism, cultural humility and professionalism.

Discussion: Findings demonstrate strong alignment between perceived and unperceived needs across stakeholders, underscoring mentorship, leadership preparation, and wellness as cross-cutting priorities. Triangulating these perspectives ensures faculty development initiatives are responsive to both educator and learner expectations. These results will directly inform a customizable program addressing faculty needs across career stages.

Patricia Huston (Lead Author), Maddie Venables (Co Author), Laura Visentin (Co Author), Doug Archibald (Co Author), Jarvis Dongmo (Co Author)

Methods: A review of the literature was conducted to identify key areas of climate change preparedness, potential barriers and facilitators, and preferred formats for learning. Validity testing was done by expert review. Readability was assessed through Think Aloud focus groups. A pilot study assessed feasibility by measuring response rate and how long it took to do the survey, and assessed reliability by measuring test-retest and conducting factor analysis.

Results: Six areas of climate change preparedness were identified: Increasing patient/public awareness; Addressing the health effects of climate change; Greening health care; Strengthening personal and emergency preparedness; and Advancing planetary health. Validity of the survey was confirmed by expert review. Readability was improved through iterative Think Aloud focus groups. The pilot study is underway, and preliminary results will be given at the time of the conference.

Discussion: To our knowledge, this is the first comprehensive survey of climate change preparedness in primary care. It will be used to inform post-graduate and CPD strategies and could be used both locally and nationally to assess and track the current level of preparedness.

Anuradha Mishra (Lead Author), Mostafa Bondok (Co Author), Mohamed S Bondok (Co Author), Michael Knafo (Co Author), Christine Law (Co Author), Nawaaz Nathoo (Co Author), Lars J Grimm (Co Author)

Methods: This is a multi-institutional, explanatory sequential mixed-methods study of medical students from four geographically representative Canadian institutions with varying levels of ophthalmology exposure completed a 17-item survey assessing perceptions, barriers and facilitators to pursuing ophthalmology. Quantitative data was analyzed using non-parametric tests and ordinal logistic regression to assess associations between both demographics and context-specific factors and survey responses. Open-ended qualitative responses were analyzed thematically using Braun and Clarke's framework.

Results: A total of 314 students participated, including 213 (67.8%) women, 125 (39.8%) visible minorities, and 73 (23.3%) Canadian immigrants. Negative perceptions of pursuing ophthalmology increased during medical school, with 29.9% of students expressing a negative view at the start of medical school compared to 45.5% at the time of the survey-a 52.1% relative increase (p=0.007). Only 30.9% viewed the field as racially diverse, and 26.8% as gender balanced. Analysis of the qualitative responses showed that barriers to pursuing ophthalmology included intense competition, limited early exposure, lack of mentorship, perceived exclusivity based on personal connections, high research expectations, and difficulties with parallel planning.

Discussion: Increased early exposure, improved mentorship opportunities, and promoting diversity may support greater gender and racial representation in ophthalmology.

Results: Nine interviews were conducted from December 2025 to January 2026. Interviews are ongoing until March 2026, and data analysis will commence concurrently with completed interviews. Findings are expected to reveal how new and known factors impact specialty selection for EMS.

Findings will inform equity-focused interventions for residency counselling and career planning at medical schools across Canada. Long-term benefits of improved representation across specialties will help the healthcare system meet Canadians' diverse needs and improve healthcare outcomes.

Ezekiel Garuba (Lead Author), Nicole Da Silva (Co Author), Maryam Taghavi (Co Author), Jocelyn Charles (Co Author), Mireille Norris (Co Author)

Methods: The study is recruiting 25 adults who self-identify as Black, are ≥30 years old, and have had hypertension for ≥1 year. Participants attend four weekly virtual seminars focused on hypertension education and a culturally adapted DASH diet. Data collection includes the University of Kansas Nutrition Literacy Assessment Instrument (UKNLAI), paired ASA24 dietary recalls, and baseline chart abstraction of blood pressure, BMI, comorbidities, and antihypertensive medications. Self-report surveys assess blood pressure self-monitoring and perceived grocery costs at baseline and 8 weeks. Focus groups at 3 months explore barriers and sustainability.

Discussion: Early findings indicate that culturally adapted nutrition education is feasible and acceptable within academic primary care. Tristan Paranavitana (Lead Author), Jodi Gallant (Lead Author), Debra Marvel (Co Author), Sofia Bzovsky (Co Author), Sheila Sprague (Lead Author), Kaitlyn Pusztai (Co Author), Paula McKay (Co Author), Jeffrey Wells (Co Author), Julie Menard (Co Author), Jamal Al-Asiri (Co Author), Joseph Patterson (Co Author), Gerard Slobogean (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a cross-sectional survey of participants with orthopedic fractures at Hamilton Health Sciences from the PREPARE trial. Following ethics approval, participants or their SDMs provided informed consent and indicated whether they wished to receive trial results, and their preferred method. A survey was administered, and participants were offered their treatment allocation. Results were summarized using descriptive statistics with 95% confidence intervals.

Results: Twenty-eight percent of PREPARE trial participants contacted agreed to participate in this study. We found that 95.5% of respondents wished to know the trial results, most preferring online summary posters. Furthermore, 82.2% of respondents requested to know their treatment allocation.

Discussion: Our study's findings are valuable to investigators. Receiving the trial results was important to a portion of participants. Sharing results may benefit both participants and the scientific community. 50.7% of respondents were more likely to participate in another clinical trial after receiving the results, and 2.1% were less likely. Barriers to contacting participants following the PREPARE trial highlighted the need for researchers to plan dissemination strategies during ethics submissions to ensure approval.

Tauqeer Iftikhar (Lead Author), Meet Patel (Co Author), Neel P. Mistry (Co Author), Sierra Leonard (Co Author), Abd Alras (Co Author), Yash Chauhan (Co Author), Adam A. Hussain (Co Author), Sean Polreis (Co Author), Rob Woods (Co Author), Stephen Lee (Co Author), Jen Chlan (Co Author), Scott J. Adams (Co Author)

Methods: A total of 7,731 de-identified evaluations were obtained from UGME pre-clerkship clinical skills sessions. Prior to scoring, all raters completed a structured training session on application of the QuAL rubric. Eleven trained reviewers then scored each evaluation using the QuAL score. The reviewer cohort consisted of medical students, resident physicians, faculty physicians, and educators.

Results: The median QuAL score was 2 out of 5 (IQR: 2-4). Although 70.2% of comments included a suggestion for improvement, only 36.9% of those suggestions explicitly referenced a specific observed performance, and 52.6% of all comments contained no direct evidence of the learner's performance. 17% achieved a maximum score of 5, indicating that excellent-quality feedback was present in a minority of cases.

Discussion: The majority of the clinical skills feedback offered suggestions for improvement; however, the quality of comments often lacked specificity and clear behavioural linkage, limiting its educational value. Evidence of learner performance was often insufficient. This human-rated dataset will next be used to train and validate a natural language processing (NLP) model for automated QuAL scoring and developing a model for real-time feedback coaching.

Simranjeet Singh (Lead Author), Nancy Dudek (Co Author), Kori LaDonna (Co Author), Jeffrey Landreville (Co Author), Andrew Hall (Co Author), Warren Cheung (Co Author)

Methods: The objective of this study was to explore clinical supervisors' perspectives on providing feedback and assessment to off-service trainees. Staff physicians from various disciplines at an academic hospital were recruited for semi-structured interviews. Constructivist grounded theory (CGT) methodology was utilized to identify common themes surrounding the assessment of off-service residents.

Results: Transcripts from interviews with fourteen (n=14) physicians demonstrated four key themes: staff physician objectives for off-service residents, resident self-awareness, limitations of assessment modalities, and organizational challenges to meaningful relationships and its impact on feedback.

Discussion: Off-service rotations offer a promising opportunity for residents to expand their knowledge base and skillset, especially in a low-stakes environment where expectations are lower than when on-service. The lack of shared understanding of training objectives create a major barrier to effective teaching and feedback. Physicians complained that residents are often unaware of their learning needs, underscoring the importance of the trainee's initiative in shaping their learning on off-service rotations. Assessment tools are currently not structured to support staff physicians perform high quality assessments and are limited by their generalizability for the wide heterogeneity of off-service residents supervised. Addressing these issues may improve the experience of off-service rotations and improve the quality of assessment and feedback.

Lisa Meeks (Co Author), Tonya Fancher (Lead Author), Mytien Nguyen (Co Author), Gabriel Abrams (Co Author), Tiffany Hodgens (Co Author), Raymond Curry (Co Author), Michael Kim (Co Author), Rylee Betchkal (Co Author), Christine Low (Co Author), Sharad Jain (Co Author), Shami Tarlanov (Co Author), William Eidtson (Co Author), Caitlyn Coats (Co Author), Charles Weiner (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a cross-sectional study of MSWD who graduated between 2020 and 2023 across nine U.S. MD-granting medical schools. Deidentified student records provided demographic data (gender, race/ethnicity), disability type, timing of diagnosis (pre- vs post-matriculation), and presence of a specialized disability resource professional (DRP). Outcomes included request for Step 1 accommodations and approval status. Chi-square and Fisher exact tests, with Bonferroni adjustments, were used to assess associations.

Results: Among 295 MSWD, 120 (41%) requested Step 1 accommodations, with 67 of 97 (69%) receiving approval. White students requested accommodations more frequently than Asian peers (46% vs 20%). Students with ADHD (50%) and chronic health conditions (53%) were more likely to request than those with psychological disabilities (30%). Approval was lowest for students with psychological disabilities (52%), ADHD (52%), and post-matriculation diagnoses (43%). Schools with specialized DRPs demonstrated higher approval rates (78% vs 53%).

Discussion: Findings reveal inequities in both requests and approvals of Step 1 accommodations, shaped by disability type, timing of diagnosis, race, and institutional resources. Expanding access to specialized DRPs and standardizing processes are critical to reducing disparities and ensuring equity in medical training and licensure

Harpreet Pangli (Lead Author), Faizal Haji (Co Author), Taryn Taylor (Co Author)

Methods: This qualitative, interview-based study utilized Constructivist Grounded Theory (CGT). Semi-structured interviews were conducted at the University of British Columbia (UBC) with eight junior surgical residents, using an interview guide sensitized to entrustment, entrustability, situated learning, legitimate peripheral participation, and CoP. Iterative data collection and analysis used a constant comparative approach to generate a theoretical framework. Interviews were recorded, transcribed, and anonymized. Coding was conducted independently and refined collaboratively. Data collection continued until theoretical sufficiency.

Results: Four themes were constructed: Making Sense of What Entrustment Requires, Performing Entrustability, When Being Prepared Is Not Enough, and Social Networks to Navigate Entrustment. These illustrate entrustment as a social exchange, dependent on residents' preparedness and attendings' willingness to grant opportunities. Learning required interpreting tacit expectations, managing impressions, and navigating social dynamics. Access was influenced by peer networks, gendered norms, and the hidden curriculum.

Discussion: Entrustment is not a momentary judgment but a negotiated process. Residents must learn tacit expectations to be perceived as entrustable. This learning is uneven, influenced by identity and informal networks. To foster equity, the hidden curriculum of entrustability should be made visible for discussion and critique.

Gill Sitarenios (Lead Author), Alexander MacIntosh (Co Author), Rodica Ivan (Co Author), Muhammad Zafar Iqbal (Co Author), Cole Walsh (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted thematic analysis of 10 North American competency frameworks across medicine, pharmacy, business, engineering, and law. A three-module SJT was designed, with scenarios linked to these domains. Piloting involved 350 students from three programs. Trained assessors rated responses using criterion-referenced definitions. Analyses examined internal consistency (α, ω), inter-rater reliability (ICC), factor structure (CFA), and construct validity (correlations with Casper, an admissions SJT).

Results: Thematic analysis identifying 95 competencies revealed four fundamental: intrapersonal, interpersonal, social/ethical responsibility, and critical thinking. Formative SJT reliability was strong (α = .84-.91, ω = .88-.92), with acceptable inter-rater agreement (ICC = .53-.60). CFA supported the four-domain model (CFI = .95, TLI = .94, RMSEA = .09). Formative SJT scores correlated with Casper (r = .25-.38, corrected r = .30-.45).

Discussion: Findings support the feasibility of a formative, open-response SJT for in-program competency development. The framework provides clarity across disciplines for interprofessional education, and psychometric results suggest utility for guiding student growth. Future work will examine sensitivity to change, feedback utility, and application across diverse learner groups.

Methods: Fifteen participants (including residency program directors and senior residents nominated for leadership potential) engaged in the program from February to May 2025. Lauren Digby (Lead Author), Rahim Dhalla (Co Author), Joshya Singh (Co Author), Eva Vieira (Co Author), Andres Jimenez (Co Author), Alice Tsui (Co Author)

Methods: Individuals with diverse IDD needs, including those with autism spectrum disorder and physical disabilities, volunteered to serve as standardized patients in two unique clinical stations. Twelve pre-clerkship students practiced history-taking, physical examination, and caregiver engagement. Kate Lim (Lead Author), Anuradha Mishra (Co Author), Korolos Sawires (Co Author)

Methods: Two independent reviewers analyzed all 70 pre-clerkship CBL cases. Each was coded for ophthalmology-related content, including ocular history and symptoms, physical exam findings, ophthalmic medications, surgical history, and systemic conditions with ocular manifestations. Thematic areas such as anatomy, cranial nerves, disability/accessibility, and age-related conditions were also assessed.

Results: Only 16% of cases included any eye-related history, and 21% mentioned eye exams. Just 1% referenced visual disabilities, ocular medications, or surgical history. "Cranial nerves" was coded 84 times and "anatomy and histology" 82 times across all cases, but other codes were infrequently noted. Several cases involving elderly patients omitted mention of cataracts or macular degeneration. Cases with long-standing diabetes often failed to address diabetic retinopathy or the need for screening.

Discussion: This review reveals a significant deficiency in ophthalmology content within the CBL curriculum, despite many clinical scenarios offering opportunities for integration. The findings highlight the need to embed ophthalmology teaching more deliberately, even in non-ophthalmology-focused sessions. This is the first study to comprehensively evaluate ophthalmology content in a pre-clerkship CBL curriculum. Future studies should expand this analysis to other Canadian institutions to assess national trends and inform curriculum reform.

Zaim Khan (Lead Author), Rebecca Khoury (Co Author), Fatima Arshad (Co Author), Shaoni Chakraborty (Co Author), Kuan-Chin Jean Chen (Co Author), Nigèle Langlois (Co Author), Douglas Archibald (Co Author), Carol Geller (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a national scan of all 17 Canadian medical schools, targeting institutional materials describing the definition, composition, role, and delivery of foundational sciences. Where clarification is needed, we aim to conduct semi-structured interviews with curriculum designers. We aim to conduct a thematic analysis using an inductive-deductive approach to identify recurring themes, conceptual definitions, and variations in how foundational sciences are framed, integrated, or justified within medical curricula.

Results: Preliminary data reveal notable variation across institutions. Some institutions present foundational sciences as traditional, rigid core disciplines like anatomy and microbiology. Others embrace a broader conceptualization, integrating biomedical, social, health systems, and clinical sciences under a more inclusive umbrella. These latter approaches emphasize socio-cultural awareness, critical systems thinking, and longitudinal themes across the human lifecycle.

Discussion: Findings will inform efforts to conceptualize foundational science education across diverse UGME contexts by featuring both shared practices and points of divergence. Meerab Majeed (Lead Author), Zainab Furqan (Co Author), Mirha Zohair (Co Author), Lama Abdallah (Co Author), Abbey Susan (Co Author), Arfeen Malick (Co Author), Hashim Khan (Co Author), Umberin Najeeb (Co Author), Javeed Sukhera (Co Author), Mohamad Matout (Co Author), Juveria Zaheer (Co Author), Raed Hawa (Co Author)

Methods: Guided by constructivist grounded theory, 23 Muslim residents participated in semi-structured virtual interviews. Participants (15 female, 8 male) included Canadian medical graduates (n=13), international medical graduates (n=8), and international fellows (n=2). Interviews explored anti-Muslim discrimination and its impact on well-being and training. Transcripts were analyzed using open and focused coding, constant comparison, and memo writing, supported by NVivo 14.

Results: Analysis demonstrated that discrimination operated at structural, systemic, and interpersonal levels. Residents reported discriminatory events from patients, peers, and supervisors, with consequences for career decision-making and professional identity. Participants described resilience through mentorship, support from Muslim communities, and inclusive environments. Barriers to reporting and difficulty classifying experiences as discrimination underscored the complexity of addressing discrimination within training.

Discussion: These findings illustrate how Muslim residents navigate discrimination while sustaining professional and personal identities. By integrating qualitative insights with prevalence data, this project provides a framework for developing systemic interventions that promote equity, safety, and inclusion in Canadian medical education.

Anuoluwa Popoola (Lead Author), Sador Bereketab (Co Author), Zia Bismilla (Co Author), James Owen (Co Author), Mireille Norris (Co Author)

Methods: Two medical students with lived experience in Black health reviewed University of Toronto clinical skills course guides. Gaps and suggested revisions were documented, after which they were thematically analyzed using a deductive-inductive approach. Results were reviewed by three MD Program faculty members, including the Pre-Clerkship Director, Clinical Skills Director, and Black Health Theme Lead.

Results: Seven themes were identified: Social Determinants of Health; Historical & Systemic Racism; Cultural Communication Variations; Equity in Learning Environment & Patient Roleplay; Diagnostic & Physical Exam Accuracy Across Skin Tones and Bodies; Inclusive History-Taking; and Education on Risk Variation. Gaps were first categorized into the seven themes, then classified by type of issue, including omission, misrepresentation, harmful bias, poor visual representation, or evidence gaps. Themes and categories were organized into an audit tool called the Black Health Audit Tool (B-HAT). Across 54 identified gaps, omissions were most common (n=43), followed by harmful bias (n=8), poor visual representation (n=2), and evidence gaps (n=1). Diagnostic and physical exam accuracy across skin tones and bodies (n=16) and inclusive history-taking (n=17) had the highest number of issues across themes.

Discussion: The B-HAT provides a structured approach for evaluating clinical skills curricula for Black health gaps. Adoption of the B-HAT across medical education programs may help guide curricular improvements, supporting more equitable medical training.

Their work ensures that these pathways are not temporary fixes, but evolving, sustainable structures rooted in justice, care, and community leadership. Darshana Seeburruth (Lead Author), Tahirih Nasseri (Lead Author), Edgar Akuffo-Addo (Co Author), Yvette Miller-Monthrope (Co Author), Marissa Joseph (Co Author)

Methods: This study evaluated the program's impact using pre- and post-program mentee surveys analyzed with paired t-tests, and post-program mentor surveys analyzed descriptively, with open-ended responses undergoing thematic analysis.

Results: A total of 306 mentees from all 17 Canadian medical schools and training years participated. Most (98.4%) recognized mentorship as essential for a successful match, yet 94.4% reported difficulty accessing it, and over 95% lacked personal connections to dermatology mentors. Following CDMP, mentees reported significant improvements in understanding dermatology as a specialty, career development opportunities, components of a competitive application, research processes, and equity, diversity, and inclusion initiatives (all p<0.0001).
