Diverse resident representation was motivational for 68.3% of mentees, and 72.5% felt the program helped them assess their fit for dermatology. Nearly all participants (99.3%) would recommend the program, and 95.8% expressed interest in future iterations. Mentors unanimously found the experience meaningful and manageable.

Discussion: CDMP demonstrates the value of structured mentorship in promoting equitable access to dermatology and provides a scalable model for other specialties with limited exposure in medical training.

Kirsten Faisal (Lead Author), Michael Quon (Co Author), Cheryl Holmes (Co Author), Shireen Faisal (Co Author), Rachel Giddings (Co Author)

Methods: A CanLII search (2010-present) was conducted using "resident," "doctor OR physician," and "duty to accommodate OR undue hardship." Of 794 cases, eight involved resident physicians or their representatives alleging disability discrimination against universities or hospitals. Legislative review included disability and employment statutes, physician licensure laws, and provincial medical association bylaws to review disability workplace rights and physician specific legislation.

Results: Of the eight relevant cases, four ruled in favour of residents, two only included preliminary hearings, and two favoured institutions. Undue hardship was established in only one case, where the resident required continuous supervision. All provinces and territories have human rights legislation affirming the duty to accommodate unless disability conflicts with an essential occupational requirement. No legislation explicitly restricts residents with disabilities beyond provisions allowing licence limitation or revocation if a condition poses patient safety risk.

Discussion: Resident physicians, like all Canadian workers, are legally entitled to reasonable accommodations. The courts and laws are clear that these rights are legally protected and upheld, underscoring the need for universities to develop transparent, standardized accommodation processes supported by knowledgeable staff. Implementation of national best-practice recommendations and updating postgraduate accreditation standards could enhance consistency, safeguard rights, and reduce litigation.

Auva Zarandi (Lead Author), Aryana Zarandi (Co Author), Quang Ngo (Co Author), Spencer van Mil (Co Author), Jason M. Harley (Co Author), Elif Bilgic (Co Author)

Methods: A partnership was formed through the SSHRC Partnership Engage Grant between McMaster University and MedVR Education, a provider of IVR medical simulations. Pediatric faculty and fellows were invited to explore IVR scenarios involving both technical and non-technical skills. Their feedback was analyzed qualitatively.

Results: Pilot findings highlight four key considerations: 1. Match scenarios to learner level and target skills. 2. Account for variation in institutional protocols when interpreting automated feedback. 3. Provide comprehensive orientation to IVR to reduce cognitive load. 4. Clearly communicate whether the simulation is intended for learning or assessment.

Discussion: While IVR offers educational benefits and scalable solutions, implementation must be aligned with learner needs and institutional goals. Clear expectations, thoughtful scenario selection, and attention to user experience are essential to optimizing its use in medical education.

Arielle Levy (Lead Author), Natasha Collia (Co Author)

Methods: A 22-item VL assessment tool was adapted from Johnston (2019) and refined through three expert review rounds. Items were scored 0-2 (not done, partially done/out of sequence, correctly done), with cumulative scores linked to training levels (novice to expert) and supplemented by a global rating scale (GRS). Ten VL intubation videos (two per level) were created. Six pediatric EM raters from three centers completed a 3-hour online training, including tool review, scoring exercises, and consensus discussions, followed by independent scoring of the videos. Inter-rater reliability (IRR) was assessed with intra-class correlation coefficients (ICC). Discriminant validity was tested with mixed-effect ANOVA, and Spearman's correlation assessed associations between composite scores and GRS.

Results: ICC for absolute agreement was 0.925 (0.835-0.977) for Preparation, 0.889 (0.754-0.966) for Intubation, and 0.951 (0.887-0.986) for composite scores. Spearman's correlation between composite and GRS scores was 0.84 (p < 0.001). Mixed-effect ANOVA showed significant differences in composite scores across training levels (F(4,5) = 61.36, p < .001), confirming the tool's ability to distinguish levels of expertise.

Discussion: We developed a VL assessment tool with strong inter-rater reliability and discriminant validity. This tool provides robust evidence to support its use in simulation-based evaluation of pediatric VL intubation competency.

Micheline Boucher (Lead Author), Shane Aubé (Co Author), Jacinthe Beauchamp (Co Author)

Methods: Building on an established simulation curriculum, we introduced new scenarios informed by faculty input, program leadership, and accreditation feedback. Five priority areas were identified: trauma care, home care, mental health, intercultural competencies, and difficult conversations. Content experts-including emergency physicians, nurses, psychiatrists, and psychologists-played a key role in the design and facilitation of these sessions, reflecting the collaborative nature of family practice. Learner feedback and after-action reviews guide iterative improvements.

Results: Residents engage in approximately 115 hours of simulation, distributed evenly across two years. Over three years of implementation, feedback has been consistently positive. Residents find the scenarios relevant and well-aligned with their learning needs, and have requested increased frequency. Improvements identified include enhanced preparatory materials and supplementary clinical learning activities.

Discussion: This expanded simulation curriculum represents a shift toward holistic, contextually grounded training in family medicine. By integrating diverse expertise and addressing complex care scenarios, it better equips residents to navigate the realities of contemporary primary care. The model may inform similar innovations in other family medicine residency programs.

Certina Ho (Lead Author), Justin Lee (Co Author), Anna Nguyen (Co Author), Sanjeev Sockalingam (Co Author), Lu Gao (Co Author)

Methods: We obtained medical students' post-DPS evaluation of the workshop and instructors via a questionnaire from 2021 to 2023. Descriptive/inferential statistics and thematic analysis were applied to quantitative and qualitative data, respectively.

Results: We received 74 responses. The DPS seminar was well received by medical students, with an overall average rating of 4.42 out of 5 on evaluation items related to the workshop quality. Virtual delivery was generally well received (4.08), though comments and data suggested students' preference for in-person DPS (4.63). Students' perceived strengths of the DPS seminar included a generally safe learning environment, a highly interactive and engaging experience, and valuable peer and tutor discussions that stimulated deep learning. Some students reported challenges with engagement due to psychological vulnerability in simulation and lack of clarity of learning objectives and the post-simulation debrief.

Discussion: The DPS is a feasible and innovative simulation teaching technique for pre-clerkship medical students according to this multi-year evaluation. There is a noted preference for in-person over virtual delivery. However, it is a well-received learning experience in both formats that can be adapted for virtual delivery based on curricular needs.

Alice Kim (Lead Author), Sylvain Boet (Co Author), Meghan McConnell (Co Author), Colleen McFaul (Co Author), Andréanne Ménard (Co Author), Renate Kahlke (Co Author)

Methods: Using a qualitative, narrative inquiry approach, staff anesthesiologists shared stories of unanticipated difficult airways in interviews. Analysis explored emotions before, during, and after the event.

Results: Nine anesthesiologists from urban teaching hospitals participated. Narratives described various difficult airways and five phases of emotions: (1) Pre-airway confidence and calm; (2) First sign of difficulty, with surprise and concern; (3) Active airway management, evoking stress, fear, and guilt; (4) Post-success relief and self-criticism; (5) Image recovery, restoring confidence. No formal debriefing occurred. Emotions were influenced by clinical experience and simulation training.

Discussion: Unanticipated difficulty airways evoke emotions beyond the immediate crisis. Integrating emotion management into training can help normalize emotions and strengthen coping. These findings are especially relevant in high-acuity settings, supporting clinician wellness and resilience.

Parsa Razeghi (Lead Author), Lilian Zhai (Co Author), Kristina Lisk (Co Author)

Methods: Thirty-three second-year medical students volunteered and eighteen were randomly selected to participate in the one-day session. Working in pairs under faculty guidance, students applied surgical techniques, including internervous plane dissection and suturing, to the deltopectoral region and cubital fossa. Their work resulted in the creation of the nine teaching specimens for future use. A post-session survey assessed student perceptions via Likert-scales and open-ended questions.

Results: Seventeen students completed the post-session survey. Most had prior interest in surgery; 94% participated to gain exposure to surgical education, and 82% reported increased interest in musculoskeletal surgery following SATRA. All students reported improved understanding of upper limb anatomy and greater confidence to guide peers in anatomy. Interest in recommending and participating in future SATRA sessions was unanimous. Open-ended responses highlighted the value of faculty guidance and the surgical approach.

Discussion: SATRA offers an innovative and scalable model to increase exposure to surgery, teach anatomy, and optimize curricular time through the creation of reusable teaching specimens. Future research should explore SATRA's impact on long-term career interests and specific learning outcomes.

Bradley McCosham (Lead Author), Kamal Lakhrif (Co Author)

Methods: A cross-sectional study was conducted using 2,063 clinical rotation evaluations completed by 170 third-year medical students across nine core clerkship rotations. Data were collected through standardized electronic surveys and analyzed using stepwise multiple regression. Reliability and validity of constructs were confirmed through Cronbach's alpha, KMO, Bartlett's test, and principal component analysis.

Results: Overall rotation satisfaction was high (mean = 4.1/5). The regression model explained 69.1% of the variance in satisfaction. Supervision and support (β = 0.300) and learning experience (β = 0.307) emerged as the strongest predictors, accounting for most explained variance. Rotation organization (β = 0.148) and infrastructure (β = 0.130) were also significant but contributed less.

Discussion: Findings highlight the centrality of faculty guidance and structured learning experiences in fostering student satisfaction, underscoring the importance of mentorship and active engagement. Infrastructure and scheduling, while relevant, played a secondary role, suggesting students can adapt to resource limitations when strong supervision and pedagogical support are present. These results offer actionable insights for curriculum design and faculty development to optimize clinical learning environments and better prepare students as future health promoters.

Alireza Jalali (Lead Author), Aly Julien (Co Author), Meghan Stevens (Lead Author), Solomon Fotsing (Co Author), Sarah Mann (Co Author), Mathilde Tardif (Co Author), Nassima Marouf (Co Author), Armita Dabirzadeh (Co Author)

Methods: Suivant une revue de littérature et un processus de sélection rigoureux, HubSpot, Zoho-CRM (plateformes spécialisées) et ChatGPT (plateforme fondamentale) ont été retenus. Pour chaque outil, les utilisateurs se sont familiarisés à l'aide des données fictives et ont complété un questionnaire style Likert évaluant l'intuitivité, le soutien technique, la prédiction, la personnalisation, l'analyse, l'automatisation, le réseautage, le coût-efficacité et la satisfaction globale. Les résultats ont été analysés avec JASP version 0.95.1.0

Results: Ces résultats exploratoires rapportent une excellente fiabilité du questionnaire utilisé notamment pour évaluer HubSpot (α=0,94) et Zoho-CRM (α=0,91) et une fiabilité limitée pour ChatGPT (α=0,51). L'efficacité globale, mesurée par le score moyen des 11 items, est la plus élevée pour HubSpot (M=6,9/10), suivie de Zoho-CRM (M=6,6/10) et ChatGPT (M=4,6/10). L'utilité des outils dans chaque critère est aussi représentée par un score moyen. HubSpot s'est distingué dans le plus d'items, le réseautage (M=8,8/10) étant le plus élevé. Zoho-CRM a obtenu son meilleur résultat en segmentation analytique (M=7,6/10). Avec une moyenne de 8,6/10, ChatGPT se distingue comme le plus intuitif selon les évaluations.

Discussion: HubSpot et Zoho-CRM offrent une fonctionnalité plus complète, HubSpot étant le plus performant. ChatGPT, bien qu'intuitif, demeure limité. Une intégration réfléchie et des considérations éthiques sont nécessaires pour assurer une mise en œuvre durable.

Lindsay Herzog (Lead Author), Nathan Cupido (Co Author), Erin Bearss (Co Author), Sabrina Kolker (Co Author), Sahana Kukan (Co Author), Anna MacLeod (Co Author), Natalie Morson (Co Author), Melissa Nutik (Co Author), David Tannenbaum (Co Author), Sakina Walji (Co Author), Milena Forte (Co Author)

Methods: Participants included family medicine residents and supervising family medicine teachers. As part of our ongoing study, we have conducted ten participant-led walking interviews of the teaching unit, allowing participants to reflect in situ on the intricate relationships between materials, practices, and experiences during clinical supervision. Drawing on constructivist approaches and sensitized by a sociomaterial lens, we qualitatively analyzed interview audio-recordings, field notes, and photographs through open, inductive, thematic coding.

Results: Our analysis identified five interconnected themes, revealing how the supervision room influences teaching and learning. (1) It enables relational, dialogical, and peer-to-peer pedagogical practices. (2) It fosters professional identity formation, shaping norms, values, and ways of being. (3) It cultivates a climate of psychological safety and trust. These roles are supported by (4) conditions including a separate, intentional, and collective space, and (5) material elements such as furniture, technology, and room layout.

Discussion: This study illustrates how the supervision room is not a passive backdrop, but an active participant in clinical education. These findings have broad implications and encourage a reimagining of learning environments, underscoring how physical space can be leveraged as a powerful pedagogical tool in medical education.

Adam Gavarkovs (Lead Author), Madison Estrella (Co Author), Vivian Lam (Co Author), Vernon Curran (Co Author)

Methods: Retention data for 119 IMG family physicians assigned to a rural community in British Columbia from 2015-2020 was provided by the BC Ministry of Health. Registration data for 206 CPD programs delivered in BC during the same period was provided by the University of British Columbia's Division of Continuing Professional Development. Cox proportional hazards regression was used to model the relationship between participation in three different CPD formats - rounds, workshops, and coaching - and the risk of leaving rural practice. Semi-structured interviews were conducted with a subsample of IMGs to explore their CPD experiences. Interview data was analyzed using template analysis.

Results: Participation in CPD was not a statistically significant predictor of retention, regardless of format. Interviews revealed that IMGs valued hands-on, relational CPD and informal networking opportunities to feel more confident and connected in their work and communities.

Discussion: These findings indicate that CPD may not be a deciding factor in whether IMGs stay or leave rural practice, but it is nonetheless important for helping them integrate and thrive in their rural community.

Adam Gavarkovs (Lead Author), Sharon Ho (Co Author), Kate Campbell (Co Author), Bill McCauley (Co Author), Ryan Brydges (Co Author), Brenna Lynn (Co Author)

Methods: Staff at two university CPD offices identified health professionals who had not started a free e-learning course 2-4 weeks after registering and invited them to participate in a semi-structured interview. Interviews were conducted with 12 health professionals, exploring their initial motivations for registering and reasons for not starting. Data was analyzed using template analysis.

Results: Four themes emerged: (1) Participants anticipated that courses would have a positive impact on their practice but were not considered essential for providing good care; (2) The flexibility of e-learning was appreciated by participants but also meant that courses were frequently pushed to the bottom of the priority list; (3) The perceived value of courses fluctuates over time; and (4) External pressures drive course completion more than intrinsic motivation.

Discussion: This study sheds light on CPD as something that health professionals dynamically (de)prioritize alongside their other professional and personal demands. This view suggests new ways of supporting health professionals' CPD efforts, while also contributing to the broader literature on self-regulation in health professions education, which has historically focused on single goal regulation.

Stephanie Milone (Lead Author), Elizabeth Wooster (Co Author), Katherine Gittins (Co Author), Brent Thoma (Co Author)

Methods: A virtual workshop was developed wherein faculty were asked to address detailed mistreatment scenarios in alignment with the TMU SoM's learner mistreatment policy. Participants included clinical faculty, academic leaders, and staff. Pre- and post surveys were administered and sessions were recorded and transcribed. Descriptive statistics summarized participant confidence and preparedness to address a learner mistreatment event. Narrative feedback underwent thematic analysis to identify helpful and confusing aspects of the policy.

Results: Fifteen participants completed the pre-survey and nine completed the post-survey. Pre-workshop, 7/9 participants felt they understood the policy but only 5/9 felt confident applying it independently. Post-workshop, 9/9 participants felt they understood the policy and 7/9 expressed readiness to apply it independently. Case-based scenarios and the policy flowchart were consistently cited as helpful in clarifying roles, processes, and reporting pathways.

Discussion: Preliminary evaluation of the results indicate that a simulation-based workshop improved participants' confidence and readiness while surfacing areas requiring refinement. Findings support the use of simulation-based faculty development to accelerate institutional readiness, strengthen clarity, and reinforce respectful learning environments.

Tanya MacLeod (Lead Author), Lara Hazelton (Co Author), Owen Connolly (Co Author), Kim Good (Co Author), Mandy Esliger (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted key informant interviews with experienced continuing professional development providers and program attendees (n = 8). Interview transcripts were analyzed thematically using the Theoretical Domains Framework (TDF), which outlines 14 domains representing determinants of behaviour. Coding was mapped to these domains, and subthemes were further refined through iterative team discussions.

Results: Bias was understood as a subjective, often unconscious influence on individual thinking and behaviour. Four TDF domains were most relevant: knowledge/skills (bias literacy, critical appraisal), intentions (self-monitoring, intent to be a critical consumer), environmental context (feedback opportunities, psychological safety, conflicts of interest), and emotion. Some participants described strong affective responses-especially anger-as important motivators for reporting bias.

Discussion: Most factors influencing the reporting of bias are modifiable. Enhancing bias literacy, creating safe learning environments, and addressing conflicts of interest may strengthen reporting practices. Further research should explore the role of emotion in learning and its impact on learner engagement.

Roy Khalife (Lead Author), Justin Phung (Co Author), Lindsay Cowley (Co Author), Lindsey Sikora (Co Author), Susan Humphrey-Murto (Co Author), Kori LaDonna (Co Author), Claire Touchie (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a systematic review and qualitative evidence synthesis of studies exploring stakeholder experiences and perceptions of EPA implementation in postgraduate medical education. Six databases were searched from inception to August 2024 and updated in April 2025. Eligible studies included qualitative and mixed-methods designs that examined learners, faculty, administrators, or other stakeholders involved in EPA use. Data were extracted in duplicate, appraised, and analyzed using Thomas and Harden's thematic synthesis approach.

Results: From 7,027 records, 23 studies involving 443 participants were included. Studies spanned Canada, the USA, Europe, and New Zealand across diverse specialties. Thematic synthesis yielded five interrelated themes: (1) Design tensions-EPAs clarified expectations but sometimes constrained authentic learning; (2) Growth versus performance-feedback opportunities increased, yet summative use fostered assessment fatigue and gaming behaviors; (3) Faculty preparedness and engagement-assessors were critical of EPA credibility but often ill-prepared for dual coaching-judging roles; (4) Psychological climate-learners experienced heightened anxiety and performance pressures that undermined safe-to-fail learning; and (5) Institutional culture-sustainable uptake required leadership, administrative support, and integration into broader curricular structures.

Discussion: Stakeholders valued EPAs for clarifying competencies and structuring feedback, but their potential was constrained by rigid design, insufficient faculty preparation, and unsupportive learning climates. Effective EPA implementation required adaptable frameworks, investment in faculty development, attention to psychological safety, and robust institutional scaffolds to ensure they functioned as catalysts for growth rather than administrative burdens.

Marla Davidson (Lead Author), Lynsey Martin (Co Author), Tanya Robertson-Frey (Co Author), Loni Desanghere (Co Author), Anurag Saxena (Co Author)

Methods: An online survey was distributed to CC members at the University of Saskatchewan. Questions addressed time commitments, platforms, challenges, and suggested practices.

Results: Fifty-eight CC members from 19 Royal College specialty programs responded. Most were women (57%) and had four or more years of CC experience (40%). Programs varied in size, with 28% overseeing fewer than 10 residents and 22% more than 30. Most CCs reviewed all residents at each meeting (62%), with members completing both primary and secondary reviews. Meeting preparation typically required 1-3 hours for secondary and 4-6 for primary reviews, and 85% found the workload manageable. Elentra was the most common platform, with 70+% reporting satisfaction across various aspects of the platform. Challenges facing CC committees included identifying residents in difficulty, member workload, and scheduling. Suggested practices included frequent reviews, resident reflection forms, and standardized tracking tools.

Discussion: Findings suggest that while CC processes are generally functional and manageable, workload and consistency remain concerns. The identification of residents in difficulty highlights the need for structured approaches to early detection and support. An increase in faculty support, shared practices, and standardized approaches could strengthen CC effectiveness and fairness.

Marco Zaccagnini (Lead Author) Andrew West (Co Author) Brandon D'Souza (Co Author) Peter Farrell (Co Author) Sébastient Tessier (Co Author) Ian D. Graham (Co Author)

Methods: Using Arksey and O'Malley's six-stage framework and PRISMA-ScR guidelines, we systematically searched seven databases (inception-December 2024) and conducted a structured grey literature review. We examined the instructional design features, pedagogical underpinnings, assessment strategies, and reported impacts of micro-credentials.

Results: We included 19 peer-reviewed papers and 35 websites describing health-related micro-credentials. Most studies were published in 2024 (42.1%), originated from the United States (42.1%), and nearly half (47.4%) provided only descriptive accounts. A wide range of instructional design features were identified, though pedagogical theories were rarely stated. Assessment strategies predominantly emphasized summative approaches (e.g., multiple-choice knowledge checks), with limited focus on higher-level competency assessment. Reported outcomes were primarily improvements in knowledge, confidence, or engagement, with no clear evidence of the distinct value of micro-credentials as a teaching modality.

Discussion: Current literature offers limited evaluation of micro-credentials and often lacks theory-informed design. We infer a pedagogical foundation aligned with constructivist, context-sensitive, and stage-based principles, which may inform the development of future micro-credential programs.

Mark Adkins (Lead Author), Samantha Inwood (Co Author), David Rojas (Co Author)

Methods: Separate confirmatory factor analyses (CFA) were conducted for 10 core clerkship courses using 3 years of LACT data (2022-23 to 2024-25). Each course's CFA model was tested for measurement invariance to assess LACT use across cohorts for indications of shared interpretations of the tool. A diagonally weighted least squares estimator was used with a mean- and variance-adjusted test statistic.

Results: Seven of 10 courses met the highest level of measurement invariance. Each model showed high internal consistency; with polychoric correlations between the overall rating and factor scores generated from the CFA models ranging from r = 0.91 to r = 0.94. Standardized factor loadings suggest comparable variable information contributing to factor scores. Scaled model fit indices demonstrate extremely good fit between models and their respective observed data (RMSEAs ranging from 0.016 to 0.028).

Discussion: These findings suggest different learner cohorts use and conceptualize the LACT similarly, perceiving the four teaching areas as related but still distinct. This is emphasized by the consistently highly skewed response thresholds at the item-level. Future analyses will test whether measurement invariance holds across courses.

Sherylan Young (Lead Author), Kulamakan Kulasegaram (Co Author), Melissa Nutik (Co Author), Maya Amar (Co Author)

Methods: We used Qualtrics to survey all clinical clerks (~500) during their core FM clerkship rotation during the 2023-2024 and 2024-2025 academic years to gather information about factors of their specific clinical rotations and their experiences. We then used performance data from their mastery evaluation exercises (ME) and clinical evaluation scores for our analysis to determine which factors, if any, made a difference on student performance.

Results: Analysis of data from 2023-2024 revealed that although students' experiences were positive on the rotation, these experience scores did not correlate with student performance. We found a limited number of factors weakly correlated with performance on the ME, including clinical and non-clinical health professional educator experiences, exposure to residents and long commutes. Notably, things like timing of clerkship rotation, number of preceptors and exposure to preceptors that did focused practice did not make a difference in performance.

Discussion: Findings from the completed 2-year study will be presented and we hope that the results will serve as a template for new undergrad sites and be used to inform quality improvement for both undergrad and post-grad education.

Nina Mazze (Lead Author), Karen Forward (Co Author), Quang Ngo (Co Author), Sabrina Lue Tam (Co Author)

Methods: To design, implement and evaluate the curriculum, Kern's six step approach was used. Royal College objectives of training were reviewed and program directors were surveyed to prioritize curriculum learning objectives. Asynchronous modules cover airway, shock, trauma, and NRP; in-person sessions address advanced airway, casting, IO insertion, suturing, anaphylaxis and seizures. In-person sessions have been implemented, with next steps in curriculum implementation to include the asynchronous modules. Descriptive statistics of post-curriculum surveys evaluate resident comfort in procedural skills and acute care management pre and post-curriculum.

Early exposure builds awareness, while ongoing practice and feedback builds competence.

Nasri Balit (Lead Author), Sofia Lizza (Lead Author), Amanda Essebag (Co Author), Tracy Liu (Co Author), Peter Malik (Co Author), Teodora Marginean (Co Author), Timothy Wideman, PhD (Co Author), Lester Liao, MD (Co Author)

Methods: 13 responses were collected pre-program to understand how the standard medical curriculum addresses physician humanity. 10 participants submitted responses to a post-program questionnaire on whether DaP fostered their humanity as physicians. Umberin Najeeb (Lead Author) Martina Trinkaus (Co Author) Alexandra Grudzinski (Co Author)

Methods: This study involved five faculty members trained in the Master Teacher Program (DoM's two-year instructional development certificate course) as Peer-Consultants (PCs) and ten faculty members with minimal/no formal teaching training as CTs. Each PC was paired with two CTs, forming 10-pairs. The process included a pre-consultation phone call, PCs observation of CT-led small-group teaching seminars, PCs offering formative feedback on the provided worksheet, and a post-observation conversation. All participants completed a survey three-months later.

Results: All 10-pairs completed their teaching observations. On a 1-5 scale, with 1-"strongly disagree" and 5 -"strongly agree," CTs were evaluated by PCs as being most effective at treating learners with respect (mean: 5; standard deviation [SD]: 0), demonstrating appropriate knowledge of the topic (mean: 5; SD: 0) and demonstrating enthusiasm (mean: 4.90; SD: 0.32). CTs need improvement with stating teaching objectives at the beginning of the session (mean: 4.30; SD: 0.95), conducting the session at an effective pace (mean: 4.56; STDV: 0.53), and using summaries, repetition and/or "take home" points (mean: 4.67; SD: 0.71). In the follow-up survey, participants found the peer consultation process helpful (mean: 4.14; SD: 0.69), valued its formative approach (mean: 4.71; SD: 0.49), and expressed a need for a permanent program (mean: 4.29; SD: 0.49).

Discussion: Peer consultations with formative feedback are a helpful resource to improve CTs teaching abilities.

Methods: Pre-clerkship and clerkship curriculum documents were reviewed to identify objectives, learning events, and assessments related to Indigenous health. Laeora Berkson (Lead Author), Alexandra Teggin (Co Author), Theresa Beesley (Co Author)

Methods: The Annual Course Review Checklist (ACRC) includes items addressing learning objectives, course implementation, student performance and satisfaction. Directors respond with 'yes' or 'no,' providing comments for negative responses. Data is compiled into spreadsheets, enabling cross-course comparisons and detection of systemic issues.

Results: Since 2021, the ACRC has been completed annually by over 85% of directors. Heshini Dalpathadu (Lead Author), Jessica Ferguson (Co Author), Kerry Yang (Co Author), Rayyan Zuberi (Co Author), Rabiya Jalil (Co Author)

Methods: We administered campus-wide surveys to students, faculty, and staff at baseline (n=115), 1-month after the launch of disposable products (n=78), and 12-months after the launch of disposable products / 2-months after the launch of reusable products (n=46). Usage was tracked via weekly logs for disposable products across the 20 washrooms at the CSM and for reusable products stocked at SAWH.

Results: At baseline, 90% of survey respondents agreed that free menstrual care products would improve inclusivity, but availability was poor. At 1 month, availability satisfaction rose to 65% (vs. 13% at baseline), 47% of respondents had used free disposable products, and 37% reported reduced financial burden. Reusable menstrual products were introduced in April 2025 with 37 menstrual cups and 73 period underwear products distributed to date. Among survey respondents, 17% had used reusable menstrual products and 61% intended to use them. Finally, 87% of respondents agreed that free reusable products positively impacted environmental sustainability and accessibility at the CSM.

Discussion: Free menstrual products were associated with rapid, sustained improvements in accessibility and inclusion, with a meaningful reduction in financial burden for many users. Expansion of sustainable menstrual care options appears to be a promising avenue for universities to address period poverty.

Alessandra Sarmiento (Lead Author), Gisselle Gallego (Co Author), Claudia Ng (Co Author), Dinesh Palipana (Co Author)

Methods: An audit, review and content analysis of publicly available admission policies and admission guidelines of all specialists' medical colleges in Australia was conducted between June and December 2024. The review followed the READ approach, a systematic procedure for collecting, extracting and conducting document analysis.

Results: Seventeen colleges were eligible. Eight colleges had an entry application process publicly available. One college had a disability policy. Five colleges had a disclosure of disability in the entry application process. Six colleges had disability mentioned in other policies. Thirteen colleges mentioned special considerations that included disability in the criteria. No college had a training program accommodation policy specific to disability only. Three colleges had training program supports in their policy. Two colleges demonstrated inclusive language in their policies. No college mentioned the number of trainees with a disability in their annual report. No college had wellbeing resources specific to disability.

Discussion: The majority of specialty colleges did not have inclusive selection processes and some used ableist language. Australian medical colleges should consider more accessible and inclusive policies and processes to support the inclusion of people with disability.

Areeba Chaudhry (Lead Author), Naganathan Gayathri (Lead Author), Valentina Shamoun (Co Author), Sheharza Mahmood (Co Author), Elias Djemai (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a meta-narrative review (Scopus, MEDLINE, July 2025) to synthesize global evidence on culturally tailored breast cancer education tools. Extraction focused on content, language/cultural adaptations, delivery formats, and co-design strategies. Studies were analyzed to identify best practices and gaps relevant to tool development for use in Canadian cancer care and medical education.

Results: Education tools that incorporate multilingual resources, culturally informed adaptations such as attention to perceptions of cancer and circle-of-care dynamics, community partnerships, and co-design strategies demonstrably improve comprehension, adherence, and psychosocial support. Yet few interventions span the full cancer care continuum or are intentionally designed as dual-use resources that support medical trainees in delivering culturally competent communication and enable patients to engage in self-directed learning.

Discussion: Findings inform co-development of a breast cancer education tool for East Toronto and Scarborough that equips trainees with culturally competent communication strategies and empowers patients to engage in self-directed learning. By embedding cultural competence into patient education and medical training, this work advances equity-informed curricula and supports medical trainees in delivering holistic cancer care.

Student survey responses addressed inclusive learning environments, with all seven students agreeing or strongly agreeing with most statements.

Discussion: This project suggests that EDIJ-focused activities for CIs can enhance self-reflection, learning, and inclusive education environments. Future research should allow greater flexibility in activity completion and improve translatability by recruiting larger, more diverse samples across health disciplines and practice settings.

Tuana Kant (Lead Author), Darshana Seeburruth (Lead Author), Ike Okafor (Co Author), Lisa Robinson (Co Author), Lisa Richardson (Co Author) Dalal Mahir (Co Author)

Methods: Between July-November 2024, UC participants in COSMOS program completed pre- and post-program surveys assessing confidence and competence in writing application materials, understanding of CanMEDS roles, and application-related anxiety, using 5-point Likert scales. Quantitative data were analyzed using unpaired t-tests and ANOVA. Qualitative responses were analyzed thematically.

Results: Of the 683 COSMOS participants, 342 (50%) completed the surveys. Confidence in writing the ABS and perceived competence increased significantly (p<0.0001) from 2.84 to 3.83, and from 2.94 to 3.86, respectively. Similarly, essay-writing confidence and perceived competence improved from 2.78 to 3.82, and from 2.88 to 3.88 (p<0.0001), respectively. Participants reported a better understanding of CanMEDS roles (p<0.0001) and reduced application-related anxiety (p<0.0001). 98% of participants recommended the program.

Discussion: COSMOS program significantly improved UC applicants' confidence, writing skills, and understanding of CanMEDS roles, while reducing subjective application-related anxiety. These findings underscore the value of accessible, mentorship-driven interventions in empowering UC applicants throughout the medical school admissions process and present a model for equity-focused educational initiatives.

Cathy Kline (Lead Author), Angela Towle (Co Author), Matthijs Bosveld (Co Author), Darren Lauscher (Co Author)

Methods: A Delphi study with international experts was conducted to develop recommendations for PPI in IPE and priorities for action. An initial set of 46 statements was developed from a literature review, key informant interviews and focus groups with stakeholders. Two rounds of online voting and a virtual meeting were held. Consensus was defined in advance with a cutoff of 70% of participants scoring at least seven on a nine-point Likert scale.

Results: 71 experts including educators (31%), students (11%), patient partners (48%), and others with relevant experience (10%) participated. All statements reached consensus. Some statements were enhanced but no new statements were added. Qualitative analysis of comments led us to combine statements that were similar and reduce the list to 33 statements.

Discussion: ICAM participants will get a preview of the recommendations and online resources created to support their implementation. By integrating PPI within IPE, PULPIT aims to better connect IPE to real world patient experiences that better prepares learners to collaborate with patients and other professionals in practice. PULPIT will improve educational outcomes and foster collaboration and shared decision making where patients are part of the healthcare team.

Aleisha Fernandes (Lead Author), Aleisha Fernandes (Lead Author), Grace Fox (Co Author), Manoj Lalu (Co Author), Dawn Richards (Co Author), Laurie Proulx (Co Author), Anna Chudyk (Co Author), Dean Fergusson (Co Author), Stuart Nicholls (Co Author)

Methods: A modified e-delphi survey process was conducted with three rounds. Round 1 solicited open text responses regarding challenges, while rounds 2 and 3 required respondents to rate challenges on a 9-point likert scale regarding the importance and ease with which it could be addressed. Surveys were distributed via social media and snowball sampling through existing networks. Qualitative data was analysed thematically, and quantitative data descriptively (median, interquartile range). Challenges scoring 7-9 on importance and 1-3 on ease were identified as priority topics.

Results: 176 individuals completed round 1, 87 round 2 and 94 round 3 of the survey. Round 1 responses generated seventy-four challenges. Rounds 2 and 3 generated 11 priority topics including 6 concerning conversations around compensation.

Discussion: Communication challenges around remuneration highlight a need for respectful and transparent discussions to ensure fair compensation of patient partners. Due to operational need of many challenges, there is also a need for practical tools to facilitate discussion and decision-making around compensation.

Laura Williams (Co Author), David Wiljer (Co Author), Reyhaneh Yousefi (Lead Author), Kerseri Scane (Co Author), Anushka Jaffer (Co Author), Rebecca Charow (Co Author), Akshay Mohan (Co Author), Sumaya Thattakkattu Abdulrahman (Co Author)

Methods: Co-design with patient partners, a Canada-wide bilingual survey and semi-structured interviews were done. Participants included individuals with lived experience of interacting with healthcare systems as patients or caregivers. Descriptive statistics of survey responses were conducted. Short-answer survey responses and interviews were inductively analyzed.

Results: A total of 593 patients responded to the survey, where 47.55% identified as cisgender female, 73.85% were from Ontario, and 65.60% identified as Caucasian. Around half of all participants strongly believed that they should understand how AI might be used in their care (n=396,67%) and have a say when AI is used to manage their health (n=270, 46%) and to make clinical decisions (n=366, 61%). From the 13 interviews, four key AI readiness factors emerged: trust through accountability and transparency, human oversight into workflows to ensure equity and representation, accessible education for informed consent, and greater agency over care and data.

Discussion: These insights advance our understanding of patient perspectives on AI, where trust of AI-enabled care is contingent on organizational commitment to upholding equity, compassion, and transparency in clinical practice. The next step is to co-design a patient charter with our partners, ensuring AI implementation and future medical education development reflects lived priorities and perspectives.

Annie Descôteaux (Lead Author), Claudio Del Grande (Co Author), Marie-Pierre Codsi (Co Author), Isabelle Croteau (Co Author), Lucie Alègre (Co Author), Tiffany Clovin (Co Author), Benoît-Pierre Stock (Co Author), Mathieu Jackson (Co Author), Vincent Dumez (Co Author), Collectif Formation PARTENAIRES (Co Author)

Methods: The programme engages over 5,000 students annually across 13 disciplines and approximately 125 patient partners. A qualitative review of programme development traced changes in patient participation in the design, delivery, evaluation, and governance of the curriculum, drawing on patient partner perspectives and institutional experience.

Results: Three areas of transformation were identified: 1. Student engagement - moving from one-way testimonies to authentic, reflective exchanges bridging the patient-student divide. 2. Interdisciplinary integration - shifting from discipline-specific patient assignments to flexible, universal contributions that enrich collaborative learning. 3. Shared facilitation and leadership - expanding from limited workshop interventions to full co-construction of content and co-chairing of the programme alongside faculty.

Discussion: The PARTENAIRES experience demonstrates that sustained institutional commitment and long-term trust enable patient partners to become co-creators of interprofessional education. This evolution highlights the cultural and structural shifts required to embed authentic partnership, offering lessons for institutions worldwide seeking to align education with patient-centered care and social accountability.

Annie Descôteraux (Lead Author)

Methods: A qualitative design based on institutional ethnography was employed. Eighteen semi-structured interviews were conducted with 7 physicians, 7 patient partners, and 4 medical students (60-90 minutes each, recorded and transcribed). Data were coded and analyzed thematically using NVivo software. The analytical framework combined clinical ethics, the Montreal Model of patient partnership and feminist approaches.

Results: Analysis revealed four major themes encompassing sixteen distinct ethical issues: (1) Structural conditions, including governance and infrastructure as well as the ecology of actors; (2) Relational dynamics, focusing on co-facilitation, alliance, and tensions; (3) Pedagogical issues, such as curriculum design, timing, and student engagement; (4) Representation and identity in tension, including legitimacy, diversity, and essentialization of patient roles;

Discussion: Ethical issues in patient partnership are simultaneously structural, relational, pedagogical, and identity-based. They shape the sustainability of engagement and influence how equity and recognition are enacted in educational contexts. Addressing these issues can guide institutions in designing stable governance structures, fostering inclusive recruitment, and supporting the moral labor of all actors. These findings contribute to strengthening the ethical foundations of patient partnership in medical education.

Maham Rehman (Lead Author), Paula Cameron (Co Author), Lorenzo Madrazo (Co Author), Tina Martiminakis (Co Author), Zahra Merali (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a meta-ethnography, a qualitative synthesis approach designed to generate new conceptual insights. Analysis involved coding first-order (participant quotes) and second-order (author interpretations) constructs. Third-order constructs were iteratively developed through reciprocal/refutational translations to generate cross-cutting insights into how COVID-19 reshaped PIF. Two electronic databases were searched using key-terms yielding 80 articles published from December 2019-January 2025.

Results: Nine third-order concepts were generated across the 11 included articles. Identity uncertainty: exclusion from frontline care or placement in atypical roles challenged learners' professional identity; alternative engagement: some learners found new COVID-specific avenues for fostering PIF; questioning professional narratives: PIF was negotiated through navigating public discourse around doctors as heroes, and reconciling social altruism with their personal safety and being.

Discussion: COVID-19 was a revealer, prompting critical reflection on enduring questions-regarding what constitutes a 'good doctor,' the societal roles ascribed to physicians, and the ways these roles are understood by doctors, patients, policymakers, and the public. The pandemic's impact was both individual and collective, highlighting the need to shift from isolated experiences toward shared, systemic approaches. This underscores the need to reimagine medical training in the post-COVID era to shape more sustainable approaches to PIF.

An Kateri Vu (Lead Author), Hajar El-Kamouni (Co Author), Jeanne Harnois (Co Author), Jade Bernatchez-Poirier (Co Author), Mandy Malick (Co Author), Elio Kechichian (Co Author), Carolina Fernandes (Co Author)

Methods: A conceptual framework informed design and evaluation. The 150-minute competency-based workshop incorporated Team-Based Learning (TBL) and immersive clinical simulation to promote active learning. Its impact was assessed through a pre-post cohort study with a control group. Residents were evaluated at baseline, immediately post-workshop, and eight weeks later.

Results: The workshop was designed using an integrated framework combining active learning, TBL, and simulation to address residents' demonstrated, expressed, and normative learning needs. This complementary approach fostered collaborative reasoning, experiential skill development, and greater confidence in applying wound care principles to practice. Sixty-six residents in internal medicine, dermatology, and family medicine at a Quebec teaching hospital participated. Knowledge improved by +13.8% (p<0.0001) and clinical confidence by +37% (p<0.0001), both sustained at eight weeks. Perceived relevance averaged 90.7%.

Discussion: This contextualized workshop addresses an educational gap, showing the effectiveness of combining TBL and simulation to improve knowledge, confidence, and retention in wound care. Feedback suggested enhancements, including a summary of dressings, shorter sessions, and preparatory materials. Continued implementation could strengthen wound care competencies among future clinicians, while its adaptable model underscores broader applicability to medical education.

Gurdit Sood (Lead Author), David Nguyen (Co Author), Kiana Agakhani Barfeh (Co Author), Keiran Pace (Co Author), Dylan Kwan (Co Author), Joy Zhao (Co Author), Nathan Lai (Co Author), Astha Patel (Co Author), Maxwell He (Co Author), Heather Braund (Co Author)

Methods: Seven databases (PubMed, Embase, MEDLINE, CINAHL, PsycINFO, Scopus, Google Scholar) were searched to June 2024 according to predefined criteria (PROSPERO CRD42024556242). We included randomized controlled trials, cohort studies, pre- and post-intervention studies, and comparative observational studies involving undergraduate and postgraduate health professions trainees. Eligible interventions included digital flashcards integrating spaced repetition (SRDF). Studies using physical flashcards, laboratory-only studies or studies without comparators were excluded. The risk of bias was assessed with the Medical Education Research Study Quality Instrument (MERSQI) and Newcastle-Ottawa Scale-Education (NOS-E).

Results: Of 5,178 records retrieved, 15 studies met eligibility criteria. Designs included 4 RCTs, 10 cohort studies, and one pre-test post-test study. Study populations varied, including medicine, dentistry, pharmacy, and residency training programs. Flashcard platforms and spacing schedules were heterogeneous, with Anki being the most common (n=6). 12 studies demonstrated significantly improved academic performance with SRDF versus comparators, while 3 studies found no significant difference. Of the nine studies assessing learner perceptions, eight reported positive results. Methodological quality was moderate to high (mean MERSQI: 12.7/18; mean NOS-E: 4.5/6).

Discussion: SRDF improve academic performance and are well received by learners. The most effective interventions used algorithmic spacing and conceptually designed cards; however, evidence remains limited by heterogeneity. Further studies are needed to confirm efficacy.

Aljeena Qureshi (Lead Author), Elizabeth Wei (Co Author), Winnie Yu (Co Author), Waleed K.S. Alsarhani (Co Author), Alexander J. Kaplan (Co Author), Michael T.B. Nguyen (Co Author)

Methods: First- and second-year students attended a two-hour workshop at a Toronto ophthalmology clinic supervised by one staff ophthalmologist and five residents. Ten self-efficacy items (5-point Likert) were completed immediately pre/post. Paired t-tests analyzed matched respondents in each cohort (α=0.05).

Results: Across two cohorts (2024-2025: 25 pre/14 post/11 matched; 2025-2026: 33/15/7) most learners had no prior shadowing (48% in 2024-2025; 73% in 2025-2026). Among those with any exposure, time was modest (medians 10 h and 8 h, IQRs 4-20 and 4-22.5, respectively). Following the workshop, all seven technical slit-lamp tasks improved significantly (Δ +1.55-2.18 and +1.57-2.71, respectively). Largest gains were changing light filters (+2.18) in 2024-2025 and adjusting beam width/height (+2.71) in 2025-2026. Global slit-lamp confidence rose (+1.55 and +2.00, both p=0.001), and confidence to examine and present increased (+0.73, p=0.012; +0.86, p=0.045). Interest in ophthalmology remained high without a significant paired change. Learner priorities support adding direct ophthalmoscopy and tonometry and developing a progressive workshop series.

Discussion: Across two cohorts, a single, low-cost resident-supervised session embedded in routine clinic infrastructure produced large, statistically significant gains in slit-lamp self-efficacy. This resident-supervised slit-lamp workshop is a scalable innovation that advances competency-based education in ophthalmology among preclerkship students.

Ann Lee (Lead Author), Oksana Babenko (Co Author), Adam Neufeld (Co Author), Olga Szafran (Co Author), Shannon Gentilini (Co Author)

Methods: This descriptive qualitative study involved semi-structured interviews with 15 family physician community preceptors. Analysis was guided by a framework synthesis informed by SDT, with template analysis used to identify and interpret motivational factors across levels (above, within and below) and types of motivation (autonomous, controlled, and amotivated).

Results: Four categories of motivational factors were identified, lending depth and nuance to SDT's layered approach: a) teaching site factors such as clinic support and learner fit; b) patient-related factors such as patient characteristics and prioritization of patient relationships; c) intentional teaching experiences such as prior teaching exposure and role models, and d) teaching opportunity factors such as technological demands and shared teaching models. These factors intersected with varying degrees of autonomous, controlled, and amotivated states.

Discussion: Findings expand current SDT-based models by highlighting context-specific influences on motivation among family physician community preceptors. Educational leaders can use this enhanced framework to inform targeted recruitment and retention strategies that go beyond incentives, supporting preceptors' self-determined or internal motivation to foster lasting engagement.

Jessica Bennett (Lead Author), Dr. Laura Stovel (Co Author)

Methods: The sites for the Asclepius Roadshow are greater than a three-hour commute from our existing Asclepius Camp programing. The programs' efficacy was measured from student participation in a pre-/post-program survey, assessing change in medicine admissions knowledge, student interest, and push and pull factors towards medical careers.

Results: In its pilot year, we engaged 72 students from 3 rural communities in the Asclepius Roadshow. Participants reported increased knowledge of MD admissions processes, interest in medical field professions, and self-confidence to pursue medical school at the conclusion of camp. Both groups identified lack of academic resources as a push factor and self-fulfillment as a pull factor towards medical career pursuit. The percentage of participants who stated interest in pursuing a medical career doubled at the program's conclusion.

Discussion: Adopting a community-based model for high school medical outreach programs is an essential step towards ensuring representation of students from rural backgrounds in undergraduate medical school classes. Our program has shown to be effective in sowing interest in a medical career, informing participants about the application process, and undergraduate curriculum.

Methods: Co-developed with the Indigenous Leadership Circle at the University of Toronto's Department of Family and Community Medicine, the elective includes a two-week block or four-week longitudinal curriculum. Felix Fernandez-Penny (Lead Author), Kathryn Roth (Co Author), Tanya Meyer (Co Author), Aishwarya Shukla (Co Author), Maya Sardesai (Co Author)

Methods: Sixteen complex scenarios were conducted in a simulation training session, each with a junior and senior resident. Medical student observers without formal debrief training provided feedback in each session. In half the sessions, observers were prompted for general feedback across domains without a rubric, and in the other half, observers were provided the NOTSS rubric to guide feedback. In both formats, participants rated feedback quality and observers rated ease of delivery across the four NOTSS domains using 5-point Likert scales.

Results: All trainees rated overall student feedback as having high quality without significant differences between NOTSS and standard debriefs. Observers rated greater ease of delivering feedback with NOTSS debriefs (p<0.05), particularly around Leadership (p<0.05) and Decision-Making (p=0.05).

Discussion: The NOTSS framework can serve as a valuable tool enabling medical students without formal debriefing training to deliver high-quality feedback to residents participating in complex scenario simulation. This approach can mitigate facilitation costs while teaching valuable debriefing skills.

Katherine Wisener (Lead Author), Minori Kato-Hopkins (Co Author), Michelle Dusko Biferie (Co Author), Daniel Budgell (Co Author), Carolyn Canfield (Co Author), Sharon Doucet (Co Author), Tara Gill (Co Author), Heather Buckley (Co Author)

Methods: Using an interpretive description methodology, 11 interviews and 2 design-based workshops with preceptors, patient partners, residents, and students were conducted to better understand their experiences and needs with layered learning in community-based family medicine teaching contexts, to inform practical, evidence-based resources. The interview transcripts were coded to identify prevalent themes across interviewed demographics.

Results: While barriers to implementing layered learning were experienced somewhat universally, each group of participants shared unique perspectives and strategies to navigate them. Latika Nirula (Lead Author), Lavinia Kajura (Co Author), Manpreet Saini (Co Author), Cate Creede-Desmarais (Co Author)

Methods: The New and Evolving Academic Leaders program (NEAL) experienced a full scale renewal that positioned Equity, Diversity, Inclusion, Indigeneity, and Accessibility (EDIIA) as the core competencies woven throughout all leadership development rather than siloed topics. The program team engaged in continuous dialogue throughout curriculum renewal and implementation which informed this study. Amber Jolly (Lead Author), Suhangi Brahmbhatt (Co Author), Dr. Chris Gilchrist (Co Author), Dr. Rajesh Girdhari (Co Author)

Methods: A cross-sectional survey was disseminated to University of Toronto Department of Family and Community Medicine (DFCM) physicians at academic teaching sites. The survey addressed: teaching background, AI understanding, perceived AI impact on medical education, faculty development needs. Quantitative questions were summarized descriptively, and qualitative responses were thematically analyzed.

Results: 47 respondents across 11 academic teaching sites were included. Most (79%) consistently assessed medical trainee reasoning through their clinical notes, while fewer (28%) consistently asked probing case questions. Only 32% reported high confidence explaining appropriate clinical use of AI tools, yet all agreed some adjustment to teaching practice is needed. Thematic analysis identified two themes: (1) concerns about AI integration into learning (accuracy, bias, learner over-reliance, privacy) and (2) need for AI adaptation (strengthening knowledge, modifying teaching and assessment methods).

Discussion: DFCM physicians recognized the need to adapt teaching in response to AI. Limited baseline AI knowledge and reliance on evaluating learner tasks that can be readily completed by AI, such as clinical note, represent actionable targets for faculty development to support responsible AI adoption in medical education.

Ram Patel (Lead Author), Peng You (Co Author)

Methods: This qualitative exploratory study uses semi-structed interviews with participants at different levels of training (medical students, residents, and attending surgeons in academic centres). Stratified sampling ensures diversity of perspectives. Interviews focus on experiences of challenge, strategies for navigating difficulty, and the role of wellness.
