Laure Billa (Lead Author), Claude Julie Bourque (Co Author), Mathieu Moreau (Co Author)

Methods: This qualitative research employed individual, in-depth semi-structured interviews with physicians who became patients. Transcriptions were analyzed using a descriptive-interpretative thematic approach, focusing on participants' experiences, the impact of these experiences, and their recommendations for training.

Results: In 2025, 12 family and emergency physicians, with an average of 24 years of experience and personal histories of cancer, other acute or chronic illnesses, or mental health challenges, participated in the study. Through their unique perspective as both healthcare providers and patients, they identified five dimensions of empathy: cognitive, affective, behavioral, ethical, and social. Their experience led to a stronger commitment to understanding patients and heightened awareness of the long-term effects of illness. These experiences also prompted a redefinition of their professional identities, integrating both professional and patient perspectives. Participants recommended that empathy training emphasizes the sharing of central values and skills, particularly listening, respect, understanding, humility and taking better care of oneself. They advocated for a multimodal training approach centered on validating patients and fostering optimal communication, utilizing methods such as experience sharing, role modeling, debriefing, and simulation.

Discussion: Training informed by the experience of 'wounded healers' - physicians who have been patients - may enhance learners' identification with their trainers or training content. This has the potential to improve and sustain communication and empathy skills among medical professionals while also serving as a preventive measure against compassion fatigue.

Mishita Dharia (Lead Author), Ibrahim Alsalkhadi (Co Author), Shreya Sharma (Co Author), Salem Abu Al-Burak (Co Author), Yaser Al Moayad (Co Author), Mark Goldszmidt (Co Author), Jacqueline Torti (Co Author)

Methods: Using a descriptive qualitative approach, field observations (22 sessions, ~120 hours) were conducted at 2 hospital teaching centers, involving 9 attendings, 10 senior trainees, and 24 junior trainees, with concurrent interviews conducted for a subset of participants. Data were thematically analyzed through a character-based leadership framework to explore how participants navigated professional tensions.

Results: Trainees frequently face professional tensions, particularly when patient care tasks conflict with institutional pressures. In these contexts, trainees often draw on character dimensions such as collaboration, drive, and humanity, which can lead to legitimate compromises. However, when these traits are constrained by institutional demands, miscommunication, or limited resources, illegitimate compromises can emerge. To navigate these situations, trainees described prioritizing patient well-being, consulting colleagues, and engaging in reflective decision-making beyond simple right-wrong judgments. Ongoing analysis will further examine how character shapes decision-making in practice.

Discussion: Character-based leadership can help trainees navigate professional tensions. Educational initiatives to develop these skills may help trainees manage competing demands, promote professional growth, and enhance patient care.

Soraiya Lalani (Lead Author), Masoud Mirimoghaddam (Co Author), Sandy Widder (Co Author)

Methods: Reports from 2013-2023 were reviewed. Quality indicators such as reporter type (learner, faculty, staff), anonymous reporting, severity, and interventions were examined. Data were analyzed across concern type, roles, setting, department, and intervention level.

Results: Annual reports increased from 22 to 98 between 2013-2023, with 85% of reports closed within six months. Most reports were directed toward faculty, staff, and health professionals (70%), 32% of these from learners. Surgery (23%) and Medicine (21%) had the most reports in the clinical setting. Most interventions were Level 1 (20-40%), or Level 2 (35-50%), while Levels 3-4 each comprised <15% of reports. Levels 3-4 were more common clinically (p = 0.004) and with attempted mitigation (p < 0.001).

Discussion: Trends show progress in recognizing professionalism lapses, yet departmental clustering and escalation clinically highlight systemic gaps. Sustained improvement will require departmental culture shifts and conflict resolution training.

Nadia Incardona (Lead Author)

Methods: A multi-pronged approach was used. A working group of faculty and professionalism experts led the revision, using consensus as its decision-making method. A literature review identified best practices and gaps in professionalism assessment. Expert consultations with faculty leaders and professionalism scholars provided theoretical and practical perspectives. Learners were engaged throughout the process to ensure their voices were incorporated. Consultations with equity-deserving groups highlighted challenges and unintended consequences of current processes. Finally, professionalism codes of conduct from medical colleges, national associations, and the CanMEDS framework were reviewed to ensure alignment with broader standards.

Results: The revised framework defines six domains of professional behaviour: (1) interactions with patients and essential care partners, (2) reliability and responsibilities, (3) growth and adaptability, (4) relationships with colleagues, (5) upholding student and professional codes of conduct, and (6) recognizing and responding to ethical issues. Each domain contains explicit, observable behaviours, reducing ambiguity and supporting meaningful feedback. The framework centres on key learner relationships and commitments, explicitly recognizes wellness and peer support as elements of professionalism, and affirms the MD Program's clear standards for student conduct.

Discussion: This framework represents a significant step toward a more equitable and supportive approach to professionalism assessment by combining evidence, expert consensus, learner input, and stakeholder perspectives.This framework also defines an updated standard of professional behaviour for medical students. Next steps will include targeted faculty development to increase comfort with providing feedback, and strategies to socialize faculty into their role as coaches and teachers of professionalism.

Sanja Kostov (Lead Author), Olga Szafran (Co Author), Lan He (Co Author), Shannon Gentilini (Co Author), Marissa Doroshuk (Co Author), Oksana Babenko (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a qualitative descriptive study using semi-structured interviews with first-year FMRs completing the FMOB LCE. Eighteen interviews were audio-recorded, transcribed, and descriptively analyzed. Clinical vignettes were used to probe decision-making.

Results: Factors influencing residents' decision-making clustered into 3 categories: systemic, patient/relational, and individual/personal. Systemic influences included societal, organizational, and local practice culture. Patient factors encompassed clinical presentation, knowledge and perspectives, and preferences. Physician individual/personal factors included educational exposures, confidence in pregnancy care, awareness of different providers' scope of practice, and personal experiences or interests/motivations.

Discussion: Preliminary analysis suggests FM residents consider a composite of systemic, patient, and personal factors when making decisions in pregnancy care and referral practice. Their decision-making reflects patient autonomy, shared decision-making, access to care, and safe triage - elements central to high-quality, respectful pregnancy care.

Ayoub Chobah (Co Author), Sophie De Roock (Lead Author), Aly Julien (Co Author), Salomon Fotsing (Co Author), Sarah Marion Elias (Co Author), Marie-Pier Dupuis (Co Author), Tiffany Ange Tchigio Djomou (Co Author), Maria Cherba (Co Author), Michelle Anawati (Co Author), Alexandre Messager (Co Author), Diane Bouchard Lamothe (Co Author)

Methods: Étude mixte convergente. Le volet quantitatif comprenait un prétest administré avant une lecture sur l'intelligence intergénérationnelle, suivi de deux post-tests, analysés avec SPSS (test t et ANOVA). Le volet qualitatif reposait sur des entrevues semi-dirigées, analysées par approche thématique inductive.

Results: Les étudiants ont complété le prétest (n=149), post-test 1 (n=93) et 2 (n=38). Les scores du post-test 1 démontrent une amélioration significative après la formation (M = -1,97 et M = -1,62 chez les francophones et anglophones, respectivement). Les francophones ont obtenu des résultats supérieurs aux anglophones au pré-test (M = 6,83 vs M = 6,43), post-test 1(M = 8,80 vs 7,96) et post-test 2 (M = 7,75 vs 6,56), avec une différence significative (p < 0.05). Les entrevues (n = 10) révèlent une perception positive de la formation, jugée pertinente et utile pour la pratique future. Toutefois, plusieurs étudiants ont suggéré d'intégrer des ateliers interactifs impliquant différentes générations.

Discussion: La formation a amélioré les connaissances des étudiants, qui ont apprécié son application concrète en milieu médical. Les résultats soutiennent son intégration au curriculum, avec un potentiel d'optimisation par des approches plus interactives et contextualisées

Rajeshkumar Gordhanbhai Kathrotia (Lead Author), Swapnil Paralikar (Co Author), Vinay Chitturi (Co Author), Pradip Barde (Co Author), Gaurav Sharma (Co Author), Naresh Parmar (Co Author), Vivek Kumar Sharma (Co Author), Chinmay Shah (Co Author)

Methods: A semi-structured interview guide was developed based on written feedback from students on creation of working model in Physiology for a FGD. FGD was conducted with 12 out of 50 students who participated in construction of working models (till the point of saturation). The FGD was audio recorded and a transcription of the interview was prepared. Manual coding was done in transcript to identify categories followed by generation of themes based on the categories. Coding, categories and themes were verified by 2 experts in qualitative analysis.

Results: The main themes identified were enabling factors, hindering factors and learning concepts. Categories under theme of enabling factors were collaboration/teamwork, support from faculty and others, while categories under hindering factors included team dynamics, logistic challenges, limiting knowledge, technical weaknesses and others. Categories under learning included learning physiology concepts and model making.

Discussion: Collaborative learning is acceptable to students as an effective active learning method for learning concepts in physiology. Engagement of students in model-building exercises through group activity escalates learning experience and facilitates soft skills development.

Jaimie Coleman (Lead Author), Ryan Brydges (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a comprehensive literature review on effort regulation in both HPE and general education contexts. Our model integrates elements from control theory and the opportunity-cost model of subjective effort. We conceptualize effort regulation as comprising two components: a cognitive process of learners' recognizing a discrepancy, and a motivational process of learners evaluating the opportunities and costs associated with acting on the discrepancy (or not).

Results: Our model helps researchers to identify key concepts for studying the mechanisms of effort regulation toward enhancing how learners allocate their efforts in HPE settings. We will demonstrate how learners cyclically regulate their effort upon identifying a discrepancy, and how they sustain motivation to reduce the discrepancy when the appraised opportunities of allocating effort outweigh the costs.

Discussion: By emphasizing both cognitive and motivational dimensions, our effort regulation model provides a foundation for educators to design instruction that helps learners make informed decisions about effort investment. We will present avenues for research using the model, to introduce the HPE community to fruitful possibilities.

Milin Patel (Lead Author), Niyoosha Yoosefi (Co Author), Shurjeel Qazi (Co Author), Jeffrey Ding (Co Author), Sabeen Tiwana (Co Author), Javed Siddiqi (Co Author), Faisal Khosa (Co Author)

Methods: A retrospective cross-sectional analysis was conducted using self-reported data from the Association of American Medical Colleges (AAMC) annual reports. Faculty demographics were analyzed by gender, race/ethnicity, academic rank, degree, and tenure status. Linear regression assessed temporal trends, while t-tests and ANOVA with post hoc Tukey tests evaluated group differences.

Results: Between 1977 and 2021, representation of white academic physicians in PHPM significantly declined (-0.74% per year), while Asian (+0.33%/yr), Black (+0.14%/yr), and multiracial (+0.07%/yr) representation increased. Hispanic and Indigenous groups remained underrepresented throughout. Women academic physicians increased from 25.5% to 53.8% (+0.8%/yr), however, remained underrepresented at senior ranks, comprising only 24.2% of tenured faculty and 14.8% of department chairs. Across all faculty, white academic physicians remained overrepresented.

Discussion: Despite increases in gender and racial diversity among academic PHPM physicians, substantial disparities persist in senior academic ranks, tenure, and leadership roles. Targeted initiatives addressing mentorship, promotion practices, and hiring practices are crucial to achieve a workforce reflective of the diverse US population.

Rola Ajjawi (Co Author), Maree Martinussen (Lead Author), Neera Jain (Co Author), Joanna Tai (Co Author), Margaret Bearman (Co Author)

Methods: In this critical literature review, we used Carole Bacchi's "What's the problem represented to be" (WPR) approach to examine how psychological safety is conceptualised, including what is overlooked. The WPR method is grounded in post-structural discourse analysis. From this perspective, language is not a neutral medium of describing reality. The search was conducted to include papers published from January 2019 to January 2025 with the term 'psychological safety' as pertains to learners.

Results: We analysed 46 papers and identified four functions that psychological safety served: (1) as a solution to a wide range of problems, (2) an achievable goal, (3) a way to manage hierarchical power, and (4) a substitute for attending to emotions. We critique each, demonstrating how psychological safety is often treated as a state that individuals can achieve. This is less effective in countering the phenomena from which unsafety originates-complex social processes that extend beyond learning environments.

Discussion: Safety is not a destination, but a process of cultivating a shared interpersonal climate. We offer a reframing of safety, as an ongoing, negotiated process among all those involved rather than an endpoint. This focus on psychological safety as a process could guide practitioners, learners and researchers to contribute to more inclusive learning environments.

Tahirih Nasseri (Lead Author), Darshana Seeburruth (Lead Author), Edgar Akuffo-Addo (Co Author), Yvette Miller-Monthrope (Co Author), Marissa Joseph (Co Author)

Methods: Nine incoming dermatology residents from six Canadian programs were selected to reflect diversity in background and training. They provided 1.5 hours of presentations followed by a 30-minute Q&A. Medical students were invited to complete pre- and post-event surveys using 5-point Likert scales. Data were analyzed with paired t-tests, and open-ended responses underwent thematic analysis.

Results: A total of 202 students representing all Canadian medical schools attended; 104 (51.5%) completed surveys. Before the event, most respondents lacked mentorship (73.1%) and networking opportunities (81.7%). Following participation, students reported significant improvements in knowledge of CaRMS components, including the application process, CV planning, extracurricular involvement, research, electives, personal and reference letters, interviews, and parallel planning (all p<0.0001). They also reported reduced anxiety about the CaRMS process (p<0.001). Most respondents (89.4%) reported receiving valuable mentorship and guidance, while nearly all found the panel valuable (92.3%), would recommend it (92.3%), and supported future iterations (97.1%).

Discussion: This structured, no-cost, virtual panel improved access to mentorship, strengthened application preparedness, and reduced anxiety among medical students. The model is scalable and adaptable to other specialties, supporting equitable access to guidance and resources nationwide.

Shira Gertsman (Lead Author), Devon Wilton (Co Author), Yasmin Dini (Co Author), Shane Neilson (Co Author)

Methods: Online admission resources from all English-speaking Canadian medical schools were reviewed to identify accessibility barriers. This was contextualized within existing literature on medical students with disabilities to generate recommendations for increasing accessibility.

Results: Lack of transparency regarding accommodations during interviews and medical school discourages students from proactively seeking out accommodations that they need to protect their health and inhibits the detection of discriminatory processes. Technical standards unnecessarily exclude students who would be capable of safe and effective practice as physicians, and rigid admissions criteria can disqualify students for disability-related reasons and devalues the strengths that living with a disability can impart. Effective accommodations policies must go beyond the legal minimum, consider intersectionality, involve comprehensive and confidential data collection, and be informed by people with lived experience.

Discussion: Currently, students with disabilities face many barriers to medical school admission. Increasing acceptance of and support for medical students with disabilities involves implementing changes that support the health and well-being of all medical trainees through increased compassion, adaptability, and focus on trainee health. It is important for physicians from all specialties to be cognizant of individual and institutional biases against physicians and learners with disabilities that perpetuate ableism in the medical field.

Claudine Henoud (Co Author), Chantal Phillips (Lead Author), Amira Abdalla (Co Author), Justin Lam (Co Author), Mark D. Hanson (Co Author), Oluwatobi R. Olaiya (Co Author)

Methods: A mixed-methods case study was conducted using the CDC's 2024 Program Evaluation Framework. A post-program online survey captured both quantitative and qualitative perspectives on financial impact, application costs, program experiences, and recommendations. Data were analyzed using both quantitative descriptive statistics and inferential statistics, including subgroup analysis and quantitative descriptive methods.

Results: In 2022, 320 students applied for waivers, a 96% increase from 2021, with 91.4% of waivers redeemed. Waiver users reported significantly lower direct application costs (median $300 vs. $780; p < .001) and applied to more schools both in Ontario (median 4 vs. 2; p = .001) and nationally (median 5 vs. 3; p = .009) compared to waiver non-users. Among waiver users, 18.13% gained admission to an Ontario medical school, compared to the 15.3% national rate. 92.6% of survey respondents (n=75) reported reduced financial burden. Noted concerns included stigma, privacy, and waiver availability.

Discussion: This evaluation demonstrated reduced financial barriers and improved admissions for waiver users, making the OMSAFW program effective in addressing current disparities in low-income access to medical school applications. The fee waivers represent a promising tool to increase socioeconomic diversity in the physician workforce.

James Hudson (Lead Author), Jacomie Strydom (Co Author), Ashley Clelland (Co Author), Victor Do (Co Author), Cheryl Goldstein (Co Author)

Methods: Three World Café discussions were conducted using a structured small-group format to explore complex questions. Eighteen students and three residents participated, responding to open-ended prompts. Transcripts were coded qualitatively using constructivist grounded theory to identify themes.

Results: Analysis revealed that PerMS development is influenced by four overlapping domains: the medical learning environment, life outside medicine, mentorship, and personal growth. Learners emphasized the importance of life experiences in skill building, such as travel, family and guidance from mentors. Support from peers, constructive feedback and a positive learning environment also played key roles. Challenges like navigating the hidden curriculum or personal setbacks could hinder growth, though many learners saw working through them as essential to skill development. PerMS development was described as individualized, ongoing, and context dependent.

Discussion: This study highlights that the development of PerMS is highly variable, shaped by personal, social and institutional factors. Our findings suggest that medical schools should recognize PerMS development as an ongoing, malleable process rather than a set of innate traits. Structural supports that facilitate reflection, mentorship, and peer support may help strengthen these skills and mitigate challenges such as stress and social comparison.

Quinten Clarke (Lead Author), Douglas Lebo (Co Author), Erene Stergiopoulos (Co Author), Lynn Ashdown (Co Author), Saleem Razack (Co Author), Shira Gertsman (Co Author), Julia Hanes (Co Author), Kathryn Fung (Co Author), Tal Jarus (Co Author)

Methods: Data was obtained from the AFMC Student Questionnaires' MD Entry Survey. Respondents were first year medical students from thirteen of seventeen Canadian medical schools who completed the survey between 2019 and 2023. All survey responses were self-reported. Disability status was categorized as a mental health concern, physical limitation, or learning disability. Demographic data were analyzed descriptively and using Pearson chi-square test. Disability status was analyzed using logistic regression.

Results: 4,289 (97%) participants were eligible for inclusion in our analysis. 991 participants (23.1%) reported having a disability, with more than half of these respondents reporting a mental health concern. Disability prevalence among survey respondents increased over the period analyzed, from 17.6% in 2019 to 23.9% in 2023. Mental health concerns ranged in prevalence from 14.4% to 20.6% during this period. Physical limitations remained largely stable, from 3.2% in 2019 to 3.7% in 2023, while learning disabilities showed a three-times relative increase, from 2.3% in 2019 to 6.9% in 2023.

Discussion: The prevalence of disability among Canadian medical students in this study is meaningfully greater than previous studies from both the United States and the United Kingdom, with a similar trend of increased prevalence

Sarah Burm (Lead Author), Dawn Burleigh (Co Author), Emily Field (Co Author), Kathy Hibbert (Co Author), Erin Kennedy (Co Author), Anna MacLeod (Co Author), Chris Watling (Co Author), Kevin Wood (Co Author)

Methods: To understand the value and transformative potential of social relationships at work, we conducted appreciative interviews with physicians (n=6) and nurses (n=9) across Canada. Data collection and analysis drew on narrative methodology to elicit stories about how workplace friendships transcend the typical collegial relationships and the extent to which they enhance wellbeing and resilience.

Results: Workplace friendships reinvigorated participants' capacity to face the stressors of their work, functioning as a buffer against burnout. Stories of shared traditions and participation in activities beyond the workplace were abundant. While informal, these practices and networks of care were far from happenstance; cultivating and sustaining friendships required both initiative and intention yet often went unnoticed or unacknowledged by employers.

Discussion: Participants recognized the systems they worked in were under pressure with no relief in sight. Yet they prioritized time to nurture friendships at work. Creating space for connection and moments of levity emerged as quiet but powerful acts of defiance in response to system pressures. Our findings suggest that friendship serves a vital role in strained professional climates and invite consideration of whether existing narratives of workplace wellbeing have underestimated its restorative value.

Brad MacCosham (Lead Author), Amy Nakajima (Co Author), K. Jean Chen (Co Author)

Methods: We analyzed all 12 CACMS standards and 96 elements for the 2026-2027 cycle using theory-driven conceptual frameworks1. This study's composite analytic lens integrated: (1) the Capability Approach2 (2) Ethics of Care3 and (3) the AFMC Social Accountability framework4. Guided by these frameworks, we applied directed content analysis5, deductively coding elements to a priori categories and inductively extending the codebook where needed. The Framework Method1 was then used to synthesize findings into overarching humanistic themes, emphasizing respect, equity, and social accountability. To ensure rigour, we maintained an audit trail, engaged in analytic memoing, and conducted peer debriefing to enhance credibility and dependability.

Results: Three overarching themes emerged: (1) Individual humanity and institutional integrity where standards addressing wellness, mistreatment, and governance frame human dignity as a structural requirement; (2) Humanity in medical knowledge and competence where curricula and assessment are framed as ethical responsibilities, emphasizing social determinants of health and cultural safety; and (3) Broader humanity in medicine where social accountability, diversity, and equity are positioned as core to the physicians' role. Collectively, these themes demonstrate that accreditation affirms medicine as both a technical and moral practice.

Discussion: Accreditation in Canada extends beyond compliance; it is an ethical commitment that positions medical schools as stewards of humanity. By embedding humanistic principles into standards, CACMS reframes accreditation as a driver of culture change in medical education. Recognizing accreditation as both a regulatory and relational act can help institutions move from simply producing competent physicians to preparing compassionate advocates equipped to meet the moral and social demands of contemporary practice.

Danielle Blouin (Lead Author)

Methods: Appreciative inquiry, a strength-based evaluation approach focusing on the positives of an organization was used to identify leaders' professional values, and accreditation practices they perceived as most engaging. Fifty-nine educational leaders, from UGME and from the six largest PGME programs at one medical school, were invited to interviews and focus groups, from 2022-24. Two coders analyzed data thematically, using emergent coding.

Results: 8 UGME and 20 leaders participated (48%). Leaders value their roles for the opportunity to contribute to learners' growth and to effect meaningful changes, the intellectual stimulation, the collaboration with learners/colleagues. They value that accreditation drives comprehensive program review, curricular changes, and resources; validates their work; contributes to their learning about education.

Discussion: Leaders value accreditation practices that lever their values: program review and curricular changes contribute to learners' growth. Ervis Musa (Lead Author), Rebecca Khoury (Co Author), Julia Thomas (Co Author), Brad MacCosham (Co Author), Julie Marois (Co Author), Laura Muldoon (Co Author), Susan Bennett (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted structured interviews (April-June 2025) with CSL leads at 16 CACMS-accredited Canadian medical schools. We designed the interview guide based on CACMS (2025) element 6.6 (preparation, structure, evaluation) and transcribed interviews verbatim. We used NVivo-15 to manage data and conducted thematic analysis using a primarily deductive framework-guided approach mapped to CACMS-6.6.

Results: Five themes emerged from 6.7 hours of interviews: (1) learning objectives, (2) implementation, (3) placement structures, (4) preparation and safety, and (5) assessment and evaluation. CSL was mandatory at 12/16 schools (mean ≈40 hours; range ≈10-80+ hours). 15/16 emphasized communication skills and understanding lived experiences of diverse populations as key learning objectives. Placement models varied: 10/16 offered online or in-person options and 9/16 required work with people experiencing health inequities. Preparation included universal orientation (16/16) with content focused on SDOH (14/16). Assessment relied on reflections (16/16; 11 end-only, 5 longitudinal), while only 6/16 schools systematically gathered community-partner feedback.

Discussion: This study reveals significant variability in CSL program design, placement criteria and evaluation. Establishing benchmarks and fostering inter-institutional collaboration could strengthen CSL's impact on students, communities and the broader goal of socially accountable medical education.

David Rojas (Lead Author), James Kelly (Co Author), Dawit Wondimagegn (Co Author), Ahmed Rashid (Co Author), Cynthia Whitehead (Co Author), STELAR Group (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a nominal group technique exercise during a one-day symposium in August 2023 in London, United Kingdom. Eighteen global scholars and thought leaders from 11 countries were purposively invited. In three groups, participants identified issues with global accreditation through structured brainstorming, ranked priorities, and presented the resulting ideas via consensus frameworks. Moderators then synthesized the three frameworks through thematic analysis and returned an overarching consensus framework to all authors for review and endorsement.

Results: The process produced strong convergence across groups. The final framework comprises seven research domains that warrant priority attention: Purpose; Quality and Sustainability; Economics; Governance; Colonialism Effects; Process; and Outcomes. Illustrative questions include accountability of global regulators, cost and value for institutions, risks of brain drain, feasibility in resource-constrained settings, and the impact of universal standards on context-specific training and social accountability.

Discussion: The consensus framework offers a practical map for researchers and policymakers to prioritize studies on the drivers, processes, and consequences of global accreditation. It centers equity, acknowledges complexity, and invites comparative and theory-informed work. While the framework is comprehensive, it should be revised as new evidence emerges, so that future accreditation practices reflect rigorous scholarship.

Bianca Giglio (Lead Author), Ithiele Princesse Tsamo Tiobou (Co Author), Helena Haddad (Co Author)

Methods: This single-session randomized controlled trial will recruit preparatory, year 1, and year 2 medical students. Participants will be randomly assigned to train on the NeuroVR simulator with ICEMS feedback either with or without Mozart music. After a baseline task, students will complete five practice tasks (5 minutes each) with ICEMS metric-specific feedback. Emotional states will be recorded pre-, peri-, and post-training using the Medical Emotions Scale (MES). A realistic 13-minute brain tumour resection task without feedback will assess skill transfer. Cognitive load will be measured post-trial using the Cognitive Load Index. ICEMS will generate composite scores, aspirator force, and instrument tip separation distance for statistical comparison.

Results: Preliminary analyses indicate that the music group achieved significantly higher ICEMS composite scores, used less aspirator force, and maintained closer instrument tip separation during the realistic task compared with controls.

Discussion: Mozart music may provide a low-cost, non-invasive strategy to enhance technical skill acquisition, emotional outcomes, and patient safety during neurosurgical training.

Daman P. Dhunna (Lead Author), Rehan R. Khan (Lead Author), Rohith Ryali (Co Author), Brian S. Tao (Co Author), Robert Azario (Co Author), Nader El-Hajj (Co Author), Matthew Kim (Co Author), Neil V. Shah (Co Author), Tony Tannoury (Co Author), Chadi Tannoury (Co Author)

Methods: Five guidelines were entered into ChatGPT-5 using a base instruction to act as a spine surgeon. The questions either aimed at general physicians (concise, precise answers for non-specialists) or at patients with health literacy at or below a 6th-grade level. Three fellowship-trained spine surgeons independently graded the 10 outputs generated on a Likert scale (3=accurate, 2=partially accurate, 1=inaccurate). Accuracy was summarized with means, ranges, and proportions of exact agreement.

Results: Clinician-facing responses generated by ChatGPT were rated with perfect agreement in 4 of 5 scenarios by all three graders with an average score of 2.93 (±0.26). The patient-facing generated responses were less consistent, with an average of 2.33 (±0.72) and a range from 1 to 3, with no complete agreement between the three graders. Graders gave lower ratings when ChatGPT overlooked mJOA cut-offs for DCM, recommendations for surgery, and strength of the supporting evidence.

Discussion: In this validation study, ChatGPT-5 reliably reflected AO Spine guidelines for general physicians, while omitting important details for patient-facing answers. These findings suggest ChatGPT may potentially extend clinical recommendations to patients, however, spine expert input remains invaluable to ensure key nuances in patient education are not missed.

Megan Clemens (Lead Author), Josheil Kaur Boparai (Co Author), Russell Dawe (Co Author), Susan Avery (Co Author)

Methods: Guided by Arksey and O'Malley's methodological framework for conducting scoping studies, six databases were searched in January 2025. Two reviewers independently screened articles and extracted data. Findings were synthesized following PRISMA-ScR guidelines.

Results: Twelve studies from four countries met the inclusion criteria. Articles varied in geographic context, delivery format, and learner composition. PBL was implemented as standalone sessions, adjunct modules, or blended with traditional methods. Learners ranged from cohorts in a single training level to those in multiple levels, often in interprofessional or mixed-specialty groups. Most studies examined learners' perceptions and satisfaction, while fewer reported objective outcomes, such as knowledge acquisition, clinical behavior, or patient-level effects. Methodological heterogeneity limited the ability to draw generalized conclusions across studies.

Discussion: This review demonstrates that research on PBL in family medicine residency is limited but diverse in scope, spanning multiple settings and curricular methods. Current literature emphasizes subjective outcomes, with few rigorous evaluations of the impact on knowledge or clinical practice. By delineating existing literature, this review identifies gaps and provides direction for future studies on how PBL can be systematically integrated and evaluated in postgraduate family medicine training.

James Macaskill (Lead Author), Mars Yixing Zhao (Co Author), Shayan Shirazi (Co Author), Thomas Goldade (Co Author), Natisha Thakkar (Co Author), Payton McKechnie (Co Author), Krista Trinder (Co Author), Ryan Krochak (Co Author), Sheldon Moellenbeck (Co Author), Meredith McKague (Co Author), Ginger Ruddy (Co Author), Greg Malin (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a mixed-methods study of fourth-year students attending a three-part "CaRMS Primer" lecture series delivered by junior residents. Post-session surveys (adapted Learning Climate Questionnaire, Perceived Competence Scale) assessed learning climate and competence; reliability was tested with Cronbach's alpha and differences analyzed with one-way ANOVA. Semi-structured interviews underwent thematic analysis, with themes mapped to SDT domains.

Results: Fifty-six of ninety-four students (59.6%) completed surveys, and twenty-five (26.6%) participated in interviews. Learning climate scores were consistently high across all sessions. Perceived competence increased significantly after the final session on interviews (p < .001). Qualitative data revealed four themes: stress and anxiety, perceived competence and readiness, autonomy and control, and relatedness and peer support. Students described NPT as credible, reassuring, and empowering, while also noting challenges related to peer comparison and limited presenter diversity.

Discussion: NPT appears to enhance readiness for the residency match by fostering psychological safety, boosting self-efficacy, and leveraging relatable near-peer experiences in alignment with SDT principles. Structured near-peer interventions may serve as a replicable, theory-informed approach to supporting learners through high-stakes transitions in medical education.

Jacquelin Forsey (Lead Author), Warren Lewin (Co Author), Maxwell Charles (Co Author), Nikki Woods (Co Author)

Methods: This study employed a within-subjects experimental design to assess changes to headline performance. Participants were 35 heathcare providers (physicians, nurses, social work, allied health), working in a hospital setting. Participants completed 10-minute simulations pre- and post-workshop with a standardized patient, and 2 simulations one month later to assess skill durability and transferability. Headlines were assessed for information, clarity, and empathy on a 3-point scale.

Results: Pre-workshop, 74% of participants delivered a headline within the 10-minute simulation; in subsequent simulations, this increased to 100%. Additionally, participants delivered headlines significantly earlier in conversations. Finally, there was a significant increase in scores for information, clarity, and empathy in all post-workshop simulations, with no decrease at the delay simulation, or during the far-transfer scenario.

Discussion: Results demonstrate that the VitalTalk method for teaching providers to deliver difficult news is effective in shaping the desired communication behaviours. These changes are maintained after a significant delay and can be transferred to other challenging communication scenarios.

Suchnoor Dhillon (Lead Author), Jolie Leung (Co Author), Yasmeen Mezil (Co Author)

Methods: This is a pre-post study using descriptive statistics on outcomes of a student-led dissection teaching session on hip anatomy and the anterior approach to the hip. The pre- and post-tests included assessing anatomical self-efficacy using the Anatomical Self-Efficacy Instrument (ASEI), and assessing hip joint anatomical knowledge and clinical applications with knowledge quizzes. These included 10 questions with 2 each on general hip anatomy, the anterior approach, consequences of intraoperative injury, hip precautions, and rehabilitation.

Results: All 7 participants completed all components. Mean knowledge score was 5.6 on pre-test (min. 2, max. 7) and 8.7 on post-test (min. 7, max. 10) with 3.1 mean increase. Mean ASEI score was 60 on the pre-test (min. 46, max. 79), and 64 on the post-test (min. 48, max. 80) with 3.4 mean increase.

Discussion: These findings suggest near-peer instruction effectively teaches clinical anatomy. Post-session knowledge quiz scores indicate successful knowledge transmission, supporting previous literature. Lack of significant change in ASEI score may suggest a single session is insufficient to influence self-confidence, or that constraints limited student autonomy. Further refinement is required to optimize student engagement and self-efficacy. Our findings suggest such sessions enhance anatomical knowledge and hold promise as a scalable complement to traditional teaching.

Chris He (Co Author), Stephen Russell (Lead Author)

Methods: We are instructors of medical language and are therefore aware of the reliable formulas that are used in creating real medical terms - and why those patterns are essential in medicine. This presentation focuses on common products, often incredibly popular ones, that either seem to use real medicalese but that actually have no meaning, or ones that clearly sound invented even to the untrained eye, yet are still structured to seem as if they are built on the solid principles of medical language.

Results: Medical language, and words that sound as if they are medically related, are important tools in marketing and selling certain products. We will discuss and list many examples of trademark names that play on their implied connection to real medical language.

Discussion: For years, schools often taught learners to be media and advertising literate. This presentation hopes to encourage another push for a similar kind of media-literacy - learning how to read through empty phrases.

Natia Landia (Lead Author), Nino Lomidze (Co Author), Mariam Gogichadze (Co Author), Inga Odzelashvili (Co Author)

Methods: Grounded in constructivism and social learning theory, CBCL applies a flipped classroom model combining asynchronous preparation and synchronous interactive sessions. Students engage with preparatory resources, readiness assessment exercises (RAEs), case discussions in small groups (4-5 learners), and large-group debriefs. A pilot implementation is planned in the integrated block Structure and Function of the Human Body I-IV. Faculty training precedes rollout to ensure effective facilitation and sustainable practice.

Results: Literature supports CBCL as an effective method for improving knowledge acquisition, inquiry, discussion, and collaboration among medical students. Anticipated outcomes of implementation include greater engagement, enhanced clinical reasoning, and improved teamwork skills.

Discussion: Key challenges include faculty/student resistance, workload in module development, and adapting CBCL to virtual or diverse learning contexts. Proposed solutions include targeted faculty development, backward design for module creation, Goldilocks' principles for RAEs, and structured faculty guides. CBCL integration is expected to strengthen active learning and better prepare students for complex clinical practice.

Anna MacLeod (Lead Author), Paula Cameron (Co Author), Victoria Luong (Co Author), Olga Kits (Co Author), Sarah Burm (Co Author), Stephen Miller (Co Author), Wendy Stewart (Co Author), Simon Field (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a longitudinal Critical Discourse Analysis (CDA), following 12 medical students through medical school (2021-5). Using Fairclough's three-phase analytical approach, we traced discourses about death and emotions over time, using 92 semi-structured and recursive interviews.

Results: Crying was a central but complex concern: an external sign of failed detachment. In clinical settings, crying was "the worst thing you can do." Participants worried about uncontrollable emotional reactions to patient death. Through words like "mask," "compartmentalize," "zone out," and "disassociate," they drew on professional norms of detachment. In attempts to avoid crying, learners declined debrief invitations and chose to cry in private spaces like bathrooms or on the drive home, or to defer crying indefinitely. Yet, participants also resisted detachment, affirming emotional investment crying as a humane response to suffering. Instead, they aligned themselves with patient and family vantage points, rejecting death as merely "part of the job."

Discussion: Despite efforts to foster humanism, discourses of detachment continue to dominate medical culture. In a profession characterized by high rates of burnout and distress, this has consequences for learner wellbeing. Informal messaging about detachment must be taken into account in efforts to improve learner health and well-being, such as debriefing. Meaningful support cannot be achieved so long as crying continues to be discursively constructed as "the worst thing you can do" as a doctor

Lisa Purdy (Lead Author), Tyler Cole (Co Author)

Methods: Using Allen et al.'s (2021) integrative belonging framework, 21 semi-structured interviews were conducted in winter 2025. Transcripts were coded inductively and deductively to capture the opportunities and perceptions of belonging.

Results: Four interrelated themes emerged: 1.recognition of progress, leadership initiatives foster inclusion and community; 2. barriers to belonging, including discrimination, underrepresentation, and inadequate accommodations; 3. communication gaps causing confusion and exclusion; and 4. recognition, with staff feeling undervalued.

Discussion: These intersecting factors illustrate a complex and nuanced belonging landscape in FoMD, marked by simultaneous progress and systemic challenges. Findings underscore leadership's critical role in fostering belonging, while highlighting persistent challenges related to diversity, communication, and recognition that continue to affect faculty engagement. Addressing these challenges through targeted strategies to improve communication, promote equitable recognition, and mitigate bias is essential to sustain workforce diversity and resilience. These insights can guide FoMD leadership in cultivating a more inclusive, supportive academic culture

Methods: Conçu en co-construction avec des acteurs du milieu, l'outil a été affiné par trois prétests et quatre pilotes menés d'avril à décembre 2024. Ce processus itératif a permis une amélioration en continu. Une formation professorale a été offerte à 20 directeurs/responsables de programme entre janvier et mars 2025.

Stefanie Sebok-Syer (Co Author), Jerry Maniate (Co Author), Kaitlin Endres (Co Author), Warren Cheung (Co Author), Eusang Ahn (Lead Author)

Methods: A narrative review methodology was used to identify and synthesize literature on collectivism and interdependence in healthcare and HPE. English-language peer-reviewed publications from 1990-2024 were included; non-English articles, perspective pieces, commentaries, and studies unrelated to healthcare or HPE were excluded. Searches were conducted in MEDLINE, Embase, PsycINFO, and ERIC in January 2024, with forward and backward citation mining in November 2024.

Results: Of 3,415 articles identified, 63 met inclusion criteria. Reflexive thematic analysis revealed four themes: (1) reshaping the culture of healthcare and the learning climate in HPE, (2) recognizing and accounting for individual contributions within teams, (3) multifaceted approaches to leadership, and (4) belongingness as foundational to collective well-being.

Discussion: Viewing healthcare and HPE through the paradigms of collectivism and interdependence challenges prevailing models of individualism and independence. Adopting these paradigms can foster collaboration, enhance team-based practice, and create learning environments that are both protective and transformative. This perspective may support the development of healthcare professionals who are better-prepared for the relational and collective nature of modern healthcare

Jonathan Chainey (Lead Author), Sandalia Genus (Co Author), Csilla Kalocsai (Co Author), Stanley Hamstra (Co Author), Nir Lipsman (Co Author), Paul Kongkham (Co Author), Ashish Kumar (Co Author)

Methods: Using critical constructivist theory, virtual interviews were conducted with current program directors (PDs) from Canada's 14 neurosurgery programs and neurosurgery residents who left training between 2013-2023. Interviews were recorded, transcribed, anonymized, and iteratively coded through descriptive thematic analysis to construct an analytical framework.

Results: Data from 8 of 14 programs revealed an average institutional attrition rate of 14.1% (range: 0%-28.6%). Interviews with 7 program directors and 7 former residents, representing 7 programs, identified key factors contributing to attrition, listed below in descending order of frequency mentioned during the interviews: high workload, poor work-life balance, gender-related challenges for women, evolving aspirations over time, limited job opportunities in Canada and high responsibility level.

Discussion: Neurosurgery residents are the future of the specialty. Our study identifies key factors contributing to the high attrition rate in neurosurgery training, highlighting the urgent need for changes at both the provincial and program levels to retain trainees. The most frequently cited reasons: high workload, poor work-life balance, and gender-related challenges, underscore the complexity of the issue. Addressing these factors through improved support, better teaching, and more sustainable working conditions is essential for the future of neurosurgery training

Lisa Chinchamie (Lead Author), Yasmine Ishmael (Co Author), Laura Leigh Murgaski (Co Author), Branden Wesseling (Co Author), David Rojas (Co Author)

Methods: We reviewed the PGME Accreditation requirements and the MD Program Data Collection Instrument to build a comparison matrix, mapped standards to shared domains, and tagged compliance artifacts (policies, minutes, reports). To avoid false positives, we only documented places where evidence for compliance could plausibly overlap, excluding items that were thematically similar but clearly required distinct evidence.

Results: Alignment emerged across seven domains: organizational mission and values; academic and learning environments; faculty preparedness and support; resources and infrastructure; social accountability; teaching, assessment, and evaluation; and learner wellness and supports. Despite thematic overlap, the construction of standards and their evidence requirements mostly precluded collaboration and unifying documents. Learner Wellness and Supports was the exception, where overlapping services, policies, and governance enabled partial reuse and coordinated reporting.

Discussion: Reducing staff and faculty burden remains elusive primarily because accreditation standards prescribe program-specific narratives and datasets. Near-term efficiencies are feasible in Learner Wellness and Supports. However, broader efficiencies linked to burden reduction will require a revision of accreditation standards and their compliance requirements so that shared evidence can be used to fulfill MD and PGME accreditation processes.

Laura Leigh Murgaski (Lead Author), Yasmine Ishmael (Co Author), Kim O'Hearn (Co Author), Nina Chana (Co Author), Emma O'Neil (Co Author), Linda Probyn (Co Author)

Methods: A 6-month pilot project was conducted with 27 program administrators participating as observers on internal review teams. 2 focus groups were held with participants to discuss their experience.

Results: Many participants described that they benefited not only from learning about accreditation processes and the standards, but also gained new ideas for managing their program, were a helpful member of the review team, and were able to build connections and share materials across programs. Participants in the pilot would overwhelmingly recommend the experience and suggested the optimal timing is 6 months prior to one's own review. Participants also noted the importance of ensuring that the observer's role is clearly defined to the review team chair so that they are not expected to be note takers.

Discussion: Despite the full day time commitment and the general level of fatigue associated with accreditation, feedback was overwhelmingly positive. Similar opportunities for administrators may be well received if expanded upon nationally.

Brad MacCosham (Lead Author), Amy Nakajima (Co Author), K. Jean Chen (Co Author)

Methods: This study thematically analyzed accreditation elements published by the CACMS for medical schools undergoing accreditation evaluations in 2026-2027. The analysis was conducted from the perspective of a humanistic lens, grounded in the belief that medical education is not only about technical training, but also about shaping moral and relational capacities. This orientation reflects the researchers' academic interests in health humanities and backgrounds in medical education, acknowledging that such a lens emphasizes humanistic values, dignity, equity and compassion, as central to interpreting CACMS accreditation elements. Data Sources and Analysis Source material consisted of the 2026-2027 Accreditation Standards and Elements as defined by CACMS (2025), which are made publicly available on their website, including their descriptions and explanatory notes. All 96 elements spanning over standards on governance, admissions, curriculum, student support and institutional accountability, among components, were examined in full to capture both structural requirements and their humanistic implications. Steps for thematic analysis proposed by Grbich (2013) and Creswell (2017) were used. The first step consisted of becoming familiar with the data by reading and re-reading it (i.e., Repeated reading of CACMS elements to identify initial impressions of humanistic content). The second step consisted of initial coding by underlining key information segments and writing descriptive comments within each element (i.e., Assigning descriptive codes such as "equity," "curriculum," "cultural safety," "safety"). The third step consisted of matching and grouping segments of data into themes (i.e., Grouping codes into broader thematic categories reflecting dimensions of medical humanities). This step also involved iteratively refining themes to ensure they reduce redundancies between accreditation elements. The fourth step consisted of linking segments to overarching labels (i.e., Finalizing the three overarching themes of Individual humanity and institutional integrity, Humanity in medical knowledge and competence and Broader humanity in medicine) and identifying sub-groups. The fifth step consisted of contextualizing each theme within a medical humanities lens to highlight alignment between CACMS accreditation standards and broader humanistic traditions.

Results: Analysis of CACMS accreditation elements revealed three overarching themes linking medical education to humanity. Theme 1: Individual Humanity and Institutional Integrity - Elements emphasized safe, respectful learning environments, policies against mistreatment and discrimination, and systems for student well-being, health, and advising. At the institutional level, strategic planning, governance structures, resource stewardship, and faculty engagement framed human dignity and justice as structural requirements of medical education. Theme 2: Humanity in Medical Knowledge and Competence - Admissions policies encouraged broad preparation in the humanities and social sciences, while curricula integrated biomedical, behavioral, and social sciences with ethics, communication, and cultural safety. Assessment was positioned as an ethical responsibility, emphasizing fairness, formative feedback, and narrative evaluation. These provisions linked scientific rigor with moral and humanistic practice. Theme 3: Broader Humanity in Medicine - CACMS standards embedded social accountability and diversity as structural obligations. Schools were required to demonstrate responsiveness to community needs, incorporate service-learning, and address determinants of health, systemic racism, and Indigenous rights. Interprofessional collaboration reinforced physicians' role as advocates and partners in advancing equity and justice. Together, these themes demonstrate that CACMS standards extend beyond compliance to affirm medicine's ethical commitments. Accreditation emerges not only as a mechanism of accountability but as a framework for cultivating physicians as stewards of humanity within institutions and society.

Discussion: Our analysis demonstrates that CACMS (2025) accreditation elements, situated within its twelve standards, embed humanity into medical education through three interrelated domains, namely, learner wellbeing, integrity in governance and broader humanity in medicine. Together, these themes shift accreditation from a compliance exercise to a framework for shaping the values, culture and outcomes of medical training. Elements addressing mistreatment, mental health and fair assessment affirm that medical education must cultivate resilience, integrity and personal growth alongside technical competence. Schools are required to build systems that monitor wellbeing, provide meaningful feedback and ensure assessments support professional identity development rather than erode it. Embedding these principles into the learning environment signals that care for medical students is not peripheral, but foundational to their ability to care for patients in the future. Accreditation also frames governance as an educational responsibility. Policies on resource allocation, transparency and inclusiveness model the same professionalism and justice expected of future physicians. Schools are called to involve faculty and students in decision-making, to protect against discrimination and mistreatment and to demonstrate institutional integrity through leadership structures. In this sense, medical schools govern curricula, teaching lessons about accountability, fairness and ethical leadership, among other qualities (CanMEDS, 2025). By embedding social accountability, diversity and cultural safety into accreditation, CACMS affirms that medicine's responsibility extends beyond individual patient care to the health of communities and society at large. Curricula must seamlessly integrate humanities, ethics and behavioural and social sciences with biomedical science, ensuring graduates are scientifically competent while also prepared to navigate cultural diversity, social determinants of health and systemic inequities patients within their community's experience. These elements therefore demand a shift from treating the humanities as an additional elective, or sub-component of curricula to putting it at the forefront, embedding it as a guiding lens for institutional decision-making, curriculum design and individual development.

Shujiao Wang (Lead Author), Nessrine Sabri (Co Author), Tracy Smith (Co Author), Melanie Mondou (Co Author)

Methods: Through a series of consultations with program leadership, course directors, assessment team, and accessibility services, we developed a stepwise, transparent framework for OSCE accommodations. These discussions led to standardized timelines, documentation requirements, accommodation types (e.g., extended time, modified materials), and communication protocols with faculty and exam staff. Both faculty (including assessors) and students were introduced to the new process through training sessions, and a clear student-facing guide was created to promote transparency, consistency, and trust.

Results: Although formal evaluation data are not yet available, early feedback from students and faculty suggests the unified process has reduced confusion, improved fairness, and streamlined coordination. The framework is being piloted for upcoming OSCEs, with a formal evaluation planned to assess stakeholder satisfaction, feasibility, and impact on exam integrity.

Discussion: A standardized process for OSCE accommodations benefits students, faculty, and programs by improving transparency, consistency, and fairness in high-stakes assessments. Clear timelines and communication reduce logistical challenges while maintaining exam integrity. As the number of students requiring accommodations grows, our early lessons-emphasizing proactive planning and collaboration-offer a model for other institutions seeking equitable and scalable solutions.

Liv Ivins (Lead Author), Andrew Deweyert (Co Author), Lisa Sherpherd (Co Author)

Methods: To address alignment, data was collected from 22 MCQ assessments administered over one academic year at Schulich School of Medicine & Dentistry, comprising a total of 1,115 unique items. Using Bloom's Taxonomy and Miller's Pyramid, each item was classified as either knowledge-recall (K1) or application (K2) to determine cognitive level. Psychometric data was extracted to evaluate item and student performance.

Results: Of the 1,115 items analyzed, 51.8% were K1 and 48.43% were K2 in year one; 39.1% were K1 and 60.9% were K2 in year two; and 34.4% were K1 and 67.4% were K2 in year three. Analysis revealed no statistical differences in cognitive level of items between years. A two-way ANOVA revealed no differences in item difficulty within pre-clerkship years but found statistical differences (p <0.0001) between pre-clerkship and clerkship years.

Discussion: The lack of significant differences in cognitive level across the three years suggests cognitive equivalence, reflecting a missed opportunity to scaffold students toward higher-order skills. While item difficulty did not significantly differ within pre-clerkship years, there was a statistically significant increase between pre-clerkship and clerkship, confirming a greater challenge at that stage. Further investigations will include potential item-writing flaws to see if their presence contributes to these findings

Dana Arafeh (Lead Author), Melanie Henry (Lead Author), Noor Ramji (Lead Author)

Methods: An evaluation survey, adapted from the validated PPEET and PIERS tools, was shared with patient partners (n = 56) and faculty (n = 7). Twenty-three statements were rated on a 5-point Likert scale across four domains: (1) communication and support, (2) sharing views and perspectives, (3) impacts and influence, and (4) overall satisfaction. Open-ended questions were analyzed thematically.

Results: Overall, 91% were satisfied with their engagement, 80% felt they made an impact, 87% agreed power was shared, and 86% felt supported. Ahmad Hassan (Lead Author), Joshua Seto (Co Author), Lea Harper (Co Author), Omar El Ferri (Co Author), Kareem Sadek (Co Author), Holly Hnatiuk (Co Author), Lisa Yeo (Co Author)

Methods: Cross-sectional student survey aligned with CEL Learning Objectives and CanMEDS Framework, including 5-point Likert scales for satisfaction (Reaction), perceived competency gains (Learning), transfer to coursework/clinical contexts (Behaviour), and perceived population/organization impact (Results). Forced-choice items map placements to themes; covariates included supervision quality and prior experience. Within-theme and between-theme comparisons are completed, as well as regression to identify predictors, and reflexive thematic analysis of open-ended responses.

Results: Respondents represent diverse exposures, with placements spanning multiple organizational themes (most frequent: Housing/Homelessness, Mental Health & Addiction, Disability & Accessibility). Overall student satisfaction and connection to served populations are positive. Students report increased comfort and empathy with populations served and improved understanding of community resources. Perceived support from community organizations may be stronger than from the medical school, with clarity of structure/time as targets for improvement. Partner-level contrasts are most pronounced for Reaction and Learning, with Behaviour and differences attenuated by supervision quality and support.

Discussion: A community partner taxonomy elucidates where community-based learning yields the strongest educational and community impacts, contextualizing the student experience. Findings provide insight into targeted improvements to student-partner matching, orientation, and supervision, supporting transferable, evidence-informed curricular design and community partnership-building across Canadian undergraduate medical education programs.

Othmane Zekraoui (Lead Author), Rabah Achab (Co Author), Farah Alem (Co Author), Mohamed Chouikh (Co Author), Bilal Amani (Co Author), Naeem Bhojani (Co Author), Ahmed Moussa (Co Author)

Methods: Heartful Visits is an on-campus student-led coordination hub facilitating volunteering. Seven medical students led awareness campaigns, managed registrations, and coordinated placements in collaboration with the volunteer department heads of four Quebec regional networks. Eligible volunteers included students from all faculties. Participants who completed three months of volunteering by April 2025 completed an online survey evaluating the registration process and self-reported growth in empathy, communication, leadership and teamwork.

Results: Among 28 students who completed three months of volunteering, 13 completed the survey. For onboarding, 92% rated registration "easy" or "very easy". Regarding empathy, 77% reported the experience helped them in building trust with vulnerable patients and understanding their perspectives, while 69% felt it improved their adaptation to patients' emotional needs. For communication, 85% improved active-listening skills and 77% felt more comfortable engaging with diverse backgrounds. Leadership and teamwork outcomes were mixed: 54% reported opportunities where they could take initiative, while only 38% reported improved collaborative skills.

Discussion: These preliminary findings suggest three months of volunteer visits improved students' self-reported empathy and communication, with modest effects on leadership and teamwork. A key limitation is the lack of formal patient satisfaction data. Replicating our model may improve patient care and students' interpersonal skills.

Asil El Galad (Lead Author), Matthew Sibbald (Co Author), Renate Kahlke (Co Author), Sharon Bal (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a qualitative study grounded in reflexive thematic analysis and informed by flexibility theory. Data sources included institutional elective policies, online resources, and semi-structured interviews with students, faculty and administrators at one Canadian medical school. Iterative cycles of analysis and inductive coding guided theme development.

Results: Three key tensions framed elective choice: (1) Structure vs Flexibility - learners experienced limited elective choice despite formal messaging of freedom; (2) Mixed Messaging - students struggled to reconcile mixed messages from peers, administrators, and institutional policy; (3) Strategy vs Interest - electives were often curated based on strategic signalling within CaRMS at the expense of interest. Participants highlighted inequities in decoding the hidden curriculum, raising concerns about fairness.

Discussion: Our findings illuminate the hidden curriculum embedded in elective selection and the inequities it creates. Targeted mentorship, clearer CaRMS guidance, and structural reforms are needed to ensure electives support authentic learning, informed strategy, and equitable access for all students.

Eric Chan (Lead Author), Katarina Padilla (Co Author), Ife Owojori (Co Author), Miaesha Khairul (Co Author), Marissa Hsu (Co Author), Alison Darnley (Co Author), Iliana Ortega (Co Author), Mara Grunau (Co Author), Rachel Ellaway (Co Author), Aliya Kassam (Co Author)

Methods: This mixed-methods study involved a literature review and qualitative interviews of physicians and persons with lived experience (PWLE) to examine what works in suicide intervention training for physicians. MEDLINE, Embase, APA PsychINFO, and JBI EBP databases were searched for existing articles on suicide intervention training delivered to physicians. Data from articles identified for inclusion were used to describe the evidence for specific approaches to suicide training for physicians. Physicians in the relevant specialties and PWLEs were recruited to participate in interviews exploring what is important and/or effective in physician suicide training.

Results: We screened 8880 titles and abstracts and identified 122 full texts for inclusion. We interviewed 23 physicians (13 family medicine, 3 emergency medicine, 3 pediatrics, and 4 dual-specialty), 17 PWLEs, and 2 support persons. Findings in the literature generally demonstrated improvements in attitudes, confidence, and skills post-training, with mixed findings on the impact of family physician suicide training on suicide rates in the region. Emerging themes from interviews include content on engaging family/supports, approaches to chronic/frequent suicidality, and the involvement of PWLE in training delivery.

Discussion: Our findings inform the development of future suicide intervention training programs, with evidence from the literature and perspectives obtained directly from physicians and PWLE.
