Shengjia (Lily) Zhong (Lead Author), Kristina Lisk (Co Author), Dominique Hueniken (Co Author)

Methods: Undergraduate students at the University of Toronto with no prior university-level musculoskeletal anatomy training were quasi-randomized to either the VR (n=12) or PM (n=12) group. Vz and WM were assessed using a Mental Rotation Test and Stroop Test, respectively. On day one, participants studied anterior hip anatomy and completed a bell-ringer knowledge test; one week later, they repeated the process with gluteal anatomy.

Results: The PM group performed better than the VR group regardless of timepoint (p<0.05). At initial exposure, performance was primarily influenced by WM and Vz. Notably, in the VR group, Vz exerted a stronger influence on outcomes when WM was weak (β =-0.78, p=0.026). By the second exposure, these effects were no longer significant.

Discussion: These findings suggest that WM may modulate the effect of Vz in VR-based anatomy learning; their impact on performance can be reduced through repeated exposures.

Ge Shi (Lead Author), Regina Leung (Co Author), Adela Gottardi (Co Author), Thanansayan Dhivagaran (Co Author), Christina Lim (Co Author), Matthew Van Oirschot (Co Author), Aaron Grant (Co Author), Sarah Knowles (Co Author), Debra Bartley (Co Author), Sayra Cristiancho (Co Author), Julie Ann Van Koughnett (Co Author)

Methods: Participants were volunteer third-year medical students at a single center, prior to their 8-week surgical rotation. They were randomized to either a control group, which followed the standard curriculum of in-person orientation and online video, or the HoloHands group, which received a HoloLens orientation and a device for independent use during their rotation. Both groups completed pre- and post-rotation theoretical and practical assessments. Scores were compared and qualitative feedback was analyzed.

Results: Twenty-eight students participated and baseline demographics were not significantly different. The HoloHands group demonstrated greater improvement in practical scores (p=0.045), while theoretical scores were comparable (p=0.12). The HoloHands group rated the app positively regarding ease of use, enjoyment, and effectiveness. Reported strengths of the HoloLens method included increased confidence, immersion, and interactivity, while challenges included technical difficulties and lack of direct feedback.

Discussion: This study demonstrates that MR can improve hand-tying performance compared to the standard curriculum. HoloHands was rated enjoyable and effective, supporting its role in strengthening surgical skills education and addressing both learner needs and system pressures.

Chirag Chopra (Lead Author), Souzan Yacob (Co Author), Bonnie Yang (Co Author), Nicholas Laflamme (Co Author), Eleftheria Laios (Co Author), Wiley Chung (Co Author)

Methods: We developed an MR surgical simulation framework using controller-free, markerless hand tracking and co-located physical task trainers to support natural precision grips and passive haptic feedback. The system integrates virtual surgical instrumentation and anatomical overlays with a custom analytics engine that captures high-frequency performance telemetry, including tool orientation, incision trajectory, simulated tissue interaction forces, and six-degree-of-freedom wrist kinematics. Medical learners will complete structured training modules focused on instrument handling, procedural execution, and ergonomic safety during simulated surgical tasks. Cognitive workload will be assessed using the NASA Task Load Index (NASA-TLX), alongside simulator-derived technical and ergonomic performance metrics. Skill transfer will be evaluated using subsequent skills-based assessments.

Results: This study will examine the feasibility of using MR-derived analytics to characterize technical performance and ergonomic risk during surgical skill acquisition. We hypothesize that the system will enable identification of common technical errors and hazardous wrist postures while maintaining acceptable cognitive workload.

Discussion: By combining immersive surgical simulation with objective technical and ergonomic analytics, this MR framework extends beyond traditional skills training approaches. Such systems may support safer skill acquisition, early ergonomic awareness, and scalable surgical skills preparation across stages of medical training.

Maximiliano Contreras-Juarez (Lead Author), Ivan A.D Medrano-Robledo (Co Author), Jesús Javier Von-Maldonado (Co Author), Grecia Betsabe Talamantes-Jimenez (Lead Author), Mariapaula López-Elenes (Co Author)

Methods: We developed the IRA project as a virtual competition, aimed at fostering the complete development of research protocols from scratch. A call for participation was sent via email to AMMEF members. Medical students from different states across Mexico were organized into teams, and over the course of one month, they independently generated research ideas with an advocacy component and transformed them into full research protocols. The final protocols were then presented and assessed by a jury of experienced researchers.

Results: A total of 56 medical students participated, 58.9% were women, and 41.1% men. They were organized into 8 teams of 7 members each. Eight research proposals were developed as complete protocols with methodological validity and potential for nationwide implementation. One winning proposal was selected to receive AMMEF support for the full execution of the research.

Discussion: The development of the research proposals demonstrates that fostering student scientific production through collaborative strategies is feasible. Moreover, the project contributed to strengthening the scientific culture, encouraging inter-institutional collaboration, and fostering the development of research competencies, ultimately supporting increased student participation in scientific production in Mexico.

Natalie Buu (Lead Author), Renée Stalmeijer (Co Author)

Methods: We employed a constructivist worldview and descriptive qualitative approach, using semi-structured interviews and reflexive thematic analysis. This approach and analysis were guided by Billett's concept of guidance and Wenger-Traynor and Wenger-Traynor's Landscapes of Healthcare Practice. We examined how PGME trainees perceive OHCP's guidance in the perioperative setting, and how they would improve interprofessional WPL in this challenging learning environment.

Results: Twelve interviews (surgery and anesthesia residents) have been conducted. Interprofessional guidance was described as mostly informal, helpful for clinical decision-making, and for navigating the environment. Surprisingly, the perioperative setting may foster OHCP guidance more than other clinical environments. Interprofessional WPL was facilitated by good relations but hindered by hierarchical barriers. Importantly, effective interprofessional WPL demands better mutual understanding of roles.

Discussion: This work contributes to a better understanding of interprofessional WPL and guidance within fluid teams in the perioperative setting. This has not been previously described. Future research could explore factors that could better encourage mutual understanding of roles for trainees. This may foster greater and more explicit engagement of OHCP guidance and improved interprofessional WPL.

Arielle Levy (Lead Author), Ilian Cruz-Panesso (Co Author), Louise-Andrée Brien (Co Author), Patrick Lavoie (Co Author), Yvonne Khamla (Co Author), Claude Mailhot (Co Author), Mona Ziadi (Co Author)

Methods: D'une durée de trois heures, la simulation s'articulait autour de trois scénarios consécutifs de téléconsultation visant le suivi clinique d'une personne avec, à tour de rôle, son pharmacien communautaire, son infirmière pivot en oncologie et son médecin de famille. Avant chaque scénario, une préparation en sous-groupes permettait aux étudiants de partager leur perception du rôle du prochain professionnel rencontré par le patient. Un débriefing commun clôturait l'activité. L'ensemble de l'activité a été enregistré et une analyse de contenu du débriefing a été effectuée.

Results: Seize étudiants volontaires (8 pharmacie, 4 sciences inf. et 4 médecine) ont participé au projet. Cette simulation a permis aux étudiants de recadrer le rôle du pharmacien de prescripteur à collaborateur, de découvrir le rôle méconnu de l'infirmière pivot et de mieux comprendre le rôle du médecin de famille dans la coordination des soins et le soutien psychosocial. Les scénarios ont aussi amené les étudiants à constater concrètement comment se traduisait la continuité des soins dans la perspective de la personne soignée.

Discussion: Au-delà de la clarification des rôles, l'utilisation du Modèle de la fenêtre IP impliquant trois professions de la santé, présente un fort potentiel pour favoriser la prise de conscience et le développement de compétences interprofessionnelles, tout en renforçant l'adoption de pratiques collaboratives dans une approche commune de soins centrés sur la personne.

Lisa Meeks (Lead Author), Mary Montgomery (Co Author), Elizabeth Petersen (Co Author), Neera Jain (Co Author), Temple Ratcliffe (Co Author), Jennifer Pascoe (Co Author), Mai Mahmoud (Co Author), Michael Kisielewski (Co Author), Nora Osman (Co Author)

Methods: We analyzed the 2024 national Clerkship Directors in Internal Medicine (CDIM) survey section on MSWD. One designated IM CD per LCME-accredited U.S. medical school was invited. Items addressed disability awareness, communication pathways, accommodation practices, barriers, bias monitoring, and faculty development. We used descriptive statistics and Fisher's exact or Pearson's χ2 tests (α=0.05). Free-text responses were analyzed using inductive content analysis

Results: Responses were received from 113/141 schools (80.1%). Most CDs (94%) reported having MSWD on the clerkship. CDs most commonly learned of disability status from students directly (prior to clerkship 57%; during clerkship 52%); fewer were informed by disability offices (49%). Formal accommodations were frequent for testing (94%) and scheduling (81%), but less common for physical environment or assistive-technology supports. Among CDs who were consulted, only 28% were engaged before accommodation determinations; 51% reported not being consulted. Perceived preparedness was mixed (very/somewhat prepared 65%). Only 27% reported monitoring for disability-related bias in assessment. Barriers were widespread (63%), including monitoring/logistical challenges (33%), insufficient resources/infrastructure (26%), unclear recommendations (21%), and limited faculty expertise (17%). Forty-one percent reported no faculty development on supporting MSWD; few institutions trained clinical supervisors.

Discussion: MSWD are routinely present on IM clerkships, yet implementation gaps persist. Earlier CD involvement in the interactive process, clearer bidirectional communication with disability offices, dedicated resources, systematic bias monitoring, targeted faculty development, and access to specialized medical-school DRP expertise are actionable steps to strengthen equitable clinical training.

Stephanie Jiang (Lead Author), Melanie Hammond (Co Author), Carol-Anne Moulton (Co Author)

Methods: This qualitative study used constructivist grounded theory to explore how retiring surgeons experienced identity transformation. Semi-structured interviews were conducted with 16 surgeons across retirement stages. Data analysis was guided by Identity Process Theory and Walkerdine's sociocultural theory.

Results: Retirement was described as "passing through a threshold where there's no turning back" (P5). Surgeons characterized this process of "identity crossing" through four stages: 1) Approaching the threshold: increasing awareness of aging, institutional pressures, or desire for departure. 2) Unravelling: leaving clinical practice and the surgical community behind; a highly emotional stage as the surgical identity is challenged. 3) Crossing the threshold: the transformation zone where participants engaged in identity accommodation or assimilation, reflecting the tension of identity crossing. 4) Emerging beyond: participants diverged in outcomes; some experienced retirement as a "sunset" - period of loss - or a "sunrise" - opportunity to explore new horizons and re-imagine the self.

Discussion: Retirement in surgeons is a multidimensional experience of "identity crossing", requiring transformation on personal, social, and political levels. While this transition can be destabilizing, it can also be a space for reinvention. Through logistical planning (e.g. early retirement preparation), and philosophical orientation (e.g. development of value systems and identities independent of surgery), the narrative of retirement can be re-written from one of decline to one of possibility.

Meera Anand (Lead Author), Heather Buckley (Co Author)

Methods: Using an end-user approach to design, we modified the TTT model to create a program that reduces the "training" and time requirements of local faculty leads. We developed an asynchronous e-learning program and a guide with scenarios to support local discussions. Dubbed a "support the porter" (STP) model, we supported one lead in the community to be our 'porter' to carry information bidirectionally from the central program and the learning group in the community.

Results: Faculty valued the learning and discussions. Aggregate end of program evaluations across 3 years demonstrated that 92% of the respondents felt the learning program content enhanced their learning. Faculty also increased their network of peers who they can talk to about their teaching.

Discussion: It remains important to preserve opportunities for social learning among DME faculty while ensuring educational resources are utilized and available. Incorporating centrally created asynchronous resources with discussion supports for local leads reduces the "training" work of local lead faculty while still allowing for robust learning to take place.

Kristen Shaw (Lead Author), Tara Baron (Lead Author)

Methods: We developed the Clinician Teacher Certificate (CTC), a hybrid program designed to enhance clinical teaching skills. The CTC combines asynchronous e-learning with synchronous workshops. Participants complete seven courses (workshops and e-learning) over two years. Content, informed by a comprehensive needs assessment, addresses orienting learners, providing feedback, supporting struggling learners, and more. To improve accessibility, a parallel Train-the-Trainer initiative prepares faculty across Northern Ontario to deliver workshops locally.

Results: The CTC launched in spring 2025. 117 faculty members from 28 communities have enrolled; 26% are within their first five years of practice. 43% have completed at least one e-learning module. In-person workshops are being delivered in 4 communities, with parallel Train-the-Trainer workshops in 3 communities. We have successfully trained 13 facilitators. Through collaboration with Faculty Affairs, completion of the CTC will be a requirement for promotion from Lecturer to Assistant Professor at NOSM U.

Discussion: The CTC demonstrates a scalable model for faculty development, strengthening the teaching capacity of faculty dispersed across a vast geographic region. Blending online and local delivery with Train-the-Trainer support increases access to faculty development that is responsive to rural, remote, and distributed campuses.

Christine De Maria (Lead Author), Henriette Fortin (Lead Author)

Methods: Nous avons analysé les données de performance en 2024-2025 du aEFP remanié, comparant les indicateurs 12 mois pré-post implantation. Les sondages, types de pratique et portée géographique ont été mesurés.

Keisha Mushianaa (Co Author), Herbert Brill (Lead Author)

Methods: We completed five stages of the Arskey & O'Malley framework, addressing the following questions: 1) How do medical educators and curriculum decision-makers in American and Canadian medical schools integrate EDI education into clinical clerkship? 2) What are the perceived barriers and facilitators of this integration? We searched MEDLINE, ERIC, PubMed, and institutional sources for: Med*Edu*, EDI, Cultur*sensiti*, Clerk* and others. We summarized data through simple descriptive statistics and quantitative grouping of themes.

Results: We identified 40 studies. One (2.5%) implemented clerkship EDI education-an applied mentorship program. 32.5% targeted residency, 20% pre-clinical phases, and 25% described longitudinal or unspecified integration. EDI topics addressed cultural humility/competence (38.6%), general EDI principles (25%), and anti-racism (15.9%). We analyzed five thematic pillars and elucidated barriers and facilitators: Foundational resources (institutional constraints vs expert input), Institutional and faculty perspectives (faculty training vs experiential learning), Proposed frameworks (absent standardization vs dedicated instruction time), Concrete frameworks (learner engagement), and Evaluation tools (absent validated evaluation).

Discussion: There is a paucity of published literature on implementing EDI concepts during clerkship. Barriers make it challenging to move from principles and learning objectives to practical application, thus preventing evaluation of effectiveness. We will bridge these gaps in future research.

Jessica Mitchell (Lead Author), Lise Mogensen (Lead Author), Brahmaputra (Brahm) Marjadi (Co Author), Jessica Smith (Co Author)

Methods: All students in the undergraduate medical program (Years 1-5) were invited to complete an anonymous online survey. A concurrent mixed-methods approach informed survey design and analysis of fixed and free-text responses. Quantitative data were analysed descriptively, while an inductive thematic analysis explored student views on and reasons for seeking flexibility.

Results: Of 819 MD students, 308 (18%) survey respondents included working students (71%; 201/283), carers (20%; 56/281), and students with disability/adjusted study plans (13%; 35/279). Of this sample 92% (151/164) would choose a flexible attendance option if offered, with the most selected reasons being work commitments (24%), mental health/well-being (19%), and financial hardship (14%). The analysis of free text identified six themes: Respect for time and independence; Rigidity, Inconsistency and inconsideration; The hidden agenda of flexibility; Clear and considerate policies; Curriculum adaptations for flexibility; and Workload and staffing pressures limiting flexibility.

Discussion: Medical students' desire for flexibility to preserve well-being was evident in this study and reflects the well-known, considerable curriculum overload in most medical programs. Structural curriculum changes that enable diverse student populations more time for employment, study, personal responsibilities, and self-care should be prioritised to support student success.

Muriel Slim (Lead Author), Kana Halić Kordić (Lead Author)

Methods: In 2021, IFMSA launched an online campaign on EDI in ME, featuring webinars and student testimonials. In 2024, a cross-regional survey assessed students' perceptions of EDI across 5 regions, collecting responses from 68 students in over 30 countries. In addition, IFMSA National Member Organizations (NMOs) reported on local initiatives promoting EDI and evaluated their impact.

Mala Joneja (Lead Author), Rukevwe Inikori (Co Author), Imogen den Otter-Moore (Co Author)

Methods: We collected publications related specifically to the inclusion of race in clinical case presentations. These were critically analyzed from an anti-racist pedagogical perspective.

Results: Twenty-eight publications were identified from 1990 to 2022. From an anti-racist pedagogical critical perspective, three key potential areas for change were identified: (1) a limited focus on scientific racism, (2) a lack of mention of pedagogical risk, and (3) a limited reference to the use of race in board-style exam questions despite the significant role these high-stakes assessments in driving learning.

Discussion: The conversation regarding the inclusion of race in clinical case presentations is ongoing. An anti-racist pedagogical approach to this issue would recommend we: (1) reframe race as a social determinant and acknowledge its impact on health through systemic racism and social context; (2) consider the risk to the learner as a participant and clinician in medical education; (3) acknowledge all areas of education, including high-stakes assessment, as areas for anti-racist interventions (4) consider alternatives to race such as genetic ancestry or racialized experience and (5) employ innovative pedagogical strategies such as teaching medical students about the history of race.

Conrad Tsang (Lead Author), Aishwaria Pathmanesan (Co Author)

Methods: We retrieved all HPE medRxiv preprints (n=475), linked to their peer-reviewed publications. We used Bayesian Cox proportional hazards models to estimate total time-to-publication. We then constructed stochastic multi-state models to focus on the duration of peer review and for generalizable predictive results.

Results: Our models predict approximately 30% probability that a preprint will be published within 1 year, and 50% within 2 years. LMIC studies took significantly longer to be published (Hazard Ratio (HR) 0.65, 95% credible interval: 0.42-0.96). However, the total time-to-publication did not depend on receiving research funding, genders of the first and last authors, number of authors, or submission during the COVID-19 pandemic. LMIC studies faced significantly longer peer review time (HR 0.65, 95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.43-0.99), whereas research funding was associated with shorter peer review (HR 1.35, 95% CI: 1.01-1.79).

Discussion: HPE research from LMIC takes 54% longer to be published. Research funding predicts 35% shorter peer review but not reduced total time-to-publication, suggesting delays are from rejection-reformatting-resubmission cycles. Our findings contextualize academic metrics for LMIC researchers.

Patricia Gerber (Lead Author) James McCormack (Co Author)

Methods: Official exam blueprints, grading criteria, and key skills informed large-language-model prompt design. Flex PharmD: an Oral Exam Simulator generates cases, provides additional patient data on request, and guides students through (1) rapid data gathering and prioritization of drug issues, (2) open-book research, and (3) verbal presentation of therapeutic-monitoring plans. PharmD SDI: custom GPTs act as interactive tutors for EBM computations (absolute/relative risk, number needed to treat, confidence intervals) promoting active engagement and critical appraisal. Iterative faculty-student testing refined accuracy, rubric alignment, and usability.

Results: User surveys showed strong engagement and self-reported gains in exam preparedness (Flex PharmD) and analytical fluency, confidence in case-management, and appraisal skills (PharmD SDI). Faculty confirmed alignment with intended outcomes and grading structures.

Discussion: These innovations illustrate how GenAI can replicate complex assessments, act as a tireless tutor, reduce workload, and provide scalable, on-demand opportunities in health professions education.

Methods: The program was conceived through a cross-border collaboration between two universities: University of Saskatchewan (Canada) and James Cook University (Australia). Tasha Wyatt (Lead Author), Emily Scarlett (Co Author), Vinayak Jain (Co Author), Tinglan Ma (Co Author)

Methods: This qualitative study recruited trainees through professional networks and snowball sampling. We conducted in-depth, two-phase interviews with 18 trainees from the U.S. and Canada, with 13 of them participating in a second interview. Data analysis used constant comparative analysis and a social movements framework, focusing on collective identity, framing processes, resource mobilization, and strategies.

Results: Despite a lack of coordination, trainees consistently narrated a reliance on a larger collective, which they actively curated to include individuals outside of medicine. From this collective, trainees gained a shared identity and a unifying "injustice frame" for understanding social harm. In return, they contributed new strategies and tactics to their colleagues. However, trainees did not receive emotional support or resource mobilization from these collectives. While some expressed a desire for more coordinated action, others felt alienated by it.

Discussion: Our findings demonstrate that uncoordinated resistance is not an isolated endeavor but is sustained by a dynamic, reciprocal relationship with a broader, self-curated collective. While this collective provides a shared identity and a steady influx of new strategies, it may not be able to offer the emotional support and resource mobilization necessary for more sustained, coordinated change.

Kyobin Hwang (Lead Author), Michael Kryshtalskyj (Co Author), Abdullah Al-Ani (Co Author), Loukman Ghouti (Co Author), Milad Modabber (Co Author), Robert Moore (Co Author), David Rabinovitch (Co Author), Marie-Claude Robert (Co Author), Saama Sabeti (Co Author), Chinmayi Vyas (Co Author), Mili Roy (Co Author)

Methods: The toolkit was developed through a national collaboration involving 29 stakeholders: ophthalmologists, fellows, residents, medical students, sustainability experts, and healthcare allies. It synthesizes global evidence illustrated by select Canadian case studies into modules on energy use, transportation, procurement, waste, anesthesia, and operating room optimization. Each module provides actionable strategies and educational resources to support implementation across clinical, institutional, and system levels.

Results: The toolkit highlights scalable interventions with proven impact. Customizing surgical packs to include only the most frequently used items reduced unopened supplies discarded by 20-30%. Transitioning to reusable gowns and drapes cut solid waste by up to 93% while maintaining sterility standards. Expanding teleophthalmology services lowered travel-related emissions by 15-20 kg of CO2 per visit in some contexts and improved rural access. At the systems level, energy-efficient retrofits at Humber River Hospital achieved a 41% improvement in efficiency compared to similar facilities.

Discussion: This initiative illustrates how specialty-driven, evidence-informed resources can embed sustainability into health professional practice and training. Broad dissemination will align education and system performance with environmental responsibility.

Chetana Kulkarni (Lead Author), Jerusha Retnakanthan (Co Author)

Jasmin Ogren (Lead Author), Shayan Shirazi (Co Author), Amanda Galambos (Co Author), Tracy Wilson (Co Author), Satchan Takaya (Co Author), Courtney Dalton (Co Author), Jackie Perrot (Co Author)

Methods: A retrospective chart review evaluated the impact of a CME intervention for ED and Sexual Assault Response Team physicians on PEP prescribing in Saskatoon EDs. A total of 625 charts met inclusion criteria: patients ≥16 years presenting following sexual assault or blood/body fluid exposure.

Results: PEP prescribing for high-risk exposures increased from 61% pre-CME to 82% post-CME (p=0.0126). In sexual assault cases, PEP prescribing increased from 38% to 73% (p=0.0054), acknowledgement of PEP during the clinical visit increased from 57% to 90% (p=0.0015), and cases where PEP was not acknowledged decreased from 43% to 8% (p=0.0005). Post-CME, 2% presented after 72 hours and were excluded from analyses. The number of unique PEP prescribers in sexual assault cases increased from 7 pre-CME to 30 post-CME.

Discussion: Targeted CME supports appropriate PEP prescribing and reduces reliance on specialist prescribers in the ED setting. High-risk prescribing improved significantly, particularly in sexual assault cases. CME strengthens HIV prevention at the point of care and improves timely access.

Qurat Ul Ain Qureshi (Lead Author), Katherine A. Collins (Co Author), Maria Cherba (Co Author), Jessica Chan (Co Author), Ishita Sharma (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a scoping review to identify studies published since 2015 that report on First Nations, Métis, and Inuit patients' and healthcare providers' perspectives on healthcare interactions. We extracted examples of communication strategies, including language use and non-verbal practices, and conducted a thematic analysis. Studies were also assessed for Indigenous involvement in research.

Results: Of 3285 records screened, 105 were included. Twenty-one papers did not specify Indigenous community involvement, but supported findings from the studies that did. Identified strategies were related to four main themes: expressing empathy and acknowledging systemic challenges; discussing traditional healing practices and holistic healthcare needs; conducting history-taking in a way to respect patients' preferences and autonomy; conveying medical information and working with Indigenous interpreters. Across themes, studies emphasize the importance of understanding each patients' unique context and avoiding generalizations, using strategies such as open questions about needs and priorities and asking permission before addressing certain topics. A few population-specific guidelines were also identified (for example, questions that healthcare providers can ask to support shared decision-making with Inuit cancer patients).

Discussion: The review highlights practical strategies that healthcare providers can implement in their practice. To conclude, the presenting medical student will share her reflections about how the results of the analysis could help identify ways to enrich medical students' communication curriculum in the Canadian context.

Adam Ali Hussain (Lead Author), Anjali Saxena (Co Author), Bev Digout (Co Author), Vanessa Hindmarsh (Co Author), Meredith McKague (Co Author), Ginger Ruddy (Co Author)

Methods: Phase I of this multi-phase study involved a comprehensive literature review using PubMed, ERIC, and institutional documents to analyze 49 articles on wellness and inclusivity integration, analytical frameworks, and teaching methodologies. Phase II (stakeholder interviews) will be presented separately. Phase III, in progress, is development and implementation of a survey to capture student perspectives on communication of PP&P, experiences with PP&P, and recommendations for supportive policy improvements.

Results: Literature analysis revealed three distinct approaches utilized at different CMS: 1) explicit integration of wellness into PP&P with non-punitive remediation, 2) emphasis on learner well-being through wellness offices and supportive environments, and 3) traditional ethical conduct definitions. Multiple analytical frameworks exist for assessing inclusivity, but wellness assessment tools remain limited. Evidence supports longitudinal, multi-method teaching incorporating role models and experiential learning. Literature review findings are informing learner survey development; results pending.

Discussion: Significant variation exists in how CMS integrate wellness and inclusivity within PP&P. While research demonstrates connections between wellness, inclusivity and professional behaviour, evidence-based strategies for policy integration remain limited. The developed survey will enable systematic collection of student perspectives to identify gaps between institutional policies and learner experiences, informing evidence-based recommendations for updating PP&P.

Shauna Hacker (Lead Author), Aryan Eftekhari (Co Author)

Methods: The session was redesigned as an interactive workshop. Residents were asked a series of questions using Wooclap, an interactive digital platform designed to enhance audience engagement, which promoted real-time reflection on professionalism. This was followed by a group activity: each group was assigned a specific CanMEDS Professionalism competency and tasked with discussing and reflecting on it, and making a creative representation of its meaning. The goal was to encourage deeper understanding through active participation and peer discussion.

Results: Residents produced four creative presentations: a spoken word poem, a dramatic re-enactment of a 1960s legal case, a human pyramid demonstrating the importance of wellness, and a skit contrasting unprofessional and professional behaviors. Word clouds generated from Wooclap responses captured diverse reflections on professionalism. Informal feedback was overwhelmingly positive. One example of anonymous feedback stated: "Laid the foundation of professional behaviour and its importance in clinical settings and patient outcomes-overall impact of professionalism on improved communication and better health advocacy." Average evaluation score improved from 3.1 in 2024 to 3.5 in 2025 with the introduction of the interactive workshop.

Discussion: Transitioning from didactic to interactive, creative methods for teaching professionalism were well received by FM residents. The session's success underscores the value of learner engagement, collaboration, and creativity in enhancing the educational experience and making professionalism relevant, memorable and, most importantly, not boring.

Sarah Triano (Lead Author), Lisa Meeks (Co Author), Matthew Sullivan (Co Author), Grace Clifford (Co Author), Rylee Betchkal (Co Author), Mytien Nguyen (Co Author)

Methods: We used a modified Delphi methodology to achieve expert consensus. Sixteen practicing specialized DRPs from diverse U.S. medical schools participated in three iterative survey rounds, rating and refining an initial list of competencies and attributes. Consensus thresholds were set at 80% for Round 1 and 90% for Rounds 2 and 3.

Results: All panelists completed all three rounds (100% response). The process resulted in eight core competencies and 111 key attributes, each reaching ≥90% consensus. Items requiring legal interpretation or graduate medical education responsibilities were excluded as out of scope, clarifying role boundaries.

Discussion: This study presents the first national framework of competencies for specialized DRPs in undergraduate medical education. The framework provides a foundation for recruitment, training, and evaluation, advancing professionalization of the role and strengthening institutional capacity to ensure equitable access for MSWDs.

Sarah Dalton (Lead Author), Jo Burnand (Lead Author)

Methods: Following a comprehensive stakeholder review, we identified priority learning needs for new HoDs and designed a four-day program. Core modules include systems leadership, high-performing teams, team wellbeing, performance and financial management, business case development, and leading difficult conversations. Iterative refinement across three years has incorporated participant feedback, including increased face-to-face time to enhance networking and peer learning.

Results: Eighty senior doctors have completed the MLMP with an average Net Promotor Score of 88.5. Evaluation demonstrates consistent positive feedback, with participants reporting increased confidence, improved understanding of system navigation and resource levers and validation that leadership challenges are widely shared. Testimonials highlight the program as engaging, relevant, and empowering. The main suggestion for improvement has been to extend the program to allow deeper exploration of selected topics.

Discussion: The MLMP addresses a critical gap in preparing doctors for leadership roles. By fostering competence, confidence, and connection, the program enhances role satisfaction, strengthens retention in HoD roles, and contributes to building sustainable, high-performing clinical teams.

Geneviève Gauthier (Lead Author), Françoise Moreau-Johnson (Co Author), Christine Landry (Co Author), Tim Dubé (Co Author), Claire Touchie (Co Author)

Methods: We adopted a qualitative case study approach guided by critical realist methodology, using curriculum-making theory and institutional entrepreneurship as sensitizing frameworks. Data collection unfolded in three sequential phases designed to progressively deepen understanding of governance mechanisms. First, a review of 42 foundational documents provided a detailed chronological framework anchoring the case. Second, exploratory interviews with early stakeholders refined this timeline and supported the development of an initial program theory. Third, in-depth interviews with 11 central stakeholders-including senior administrators, academic leaders, financial officers, senior analysts, and external funders-examined governance dynamics across institutional levels.

Results: We identified nine governance mechanisms operating across three overlapping phases: developing a vision, gaining approval, and implementation. Mechanisms such as building coalitions, navigating governance structures, and strategically leveraging accreditation functioned across micro, meso, macro, and supra levels, revealing governance as a distributed and adaptive process.

Discussion: Rather than treating curriculum primarily as a pedagogical blueprint, we argue it is an institutional endeavour shaped through multi-level negotiation and strategic agency. These findings advance theoretical understandings of curriculum governance and underscore the importance of governance literacy for educators and leaders engaged in curricular development.

Pouriya Sadeghighazichaki (Lead Author), Apameh Golkar (Co Author)

Methods: A comprehensive literature search of PubMed was conducted in May 2025, focusing on English-language articles from the past 10 years. Eligible studies examined leadership education interventions in undergraduate medical programs, targeting pre-clerkship and clerkship learners. Forty titles were screened, and 23 articles met inclusion criteria. Data extraction captured study characteristics, learner populations, intervention types, settings, frameworks used, and reported outcomes. A narrative synthesis identified recurring themes.

Results: Of the 23 studies included, quantitative (35%) and mixed-methods (30%) designs predominated, with additional qualitative and commentary-based approaches. Most studies originated from the United States (43%), though international representation was noted. Leadership interventions varied, with workshops and simulations (43%) most common, alongside structured curricula (22%) and extracurricular programs (22%). Target populations ranged across pre-clerkship (30%), clerkship (22%), and combined cohorts (26%). Outcomes demonstrated improvements in leadership skills, confidence, identity development, and sustained behavioral changes. Frameworks such as CanMEDS, the Medical Leadership Competency Framework, and Leadership Identity Development model were variably applied.

Discussion: Leadership education in undergraduate medicine is fragmented, with no consensus on optimal models. Evidence supports systematic, competency-based integration emphasizing experiential, reflective, and inter-professional learning. Embedding leadership training early and longitudinally within curricula is essential to prepare future physicians for the evolving demands of healthcare systems.

Eusang Ahn (Lead Author), Jason Frank (Co Author), Kori LaDonna (Co Author)

Methods: Using constructivist grounded theory, we conducted semi-structured interviews with 12 HPE stakeholders (e.g., clinician educators, researchers, trainees). Participants were purposefully and theoretically sampled across roles and geographies. Data were collected and analyzed iteratively in three stages-initial, focused, and theoretical coding-supported by constant comparative analysis. Theme generation was informed by sensitizing concepts from social network theory and refined through reflexive team discussions.

Results: Participants initially struggled to articulate brokering, often conflating it with mentorship and sponsorship. However, through reflection, they identified brokering as a powerful, intentional, and relational act that influences professional identity formation, access to opportunity, and equity. Trust, reputation, and social capital were seen as prerequisites; risks included gatekeeping by omission, perpetuation of privilege, and reputational loss.

Discussion: Brokering is a fragile yet powerful force in HPE. When done intentionally, it can disrupt inequities and foster inclusion; when left implicit, it risks reinforcing the status quo. Recognizing and teaching brokering may be essential to building a more equitable and collaborative academic culture in HPE.

Lorenzo Madrazo (Lead Author), Kori LaDonna (Co Author), Susan Humphrey-Murto (Co Author), Saada Hussen (Co Author), Katherine Scowcroft (Co Author), Jacqueline Torti (Co Author)

Methods: Guided by constructivist grounded theory, we conducted semi-structured interviews with 26 resident leaders from multiple specialties and institutions. Transcripts were analyzed iteratively using Charmaz's three-stage approach to generate a framework on resident inclusion.

Results: Resident leaders often had prior leadership experience, were personally motivated, and prioritized which issues to advance. They described a spectrum of experiences, from feeling dismissed, to tokenistically included, to authentically heard. This spectrum of inclusion unfolded at three levels: articulation of resident concerns, legitimization by faculty, and realization through tangible change. Faculty--especially program directors--were pivotal in determining whether resident input progressed through these stages. System-level features, including program culture, committee structure, and the specific issue raised, further shaped responsiveness to resident input. Transparent communication, the presence of faculty champions, and the cultivation of trusting relationships were particularly critical in ensuring resident involvement was experienced as meaningful rather than symbolic.

Discussion: Our findings outline resident, faculty, and system-level factors that programs can act upon to make resident engagement meaningful. These build toward a framework that helps identify local areas for improvement and sets the stage for future scholarship on resident participation in leadership.

Colin Whaley (Lead Author), Jill Shah (Co Author), Deborah Ocholi (Co Author), Brandon Tang (Co Author), Seema Marwaha (Co Author), Blair Bigham (Co Author)

Methods: We employed purposive sampling to recruit 18 Canadian physicians with advocacy experience across diverse specialties and regions. Semi-structured interviews were conducted, recorded, and transcribed verbatim. We used thematic analysis and identified identified 106 codes which were organized into themes 7, which were mapped onto the socioecological model. Coding was performed independently by 2 researchers, and themes were refined through iterative team analysis.

Results: Seven key themes were identified: intrinsic motivation rooted in personal and professional experiences, the synergy between advocacy and clinical roles, the influence of peers and institutional culture, the importance of structured advocacy training and mentorship, organizational constraints, and the need for strategic approaches to advocacy. Participants described a hidden curriculum that discouraged advocacy, with institutional and reputational risks serving as significant barriers. Mentorship, visible institutional support, protected time or roles, and the inclusion of advocacy in formal curricula were seen as enablers. Many participants emphasized the importance of normalizing advocacy within medical training and practice.

Discussion: Participants described advocacy as deeply intertwined with their professional identity and clinical work, often viewing advocacy as an extension of patient care. Key motivators included personal values shaped by formative early experiences and a commitment to social justice. Enablers included strong mentorship, protected roles, and visible institutional support, which helped mitigate common barriers. Notable obstacles were unsupportive organizational cultures, reputational risks, high clinical demands, and a pervasive "hidden curriculum" that discouraged advocacy during training. Recommendations emphasized more structured teaching, institutional recognition, and support to foster ongoing advocacy engagement.

Georgina McKenna, MPH (Lead Author), Anne Mahalik, MAHSR (Co Author), Helen Mullaney, MEd (Co Author), Jillian Filliter, PhD (Co Author), Roberta Preston, PhD (Co Author), Katrina Hurley, MD (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a CARDA-informed document analysis, retrieving documents from FoM units. Included documents referenced faculty and may impact wellness. Such documents were analyzed using a reflexive thematic analysis that involved a template based on the Okanagan Charter.

Results: Of 170 documents, 88 were included. Three themes emerged: 1) faculty often had implicit responsibilities within student-facing policy, 2) faculty wellness was rarely referenced or was framed as a professional duty for patient safety, and 3) while some policies cited the value of a health-promoting environment, action to build one seemed limited.

Discussion: FoM policies often mention faculty responsibility but lack clarity or support. Framing faculty wellness as a duty rather than a human need shapes the culture of academic medicine toward the individual, reinforcing messages of servitude and stoicism. The FoM claims commitment to health-promotion but many policies ultimately prioritized duty over wellness.

Nadine Abu-Ghazaleh (Lead Author), Michael Quon (Co Author), Stephanie Quon (Co Author), Erene Stergiopoulos (Co Author), Lisa Graves (Co Author), Sydney Ruller (Co Author), Lorenzo Madrazo (Co Author)

Methods: A qualitative content analysis was conducted on the most recent, publicly available PGME accommodation policies from 13 of 17 Canadian medical schools. Policies were evaluated using a structured six-domain coding framework: leadership and governance, confidentiality and disclosure, process infrastructure, support and wellness systems, communication and transparency, and legal/regulatory compliance. Two reviewers independently coded each policy; inter-coder reliability was assessed with Cohen's kappa and intraclass correlation coefficients (ICC).

Results: Scores ranged from 6 to 11 out of 12, with a mean score of 9.1. All policies affirmed voluntary disclosure (13/13) and limited confidentiality to designated staff (13/13), and most referenced provincial human rights legislation (11/13). However, few provided detailed accommodation procedures (4/13), and disability-specific wellness supports (2/13). Inter-coder reliability was high (κ = 0.82, ICC = 0.91).

Discussion: Most policies incorporate key national recommendations but remain inconsistent in scope and clarity. Critical procedural details and wellness supports are often missing, and access to policies remains variable. Comprehensive, transparent policies are essential to ensure equity and support for residents with disabilities.

Angelyn Xie (Lead Author), Ivan Silver (Co Author), Paul Bernstein (Co Author), Phyllis Glanc (Co Author), Karen Leslie (Co Author), Umberin Najeeb (Co Author), Leslie Nickell (Co Author), Jolie Ringash (Co Author), Jacob Langer (Co Author)

Methods: In 2024-25, a series of semi-structured interviews were conducted with 7 physicians across 7 departments at the Temerty Faculty of Medicine (TFOM), University of Toronto, to assess perceived enablers and barriers to late career transitioning. Geneviève Moineau (Lead Author), Ricardo Leon-Borquez (Co Author)

Methods: The WFME undertook the development of a Declaration on Institutions' Responsibility to Support Medical Students, Physicians in Postgraduate Education and Physicians in Practice (Declaration) to guide institutions in addressing the need to support the health of learners. An expert working group consulted with members of the medical education community from around the world and recommended the Declaration that was approved by WFME. The Declaration was launched at the WFME World Conference in May 2025.

Results: Addressing wellbeing in medical education and practice requires a holistic, systems-based approach. The Declaration includes a Call to Action in the following areas: Admission and selection; Curriculum; Academic support and counselling; Career counselling; Mentorship opportunities; Coaching; Financial support; Health (including mental health) services; Accessibility and accommodation; Peer support; Transitions; Recognition. Institutions must advocate for health system change as well as ensuring that Institutional leaders, Faculty members, lecturers and staff, Designated faculty and staff and Medical students and physicians in postgraduate education and practice all contribute to a positive learning environment. Continuous quality improvement in wellbeing should be undertaken by institutions using an evidence-informed approach.

Discussion: Only through collaborative institutional commitment can we safeguard the wellbeing of those who care for our communities. By working together, we can forge a healthier future for physicians and the patients and populations in their care for generations to come.

Alexandre Petiquan (Lead Author), Brent Faught (Co Author), Mostafa Shokoohi (Co Author), Laura Wells (Co Author)

Methods: Percent agreement was assessed between open generative AI-(i.e., ChatGPT) and human-generated matches of PHPM EPAs to syllabi from two core Master of Public Health (MPH) courses: Biostatistics and Epidemiology. AI was prompted to generate an Excel-based EPA line list and align relevant objectives with course syllabi. Independently, a PHPM resident and a subject matter expert (SME) performed the same mapping. Percent agreement across all raters and between pairs was then compared.

Results: Preliminary results show AI-human agreement was relatively low: Biostatistics (7/51 = 13.7%) and Epidemiology (12/45 = 26.6%). The highest concordance (6/8 = 75%) occurred within Biostatistics for "Foundations EPA #1 - Applying qualitative and quantitative methods of data analysis and interpreting results." Human raters demonstrated stronger alignment than AI, with moderate agreement across courses (Biostatistics: 21/51 = 41.2%; Epidemiology: 21/45 = 46.7%).

Discussion: Human-to-human agreement exceeded AI comparisons, though human raters' familiarity and closeness to the courses content may have impacted results. AI shows promise as a supplementary tool for EPA-course mapping but currently lacks the reliability needed for independent application. Further refinement of AI methods is needed to support CBME curriculum mapping to ensure reproducible results between institutions.

Michelle Anizio Ribeiro (Lead Author), Chris Ramnanan (Co Author), Genevieve Gauthier (Co Author)

Methods: Guided by interpretivist epistemology-assuming meaning is co-constructed through participants' narratives and shaped by context-this study examined graduate students serving as NPTs in a Canadian cadaver-based anatomy program. Six participants were interviewed at their teaching cycle's end using semi-structured interviews prompting reflection on teaching dilemma, emotions, and identity development. Interviews were transcribed and analyzed thematically through hybrid inductive-deductive approach, informed by frameworks on teaching dilemma and teaching identity.

Results: Emotions played central roles in NPT identity development, particularly during dilemmas involving anatomical variation. When cadavers diverged from textbook norms, participants reported anxiety and credibility concerns. Most participants, rather than withdrawing, ultimately emphasized their responsibility for student learning. These moments led them to redefine "good teaching," shifting from always having correct answers to demonstrating adaptability, honesty, and commitment to learning alongside students.

Discussion: Anatomical variation, though initially unsettling, became a catalyst for teacher identity development. When normalized and supported through reflection and mentorship, such challenges foster adaptability, confidence, and authentic professional growth, benefiting both NPTs and their students while preparing future educators for real-world teaching complexities.

Methods: IM residents participated in a formative OSCE station with an AI-powered chatbot and assessment tool. Simultaneous AI tool and MD assessments were compared using percent agreement. Passing scores were generated with borderline regression. Residents and MDs were surveyed on their perceptions of AI in medical education.

Results: Assessments of 35 residents were included. Agreement between MD and AI assessments was strongest for ethical conduct (74.3% were exact matches on a 0-5 scale) and professional behaviour (54.3%). Listening skills (48.6%), questioning skills (42.9%), and rapport with patients (42.9%) demonstrated moderate agreement. Although information-giving had 28.6% agreement, means scores remained within one SD. Scaled overall performance showed moderate concordance (42.9%), with nearly identical pass marks generated by borderline regression (MD 6.7 vs AI 6.8). MD and AI checklist scores showed high agreement (>80%) for 3/7 checklist items and moderate agreement (>60%) for 3/7 items. One item had 54.3% agreement. Resident surveys indicated strong support for AI in communication training (60.5%) and openness to AI assessment (41.0%). All physician assessors (100%, n=4) supported AI for medical training, and most (75%) supported AI in trainee assessment.

Discussion: This study highlights the potential for AI-based simulation of standardized patient encounters and for learner assessment. Further work should assess validity and reliability across diverse learners and contexts.

Vie Tran (Lead Author), Christopher Ramnanan (Co Author), Nadine Wiper-Bergeron (Co Author)

Methods: Information regarding admission requirements, courses containing anatomy, and cadaveric laboratories in PA curricula were extracted from public online sources. To address remaining gaps, structured virtual interviews were conducted with anatomy leads from 5 out of 6 Canadian PA programs. Teaching approaches, assessment methods, and learning processes in cadaveric laboratories were characterized. Subsequently, inductive thematic analysis was used to identify emergent themes.

Results: revealed the general drive to adapt PA anatomy delivery after a local institution's medical school curriculum in a truncated format. An Kateri Vu (Lead Author), Hajar El-Kamouni (Co Author), Jeanne Harnois (Co Author), Jade Bernatchez-Poirier (Co Author), Mandy Malick (Co Author), Elio Kechichian (Co Author), Carolina Fernandes (Co Author)

Methods: A conceptual framework informed design and evaluation. The 150-minute competency-based workshop incorporated Team-Based Learning (TBL) and immersive clinical simulation to promote active learning. Its impact was assessed through a pre-post cohort study with a control group. Residents were evaluated at baseline, immediately post-workshop, and eight weeks later.

Results: The workshop was designed using an integrated framework combining active learning, TBL, and simulation to address residents' demonstrated, expressed, and normative learning needs. This complementary approach fostered collaborative reasoning, experiential skill development, and greater confidence in applying wound care principles to practice. Sixty-six residents in internal medicine, dermatology, and family medicine at a Quebec teaching hospital participated. Knowledge improved by +13.8% (p<0.0001) and clinical confidence by +37% (p<0.0001), both sustained at eight weeks. Perceived relevance averaged 90.7%.

Discussion: This contextualized workshop addresses an educational gap, showing the effectiveness of combining TBL and simulation to improve knowledge, confidence, and retention in wound care. Feedback suggested enhancements, including a summary of dressings, shorter sessions, and preparatory materials. Continued implementation could strengthen wound care competencies among future clinicians, while its adaptable model underscores broader applicability to medical education.

Taunia Rifai (Lead Author), Justine Callahan (Lead Author)

Tahar Ouakaa (Lead Author), Sébastien Pelletier (Lead Author), Rami Issa (Co Author), Marie-Ève Belanger (Co Author), Pierre Drolet (Co Author), Mihai Georgescu (Co Author), Sébastien Garneau (Co Author), Geneviève Lachance (Co Author), Issam Tanoubi (Co Author)

Methods: 26 anesthesia residents with no prior experience in spinal ultrasound were recruited to complete the PLM. Participants viewed 43 lumbar spine ultrasound videos recorded from patients and independently reviewed by two experts. Participants were instructed to identify the optimal moment to perform a neuraxial technique (Optimal Time Intervals OTI). In the feedback version of the PLM, participants were provided with immediate comparison between their selections and two-expert-predefined OTIs after each video (Intervention Group IG). In the control group (CG), participants completed the same task without access to expert comparison (Control Group CG). All participants completed an immediate post-test, and a one-month retention post-test, each consisting of five newly selected expert-annotated videos. The primary endpoint was binary success to match the expert-defined OTI.

Results: Immediately following the PLM, the IG demonstrated more than twice the odds of correctly identifying the OTI compared with the CG (OR=2.72, 95% CI [1.08-6.76], p=0.046). At one-month follow-up, this effect strengthened with the IG exhibiting over threefold higher odds of correct OTI identification (OR=3.44, 95% CI [1.44-8.16], p=0.005).

Discussion: PLM enriched with expert feedback significantly improved short- and long-term retention of spinal sonoanatomy among novice anesthesia residents. The sustained improvement observed at one month suggests durable learning benefits attributable to expert-guided feedback.

Michelle Cruickshank (Lead Author) Ewaoluwa Akomolafe (Co Author) Heather Gadalla (Co Author) Natasha Johnson (Co Author) Patricia Farrugia (Co Author)

Methods: This cross-sectional study surveyed 95 current undergraduate medical students at McMaster University to assess their attitudes toward Black and Indigenous facilitated admissions pathways and examine whether educational background, particularly exposure to humanities and social sciences, shapes these perceptions.

Results: Quantitative responses indicated strong support for the importance of diversity in medical education, though uncertainty remained regarding the transparency and fairness of equity pathways. Qualitative findings revealed concerns about unclear admissions standards, potential socioeconomic disparities among facilitated pathway applicants, and limited institutional communication about the purpose and implementation of the pathways. When results were categorized by educational backgrounds, students with greater exposure to humanities and social sciences were not uniformly more supportive; some expressed stronger skepticism regarding fairness and impact.

Discussion: Participants broadly acknowledged the value of equity pathways but emphasized the need for greater transparency and expanded inclusion criteria, such as attention to intersecting barriers such as socioeconomic status. These findings underscore the emphasis many students appear to place on fairness to individual applicants as opposed to the overarching goal of increasing numbers of Black and Indigenous physicians for Canadian society, and highlight the importance of pairing structural admissions changes with ongoing education and communication to build trust and improve understanding within the medical community.

Dua Hussain (Lead Author)

Methods: Data were collected through an optional anonymous survey administered to attendees after they participated in various health-promoting activities, including glucose and dental screenings, nutrition education, and information on insurance and community resource centers. Simple statistics were obtained using RedCap and analyzed using Fisher's exact test.

Results: Findings reveal disparities across Albuquerque regions. 94% of respondents from Northeast Albuquerque reported having a primary care provider compared to 17% in the Southeast (p < 0.001). Self-rated health also differed significantly, with 56% of Northeast participants reporting "very good" health compared to 58% of Southeast participants reporting only "fair" health (p < 0.001). Barriers most commonly reported included appointment availability, cost, and inability to take time off work. Many participants reported increased awareness of health resources and greater empowerment to manage their well-being.

Discussion: These findings highlight the value of health fairs in addressing systemic inequities, providing free resources in areas where healthcare providers are limited, and emphasize the importance of incorporating community feedback to inform future outreach efforts. The research provides actionable recommendations to enhance community engagement and tailor interventions that address unique needs of AANHPI and other marginalized groups.

Matey Juric (Lead Author), Dr. Anna Byszewski (Co Author), Kayla Hébert-Desnoyers (Co Author)

Methods: An anonymous electronic survey was distributed to undergraduate and postgraduate medical, PharmD, and Translational and Molecular Medicine learners at the University of Ottawa between August to September, 2025. Questions explored awareness of policies and pathways to report, prior experiences, comfort with reporting, and perceived obstacles. Quantitative data were analyzed descriptively while qualitative comments underwent thematic review.

Results: Among 102 respondents, 81% were aware of the professionalism policy, but only 55% knew how to file a report. 54% of respondents felt "somewhat or less familiar" with the policy and only 18% felt "very or extremely comfortable" reporting concerns. 39% had witnessed or experienced an incident they considered unprofessional; of these, just 12% reported it. Major barriers included fear of retaliation (80%), belief that nothing would change (70%) and concerns about anonymity (70%). Learners recommended clearer communication, easy-access online portals and transparent follow-up to build trust.

Discussion: Despite high policy awareness, significant gaps in procedural knowledge and trust persist, discouraging reporting. Findings support targeted interventions such as streamlined reporting links in learning platforms, concise flowcharts, and regular feedback on outcomes. These learner-informed strategies aim to create a safer, more transparent culture of professionalism reporting.

Ceen-Ming Tang (Lead Author)

Methods: A qualitative study design was used in this inquiry, and 10 semi-structured interviews were conducted with Foundation Doctors at Oxford University Hospitals NHS Foundation Trust over the phone. Transcripts were produced verbatim.

Results: Thematic analysis identified four themes in relation to Foundation Doctors' perception on their teaching practices. Foundation Doctors take part in a range of teaching activities, and their sense of responsibility affects perception of their role in teaching. Four main factors were identified as affecting confidence in teaching, that is support and feedback, sense of preparedness, perception of own knowledge level, and experience and training in teaching. Foundation Doctors were motivated to teach for intrinsic and extrinsic factors such as enjoyment in teaching and improving their CV respectively. Clinical workload limited the teaching activities they could provide.

Discussion: Recommendations as a result of this study include empowering Foundation Doctors to opportunistically teach at the bedside through brief clinical encounters, encouraging use of trainee-directed days, and facilitating formal teaching training.

Husnaat Bajwa (Lead Author), Huzaifa Saeed (Co Author), Adam Hussain (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a narrative review of literature published between 2000-2025 using PubMed and MedEdPORTAL. Key terms included simulation, anesthesiology, crisis management, and medical education. Articles were thematically analyzed for teaching methods, learner outcomes, and curricular integration strategies.

Results: Simulation-based training consistently improved learner confidence, teamwork, and adherence to algorithms in scenarios such as malignant hyperthermia, local anesthetic systemic toxicity, and airway crises. Several curricula embedded simulation longitudinally, enhancing retention compared to one-time sessions. Barriers included high resource requirements, faculty training, and variable assessment tools.

Discussion: Simulation-based CRM is an effective adjunct to traditional anesthesiology training, improving readiness for rare crises. Expanding access, standardizing assessments, and incorporating interprofessional components are critical next steps.

Qi Guo (Lead Author) Andrea Gotzmann (Co Author) Cecilia Alves (Co Author)

Methods: To evaluate redundancy, TDM scores were correlated with MCCQE Part I and NAC Examination scores. Guided by family physician experts and approved by the MCC Examination Oversight Committee, the exam format was revised to reduce barriers and enhance validity. Key changes included shifting from written-response to short-menu selection items, incorporating an optional 20-minute break, and creating distractors from the most common incorrect responses in prior administrations. Items underwent national review by family physicians and professional editing/translation.

Results: Correlations between TDM, MCCQE Part I, and NAC scores were low (ranging from .1 to .2), supporting unique value. Pilot items demonstrated increased reliability (.79) and strong correlation (.53) with written-response predecessors. The upcoming standard-setting exercise will be conducted using a balanced national panel of family physicians. Psychometric outcomes from the January 2026 exam will be presented.

Discussion: The revised TDM exam improves accessibility, fairness, and psychometric defensibility while preserving focus on therapeutic decision-making in the Canadian context. These innovations aim to support candidate preparation, reduce anxiety, and strengthen confidence among PRA programs and regulators. The updated TDM exam contributes directly to safe, effective integration of international physicians into Canada's health care system.

Teresa Killam (Lead Author), Irene Ma (Co Author), Mike Paget (Co Author), Johanna Holm (Co Author), Sarah Weeks (Co Author), Janeve Desy (Co Author), Adrian Harvey (Co Author), Lea Harper (Co Author)

Methods: This study was ethics-approved (CHREB23-0630). A consent form and an anonymous, voluntary Qualtrics survey consisting of the Oldenburg Burnout Inventory (measures disengagement (OLBI-d) and exhaustion (OLBI-e)) and the General Health Questionnaire-12 (GHQ12; measures minor psychiatric distress) were distributed to all medical students from the classes of 2025 (Legacy), and 2026 (RIME) cohorts approximately one month prior to the end of pre-clerkship and one year into clerkship. An independent samples t-test was performed.

Results: The survey response rates at the end of pre-clerkship were 25.8% (Legacy) and 10.2% (RIME). A significantly higher proportion of Legacy respondents met the criteria for disengagement (OLBI-d > 2.1, 89.4%), and exhaustion (OLBI-e > 2.25, 80.8%) when compared to the RIME cohort (OLBI-d, 77.8%, p = 0.024; OLBI-e, 66.7%, p = 0.035). There were no differences in the respondents who met the criteria for GHQ12 (total score > 2) for either cohort.

Discussion: Survey fatigue was evident from the low response rates from both cohorts, however, significantly more respondents from the Legacy cohort met the burnout criteria one month prior to the end of clerkship.

Yekta Sharafaddin-Zadeh (Co Author), Deborah Okusanya (Lead Author)

Methods: We conducted a scoping review (MEDLINE, EMBASE, Scopus, Cochrane Library) of North American undergraduate medical education. Eligible studies assessed interventions beyond the formal pre-clerkship curriculum (e.g., orientations, boot camps, simulations, workshops, mentorship, technology-enhanced learning). Two reviewers independently screened, extracted, and charted data; findings were synthesized descriptively by intervention domain and outcome type.

Results: Interventions clustered into educational (simulation-based procedural skills, radiology/surgery boot camps, VR/technology-enhanced clinical exposure) and social (near-peer primers, mentorship). Outcomes included preparedness, confidence, anxiety/stress, knowledge, and basic skills. Most evaluations showed improved confidence, knowledge gains, and reduced anxiety, though few assessed workplace performance, long-term retention, or team integration. Outcome measures were heterogeneous and follow-ups were infrequently implemented.

Discussion: Targeted interventions appear to enhance confidence, readiness, and wellbeing entering clerkship. Incorporating bridging activities may strengthen preparedness and support team integration. Future work should standardize outcomes, extend follow-up, and examine equity and hidden-curriculum impacts to guide program design.

Jordan Volpato (Lead Author), Mathieu Hanna (Co Author), Julie Desmeules (Co Author)

Methods: Our project incorporates social learning theory, longitudinal continuing professional development, and continuous quality improvement principles to help participants develop their leadership skills and to create and support their professional identity as medical educators as well as strengthening group cohesion. The program initially consisted of six themed group meetings (2 hours each) with themes such as "Inclusive leadership" and "Strategic planning". As a follow-up to the initial iteration of the program, we created 3 new themes in version 2.0 : "Dealing with disruptive behaviour", "Science of meetings" and "Leadership in Change Management". Participants complete a preparatory document prior to each activity. During the meetings, participants share their issues and challenges, receive feedback from the group, and set objectives and action plans based on the issues identified.

Results: We measured marked improvement in participants' comfort level regarding their role as medical leaders and in their ability to mobilize team members. A better understanding of how to manage disrupted behaviours within a team, how to efficiently conduct meetings and how to lead in times of change were examples of acquired competencies.

Discussion: The objective of the second iteration of this program is to improve the quality of the administrative and academic leadership within our department. By fostering a sense of belonging, sharing sound management practices, and promoting a shared vision of continuous quality improvement for our leaders we hope to ensure the continuity of excellence in our department.

Abdul Al-Shawwa (Lead Author), Daphne Nakhid (Co Author), Mike Paget (Co Author), Sylvain Coderre (Co Author)

Methods: A stratified analytics review (2023-2024) analyzed user demographics and scheme-level metrics (n=573), including active users, sessions, engagement rate, and time-on-page.

Results: Canada had the largest user base (21,623), with notable growth in Portugal, Belgium, and Japan. Usage peaked during medical school and clerkship transitions, aligning with cognitive learning theory. Smaller user bases in Belgium, Austria, and Jordan showed high engagement times (>200 min/user), indicating deep content interaction. Frequently accessed schemes reflected generalist relevance (GI, pediatric, renal, cardiovascular). Over 20 schemes had high time-on-page but low traffic, suggesting underutilized yet cognitively demanding content.

Discussion: Blackbook's schema-based design supports effective knowledge encoding and retrieval, affirming its value as a global learning asset.

Byunghoon (Tony) Ahn (Lead Author)

Methods: This pilot analyzed articles from two prominent simulation journals Advances in Simulation and Simulation in Healthcare (n=1,613). Each sentence featuring "fidelity" plus the pro- and pre-ceding sentences were captured (1,322 windows). I hierarchically clustered these windows using embeddings (machine-generated numbers reflecting meaning), yielding 250 clusters. Five dominant clusters are reported.

Results: The clusters (28.1%; 371 windows) were: 1) Operational fidelity dimensions (n=138)-focused on overall quality of the environment, equipment, and engagement; 2) Fidelity as a contested concept (n=87)-focused on critiquing "more realism is better"; 3) Comparative high vs. low fidelity (n=57)-focused on linking fidelity levels to performance; 4) "High-fidelity" as tool-realism shorthand (n=52)-focused on simulator sophistication; and 5) Three-dimension framing (n=37)-focused on physical, conceptual, and experiential strands.

Discussion: Findings show tension between critiques of fidelity and framing fidelity as something to maximize. A smaller but noticeable share describes fidelity in specific strands, suggesting a more intentional use of the term. Future work will extend this mapping to additional journals and capture trends over time.

Robert Henderson (Lead Author), Rhonda Bryce (Co Author), Kieran Johnson (Co Author), Tatiana Fras (Co Author), Jason Hosain (Co Author), Tracy Guselle (Co Author), Jill Farrukh (Co Author), Kevin Ledding (Co Author), Angela Baerwald (Co Author)

Methods: This was a prospective, randomised, controlled trial of IUD placement by ultrasound guidance versus control (no ultrasound) at the family medicine resident training clinic in Saskatoon, Saskatchewan, Canada. Informed consent was obtained from patients and residents prior to initiating study procedures. Resident confidence pre- and post-procedure, patient-reported pain scores during the procedure, and total procedure time were compared between the two groups. Pain scores and confidence used Likert scales from 0-10.

Results: Thirty residents and 49 patients were enrolled. Resident-reported confidence increased post-procedure in both groups; however, this increase was 1.3 (0.4-2.2, 95% CI, p=0.008) points greater in the ultrasound group. Patient pain scores were similar between the groups; mean difference -0.2 (-0.8-0.4, p=0.45). Mean procedure time in the ultrasound-guided group was longer (23.6 min) compared to controls (13.0 min); however, with adjusting for more procedural experience among control placements, this difference was estimated at 11.2 (-0.02-22.4, p=0.05).

Discussion: Ultrasound-guided IUD placement within a family medicine postgraduate educational setting results in improved trainee-reported confidence without compromising patient comfort. However, procedural duration may be increased.

Husnaat Bajwa (Lead Author), Adam Hussain (Co Author), Huzaifa Saeed (Co Author)

Methods: We performed a narrative review of orthopedic simulation literature (2000-2025), focusing on arthroscopy, joint replacement, and trauma procedures. Data were synthesized for learner outcomes, curriculum models, and barriers to implementation.

Results: Simulation improved technical performance, reduced operative time, and enhanced trainee confidence, particularly in arthroscopy. Barriers included high costs and lack of faculty time. Integration was most successful when linked with competency-based assessment.

Discussion: Simulation is an effective adjunct in orthopedic training. Broader integration, faculty buy-in, and resource-sharing across programs are needed to optimize its impact.

Huzaifa Saeed (Co Author), Husnaat Bajwa (Lead Author), Adam Hussain (Co Author)

Methods: Following Arksey & O'Malley's scoping review framework, databases including PubMed, CINAHL, and Scopus were searched for studies published between 2000-2025. Eligible studies addressed disparities in access to interventional pain services by geography, socioeconomic status, or marginalized identity.

Andrea Guerin (Lead Author), Rhonda Newhook (Co Author), Jennifer Fowler (Co Author), Leslie Flynn (Co Author), Denise Stockley (Co Author)

Methods: Applicant data from 2020 to 2025 were extracted from program records and categorized into three discipline groups: Medicine, Nursing, and Allied Health. Scott Tunison (Lead Author), Trustin Domes (Co Author), Rebecca Mueller (Co Author), Kristen Burrows (Co Author)

Methods: The PAC engaged in 3 two-round Delphi processes to identify curriculum topics essential for didactic instruction in year 1. In each round, PAC members rated the relative importance of typical UGM topics using a 7-point Likert scale. Topics rated low by ≥75% of participants were eliminated, and the remainder were re-rated on a binary scale.

Results: The modified Delphi process systematically sought PAC collaborative expertise to establish an appropriately dense curriculum. For example, the PAC distilled a Term 1 Foundations curriculum that will include 55 topics (a 37.5% reduction in curriculum density from the 88 topics typically included in UGM programming) - demonstrating the power of collective expertise. for the overall curriculum will be shared.

Discussion: This study provides a curriculum development model that ensures robust learner knowledge while establishing a patient-centered approach merging related topics for efficiency. Maia von Maltzahn (Lead Author), Emma-Lee Rhyno (Co Author), Shanna Trenaman (Co Author)

Methods: We began with a root cause analysis to examine local factors contributing to increased antipsychotic prescribing at the Veteran's Memorial Building (VMB) in Halifax, NS. Process mapping identified knowledge gaps as a contributor to potentially inappropriate prescribing. Brief, anonymous feedback will be collected at the end of the module.
