Dhruv Patel (Lead Author), Johnathan Babulic (Co Author), Andrea Winthrop (Co Author), Steve Mann (Co Author)

Methods: Twenty-four second-year medical students were recorded performing a simple interrupted suturing task. Videos were scored independently by two distinct VLMs (Gemini 2.5 Flash & Gemini 2.5 Pro) and a board-certified senior general surgeon as the expert rater, using a standardized Objective Structured Assessment of Technical Skills (OSATS) rubric. Inter-rater reliability (IRR) was assessed using intraclass correlation coefficients (ICC) and Cohen's K for overall scores and for each component of the OSATS rubric.

Results: Both VLMs showed moderate to good agreement compared to the expert rater, with Gemini 2.5 Pro (ICC = 0.72) showing stronger performance over Gemini 2.5 Flash (ICC = 0.61). Cohen's K values ranged from 0.3 to 1.0, varying based on rubric items.

Discussion: This study shows that VLMs can reasonably approximate expert assessment of suturing skills. Interestingly, the model exhibited more accurate assessments of procedural steps but struggled with assessing suture lengths and depths. Further validation across additional AI models and larger sample sizes is warranted to explore the adoption of VLMs in more generalized assessment settings. Overall, VLMs offer a novel approach to provide individualized feedback on procedural skills with good agreement compared to an expert rater.

Laura Cummings (Lead Author), Taunia Rifai (Lead Author), Laura Visentin (Lead Author), Zack van Allen (Co Author), Kailash Radhakrishnan (Co Author)

Methods: Baseline and follow-up surveys were administered to family medicine residents to inform curriculum design and early evaluation. Survey items were adapted from published tools assessing planetary health knowledge among medical trainees and were structured using the COM-B framework (Capability, Opportunity, Motivation-Behaviour). Descriptive statistics were used to summarize responses.

Results: Baseline survey response rate was 15/33 (45%). Most respondents (87%) reported low knowledge of planetary health, and only 6.7% indicated moderate confidence discussing planetary health issues with patients. Residents were evenly divided on whether planetary health concepts were currently included in their training. Motivation was high, with 80% agreeing that planetary health should be incorporated into residency education, though barriers cited included limited time, institutional support, and mentorship. 20% of respondents indicated plans to engage in sustainability-focused quality improvement initiatives. A follow-up survey (n=15, 45% response rate) conducted four months after initial curricular efforts showed no change in perceived knowledge, but slightly increased confidence. There remained a mixed perception of whether planetary health was currently included in teaching.

Discussion: These findings highlight gaps in residents' capability and opportunity to engage with planetary health, despite strong motivation. Results will guide the ongoing co-creation, implementation, and evaluation of a structured planetary health curriculum.

Auva Zarandi (Lead Author), Elif Bilgic (Lead Author), Adam Fleming (Co Author), Leslie Martin (Co Author)

Methods: Through a descriptive exploratory approach, we conducted formal and informal discussions with physician educators who hold residency/fellowship program leadership roles and/or have experience with research at the intersection of CBD and cross-disciplinary training. Guided by the Systems Engineering Initiative for Patient Safety (SEIPS) 3.0 framework, we explored structural, pedagogical, and experiential shifts in EPA-driven training and synthesized perspectives on potential implications for cross-disciplinary education and patient care.

Results: We identified three possible key concerns regarding EPA-driven curricula: (1) reduced cross-disciplinary exposure due to specialty-specific EPA prioritization (e.g., EPA-driven rotation scheduling), (a) decreasing informal relationship-building and cross-disciplinary understanding among residents, and (b) limiting trainees' familiarity with the workflows and perspectives of other specialties; (2) deprioritization of valuable, unassessed learning not explicitly tied to EPAs; and (3) variable implementation of CBD, leading to potential inconsistent training experiences across programs.

Discussion: While CBD offers structure and clarity, its implementation may unintentionally narrow clinical exposure and reduce opportunities for systems-based, collaborative learning. These findings raise important questions about how to maintain educational breadth within evolving frameworks. We recommend system-level reviews of cross-disciplinary training, formal evaluation of learning beyond EPA completion, and curricular adjustments reflecting the realities of interdisciplinary clinical practice.

Khoa Duong (Lead Author), Andrea Quaiattini (Co Author), Meredith Young (Co Author)

Methods: Guided by Arksey and O'Malley's scoping review framework, we conducted a comprehensive search across seven databases to identify articles addressing health advocacy and referencing the CanMEDS framework. Of 823 identified studies, 430 were retained post-deduplication. Two reviewers independently screened titles and abstracts, selecting 356 for full-text review. Ultimately, 263 articles met inclusion criteria. Bibliometric analysis was conducted using VOSviewer 1.6.20, including co-author and co-occurrence analyses.

Results: Co-author analysis revealed a small, tightly connected group of researchers - 9 authors - driving this field. Co-occurrence analysis showed emphasis on patient-level advocacy over health policy or promotion. Postgraduate medical education (PGME) dominated over undergraduate contexts. Curriculum development emerged as the central educational focus, with limited attention to assessment. Qualitative methods, especially grounded theory and interviews, were most common. Geographically, Canada - particularly Ontario and British Columbia - was most represented.

Discussion: Current literature focuses on patient advocacy, PGME, and curriculum development, predominantly shaped by a small North American author network - highlighting a limited evidence base. Further analysis is needed to explore how prevailing notions of health advocacy intersect with broader global and structural contexts.

Kisanet Kebedom (Lead Author)

Methods: A combination of archival research, literature and media review were used to identify stories of Black women who shaped Canadian medicine but remain largely unacknowledged. A case study approach was used to examine the lives of select individuals in depth.

Results: Several notable figures emerged, including Dr. June James, the first Black woman admitted to the University of Manitoba's College of Medicine; Dr. Yvette Bonny, a pioneer in pediatric hematology and transplantation in Quebec; and Dr. Anna Jarvis, an innovator in pediatric emergency care. Discussion: This work underscores the need for archival justice and curricular reform to restore erased legacies. Reclaiming these narratives not only enriches our understanding of medical history but also empowers future Black women in medicine through representation and belonging. Highlighting the stories of these physicians in our medical curriculums will allow us to fully recognize their contributions and pave the way forward for the next generation of physicians.

Rukia Swaleh (Lead Author), Victor Adarquah (Co Author), Modupe Tunde-Byass (Co Author), Nnamdi Ndubuka (Co Author)

Methods: We hosted a November 2024 workshop on career advancement and distributed pre- and post-workshop surveys, adapted from a CMAJ study of Black medical students. Data were analyzed descriptively.

Results: Of 28 attendees, 7 completed the pre- and 8 the post-workshop survey. Respondents included a second-year resident, one early-career physician (<5 years in practice), two mid-career physicians (6-20 years), and three late-career physicians (>20 years). Over half (57.2%) identified racial discrimination as a barrier to career success, and 42.9% noted exclusion from advancement opportunities. Most (85.7%) reported that disproportionate equity-related work diverted time from scholarship. In contrast, many did not view racial identity as influencing remediation (57.2%), attrition (87.5%), or access to reference letters (71.4%). Barriers to promotion were reported to include lack of mentorship, sponsorship, and belonging (28.6% each). Participants rated the workshop as valuable, and expressed greater confidence in career planning (87.5%).

Discussion: Our study contributes to the first generation of Canadian race-based data on Black physicians' experiences. Survey responses align with emergent themes from an ongoing Pan-Canadian Black Physician Census. While some findings parallel U.S. data, others diverge; for example, remediation, attrition, and reference letters were not majorly influenced by race in this cohort, signaling the need for targeted systematic research.

Nujud Al-Jabouri (Lead Author), Zarah Rahman (Co Author), Kahono Hirasawa (Co Author), Eric Wooltorton (Co Author)

Methods: A systematic search was conducted on Medline, Embase, and Scopus up to June 2025. Titles and abstracts (n=2248) and full-texts (n=323) were independently screened against the eligibility criteria by three reviewers, with disagreements resolved by discussion. Data from the included studies were extracted and thematically analyzed.

Results: 82 studies were included, yielding five themes: effectiveness for skill acquisition, learner perceptions and acceptability, practicality and feasibility, transfer of learning, and platform design factors. Simulators included virtual reality headsets, high-fidelity simulators, and interactive applications. When mapped to Kirkpatrick's framework, outcomes were heavily concentrated at Levels 1 and 2. Level 1 highlighted increased learner satisfaction, engagement, and skills confidence. At Level 2, simulator training improved efficiency, technical precision, error reduction, and retention of procedural skills compared to traditional methods. However, limitations included high costs, technical malfunctions, cybersickness, and uncertain durability of skill improvements. No studies demonstrated Level 3 or 4 outcomes.

Discussion: Virtual simulator-based learning demonstrates benefits for student engagement and skill acquisition, but limited evidence exists on behavioural impacts and real-world impact. Further research should focus on gathering higher-level Kirkpatrick evidence to understand the broader implications of using virtual procedural simulators in UGME.

Andrea Guerin (Lead Author), Rhonda Newhook (Co Author), Jennifer Fowler (Co Author), Denise Stockley (Co Author), Leslie Flynn (Co Author)

Methods: We reviewed all project and sorted them into one of three themes: scholarly approaches, interprofessional collaboration or indigenous perspectives. These themes were selected as they represented the greatest areas of difference, as the other principles were found in every capstone. Projects were sorted by cohort year and discipline (medicine, nursing or allied health).

Results: A total of 54 capstones were included. There was a clear difference in popularity of the theme and discipline. Scholarly approaches was the most common theme overall (61%) and most likely to be selected by medicine providers (65%). Interprofessional collaboration was most likely to be selected by allied health providers (38%). Indigenous perspectives was most likely to be selected by nurses (17%). The number of indigenous perspectives-based capstones decreased over time between cohorts (57% to 14%).

Discussion: Review of the capstones is informative for curricular renewal. Each of the three learner groups had predispositions towards capstone themes. Despite interprofessional collaboration and indigenous perspectives' prominence as core principles, indigenous perspectives declined over time and interprofessional collaboration was not as prominent for nursing or medicine providers. Further exploration of how students select their capstone project warrants our attention.

Einas Yousef (Lead Author), Beenish Mukhtar (Co Author), Mohammed Moizuddin Khan (Co Author), Shaima Zafer Khan (Co Author), Sadia Wazir Khan (Co Author)

Methods: In a prospective, randomized study, third-year medical students (n≈50) at a simulation center were allocated to FC or TL. FC participants received short, targeted pre-class videos and guiding questions; TL received standard in-class instruction. During on-site sessions, both groups completed the same case-based activities with faculty facilitation. Outcomes were domain scores for history, examination, investigations, and treatment planning, plus an anonymized post-session perception survey. Engagement was indexed by the percentage of pre-class videos watched.

Results: FC students outperformed TL in multiple domains of clinical-skills assessment (notably history taking, physical examination, investigations, and initial management planning). Performance in the FC arm showed a positive association with pre-class video completion, indicating a dose-response relationship between preparation and outcomes. Survey feedback favored FC for clarity, time efficiency, confidence building, interactivity, and immediacy of feedback, while noting predictable challenges (technology reliance and preparation load when time is limited).

Discussion: Implementing a flipped classroom in simulation-based medical education improved clinical-skills performance and student experience compared with traditional instruction. The observed preparation-performance relationship highlights the importance of high-quality, concise pre-class materials. Findings support broader FC adoption and motivate future studies on long-term retention, OSCE outcomes, and scalability across courses and institutions.

Aanya Singh (Lead Author), Catherine Patocka (Lead Author)

Methods: We are conducting a realist evaluation of the Precision Health Graduate Program at the University of Calgary. Data sources will include program documents, course materials, and semi-structured interviews with faculty and students, analyzed into context-mechanism-outcome configurations. Guided by a realist stance, we ask not only whether precision initiatives "work," but how, why, for whom, and under what circumstances they generate benefit-or not. This approach illuminates mechanisms and contextual conditions that shape outcomes and moves beyond technologically deterministic accounts of precision health to generate transferable explanatory theories.

Results: Early findings suggest that precision is enacted not only through advanced analytics but also by aligning educational systems with learners' and institutions' needs.

Discussion: Clarifying how precision health is understood and operationalized will guide local program improvement and contribute to broader debates about meaningful and equitable integration within healthcare and academic systems.

Omar El Ferri (Lead Author), Holly Hnautik (Co Author), Joshua Seto (Co Author), Kareem Sadek (Co Author), Ahmad Hassan (Co Author), Lisa Yeo (Co Author), Lea Harper (Co Author)

Methods: Cross-sectional analysis of an institutional survey of pre-clerkship medical students. The analysis uses Likert-scale responses, with agreement that "my work was useful to the partner organization" as the primary predictor and agreement that the program "fosters meaningful, reciprocal partnerships" as the primary outcome. Secondary outcomes include perceived population impact and overall satisfaction. We examined the relationship between perceived usefulness and meaningfulness while considering contextual factors, including organization support, medical school support, time appropriateness, orientation preparedness, and prior exposure. Content analysis of open-ended responses identified what felt useful versus not useful.

Results: show that perceived usefulness may be associated with perceived meaningfulness of CBL. Similar trends were found for perceived population impact and overall satisfaction, in consideration of support, time, orientation, and prior exposure. Qualitative themes indicate that meaningful experiences cluster around direct client contact, tangible deliverables, outreach, and student-led initiatives. Perceptions of low usefulness may be linked to a lack of a defined role, unclear scope or direction, unstructured shifts, and routine tasks.

Discussion: Perceived usefulness of student work is associated with perceived meaningfulness and impact of CBL experiences. Our findings suggest that CBL programs should co-design placements with community partners and students to ensure there is alignment between the needs of the community organization and the actual student roles and deliverables.

Ada Man (Lead Author), Ashiqul Haque (Co Author)

Methods: Registration data from 3,792 entries (2016-2025) was analyzed. Unique ever-registrants per academic year were identified. Key variables including profession, medical specialty, geographic work setting, and years of experience, were categorized. The statistical significance of observed trends was assessed using the Cochran-Armitage test and Poisson regression modeling. We reviewed needs assessment surveys from 2024-2025 to explore reported barriers to attending CPD.

Results: Family physicians were the largest attendee group. Engagement from non-physicians (28.8-41.4%) and rural practitioners (15.5-30.6%) was substantial, with rural participation peaking notably during the 2020-21 pandemic year. Participation from early-career clinicians (≤5 years) declined markedly from 26.5% to 12.3% over the study period (Z = -6.62, p < 0.001), representing an approximate 10% annual decrease. Needs assessment survey data indicated that early-career clinicians identify costs as one of the major barriers to attending CPD, more than later career clinicians (e.g., 44.4% vs 23.2%).

Discussion: The program demonstrates success in engaging a broad interprofessional and geographic audience. However, the precipitous decline in early-career physician participation poses a major risk to the program's sustainability and equity mission. These findings necessitate further investigation into barriers faced by new clinicians and the development of tailored initiatives to reverse this trend.

Shania Sheth (Lead Author), Jenn Carpenter (Co Author)

Methods: A pilot case was developed through stages including patient case development, dialogue writing, and platform development. The pilot test recruited students from Queen's University through convenience and purposive sampling. The pilot test employed a mixed methods design including a pre/post survey and focus groups.

Results: The pilot test consisted of 10 medical students (n=10) and 300 undergraduate students (n=300). The results were overwhelmingly positive. Students praised the design, realistic dialogue, informative resources, and visually appealing inference. The simulation significantly increased students' knowledge on topics such as health equity, social determinants of health, and patient-centred care. Students also reported a significantly greater level of perceived comfort interacting with specific patient populations. All 10 medical students (100%) and 290 undergraduate students (96.67%) stated integrating the Health Equity Simulation into the medical school curriculum would be beneficial.

Discussion: The pilot test revealed promising results, highlighting how a Health Equity Simulation has the potential to become an effective learning resource to empower the next generation of healthcare providers.

Timothy Nunes (Lead Author), Alexandra Hauser (Lead Author), Eleftheria Laios (Co Author), Wiley Chung (Co Author)

Methods: Searches were conducted in MEDLINE, Embase, APA PsycInfo, and CINAHL, yielding 1092 unique references. Title and abstract screening removed 904 studies, with 160 moving to full-text review, which is ongoing. Included studies examine resilience, adaptability, or related human factors in non-OR patient care teams with respect to patient safety. Screening and extraction stages are conducted in duplicate according to Joanna Briggs Institute and PRISMA-ScR review guidelines.

Results: The results will provide a high-level assessment of the contexts and extent to which resilience has been studied in non-OR healthcare teams, including analysis of existing conceptual models and frameworks.

Discussion: Our review will provide a novel perspective on how team resilience is applied across different clinical environments, highlighting both commonalities and unique challenges. Sami Sabbah (Lead Author), Ragav Chona (Co Author), Julianna Botros (Co Author), Alisha Awadia (Co Author), Timothy Moran (Co Author)

Methods: The purpose of the current study is to identify potential gaps in naloxone education and exposure within the medical curriculum by disseminating a survey to University of Ottawa students in the undergraduate medical program. The results of this survey may aid in characterizing students' ability to effectively respond to opioid-related emergencies in clinical or community settings.

Results: A total of 103 respondents completed the survey. The data indicates that 80% of respondents either did not receive or were unsure if they had received formal naloxone training during their medical education. Nearly 65% reported independently seeking information on naloxone outside of formal training. While 70% indicated that naloxone access was somewhat or very easy, 35% had never seen or handled a naloxone kit, and 45% had not observed naloxone use in clinical settings. Despite this, about 75% of medical students expressed a willingness to administer naloxone in an overdose situation. However, 35% felt not at all prepared based on their current medical training. Almost 95% agreed that naloxone education should be a mandatory part of core medical school curricula.

Discussion: These results suggest that medical students are willing to use naloxone and interested in learning more about it. However, there is a clear shortcoming in formal medical school education on the topic. This gap highlights the need for the integration of naloxone-specific learning objectives within medical curricula to ensure that students are adequately supported in using naloxone safely and effectively in clinical practice.

Methods: A systematic search was conducted across three databases, Embase, ERIC, and Web of Science, followed by a comprehensive review by three reviewers to synthesize overarching themes. Keywords included "clinical teachers," "teaching identity," and "faculty development." Of the 277 identified studies, 17 met the inclusion criteria.

Results: Preliminary themes identified include positive influence of self-efficacy, mentorship, early success as a physician, and involvement in educational research. Barriers include inadequate training, lack of faculty development programs, and insufficient community support. Effective faculty development programs emphasize community, mentorship, and blended learning with agency and collaborative opportunities. The findings highlight need for longitudinal faculty development programs and the early integration of clinical educator streams within residency training.

Dr. Munira Abdulwasi (Lead Author), Dr. Kathryn Parker (Lead Author), Dr. Nicole N. Woods (Co Author), Dr. Stella Ng (Co Author), Natasha Ross (Co Author)

Methods: Informed by Appreciative Inquiry and critical reflexivity, a 6-month co-design process engaged over 100 CHWs and interest holders through interviews, 7 group storytelling sessions and 2 summits. CHWs shared what they valued most about their work, their peak experiences, unique skills and aspirations, culminating in 51 stories. Themes derived from the stories were shared through 18 representative stories at a Storytelling Summit, where participants envisioned core program elements. At a Rapid Prototyping Summit, participants collectively developed a prototype curriculum.

Results: The process resulted in the Community Health and Social Medicine (CHASM) Care Worker Program, an Advanced Certificate now offered by the Michener Institute of Education. Key outputs included a community-rooted curriculum, a sustainable funding model, and a tailored marketing strategy.

Discussion: The co-design process demonstrates how story-based, community led design can reframe health professions education. Mia Gisselbaek (Lead Author), Joana Berger-Estilita (Co Author), Sarah Saxena (Co Author), Mohamed Seyour (Co Author), Nadia Elia (Co Author), Kori A. LaDonna (Co Author), Georges L. Savoldelli (Co Author)

Methods: This umbrella review was conducted and reported in accordance with PRISMA-ScR guidelines. Comprehensive searches were carried out in Medline, Embase, PubMed, PsycINFO, Scopus, Web of Science, CINAHL, Cochrane Library, and ERIC between November 27 and December 4, 2024. Eligible studies included systematic and scoping reviews addressing IP and related interventions. Methodological rigor was appraised using AMSTAR-2 and the Joanna Briggs Institute (JBI) Critical Appraisal Tool.

Results: Sixteen reviews (eight systematic and eight scoping) spanning diverse populations and methodologies were included. Findings revealed marked variability in both the conceptualization and measurement of IP, with no universally accepted assessment instrument. Prevalence estimates ranged widely, with risk factors such as perfectionism, minority status, and hierarchical workplace dynamics frequently noted. Interventions including coaching, digital self-directed modules, and mindfulness-based approaches demonstrated promise in alleviating IP symptoms; however, overall methodological quality of intervention studies was inconsistent, with few rated as high-quality.

Discussion: The results underscore the need for greater conceptual clarity, standardized measurement tools, and more rigorous evaluation of interventions addressing IP. Future research should prioritize refining theoretical frameworks, developing multidimensional assessment strategies, and systematically testing evidence-based interventions to enhance confidence and professional competence among individuals experiencing IP.

Katerina Schwab (Lead Author), Patrick Murphy (Co Author), Umberin Najeeb (Co Author), Susanna Cheng (Co Author), Geoffrey Watson (Co Author)

Methods: Participants in the Master Teacher Program (MTP) at U of T in 2022 (n=9) and 2025 (n=19) were included. Both groups of MTP learners completed the same questionnaire, which was designed to evaluate the psychosocial impact of the VL environment.

Results: MTP learners in virtual classes from the 2025 cohort report that VL continues to impact mental health (32%), and only 68% endorse addressing feelings of burnout. There were improvements in 'Zoom Fatigue' (100% in 2022 vs 68% in 2025) and attentiveness (100% vs 79%) during VL, however social disconnect remains prevalent (89% vs 84%). Many learners felt they had social supports (89% vs 95%) however only 53% felt they had institutional support in 2025 compared to 89% in 2022. Only 32% of learners were aware of support and wellness programs in 2025. There was a decline in overall satisfaction with VL (100% in 2022 vs 68% in 2025), however many learners still wish to maintain this model of learning in their curricula (78% vs 79%).

Discussion: While VL will likely continue, it is evident that more work is needed to address the psychological challenges associated with its use. A similar comparative study involving medical students is also in progress.

Spencer Pasternak (Lead Author) Doug Archibald (Co Author) Christopher Ramnanan (Co Author) Sohail Anwar (Co Author)

Methods: PoCUS-guided dissection was implemented in upper limb (axillary fossa) and lower limb (femoral triangle) laboratories within a musculoskeletal course for Master of Anatomical Sciences Education (ASE) students at the University of Ottawa. Nineteen students across six lab groups were randomly assigned to a session. Before each session, PoCUS groups completed a brief orientation to relevant ultrasound images. Student perceptions were collected through post-lab surveys modeled on the Technology Acceptance Model.

Results: Students in both regions reported PoCUS enhanced the educational value of dissection and improved their ability to identify structures. Lower limb participants perceived greater benefit, particularly for identifying vascular and neural structures and understanding spatial relationships. Students described increased engagement, but noted challenges such as unfamiliarity with ultrasound operation, image quality, and time constraints. Most recommended additional PoCUS training.

Discussion: PoCUS-guided dissection appears feasible and valuable for ASE students, with lower limb integration offering greatest perceived benefit. Further research with larger cohorts is warranted.

Anuoluwa Popoola (Lead Author), Kyobin Hwang (Co Author), Kevin Wang (Co Author), Eleanor Weisbaum (Co Author), Tea Christopoulos (Co Author)

Methods: Following a 5-week AMP-MP training that consisted of weekly 2-hour didactic and experiential sessions, we conducted semi-structured post-program interviews (30-60 min) with resident and staff physicians in the Greater Toronto Area who identify with or serve equity-deserving communities. Field notes supplemented interview data. Twenty-one participants attended the program. Purposive selection ensured strong information power through diversity in clinical experience, mindfulness background, and engagement with equity-deserving communities. Transcripts were analyzed inductively using thematic analysis within an interpretive paradigm.

Results: Twelve physicians were interviewed, with data saturation achieved. Participants described several contributors to burnout, including bullying and overwhelming workloads. They discussed how their identity affects their experience and management of work demands. Drawing on the Maslach Burnout Inventory framework, participants reported that engagement with AMP-MP reduced emotional exhaustion through promoting self-compassion, decreased depersonalization by fostering presence with patients and colleagues, and renewed personal accomplishment by enhancing meaning in work and confidence in leadership roles.

Discussion: This pilot study demonstrates that AMP-MP can meaningfully mitigate burnout and strengthen relational care among physicians serving equity-deserving groups. Integrating AMP-MP into medical curricula and professional development for learners and physicians could promote wellness and catalyze cultural change. Future studies are needed to evaluate the program's impact and guide implementation.

Kathleen Singh (Lead Author), Mandy Esliger (Co Author), Mark Bosma (Co Author), Maia von Maltzahn (Co Author), Cindy Shearer (Co Author), Luc Rivet (Co Author), Cheryl Murphy (Co Author)

Methods: Following Kern's six-steps of curriculum development, a preparatory didactic session and two simulation scenarios to assess financial capacity and personal care capacity were developed. A mixed-methods approach evaluated the curriculum's impact. Residents completed pre-post Likert scale surveys and participated in a focus group and three-month follow-up individual interview. Survey and interview data were analyzed using independent t-tests and thematic analysis, respectively.

Results: Six residents participated in the simulation, surveys and focus group. Five participated in individual interviews. Statistical analysis showed learners felt more confident in their knowledge post simulation. Four themes were generated from the interviews, confirming that capacity assessment is a challenging skill to learn with few opportunities for supervision; the simulation provided a good opportunity to practice this skill; working with peers and preceptors in the simulation helped navigate uncertainty and anxiety; and simulation resulted in increased confidence and a change in practice.

Discussion: This novel simulation provided observed training opportunities for GP and GM residents in a low-stakes formative experience. It could be scaled for a wider audience or serve as a model for programs where residents have limited opportunities for assessment of competencies requiring demonstration of capacity assessment skills.

Liisa Johnston (Lead Author), Marijana Jovanovic (Co Author), Bronwyn Thomson (Co Author), Heidi Eccles (Co Author), Lauren Hishon (Co Author), Phillippe Hwang (Co Author), Athena Ko (Co Author)

Methods: A convenience sample of postgraduate medical residents from a tertiary children's hospital in Canada was recruited. Residents were surveyed February/March 2025. Learners' connectedness with each supervisor was measured using the 12-item Connection Index (score range 1-7), and burnout was assessed using the three dimensions of the Maslach Burnout Inventory (MBI).

Results: Total of 32 residents participated; 27 identified as female, 4 as male, and 1 as another gender. Almost one third of residents were in their second year of the program. The average Connection Index score was 5.88. Higher connectedness with supervisors was associated with lower levels of burnout in two of the three MBI dimensions: emotional exhaustion (β = -4.41, 95% CI: -7.04 to -1.80) and depersonalization (β = -3.38, 95% CI: -6.05 to -0.72). No significant association was found between connectedness and the third dimension, personal accomplishment.

Discussion: Overall, residents reported moderate levels of connectedness with their supervisors. However, those with lower connection scores experienced higher levels of burnout. Interventions aimed at strengthening supervisor-learner relationships may help mitigate burnout among medical residents.

Tovan Lew (Lead Author), Jade Gilchrist (Co Author), Victor Ng (Co Author), Lindsey Sutherland (Co Author)

Methods: A mixed-methods survey distributed to secondary school students (Grades 9-13) gathered demographic data, Likert-scale ratings, and open-ended responses. Quantitative data were analyzed with descriptive and comparative statistics; qualitative responses underwent thematic analysis.

Results: In total, 262 responses were collected. Participants agreed rural healthcare challenges are unique (87-94%), with less consensus on adequate access (57-65%). François Gallant (Lead Author), Eve-Catherine Marquis (Co Author), Mariève Allain (Co Author), Julie Gallien (Co Author), Shane Aubé (Co Author), Jeffrey Gaudet (Co Author), Michel Landry (Co Author), Nicole LeBlanc (Co Author)

Methods: Using a historical registry of NB francophone students linked with the provincial physician registry, we identified physicians who trained in Francophone medical education programs since 1981. We describe practice location (2024) and compare the likelihood of practicing in NB using logistic regression.

Results: Of 845 physicians identified, practice location was determined for 812 (96.1%). Most were family physicians (486; 60.1%). In 2024, more family physicians were practicing in NB than specialists (70.7% vs 51.5%). Retention was highest among family physicians who completed both medical school and postgraduate training in NB [OR (95%CI) 27.9 (11.0,70.9)], reflecting the magnitude of the effect of training and a smaller comparison group (e.g., medical school in NB but not residency in NB, n=36).

Discussion: Francophone medical education in NB represents a compelling case study of how DME strengthens local physician retention. Our findings demonstrate that training physicians locally, particularly in family medicine, has important and positive impacts on provincial workforce supply. This is especially important for improving access to health services in French, where needs remain high.

Aida Bangoura (Lead Author), Kelsie Campbell (Co Author), Nada Strathearn (Co Author), Aarzoo Nathani (Co Author), Marie Horton (Co Author)

Methods: Drawing on evidence-based practices in accessibility, bilingualism, and equity, as well as feedback from committee and network members, the AFMC Equity, Diversity, Inclusion and Belonging Committee and Social Accountability Team designed structured recommendations for both meeting chairs and participants. The guides outline inclusive practices across all meeting stages-before, during, and after-while addressing accessibility domains including linguistic inclusion, physical and cognitive access, and considerations for mental health. Highlighting the before/during/after structure makes clear the systematic application of inclusion practices, which is central to the innovation of these guides. Practical strategies, such as framing agenda items as questions, embedding land acknowledgements, providing live transcription, and encouraging reflection post-meeting, were incorporated to ensure usability and adaptability in diverse contexts.

Results: The dual-guide approach supports inclusivity from multiple vantage points: Chairs are empowered with tools to facilitate equitable discussions, while participants receive guidance to engage respectfully and actively. Early implementation across AFMC committees and networks has demonstrated improved engagement from Francophone and accessibility-seeking participants, alongside greater awareness of inclusive facilitation strategies among chairs. Feedback has highlighted the clarity, practicality, and adaptability of the guides for both virtual and in-person contexts.

Discussion: These resources contribute to embedding equity and social accountability into the culture of academic medicine by transforming routine meetings into inclusive learning and decision-making environments. The guides provide a replicable model for medical faculties, healthcare organizations, and academic associations seeking to operationalize inclusivity in governance and collaboration. Given the international audience of ICAM, the global applicability of these resources is an important consideration-they can be adapted to diverse regional, institutional, and cultural contexts. Future work will focus on evaluation metrics and periodic enhancements, including participant satisfaction surveys, observed changes in participation patterns, and documentation of accessibility practices, to strengthen the evidence base for inclusive meeting practices.

Naisha Dharia (Lead Author), Aimun Shah (Co Author), Tasnia Noshin (Co Author), Jean-Michel Leduc (Co Author), Maria Hubinette (Co Author), Meredith Vanstone (Co Author), Alice Cavanagh (Co Author), Mei-Ju Shih (Co Author), Lenore Lukasik-Fos (Co Author), Hartley Jafine (Co Author), Kathleen Nolan (Co Author), Catharine Munn (Co Author), Natasja Menezes (Co Author), Stacey Ritz (Co Author), Lawrence Grierson (Co Author)

Methods: To begin this inquiry, we conducted an environmental scan of the rationale, potential impacts, and influence on selection decisions associated with publicly available admissions policies concerning full course load requirements from Canada's 19 medical schools. Our goal was to qualitatively describe the policy discourse with specific consideration for the implications to applicants facing adversity. This process was guided by the tenets of critical discourse analysis, with an iteratively developed coding schema informed by the 3-i policy analysis framework.

Results: Our analysis reveals substantial variation in how full and reduced course loads are contemplated in Canadian medical school admission policies. Some policies reflect strong institutional values around the "ideal" student and educational norms, which have the potential to deter medical aspirants from seeking accommodations or reducing their course loads in the face of adversity.

Discussion: The findings inform actionable recommendations for education leaders and policymakers to promote admissions practices that promote applicant and, in turn, medical student well-being.

Methods: Seventy-one medical students from Université Laval and the University of Ottawa participated in the experience in early clerkship. Fatima Gafoor (Lead Author), Andrew D'Elia (Lead Author), Armaan Dhanoa (Co Author), Sabrina Zhu (Co Author), Eisar Al-Sukhni (Co Author), Jory Simpson (Co Author)

Methods: PRECISE launched at the University of Toronto with 15 educational events spanning surgical subspecialties, patient perspectives, and skills workshops. Sessions include: Introduction to Surgery and the Operating Room, The Art of Surgery, Patient Perspectives: Breast Cancer, Women in Surgery, and Introduction to CaRMS and Rotations in Surgery. Sessions were co-designed by students with faculty, residents, and patients. Post-session surveys assessed students' perceptions of session usefulness, educational value, and impact on surgical exposure, along with overall satisfaction.

Results: On average, PRECISE attracted over 40 students per session, with a 70% post-session survey response rate. Perceived usefulness was high (mean = 4.64/5), with the majority of students strongly agreeing that sessions were valuable. Students reported increased exposure to surgical topics not typically covered in the medical school curriculum (mean = 4.28/5) and that the sessions effectively highlighted the humanistic side of surgery (mean = 4.40/5). Participants valued early surgical exposure, mentorship, and patient perspectives, while qualitative feedback suggested increased confidence in considering surgical careers. Students also indicated a strong likelihood of recommending the event to peers (mean = 4.59/5).

Discussion: PRECISE demonstrates that a learner-led surgical curriculum is feasible, well attended, and positively received. Initial feedback suggests it enhances perceptions of surgery, supports career exploration, and provides meaningful early engagement. This model may inform future initiatives to increase surgical exposure in preclerkship education.

Huzaifa Saeed (Co Author), Husnaat Bajwa (Lead Author), Adam Hussain (Co Author)

Methods: A narrative review of literature was performed using PubMed and Google Scholar (2000-2025). Search terms included multimodal analgesia, chronic pain, curriculum, undergraduate medical education, postgraduate education.

Results: Pain curricula were inconsistent across institutions, with emphasis often limited to pharmacology. Few programs provided structured exposure to interventional procedures, non-opioid strategies, or psychosocial approaches. Integrated, case-based learning and interprofessional education models showed the greatest impact on learner confidence.

Discussion: Structured curricula emphasizing multimodal analgesia are needed for trainees in primary care, surgery, and internal medicine. Embedding chronic pain modules into existing programs may reduce opioid overreliance and improve patient outcomes.

Dr. Claire E. Kendall (Lead Author), Christine Mathew (Co Author), Isabelle Décarie (Co Author), Dr. Stephane Brutus (Co Author), Dr. Nadine Wiper-Bergeron (Co Author), Christian Tremblay (Co Author), Alain Malette (Co Author)

Methods: The inaugural sprint, scheduled for November 2025, will run over three days (20-25 hours) and engages undergraduate, graduate, and medical students. Mian-Mian Cindy Kao (Lead Author), Shelby Wallace (Co Author), Katelyn Wang (Co Author), Erika Haskings (Co Author), Simone Crooks (Co Author), Eric St Amand (Co Author), Michelle Chiu (Co Author), Curtis Nickel (Co Author)

Methods: An electronic survey was distributed to all ACLS milestone participants, followed by a focus group to enable qualitative thematic analysis. Coding and analysis were completed by 2 independent reviewers in an iterative process to ensure all important themes were identified.

Results: A total of 34 participants responded to the survey. All participants felt that peer-assessment of their performance was accurate. 62% of participants endorsed a change in stress when being evaluated by a peer, with 5 residents noting increased stress. The focus group consisted of 3 residents with the most common theme being that they enjoyed peer feedback. However, it was noted by 1 participant that feedback may be inaccurate if given by a peer.

Discussion: In high-stakes settings such as perioperative cardiac arrest, residents endorse findings similar to those observed in low-stakes settings. They report positive experiences but do have concerns with evaluation accuracy and increased stress from peer evaluation. This demonstrates that peer assessment has similar effectiveness in high-stakes learning environments to what is described in literature.

Methods: This innovation was developed using Kern and Thomas' six-step model of curriculum development. Implementation begins with a didactic lecture on effective telephone consultations and the completion of the Postgraduate Medical Education (PGME) Quality Referral and Consultation Education module (University of Saskatchewan). Approximately three months later, participants join a three-hour Zoom session with two facilitators, one confederate, and approximately 15 learners. Petar Petrov (Lead Author), Lana Bistritz (Co Author)

Methods: The curriculum was comprehensively mapped. Each teaching and learning activity was linked to relevant topic tags (e.g., content areas, MCC topics), and each Session-level objective (SLO) was aligned with the CLOs it supports. Assessment items (e.g., multiple-choice, short- and long-answer questions) were similarly mapped to topic tags and CLOs. This tagging enabled the generation of Course Analysis Reports highlighting direct links between CLO's, SLO's and assessment items to identify areas of gaps, under- or over-representation in both teaching and assessment.

Results: The reports were piloted in the 2024-2025 academic year for Pre-Clerkship Working Groups as part of our cycle of continuous curriculum quality improvement. Petar Petrov (Lead Author), Lana Bistritz (Co Author), Kelly Gibson (Co Author), Joanne Rodger (Co Author)

Methods: A comprehensive mapping of program assessments was undertaken, linking each assessment item (e.g., multiple-choice, short/long answer questions) to Course Level Objectives (CLOs) and topic tags (such as content areas and MCC topics). This tagging enabled the generation of individualized Student Performance Reports reflecting performance across these domains, with initial emphasis on Medical Expert CLOs. The reports provided students with quantitative information on how they performed against each Medical Expert CLO as well as the number of assessment questions that tested said CLO. To better understand the utility of the Student Performance Reports, data will be collected from students using surveys and focus groups.

Gabrielle Trépanier (Lead Author), Henri Garneau (Co Author), Luc Goscha (Co Author), Audrey-Anne Turcotte-Brousseau (Co Author)

Methods: A scoping review was conducted in July 2025, following the PRISMA-ScR methodology, to identify key behaviors of efficient emergency physicians. Medline/PubMed, Embase, Google Scholar, and grey literature were searched using controlled vocabulary, and included all types of methodologies except editorials and commentaries. The data were extracted through a standardized, structured process within the Covidence platform.

Results: After screening 6,343 articles, 20 articles met full inclusion criteria. Although no study directly demonstrated a specific set of strategies that improve efficiency, six major teachable themes emerged. Efficient physicians showed greater effectiveness in communication, targeted investigations, overall documentation, and clinical teaching. In addition, they employed specific cognitive and physical strategies like flow management and movement efficiency. Each theme yielded behaviors that either promoted or impeded efficiency.

Discussion: Although our review identified a limited number of articles linking specific physician behaviors to efficiency, it provides insights into behaviors and strategies that can be taught and observed in emergency medicine residents. These findings represent the first step in a broader initiative aimed at developing a standardized observational tool to support the teaching of this competency to emergency medicine residents.

Meredith Li (Lead Author), Coralea Kappel (Co Author), Kevin Fan (Co Author), Susanna Cheng (Co Author), Watson Geoffrey (Co Author)

Methods: A survey evaluating trainees' perception of the CT track was administered in July 2025 to DMO residents in PGY 4-5 and clinical fellows at U of T.

Results: A total of 20 participants (6 residents and 14 fellows) were included. 50% of trainees expressed an interest in the CT track. Only 35% of trainees knew the difference between the academic tracks and 80% did not know where to find more information. Almost all (95%) trainees wanted to know more about the CT track and 80% would like to learn about this early in their training. A minority (20%) was aware of the existing opportunities for training in medical education and teaching, however 85% still reported there was insufficient access.

Discussion: It is evident that many trainees are interested in pursuing the CT track. However, barriers such as late awareness and access to training must be overcome if we wish to provide them with the platform to pursue the CT path.

Nathan Li (Lead Author), Cheryl Holmes (Co Author), Lisa Graves (Co Author)

Methods: A narrative review (2010-2025) synthesized peer-reviewed literature on disability in medical education, admissions transparency, and the hidden curriculum, highlighting gaps, including inaccessible standards, missing accessibility contacts, limited role models with disabilities, and stigmatizing cultural signals. These were synthesized into two domains: (1) Policy Transparency (binary checklist items) and (2) Climate and Belonging (Likert-scale items). Kotter's Change Management Framework guided linking measurement to institutional change. Researcher reflexivity acknowledged the student perspective and the need to include learners with disabilities and faculty for validity.

Results: The review informed a four-step protocol for tool development: (1) literature and policy synthesis; (2) collaborators' engagement with learners with disabilities and faculty with or without disabilities; (3) item generation within the domains; and (4) testing with dual raters for reliability. The protocol defines domains and planned steps but remains under development.

Discussion: This project translates literature findings into a reproducible roadmap for medical schools to interrogate ableism and antidisableism in admissions. By addressing transparency and climate, this protocol for a self-assessment tool represents an important step toward embedding disability inclusion in Canadian medical school admissions.

Stacie Carey (Lead Author), Ryan Brydges (Co Author), Nivetha Chandran (Co Author), Dean Lising (Co Author), Melanie Anderson (Co Author), Monika Kastner (Co Author)

Methods: We developed two initial program theories (IPTs) reflecting KT researchers' assumptions: (1) education is necessary but insufficient for practice change, requiring multifactorial KT interventions; and (2) effective educational strategies incorporate active learning and/or adult learning principles. We systematically searched PsycINFO, MEDLINE, and ERIC (2010-2024), yielding 1,193 records and 36 articles for inclusion. Data were extracted using a structured template to identify study characteristics and context-mechanism-outcome configurations (CMOCs).

Results: Most studies aligned with IPT2 (74%), with only one-third of these studies reporting objective measures of practice change. Analysis of CMOCs revealed that successful practice change appeared to rely on context-specific educational strategies, ongoing consultation, and substantive system- and organizational-level enablers. Among studies aligned with IPT1 (26%), most reported objective practice change measures, and recurring CMOCs suggested successful practice change was associated with education strategies, participation incentives, tailored implementation plans, and system-level supports.

Discussion: We found that KT researchers infrequently report consulting with educational theory to tailor strategies, and establish clear organizational supports when designing KT interventions for practice change in mental health care. KT researchers would benefit from regularly attending to the context-specific learning mechanisms required to ensure the sustained practice change they intend to cultivate via their interventions.

Methods: An online course was developed with three modules, each featuring one infographic and its instructional video. Of 76 registered participants, 25 clinicians completed one or more modules and provided structured feedback through surveys. Results: Clinicians demonstrated statistically significant improvements in SC knowledge and application (p<0.05). Yazmeen Wardman (Lead Author)

Methods: This study is a retrospective data analysis of admission data from all English speaking medical school programs in Canada (2005 to 2024) from direct request or published reports. We calculated whether the proportion of admitted Indigenous medical students met or exceeded provincial and national (5%) averages of the Indigenous population of Canada for each school. Admission trends over time were determined using Poisson regression.

Results: Medical school admissions data were available from 12/14 medical schools. The proportion of Indigenous students admitted relative to the medical school class ranged from 0% to 17% with a median of 4.2% (mean 5.8% (SD 4.07)) during the period evaluated. Overall the number of Indigenous student applications and admissions to medical school increased over time. Four schools met the provincial averages and five met national averages based on the most recent year's data. The Northern Ontario School of Medicine, University of Calgary and the University of Saskatchewan had the highest national and provincial averages.

Discussion: Although the number of Indigenous student applications is increasing, indicating Indigenous-specific programs have helped meet the goal of enrolling a representative proportion of Indigenous students into medical schools in Canada, less than half of schools surveyed are currently achieving Indigenous admissions parity with provincial or national Indigenous population averages.

Sierra Land (Lead Author), Hailey Land (Co Author), Jordyn Linders (Co Author), Kay-Anne Haykal (Co Author)

Methods: An anonymous bilingual survey was distributed between May and August 2024 to Doctor of Medicine students at the University of Ottawa. The validated 40-item Medical Student Stressor Questionnaire was used to assess six domains of stress. Sociodemographic variables included age, year of study, sex, LGBTQ2S+ identity, ethnicity, rural or urban upbringing, distance from campus, living arrangements, income, financial status, non-academic work hours, disability status, and dependents. Between-group differences were assessed using t-tests and ANOVA, with multivariate linear regression to examine associations.

Results: Sixty students participated. Academic-related stressors (ARS) were most prominent, particularly "Large amount of content," "Need to do well," "Workload," and "Exams and tests." Pre-clerkship students reported higher stress than clerkship counterparts. Male students experienced significantly greater ARS (p = 0.006), while students with disabilities reported higher group activity-related stress (p = 0.034). Living with family was associated with greater teaching- and learning-related stress (p = 0.005). No significant associations were found with ethnicity, income, or financial supports.

Discussion: Stress is widespread among medical trainees, with distinct sociodemographic vulnerabilities. Findings underscore the need for both universal and targeted wellness interventions to foster equity and learner well-being in undergraduate medical education.

Ahmad Hassan (Lead Author), Kareem Sadek (Co Author), Joshua Seto (Co Author), Omar El Ferri (Co Author), Mike Paget (Co Author), Irene Ma (Co Author), Janeve Desy (Co Author)

Methods: A series of audio transcripts from faculty-recorded comprehensive single-topic lectures are summarized using four LLMs (OpenAI ChatGPT, Anthropic Claude, Google Gemini, and Microsoft Copilot), with a fixed prompt for comparability. Generated lecture summary notes are rated with a modified version of the Provider Documentation Summarization Quality Instrument (PDSQI-9) by three blinded medical student reviewers and a faculty content expert. REB25-1304 pending.

Results: Each LLM is evaluated in terms of accuracy, thoroughness, usefulness, organization, comprehensibility, succinctness, and synthesis. Summary notes are analyzed for substantive validity, supported by correlations between note length and scoring for succinctness and organization. Structural validity and content validity are highlighted by factor analysis of accuracy, thoroughness, usefulness, and comprehensibility. Within-model and between-model LLM differences are demonstrated by inter-rater reliability and discriminant validity.

Discussion: This study represents the first comprehensive benchmarking of widely used LLMs for summarization of medical lectures and generation of educational lecture notes, providing evidence-based guidance for safe and effective integration in undergraduate medical education and beyond. It also lays the groundwork for a validated, centralized LLM-supported learning workflow which UME programs can use.

Luxshikka Canthiya (Lead Author), Amirashkan Bouriayee (Co Author), Michael Wang (Co Author), Rida Qamar (Co Author), Anita Acai (Co Author), Beatrice Preti (Co Author)

Methods: Twelve pre-medical students were recruited through online forum posts and snowball sampling. Semi-structured interviews explored their perceptions and decision making. Reflective thematic analysis was conducted, with regular team debriefings.

Results: Four themes were developed: a) students sought formal and informal influences (medical role models, media depictions) to construct understandings of the application process, both informing and perpetuating accurate and inaccurate expectations; b) they engaged in early, performative strategizing, to navigate the perceived competitive system, often at the expense of intrinsic exploration and well-being; c) this iterative process involved trial, adaptation and reflection, with pressures to conform to perceived norms of the ideal medical student "archetype"; and d) students experienced a tension between authenticity versus "what the system wants," often perpetuating unhealthy attitudes towards identity formation and self-concept.

Discussion: Findings aligned with a contemplation-led constructivist model, where limited transparency and confirmation bias formed an "echo-chamber." This authenticity-desirability tension reflects impression management and the hidden curriculum. Our findings suggest a need for more transparent, outreach opportunities between the medical community and pre-medical students, to avoid the echo-chamber and reduce inauthenticity and burnout.

Andrea Guerin (Lead Author), Eleni Katsoulas (Co Author), Danielle Blouin (Co Author)

Methods: We introduced a new "Zone Number" entry into the gradebook for pre-clerkship final exam assessments in large, exam-based courses. Each student's score was converted into a z-score using the formula =STANDARDIZE(score, mean, standard deviation). Based on their z-score, students were categorized into one of four performance zones: Red (Zone 1: >2 SD below mean), Yellow (Zone 2: >1.5 SD below mean), Green (Zone 3: within ±1.5 SD), or Blue (Zone 4: >1.5 SD above mean). Each zone corresponded to a standardized narrative description, delivered via the learning management system. This narrative contextualized the student's performance relative to the class and provided encouragement or guidance as appropriate.

Results: In a pilot course, 93% of students were categorized in the Green zone, 4% in Red, 3% in Yellow, and the remainder in Blue. This distribution allowed for clear communication of performance benchmarks and facilitated early identification of students requiring support. Each zone corresponds to a personalized narrative message consistent with the performance zone that can be provided to the student.

Discussion: This standardized z-score and zone-based narrative assessment strategy offers a feasible, efficient approach to providing individualized feedback in large classes. It enables educators to promote student reflection and support academic success, even in high-enrollment settings without individualized observation.

Joshua Choi (Lead Author), Jenny Zhao (Co Author)

Methods: A within-subject experimental design was employed. Eight participants (academic clinicians, faculty researchers, medical students, and a community member) rated essays written by 24 undergraduate students and recent graduates from McMaster University. The students were divided into four groups: medical school aspirants with AI assistance (ASP-AI), aspirants without AI assistance (ASP), non-aspirants with AI assistance (NASP-AI), and essays generated solely by ChatGPT 3.5 (AI-ONLY). Participants were provided training in the application of single Likert scale assessment tool before rating. Differences in ratings by writer group were determined via one-way between group ANOVA.

Results: Analyses revealed no statistically significant differences in ratings across the four writer groups (p = .358). The intraclass correlation coefficient was .147.

Discussion: The proliferation of AI adds to prevailing questions about the value personal statements and essays have in supporting applicant selection. We speculate that these assessments hold less value than ever in providing authentic insight into applicant attributes. We use our findings to explore recommendations for medical schools to move away from using essays in admissions.

Meera Rayar (Co Author), Donna Johnston (Lead Author), Serina Patel (Co Author), Roona Sinha (Co Author), Christine Sabapathy (Co Author), Chiquita Hessels (Co Author), Laura Wheaton (Co Author), Dawn Pickering (Co Author), Stephanie Grover (Co Author)

Methods: The 2025 PHOCESD was held in Toronto over two days. All Canadian PHO trainees and program directors (PDs) were invited. The agenda was developed collaboratively by RCPSC PHO committee members, Persons with Lived Experience (PWLE), and Advancing Childhood Cancer Experience, Science and Survivorship (ACCESS), the primary sponsor. Sessions covered career development, research communication, a PWLE panel, and a trainee research showcase. Conference evaluation followed Kirkpatrick's model.

Results: Twenty-one trainees and three PDs attended. Of the 21 who completed evaluations (15 trainees, 5 attending physicians, 1 other), all somewhat or strongly agreed they gained new, applicable career knowledge, and felt more enthusiastic about their future. Additionally, 76% strongly agreed and 24% somewhat agreed that the conference enabled valuable networking beyond their home institutions. Trainees highlighted the value of patient-perspective sessions on cancer treatment and survivorship.

Discussion: A dedicated subspecialty conference fosters career development and networking. Integrating PWLE into the program provides unique, meaningful insight and enhances the educational experience for PHO trainees.

Melissa Graham (Lead Author), Abbas Ghavam-Rassoul (Lead Author), Christie Billings (Co Author), Ancy Jacob (Co Author), Tobi Lam (Co Author), Sandra Fournier (Co Author), Anna Loi (Co Author)

Methods: An anonymous survey was sent to 146 graduates and current students from the past 10 years who were in a Family Medicine stream graduate degrees or who were Family Physicians in the Health Professions Education stream. We conducted follow-up interviews with selected participants to clarify responses and obtain richer data. Descriptive statistics were used to quantify the survey responses. Content analysis was conducted with open text comments; using quantifiable metrics and themes in aggregate to describe the data.

Results: We received 36 survey responses (24.7% response rate) and conducted 9 semi-structured interviews. Themes pertaining to learner and program outcomes included enhanced understanding of healthcare systems lens, feeling empowered to lead change, and broadened career paths and new opportunities. Program components important to maintain include subject matter relevance and small classes that allow for networking and collaboration. Participants identified the practicum experience as an important learning opportunity to apply new skills. Areas for future program innovation included curriculum augmentation and review of course delivery methods. Added coursework in change management, program evaluation, and leadership was recommended. There was variable preferences for delivery methods. 64% preferred a mix of both in-person and virtual learning while only 2.7% preferred all online and 11% preferring all in-person.

Discussion: While academic medicine continues to evolve in terms of necessary knowledge and skills, foundational courses in education, scholarship, policy, and leadership remain essential for today's graduate learner. Online options enhanced access to courses for practicing professionals while in-person experiences allowed for discussion and development of connection with peers and faculty. Findings suggest learners desire is mixture of experiences rather than fully online or all in-person learning.

Patricia Farrugia (Co Author), Andy Deprato (Lead Author), Lana Moayad (Co Author), Abia Sunil (Co Author)

Methods: A mixed-methods survey was distributed to the McMaster University medical school class of 2026 assessing tutorial, clinical skill, and anatomy components of the MSK pre-clerkship curriculum and how this shaped preparedness for clerkship. Rating-based and open-ended questions were analyzed through descriptive and thematic analyses with an inductive approach.

Results: Experiences from twenty-six students identified satisfaction with clerkship preparation from clinical skills, though students felt underprepared by tutorials and anatomy. Thematic analysis revealed difficulty with self-teaching the large volume of MSK content, concerns with limited curricular time dedicated to MSK teaching, questions regarding the applicability of PBL for learning anatomy, and requests for didactic anatomy sessions with cadaveric models and summative assessments.

Discussion: This study demonstrated strengths in the PBL approach for in-depth procedural knowledge, though limitations became apparent with the significant MSK content volume in McMaster's four-week pre-clerkship subunit. Noted shortcomings in Canadian medical school MSK curricula and student calls for increased structure suggest that a hybridized delivery through multiple teaching modalities could optimize student learning in the short periods allocated to MSK teaching in the undergraduate medical curriculum.

Results: Both resident and PFAC members described an overall positive experience, noting value in incorporating the patient's voice in QI projects. Whereas residents unanimously described a positive experience, PFAC members' experiences were mixed.

Maximilien Roy (Lead Author), Magali Brousseau-Foley (Co Author), François Parent (Co Author), Marie-Hélène Girouard (Co Author)

Methods: The Mauricie campus of the University of Montreal's Faculty of Medicine, in collaboration with a partner university's healthcare training programs, developed a two-week sports medicine rotation to provide hands-on experience in medical care at competition sites and at the central health hub during the eight-day event.

Results: Seven medical clerks and four family medicine residents participated. Pre-clerkship online training covered safe sport interventions for young athletes and common medical scenarios encountered in sports medicine, supplemented by optional emergency simulations at competition sites during the event. Under the supervision of physicians and licensed professionals, learners collaborated with athletic therapists, chiropractors, nurses, occupational therapists, and psychologists. For most medical trainees, being present at the sidelines of the sport fields provided a novel setting for their experience. The team conducted nearly 800 diverse interventions over eight days.

Discussion: The preparation of the rotation, its interdisciplinary structure, its implementation, and its outcomes are presented. The pedagogical objectives of the rotation and the learning methods (pre-training, simulations, activities across different sites, and feeback) are explained. The results of the trainees' experience are presented. This innovative rotation provided a robust, unique learning environment. Learners highly valued the targeted training, practical experience, and interdisciplinary collaboration.

Anurag Saxena (Lead Author), Loni Desanghere (Co Author), Tayyab Shah (Co Author), Kathy Lawrence (Co Author), Steve Milosavljevic (Co Author), Marla Davidson (Co Author)

Methods: Physician data (2014, 2024) from CPSS website, were cleaned, categorized by specialty, gender, and training background, and standardized for geocoding. Practice locations were aggregated and mapped in ArcGIS Pro. Population data (2011, 2021) from Statistics Canada were joined with census geography to generate centroids. Accessibility was analyzed using the 3-Step Floating Catchment Area (3SFCA) method, which modeled how access diminishes as population distance from a FP grows. Access scores were mapped and categorized into five levels (poor- to-high physician access).

Results: Saskatchewan is served by 1,445 family physicians across 295 clinics, with most trained outside the province. Between 2014 and 2024, physician numbers grew faster than the population. Access remains uneven, with urban centres offering the best availability, as shown by access scores. Physicians increasingly practice in cities, especially University of Saskatchewan graduates, who are significantly less likely to work in rural areas compared to those trained elsewhere.

Discussion: These findings offer vital insights to guide strategic planning and quality improvement efforts within the province. Limitations include discrepancies between this research and other sources, such as the medical services branch, which are likely due to definition or methodological differences. Resolving discrepancies is essential for accurately identifying and addressing health system gaps to support targeted improvements.

Marianne Yeung (Lead Author), Elif Cakal (Co Author), James Kwan (Co Author), Stella Yiu (Co Author)

Methods: ICEM 2025 delegates from 65 nations and ranging from trainees to physician leaders were invited to an interactive poster session "How to Build a Toxic Emergency Department." They discussed the literature, applied +/neutral/- stickers to reveal perceptions of their workplaces on 6 key elements of culture, and wrote solutions for implementation by institutions, departments, individuals. We tallied stickers, transcribed responses, analysed for themes.

Results: Of 87 responses, most agreed/neutral: group norms are explicit, organizational barriers are addressed, all may speak freely, can access mentorship/peer support. Most were neutral/negative that their workplace provides conflict management strategies or addresses bullying/harassment. Solutions - 30 institution, 36 department, 28 individual, focused on relationship building, expectations/accountability, self-reflection and wellness, supports/training.

Discussion: This work provides insights to physicians looking to implement improvements in workplace culture and wellness.

Shujiao Wang (Lead Author), Sabrina Fallavollita (Co Author), Marian Jazvac-Martek (Co Author), Melanie Mondou (Co Author)

Methods: New EPA forms combining structured checklists with overall entrustment ratings were developed and harmonized with existing PGME forms. To accommodate workload constraints, conceptually linked EPAs were consolidated and strategically assigned based on course length and characteristics. Completed EPA forms were used as data to quantitatively analyze progressions, spread and usage of the form , while narrative feedback was analyzed thematically for patterns, strengths, and areas for improvement.

Results: Implementation yielded high completion and pass rates across courses, and entrustment ratings increased with learner seniority, establishing initial construct validity. Narrative feedback to students was overall specific and actionable, providing valuable insights for learners' development. Certain comments, however, were less focused, suggesting users framing comments in terms of ITER feedback structure. Additionally, some inconsistent use of the "next steps" sections of the forms, suggests further ameliorations required with support from faculty development.

Discussion: Our findings support the feasibility of comprehensive EPA implementation in UGME with strategic design and integration. This approach captured students' developmental progression, supporting the potential of such systems to track growth over time (Lomis et al., 2021). This underscores the importance for faculty development to support consistent and effective feedback. These insights advance the refinement of EPA use in UGME and offer practical guidance for institutions pursuing similar initiatives.

Certina Ho Nguyen (Lead Author) Justin Lee (Co Author) Anna Nguyen (Co Author) Jana Lazor (Co Author) Lu Gao (Co Author)

Methods: Following the 2020-2021 academic year, pre- and post-targeted faculty development student evaluations of the DPS seminar and faculty tutors were collected, analyzed, and compared using 5-point Likert scale items.

Results: One hundred and forty-five medical students provided evaluation responses between 2019 and 2024. After the targeted faculty development implementation in 2022, thematic analysis of student evaluations showed no further concerns about psychological safety, clearer learning objectives and debrief. Students perceived improved engagement and expressed a desire to have similar simulations added to the curriculum. There was significant improvement in student evaluations of the DPS simulation (3.99 vs 4.65, p<0.001) and faculty tutors (4.21 vs 4.77, p<0.001).

Discussion: A targeted faculty development approach can be initiated from learner evaluations to address specific teaching gaps for simulation seminars. Going forwards, a similar approach may be considered for curriculum and faculty development in medical education.

Motahareh Karimijashni (Lead Author), Aleisha Fernandes (Co Author), Ramin Banimahdi (Co Author), Asefeh Memarian (Co Author), Elham Shakiba (Co Author), Amanda Doherty-Kirby (Co Author), Yan Défossés (Co Author), Jacqueline Josee van Ierssel (Co Author), Annette Majnemer (Co Author), Ada Tang (Co Author), Maureen Smith (Co Author), Cory E. Goldstein (Co Author), Monica Taljaard (Co Author), Dean Fergusson (Co Author), Stuart Nicholls (Lead Author)

Methods: A scoping review was conducted in accordance with PRISMA guidelines. Eligible RCTs were identified by searching ClinicalTrials.gov registered between 2010 and 2022, aligning with Accelerating Clinical Trials (ACT) consortium initiatives. All age groups and phases of trials were included . Data extraction was guided by established patient engagement frameworks, focusing on patient engagement beyond participation roles.

Results: We identified 6,148 RCTs, for which we could identify 2,319 published trial reports. Of these, 367(15.8%) had a published protocol. A total of 142 RCTs (6.1%) reported patient engagement, with 34/142 (24%) only reporting details in study protocols. Among RCTs reporting patient engagement, this was primarily in designing, developing or delivering the intervention (56%), conducting formative research activities (17%), and developing research questions (14%). Patients were co-authors in 14/142 RCTs (10%).

Discussion: Reported patient engagement in Canadian RCTs is low. However, until recently SPIRIT and CONSORT guidelines did not require reporting patient engagement, which may have led to under-reporting. The 2025 SPIRIT and CONSORT updates recommend including patient engagement in trial reporting. This may increase reporting in future RCTs.
