Sachdeep Rehsia (Lead Author), Sheila Holmes (Co Author), Suzanne Cook (Co Author), Clare Hutchinson (Co Author)

Methods: PCC provides structured opportunities for students to learn, practice, and be assessed on their communication and clinical skills. The course is deeply integrated with TMUSoM's case-based learning curriculum, using patient profiles inspired by the diverse community of Brampton and the Peel Region. Students practice with standardized patients (SPs) and mannequins, with a strong focus on person-centred, trauma and violence-informed, strength-based, and culturally safe care. A key component is early clinical immersion through a Primary Care Longitudinal Experience (PCLE), where students join a family medicine practice biweekly over one year. Entrustable Professional Activities (EPAs) are the primary assessment tool in classrooms, clinics, and OSCEs.

Results: Launched in September 2025, PCC offers all medical students early clinical immersion via PCLE and early integration of standardized patients. The content is delivered through small-group, case-based learning sessions facilitated by physicians, with clinical scenarios reflecting the diversity of the local community. EPAs serve as the main assessment tool throughout the pre-clerkship phase and in streamlined OSCEs. A significant challenge identified is finding a sufficiently diverse SP population to provide an authentic learning experience for students.

Discussion: PCC offers students innovative ways to learn clinical skills with SPs and practice them in a real clinical environment through PCLE, better preparing them to meet patient and community needs. The curriculum uses innovative approaches such as a flipped classroom structure, integration with other courses, and early clinical experiences. The goal is to emphasize the importance of diversity to reflect the population surrounding the school and many parts of Ontario.

Christine Comer (Lead Author), Ben Foxcroft (Lead Author), Victoria Carruthers (Co Author), Soujanya Yarlagadda (Co Author), Jill Halstead (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted four interrelated workstreams to evaluate research activity and perceptions of research culture: (1) A database review to quantify research studies delivered; (2) A literature review of journal publications or conference abstracts by staff; (3) An online survey of staff using two validated rating scales; (4) Semi-structured interviews to explore research perceptions and aspirations across the organisation.

Results: One-third of services were research-active, delivering research and/or publishing research. Services with embedded clinical academics demonstrated the highest research activity. Service-level managers' perceptions of research capacity did not match the more optimistic perceptions of senior Trust leaders. Staff and senior leaders reported an aspiration to improve the level of research attainment across all staff groups. Senior leaders aspired to the highest level of research attainment for advanced practitioners.

Discussion: Whilst there was variation in research activity across services and disparities in perceptions across staff groups, there was a universal aspiration to achieve higher levels of research attainment. To facilitate this, developing advanced practice research capability and establishing research leadership within services may be important. These findings informed an updated research strategy and provides a replicable framework for other healthcare providers to evaluate their organisation.

Steve Truong (Lead Author), Sylvain Boet (Co Author), Melyna Désy Bedard (Co Author), Cole Etherington (Co Author), Pierre-Marc Dion (Co Author), Megan Delisle (Co Author), Alexander Hannenberg (Co Author), Nicholas Prudhomme (Co Author), Manon Denis-Leblanc (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a pilot study delivering the VISION Simulation Training to assess feasibility, acceptability and identification of facilitators and barriers to implementation. Two training sessions were offered to full-time emergency department (ED) staff at a single academic hospital in Ontario. Feasibility defined as ≥50% attendance of full-time staff. Acceptability was assessed using a 37-item survey adapted from the System Usability Scale. Barriers and facilitators were assessed using the Theoretical Domains Framework survey and subsequently barriers to implementation were mapped on the capability, opportunity and motivation model of behaviour and in turn onto the Behavior Change Wheel to identify intervention strategies for each barrier.

Results: There were 45 attendances. Feasibility was achieved for physicians (14/17), but not for nurses (12/40) or respiratory therapists (0/6). All SUS Likert items had medians >4.

Ewa Akomolafe (Co Author), Aisling Martins-Ezeifeaku (Lead Author), Darshana Seeburruth (Co Author), Edgar Akuffo-Addo (Co Author)

Methods: Using purposive sampling to ensure representation across geographical context, we recruited Canadian medical students from schools with (WDR) and without (NDR) dermatology residency programs. Online semi-structured interviews were conducted and transcribed verbatim. Transcripts were analyzed inductively using reflexive thematic analysis to identify key themes and subthemes.

Results: Data collection and analysis are ongoing. Emerging themes highlight disparities in career exposure, teaching, and research opportunities between students at schools WDR vs NDR. Students at regional campuses of schools WDR reported limited access to local dermatologists for shadowing and fewer opportunities compared to their peers on the main campus. Students at schools NDR described a paucity of formal teaching, a reliance on dermatologists at other institutions, and a lack of departmental support.

Discussion: This study provides insights into how institutional structures shape access to dermatology. Students at NDR face barriers such as limited career exposure, fewer research opportunities, and a paucity of formal mentorship and curricula. These inequities may disadvantage students in preparing for electives and pursuing dermatology as a specialty. Findings may inform the development of national strategies to improve equity in dermatology training opportunities across Canada.

Tania Dumont (Lead Author), Anat From (Co Author), Glenn Posner (Co Author), Linda McLean (Co Author)

Methods: The study involved curriculum design with an embedded evaluation component. Pediatric and adolescent gynecology (PAG) fellows participated in a 1-hour simulation-based session and completed pre- and post-session surveys assessing self-reported confidence in performing a Y-plasty on a 5-point Likert scale. Confidence ratings before and after the session were compared using the Wilcoxon Signed Rank test. Curriculum acceptability was evaluated using the NPS, with a score >20% indicating strong dissemination potential.

Results: A high-fidelity 3-D printed TVS model and structured hands-on curriculum were delivered to 36 fellows during their 2025 international "bootcamp." Of these, 24 fellows (66.7 %) had never performed a Y-plasty prior to the session. Median confidence improved significantly from 2/5 ("slightly confident") to 3/5 ("moderately confident") following the session (p < 0.001). Fellows estimated a maximum of four additional sessions would be required to achieve procedural competency. The curriculum achieved an NPS of 51.5%, indicating high perceived value and strong potential for adoption and dissemination.

Discussion: A single exposure to this 3-D printed simulation curriculum significantly improved fellows' confidence in performing Y-plasty for TVS resection. An NPS exceeding 50% underscores the curriculum's perceived value and supports broader implementation across fellowship programs.

Eola Saltibus (Lead Author), Alexa Hryniuk (Co Author), Terry Li (Co Author)

Methods: Data extraction focused on six of Harden's ten windows for curriculum map exploration using curricular content from the 2022/2023 academic year. A 32-question assessment of medical image interpretive skills was distributed among graduating students. Basic descriptive statistics were used to characterize student performance while one-way ANOVA was used to compare performance across four imaging modalities.

Results: While 25 medical imaging sessions were identified representing 35.4 curricular hours most medical imaging references in learning resources were found integrated outside of these formal sessions. Radiographs were integrated more often especially compared to ultrasound (58% and 9% respectively). A mean score of 64% ± 4.5 (with a range from 25% to 97%) was achieved by students who completed the medical imaging assessment. Discussion: Students' performance prior to graduation reflects their imaging exposure and its integration within the curriculum. This evaluation has identified gaps which can be improved specifically the inadequate instructional time and limited integration of ultrasound training. Program administrators and curriculum developers at other institutions can use similar methods for curriculum exploration, assessment and improvement.

Jaimie Coleman (Lead Author), Alison Shaw (Lead Author), Briana Neang (Co Author), Erica Long (Co Author), Janet Li (Co Author), Yunqi Ji (Co Author), Ryan Brydges (Co Author), Sharon Gabison (Co Author)

Methods: A quasi-experimental design was conducted with 150 pre-clinical Master of Physical Therapy students. All participants completed a virtual simulation on oxygen titration during regular coursework. We only included those participants consenting to complete the outcome measures, randomly assigning them to either a prompt group (PG) or a no-prompt group (NPG). The prompt consisted of a text encouraging participants to carefully evaluate each response option before making a decision. Clinical reasoning was assessed using a multiple-choice quiz, and perceived usefulness was measured via a modified Technology Acceptance Questionnaire. We conducted group comparisons using the Wilcoxon Rank Sum test.

Results: Forty-four students participated (PG: n=24; NPG: n=18). We did not observe any statistically significant differences between groups in clinical reasoning scores (p = 0.660) or perceived usefulness (p = 0.437).

Discussion: In exploring the lack of impact of the prompt, we judged that participants likely did not find the prompt engaging, nor directive enough regarding what 'carefully evaluating responses' means. Future research should explore alternative prompt designs, timing of prompts, and whether complementary activities will better support reflective decision-making in virtual simulations.

Nadine Wiper-Bergeron (Lead Author), Holly Adam (Co Author), Claire Kendall (Co Author), Chris Kennedy (Co Author), Kaylee Eady (Co Author), Katherine Moreau (Co Author)

Methods: First-year graduate students (n=111) in a mandatory professionalism course at our faculty of medicine were invited to complete a survey assessing the perceived relevance of SA, intentions to integrate SA in research, and perceived confidence/support to do so.

Results: Over 80% of respondents (n=87) agreed SA is relevant to their training and expressed commitment to integrating SA principles into future research. However, relatively few felt confident or institutionally supported to enact SA in their projects, revealing an implementation gap between attitudes and actionable capacity.

Discussion: Graduate learners endorse the importance of SA but need explicit training, supports, and structures to operationalize it in biomedical research. We propose that SA awareness should anchor professionalism curricula for biomedical scientists and that the Boelen & Heck's WHO "partnership pentagon" should be expanded to a "double partnership pentagram" that adds research-ecosystem partners (funders, certification bodies) alongside health-system stakeholders to align academic research priorities with social need and accountability metrics. This framework offers a practical pathway for faculties of medicine to embed SA in graduate programs, bridging intention and implementation and advancing socially accountable discovery.

Vidhi Bhatt (Lead Author), Dream Tuitt-Barnes (Co Author), Dionne Banton (Co Author), Ike Okafor (Co Author)

Methods: A one-time electronic survey was administered to all 2025 SMP graduates, assessing pre- and post- program changes in i) academic skills, ii) identity, and iii) career aspirations. All graduates (n=102) were Ontario high school students and participated voluntarily, independent of program evaluation. Survey completion implied consent, yielding a 100% response rate.

Results: The cohort was 50.0% male (51/102); 86.3% (88/102) identified as Black, 9.8% (10/102) as Indigenous, and 3.9% (4/102) as both. Anjali Saxena (Lead Author), Adam Hussain (Co Author), Bev Digout (Co Author), Vanessa Hindmarsh (Co Author), Meredith McKague (Co Author), Ginger Ruddy (Co Author)

Methods: Phase II of this multi-phase study (Phase I: literature review, Phase III: student survey) involves semi-structured interviews of leaders and faculty in Student Affairs and Professionalism Offices across 19 CMS. Institutional and personal approaches to teaching, assessing, supporting, and expanding conceptualization of professionalism were explored. Transcripts and summaries were inductively analyzed for emergent themes.

Results: 9/19 institutions have participated in interviews thus far. There is widespread recognition that individual identities, wellness, and professionalism are interrelated, yet challenges persist in incorporating these relationships in PP&P. Seven key themes emerged across policy (transparency, operationalization, diversity in collaboration, and social accountability), learning (longitudinal and community-based) and support (non-punitive approaches). All CMS utilize multiple teaching and assessment methods and have confirmed recent or incoming PP&P revisions. 1/9 CMS explicitly incorporate EDI concepts into PP&P; other institutional revisions include inter-professional collaboration, procedural clarity, and protocol for addressing minor lapses.

Discussion: The need to incorporate wellness and inclusivity in PP&P is recognized in the contemporary context of societal expectations and learners' needs, yet it is not uniformly executed. Understanding the theoretical grounding, rationales, and approaches used to update PP&P at CMS may inform further revisions to policies and procedures for teaching, assessing and addressing professionalism.

Megan Saad (Lead Author) Azin Dolatabadi (Lead Author) AlexaKouroukis (Lead Author) Qurrat ul Ain (Co Author) Melissa Graham (Co Author) Fok-Han Leung (Co Author)

Methods: Five semi-structured Zoom interviews (25-45 minutes on average) were conducted from February to March 2025 with IMGs enrolled in family medicine residency at the University of Toronto. A qualitative thematic analysis identified key themes.

Results: Five main themes emerged: 1) general challenges of residency, 2) IMG-specific challenges, 3) factors that mitigated challenges specific to IMGs, 4) wellness supports, and 5) suggestions to improve IMG wellness.

Discussion: Our results are consistent with previous research highlighting that IMGs have a unique and specific subset of challenges that threaten their wellness in residency, in addition to the general demands of residency training. Participants highlighted various factors that helped mitigate these specific challenges in addition to the need for personal wellness strategies and ongoing program development to better support future IMGs. Future research should focus on developing targeted strategies to enhance the wellbeing of IMGs in order to improve patient care and promote diversity and inclusion in residency programs.

Francesco Vito (Lead Author), Nick Teller (Co Author), Aran Bains (Co Author), Lauren Digby (Co Author), Kim Nguyen (Co Author), Terri Lawrence (Co Author), Anna Farias (Co Author)

Methods: The sessions were run in a station-based format, with multiple cadaver stations that M1 students would rotate through. An optional, anonymous survey was given to participating M1s, with a separate form used for a gift card incentive. The survey assessed program effectiveness, perceived course performance, and confidence in course content.

Results: Overall, 70.5% (n=31) of the M1 class attended WAM sessions, and 54.8% (n=17) of attendees completed the survey. Among respondents, 88.2% strongly agreed that WAM improved their preparation for the bell-ringer, 88.2% reported greater confidence in anatomy knowledge, and 58.8% felt the sessions supported retention compared to self-study. All students agreed that the near-peer teaching model fostered a positive learning environment, and 94% endorsed the station-based format as effective for learning. No participants reported additional stress, and 100% recommended continuation of the program for future classes.

Discussion: In summary, M1 students found WAM helpful and M2 mentors appreciated the opportunity to strengthen their anatomy knowledge. Near-peer led initiatives represent a valuable model for anatomy education, especially at distributed campuses with limited teaching resources.

Methods: Des entrevues semi-dirigées furent réalisées auprès des stagiaires, puis transcrites et analysées à l'aide du logiciel NVivo. Valérie Rochon (Co Author), Rania Bouzeriba (Co Author), Geneviève Gauthier (Co Author)

Methods: L'étude comprend un sondage et des entrevues semi-dirigées, guidées par un cadre conceptuel sur les usages du plan de cours. Elle cherche à comprendre, du point de vue d'étudiants en santé, comment ce document soutient la communication, la planification et la socialisation. L'analyse thématique tient également compte des contextes institutionnel et programmatique.

Results: Les analyses révèlent que les étudiants considèrent le plan comme un repère essentiel, mais qu'ils n'en lisent que les sections jugées pertinentes. Un « bon » plan précise attentes, objectifs et critères d'évaluation, offrant une première impression de la qualité du cours. Il facilite la planification en aidant à anticiper les échéances, à prioriser les tâches et à gérer la charge inter-cours, réduisant stress et incertitude. À l'inverse, des incohérences ou un manque de détails poussent les étudiants à consulter leurs pairs ou les cohortes précédentes pour clarifier les attentes. Le plan prépare aux modalités de socialisation, même si la classe demeure le principal lieu d'intégration.

Discussion: Le plan de cours joue un rôle clé dans les relations étudiants-enseignants-institution. Ses limites montrent que sa fonction varie contextuellement et invitent à repenser son élaboration pour maximiser sa valeur pédagogique.

Manveer Bal (Lead Author) Marie Leung (Co Author) Allie Chown (Co Author) Taylor Murie (Co Author)

Methods: An Internal Medicine simulation program was developed for clerkship students, featuring four high-yield cases (delirium, chest pain, dyspnea, hypotension). To promote psychological safety, the curriculum incorporates the early distribution of learning outcomes with pre-reading materials, a standardized pre-briefing script that emphasizes safety and a two-learner leadership model that fosters shared responsibility while distributing cognitive load. A near-peer facilitation model is also employed, with residents rather than faculty delivering sessions to create a supportive, approachable atmosphere. Post-session surveys will evaluate perceptions of psychological safety.

Results: Data collection is ongoing. Planned evaluation includes quantitative survey items (perceptions of psychological safety) and qualitative feedback (free-text reflections on session design). Participants will also be asked to specifically evaluate the dual-leadership structure, resident-led facilitation, and clarity from the early distribution of learning outcomes and pre-reading materials.

Discussion: This project integrates psychological safety into simulation design through structured pre-briefing, dual leadership, and near-peer facilitation. We anticipate that these strategies will strengthen learner engagement. The curriculum provides a replicable model for clerkship-level simulation, with future evaluations focusing on assessing its impact on clinical reasoning, teamwork and readiness for transition to residency.

Nermin Gergis (Lead Author), Heather Nowak (Co Author), Shawn Thomas (Co Author), Sarah Harvey (Co Author), Carrie Fruehm (Co Author), Angela Hahn (Co Author), Jonathan Gabor (Co Author), Tabitha Bear (Co Author)

Methods: Multiple sources were used to collect data. We conducted extensive chart review for all critical incident reports, Code 25 codes, and patients' complaints. Code 25 is called when any critically ill family medicine ward patient requires an emergent reassessment of their clinical situation. Detailed reviews were conducted on Fifty patients charts during the period April 1, 2024, to October 31,2024. Reviews were conducted by the family medicine site lead and the nursing director.

Results: Many unperceived needs were common amongst the various health care team members and interfered with their reasoning and decision-making skills. This resulted in inaccurate judgments and the inability to reach the appropriate diagnosis. The most identified cognitive biases were anchoring, premature closure and bandwagon effect.

Discussion: A team-based learning event was planned. Team members demonstrated managing multiple case-based scenarios developed from the chart revies. Team members were given the opportunity to identify the presence of cognitive biases and to implement strategies to address them.

Julia Howcroft (Lead Author), Elif Bilgic (Co Author), Quang Ngo (Co Author), Anita Acai (Co Author), Spencer van Mil (Co Author), Jonathan Duff (Co Author)

Methods: This interpretive descriptive study, funded by a Royal College of Physicians and Surgeons of Canada Medical Education Research Grant, is situated within a social constructionist framework and guided by the Core Components Framework for evaluating CBD implementation. Participants included pediatric and subspecialty residents and faculty from Canadian programs. Semi-structured interviews followed a demographic survey, and data were analyzed using Braun and Clarke's thematic analysis.

Results: Nineteen participants (eight faculty, 11 residents) from multiple Canadian institutions and specialties (pediatrics, pediatric emergency medicine, neonatology) were interviewed. Preliminary findings highlight key skills, including knowledge of anatomy and indications, the ability to identify landmarks and troubleshoot complications, and decision-making throughout the procedure. For IO, decision-making and managing emotions were most challenging. For CTI, the greatest difficulties were psychomotor, particularly depth perception and needle positioning.

Discussion: Findings will inform curriculum and assessment strategies to enhance competence and improve patient safety.

Roy Khalife (Lead Author), Emily Kraft (Co Author), Lindsay Cowley (Co Author), Vanessa Bourck (Co Author), Kelsey Uminski (Co Author), Hao Wei (Linda) Sun (Co Author), Calvin Kruger (Co Author), Hans Rosenberg (Co Author), Warren Cheung (Co Author), Susan Humphrey-Murto (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted an exploratory, sequential mixed-methods study. An online survey of ED nurses and physicians, distributed via national organizations, used four clinical vignettes to probe triage, diagnostic approach, initial management, and self-rated knowledge; quantitative data were summarized descriptively. Virtual focus groups then explored barriers and solutions and were analyzed using reflexive thematic analysis.

Results: Three hundred ten clinicians completed the survey (250 nurses; 60 physicians). Most reported prior PwBD experience, but fewer had administered specialized hemostatic products. Across vignettes, respondents assigned high acuity and endorsed a ≤30-minute treatment target, yet empiric "factor-first" therapy was inconsistently selected, particularly for non-articular presentations (severe headache, abdominal pain, early pregnancy bleeding). Reported gaps included use of hemostatic therapies; interpretation of coagulation assays; inhibitor management; and limited familiarity with treatment pathways. Barriers included absence of medical directives, treatment release processes, staffing and crowding pressures, and rural resource constraints. Six focus groups (nurses n=10; physicians n=4) explained these patterns, revealing a pathway from high perceived acuity to delayed treatment through intersecting knowledge, scope, and system factors, alongside strong preference for concise, point-of-care education, simulation, and web-based learning.

Discussion: Co-designed education, nurse-initiated directives, streamlined product release, and accessible point-of-care tools are complementary levers to compress triage-to-therapy intervals for PwBD.

Sussan Askari (Lead Author), Bonnie Yang (Co Author), Leslie Flynn (Co Author), Maria Theodorou (Co Author), Henry Swoboda (Co Author)

Methods: The HPHP Academic Enrichment Program (AEP) lunch-and-learn session will run throughout the 2025-2026 academic year, for first- and second- year medical students. This voluntary, in-person program will discuss eight topics covering 6 pillars of lifestyle Medicine including optimal nutrition, physical activity, restorative sleep, stress management, social connection, and risky substance avoidance. Students who complete an AEP receive a certificate from the UGME.

Results: A post-program survey will be sent to all participants. Clare Cook (Lead Author) Tara Baron (Co Author) Tara Leary (Co Author) Mat Litalien (Co Author)

Methods: Registration data from office-managed conferences over three years (2022-2025) were pulled. Activities included CME and Faculty Development ½-1-½ day conferences offered in-person; virtually, or hyflex. Demographic data analyzed included method of attendance (virtual/in-person); clinical/academic role (physician/allied health professional/learner); community (including whether rural or not); and years in practice (0-5; 6-10; 11-15; 16+).

Results: Since 2022, 14 conferences (9 CME/5 FD) were offered 36 times (22 CME/14 FD; 10 virtual/12 in-person; 14 hyflex). FD programs were more frequently offered in-person; CME programs were most often virtual/hyflex. After 2022, average overall attendance was higher for in-person opportunities. However, virtual/hyflex deliveries had a higher number of allied health professional attendees; participants from a higher number of communities, including rural communities; and a higher proportion of early-career attendees. Among hyflex programs, 53-58% of attendees are virtual; 62-67% of physician attendees are virtual; and 80+% of represented communities are from virtual attendees.

Discussion: Attendees report attending in-person programs for networking; lack of distraction. However, virtual delivery methods make many programs accessible, and hyflex programs show a stable pattern of high virtual participation rates, reaching communities and providers that may not otherwise be able to attend.

Anthony Seto (Lead Author), Connor Hass (Co Author), Melissa Monaghan (Co Author), Leo Ochieng (Co Author), Liam Montgomery (Co Author), Jan Rossiter (Co Author), Ali Bayrouti (Co Author), Nicole Ertl (Co Author), Ella Krane (Co Author)

Methods: HID SIM was piloted in a SIM theatre. After drawing a hidden identity card, teams of 4-6 progressed through a case, while roleplaying their character. In the debrief, learners revealed their roles and discussed strategies for managing difficult team dynamics.

Results: Of 29 participants, 28 (97%) completed a program evaluation survey. Among respondents (n=28), HID SIM received a mean rating of 4.75/5 (median 5/5). Respondents reported a mean agreement of 4.43/5 (median 5/5) that HID SIM was engaging. The mean agreement that HID SIM was less stressful than anticipated was 3.54/5 (median 4/5). Among participants with prior SIM experience (n=19), the mean rating for agreement that HID SIM was less stressful than other SIMs was 3.68/5 (median 4/5). Participants rated their pre-/post-SIM self-efficacy on identifying barriers to effective team dynamics, optimizing team dynamics, comfort in managing challenging team dynamics, using closed-loop communication, communicating professionally, sharing mental models, discussing clinical presentation signs/symptoms, and suggesting treatment plans. All competencies showed an increase (p<0.001) post-SIM.

Discussion: HID SIM is a well-received, engaging gamified SIM designed to practice handling difficult team dynamics. This format improves participants' teamwork and medical self-efficacy.

Brad MacCosham (Lead Author), Amy Nakajima (Co Author), K. Jean Chen (Co Author)

Methods: We analyzed the twelve CACMS standards and 96 elements published for accreditation cycles in 2026-2027. Using a deductive approach, elements were categorized into SEM domains-individual, interpersonal, institutional, community, and policy/systems-based on predominant accountability. Coding discrepancies were resolved iteratively. Tailored interventions were identified by integrating CACMS guidance, relevant literature, and institutional practices.

Results: Mapping revealed that most elements (53%, n=51) align with the institutional domain, emphasizing governance, resources, and oversight. Interpersonal elements (16%, n=15) addressed supervision and feedback, while individual elements (12%, n=11) focused on learner competencies. Community-level elements (10%, n=10) reflected social accountability and partnerships, and policy/systems elements (9%, n=9) emphasized compliance and safety. Interventions linked to each domain included simulation training (individual), mentorship programs (interpersonal), accreditation steering committees (institutional), community advisory boards (community), and compliance tracking (policy/systems).

Discussion: Applying the SEM reframes accreditation from a checklist into a systemic framework for strategic action. This approach enables schools to prioritize resources, align interventions with domains, and strengthen readiness. The model offers both conceptual clarity and practical utility for advancing accreditation in Canadian and international contexts.

Kashmala Qasim (Lead Author), Joanna Krongold (Co Author)

Methods: This qualitative study utilizes individual semi-structured interviews, conducted virtually (60-90 minutes) by a postdoctoral fellow with lived experience. Participants reviewed and approved their transcripts prior to analysis. To date, seven participants have been interviewed, with most participants from the Department of Family Medicine, and one from Internal Medicine and Anesthesia, respectively.

Results: Preliminary themes include: (1) The tension between hypervisibility and invisibility in clinical and academic spaces, particularly affecting visibly Muslim women who wear the hijaab; (2) Reluctance on the part of physicians to label negative comments or questions especially from patient interactions as Islamophobic; and (3) Criticism about institutional EDI initiatives perceived as performative or politically sanitized.

Discussion: Overall, most participants did not label or report incidents of subtle or overt bias as Islamophobia and rather, found them as opportunities for educating and addressing the curiosities of their patients as well as peers. Jean-Michel Leduc (Lead Author), Annie Ouellet (Co Author), Patricia Vohl (Co Author), Joshua Chinks (Co Author), Isabelle Gauthier (Co Author), Renaud Jean-Sébastien (Co Author), Marie-Pier Carrier (Co Author), William Sauvé (Co Author), Sébastien Béland (Co Author), Christian Bourdy (Co Author), Claude Julie Bourque (Co Author), Geoff Barnum (Co Author)

Methods: An electronic sociodemographic survey was sent to medical school applicants to any of the four medical school in Quebec in 2024 (n=5716) and compared to the 2021 Quebec census data (age 15-34) whenever possible.

Results: Participation rate was 23.9% (n=1950/5716). Overall, 67.7% identified as female and median age was 21. Among participants, 30.8% self-identified as a visible minority (census: 21%), 11.6% of applicants as Black (census: 6.7%) and 1.3% as Indigenous (census: 2.7%). Regarding socioeconomic status, 76.7% of responders had at least one parent with university-level education (census: 29.5%), 50.8% declared a household income over 100,000$ (census: 33.2%) and 21.2% had at least one parent with an MD degree. Lastly, 11.1% of responders lived in a small population area (<10,000) at the age of 16 (census: 19% overall rural population).

Discussion: These results suggest that most medical school applicants in Quebec are female, come from a high socioeconomic background and from an urban area. The applicant pool in Quebec appears to be ethnoculturally diverse, with a possible increase in the number of Black applicants compared to published historical data (4.5% in 2019). Similarly to other areas of Canada, challenges remain to attract people from lower socioeconomic backgrounds, rural areas and indigenous origins in the applicant pool.

Eleftherios Soleas (Lead Author), Wiley Chung (Lead Author), Devina Ramesh (Co Author), Emily Shostal (Co Author), Alyssa Reddi (Co Author), Lauren Breslin (Co Author), Amireza Goli (Co Author), Nooh Kabir (Co Author), Olivia Hendrikx (Co Author), Tim Song (Co Author), Shanzey Ali (Co Author), Theresa Suart Nowlan (Co Author)

Methods: Year(Y) 1 introduces foundational concepts including colonial history, privilege, and reflective practice. Y2 emphasizes systemic oppression, bias navigation, and grassroots advocacy. Y3 centers on trauma-informed care, power dynamics, and sustainable advocacy strategies. Y4 develops leadership in dismantling hierarchies, policy reform, ethical technology use, and advocacy career planning. Instructional methods include interactive lectures, reflective exercises, patient/community panels, scenario-based learning, small-group discussion, and advocacy skill-building. Teaching shifts from faculty-led to peer-led sessions, integrating lived-patient voices, and culminates in a personal leadership vision statement.

Results: This constructivist spiral-curriculum fosters skill development, critical self-awareness, and actionable strategies to promote equity in clinical and institutional settings. Preliminary evaluations indicate high learner engagement, improved understanding of systemic oppression, and enhanced readiness for advocacy. Incorporation of lived experiences and digital ethics deepens cultural humility and ethical decision-making.

Discussion: The longitudinal EDIIA curriculum offers a comprehensive, replicable framework for embedding equity education in medical training. By advancing learners from awareness to leadership, it prepares future physicians to foster inclusive care environments and sustain advocacy efforts.

Andrea Guerin (Lead Author), Eleni Katsoulas (Co Author)

Methods: Assessment data from ExamSoft was reviewed for all Term 1 and Term 3 preclerkship courses at two campuses (Kingston and Lakeridge) during the 2024-2025 academic year. Each exam's allocated time was compared with the actual time taken by students, measured by the average time taken per question by the student for multiple choice or set time per question for short answer/clinical reasoning. Surplus time was calculated by subtracting actual from allocated time.

Results: Across both campuses, students consistently completed assessments in less time than allocated. At the Kingston campus, surplus time ranged from 8 to 55 minutes, with most exams completed 15-35 minutes ahead of schedule. At the Lakeridge campus, similar trends were observed, with surplus time ranging from 10 to 45 minutes. There was one course which showed a slight deficit of 5 minutes.

Discussion: The findings support that current exam allocations are generous, with students completing assessments significantly faster than expected. Standardizing time allocations closer to actual use-while preserving accommodations-could reclaim curriculum time without compromising student performance. Further qualitative work may explore student perceptions of exam timing to complement these quantitative findings.

Methods: Psychiatry residents shared meaningful strengths of EDI competencies within the curriculum. However, they emphasized the need for EDI topics to be incorporated across all teaching sessions and to be taught through various learning modalities. Results: Students' perspectives of strengths within the curriculum have been categorized into two themes and five sub-themes. Limitations in the curriculum identified by students have been categorized into three themes and seven sub-themes. Discussion: Psychiatry residents shared meaningful strengths of EDI competencies within the curriculum. However, they emphasized the need for EDI topics to be incorporated across all teaching sessions and to be taught through various learning modalities. The perspectives of students from this study offer valuable insight for educators into curriculum enhancements that embed EDI content across academic and clinical domains of psychiatry training.

Ashwin Rao (Lead Author), Patrick Salwierz (Co Author), Marina Ivanova (Co Author), Eleftheria Laios (Co Author), Celine Bruce-Lepage (Co Author), Freya Shah (Co Author), Rylan Egan (Co Author), Nancy Dalgarno (Co Author), Ashly Fraser (Co Author), Amy Hickey (Co Author), Wiley Chung (Co Author)

Methods: Qualitative semi-structured virtual interviews were conducted to assess the experiences of those working within the traditional and NFS call schedules. Using snowball sampling, current and former surgical residents at Queen's University General Surgery Residency Program, and staff physicians employed at KHSC were recruited. Reflexive thematic analysis was used for data analysis with NVivo.

Results: Twenty-four interviews were conducted (11 residents, 3 former residents, 10 staff physicians). Analysis identified that views of call schedules are influenced by the following themes: Individual perceptions of wellness and learning, level of training, and medical education culture. These perceptions are backdropped by administrative decisions (i.e., scheduling surgeries in day vs night) and program characteristics (e.g., having sufficient coverage).

Discussion: This study highlighted a proliferating perception that operative learning was most valued. Junior residents sought opportunities to observe surgeries; seniors aspired to operate autonomously. Residents reported working nights was hard on wellness, but many appreciated the consolidation of call which eliminates post-call days, allowing unimpeded access to learning when not on call. Some staff had concerns about preparedness during NFS, while others believed wellness was balanced with learning.

Alexa Moschella (Lead Author), Claire Touchie (Co Author), Evan Sterling (Co Author), Scott David Rauscher (Co Author), Meron Samuel Demissie (Co Author), Tiffany Ange Tchigio Djomou (Co Author), Elaine Kilabuk (Co Author), Genevieve Lemay (Co Author), Thirusha Naidu (Co Author), Michael Quon (Co Author), Jerry M. Maniate (Co Author), Anna Romanova (Co Author)

Methods: Following the Joanna Briggs Institute methodology for scoping reviews, MEDLINE, Embase, ERIC, and CABI Global Health databases were searched from inception to November 2024. Data were analyzed using quantitative and qualitative analyses.

Results: Among 7716 studies screened, 145 met inclusion criteria. The majority (71%) originated from the United States. The definition of URiM shifted from a focus on ethnicity and binary gender identity prior to the 2010s, to an emphasis on broader sociodemographic characteristics, including applicants from non-binary identities and different educational, income, geographic, and ability backgrounds. Similar intrinsic and extrinsic barriers and facilitators were found throughout the admissions process, related to identity and discrimination, financial and social capital, and application process. Identified application support strategies include financial assistance, mentorship and advising programs, and specialized admission pathways.

Discussion: URiM groups are evolving and becoming increasingly diverse. Despite formal support strategies, various groups remain underrepresented in medicine. Long-standing systemic and structural inequities must be addressed to improve URiM access to medical education and foster medical school classes reflective of the populations they will serve.

Ron Siemens (Lead Author) Tammie Muise (Co Author) Alyson Campbell (Co Author) Ashton Dougan (Co Author) Pat Davis (Co Author)

Robert Henderson (Lead Author), Carolyn Augusta (Co Author), Rhonda Bryce (Co Author), Jason Hosain (Co Author)

Methods: We used two data sources: i) a survey, and ii) an analysis of call schedule data. The survey included Likert-style questions and open-ended comments. Schedule data were analysed over 2021-2023 for Saskatoon and Regina.

Results: All active FMRs (100) at the University of Saskatchewan in early 2024 were surveyed (response rate 52%). Participants were stratified into three groups: the two large urban centres, and the rural/remote sites. Rural/remote residents were the most satisfied with the learning experience; Regina residents were markedly more dissatisfied. Residents in Saskatoon were more positive about their general experience (e.g., reported less stress/fatigue). Half of Regina residents reported coming into clinic every shift, while Saskatoon residents very rarely attend in person while on call. Call schedules were found to be unevenly distributed in the urban centres, with some FMRs scheduled for an average of one shift every two weeks, while others had more than double that.

Discussion: There is substantial variation in the primary clinic call experience among FMRs in Saskatchewan. Discovery of the uneven distribution of call shifts has prompted a re-evaluation of the call scheduling protocol at our institution.

rahim valani (Lead Author), Shaveta Gupta (Co Author), Seddiq Weera (Co Author), Larry Chambers (Co Author)

Methods: In 2020 the core committee developed a needs assessment, the results of which guided the development of an EBM Education Primer Series curriculum. After 3 successful iterations, we delivered the Perception of Information Literacy Skills (PILS) questionnaire to understand the confidence of students in EBM. Finally, using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR), we developed a semi-structured interview guide to examine barriers to and facilitators of implementing the EBM Primer.

Results: Thirty four students completed the needs assessment. Most (94%) were familiar with the concept of EBM but could not apply it well. The average rating to critically appraise an article was 2.97 (Likert scale 1-5, SD 0.85), and their understanding of statistics was 2.58 (SD 1.03). The PILS score jumped from 145 to 160 (P<0.001). The largest improvements document by the PILS survey were in overall skills in search strategy (from 6.3 to 7.3), knowing the right commands for the search (2.8 to 5.0), knowing how to evaluate new information (5.4 to 6.4), and understanding the ethical aspects of questions (5.9 to 7.3). Based on the CFIR interviews, many students felt that the content was helpful. However, they also thought it would be more helpful later in the curriculum and also having it recognized with a certificate of completion or included in the formal curriculum to be included on their CV.

Discussion: EBM information literacy skills and knowledge acquisition are important components of medical education, including distributed campuses. Studies have shown improvement in EBM skills when implemented in the program, and students were motivated if the program could be certified.

Oksana Babenko (Lead Author), Victoria Meeker (Co Author), Polina Morilova (Co Author)

Methods: This was a survey study. The survey included the following measures: Self-Pursuits, Aspirations, & Knowledge Endeavors in the Digital Era (SPArKED); Jefferson Scale of Lifelong Learning (JeffSLL); and Human-Computer Trust (HTM) scale that was adapted to reflect trust in generative technology. In total, 558 students (age: 18-25 years old) representing various health education programs in Canada and other countries responded to the survey. Descriptive and regression analyses were performed.

Results: Descriptive analyses revealed a disproportionate use of digital and generative technology by students for individual learning compared to collaborative learning, further hindering social learning and interaction. In the regression analysis, greater use of digital and generative technology (beta = 0.63, p < 0.001) and lower lifelong learning abilities (beta = -0.15, p < 0.01) were associated with increased trust in generative technology to support lifelong learning.

Discussion: The findings indicate a complex relationship between digital and generative technology use, lifelong learning abilities, and trust in generative technology to support lifelong learning among students in health professions education. Further research is warranted to fully understand the long-term effects of the relationship on learning outcomes such as competence development and maintenance, collegiality, and teamwork.

Rebecca Mueller (Lead Author), Kristen Burrows (Co Author)

Methods: This session presents a structured approach for evaluating PA learners on clinical rotation. Core competencies from the CanMEDS-PA and EPA-PA frameworks were mapped to key areas of assessment, including clinical reasoning, communication, professionalism, and patient care. Practical tools and guidelines were developed to support clinicians in providing constructive feedback, tracking learner progress, and fostering interprofessional collaboration.

Results: Clinicians engaging with this session will gain clear, actionable methods to evaluate PA learners across rotations. Discussion: Effective learner assessment is central to competency-based PA education. This work aligns with national efforts to advance health professions education and provides clinician educators with resources to elevate the quality and consistency of PA training.

Asiana Elma (Lead Author), Vyshnave Jeyabalan (Co Author), Emma Wilson (Co Author), Patti Kishimoto (Co Author), Tavis Apramian (Co Author), Jeff Myers (Co Author), Ilana Halperin (Co Author), Sarah Wright (Co Author), Giovanna Sirianni (Co Author)

Methods: Arksey and O'Malley's framework was used to identify and synthesize peer-reviewed empirical studies published between 2015-2025. Seven databases were searched, with screening conducted by multiple reviewers, and consensus used to resolve disagreements. Extracted data included study characteristics, training interventions, and outcomes. Outcome-related data were thematically analyzed and refined through team consultations.

Results: Thirty-six articles have been included for data extraction to date. Educational interventions focused on assessing communication and technical skills, including structured history-taking, active listening, and triaging between digital and in-person visits. While these activities helped learners adapt to digital care, they were not positioned as explicit opportunities to cultivate compassion. In practice, clinicians described offering affirmations, maintaining eye contact through webcams, asking probing questions to help patients feel acknowledged and supported. However, they reported difficulty interpreting nonverbal cues, competing documentation demands, and blurred boundaries from continuous virtual access.

Discussion: Compassionate practices were often enacted as individualized workarounds rather than being systematically-taught or embedded within training programs and organizational workflows. Future efforts should focus on developing structured curricula that integrate technical, relational, and clinical skills to better support therapeutic relationships and promote digitally-mediated compassionate primary care.

Batya Grundland (Lead Author), Mahan Kulasegaram (Co Author), Maria Mylopoulos (Co Author), Karina Prucnal (Co Author), Nicole Woods (Co Author), Risa Freeman (Co Author)

Methods: Semi-structured interviews were conducted with practicing family physicians to explore their approach to four clinical scenarios in a virtual environment. Scenarios varied along two dimensions: 1. Patient capacity for self-management (high/low) 2. Severity or risk of illness (high/low). Recruitment ensured representation across years of experience and location of practice. Data were analyzed thematically and inductively for key components unique to clinical reasoning in the virtual care setting.

Results: Twelve participants were interviewed. Key themes included: 1) Clinical reasoning in virtual care requires flexible application of the 'usual' order of information gathering routines; 2) The pre-existing relationship with the patient impacts physicians' confidence in their clinical reasoning in the virtual environment; 3) The virtual environment offers new opportunities for optimizing care (e.g. triage, acquisition of background information prior to an in-person visit).

Discussion: Clinical reasoning in virtual care is adapted by clinicians in both expected and surprising ways. Therefore, teachers and curriculum developers should emphasize core concepts as well as practice variability when preparing learners for effective clinical reasoning in virtual care.

Shivani Dadwal (Lead Author), Geordie Linford (Co Author), Heather Braund (Co Author), Gurdit Sood (Co Author), Nancy Dalgarno (Co Author), Anna Tomiak (Co Author)

Methods: A qualitative study was conducted using semi-structured interviews with 15 graduates across Canada. Interviews focused on Entrustable Professional Activities (EPAs), feedback, preparation for practice, and training gaps. All interviews were virtual, transcribed, and analyzed thematically.

Results: Five themes emerged: (1) Assessment and EPA tools, (2) Feedback and coaching, (3) Preparation for practice, (4) Implementation of CBME, and (5) Recommendations. Participants valued broad exposure and flexible learning but noted challenges, including pressure to achieve EPA numbers, delays in receiving feedback, and deficiencies in assessment technology. Feedback quality varied by faculty, though bidirectional observation was viewed positively. Graduates cited self-directed learning as a strength, but highlighted educational gaps in the logistics of independent practice, research, and clinical trials training.

Discussion: Our findings suggest that while CBD supports structured progression and offers valuable learning opportunities, it falls short in addressing essential non-clinical competencies and in fostering meaningful, timely feedback. Future efforts should focus on integrating clinical trials and administrative training into the curriculum, refining assessment processes, and promoting faculty development. These improvements will be essential to ensure medical oncology graduates are fully equipped for the multifaceted demands of independent practice.

Emily Dagasso (Lead Author), Laura Stovel (Co Author)

Methods: Statistical analysis was conducted among 738 students that graduated from the UAlberta Doctor of Medicine program, 2012-2016. Data was reviewed from published Canadian databases and UAlberta archives. Descriptive statistics were used for data summarization and presentation.

Results: The results of this analysis determined 61% of UAlberta medical graduates who were in-province applicants (n=450) were practicing in Alberta in 2024. Among the out-of-province UAlberta applicants (n=98), 34% were practicing in Alberta in 2024.

Discussion: The findings of this study suggest that the majority of medical learners who resided in Alberta prior to medical school practice in Alberta as licensed medical doctors whereas out-of-province medical learners are more likely to practice outside of Alberta following graduation. Jordana Waserman (Lead Author), Khushi Goyal (Co Author), Quinten Clarke (Co Author), Shira Gertsman (Co Author), Joanna Cheek (Co Author)

Methods: A post-program survey was distributed to the 2024-2025 participant cohort (n=97). 9 responses were received (response rate 9%). Of these, 4 were mentees, 3 were mentors, and 2 participated in both capacities. Quantitative data were analyzed descriptively.

Results: 89% of respondents reported achieving their initial goals for the program "somewhat", "quite a bit" or "extremely." 100% of participants would recommend the mentorship program to others. Methods: Medical trainees with religious beards were recruited from medical undergraduate and postgraduate programs across Canada. Semi-structured virtual interviews explored their experiences with accommodations processes and the impact on medical training and career decisions. Interviews were recorded, transcribed and coded using NVivo. Inductive thematic analysis is currently ongoing in an iterative approach.

Results: Preliminary findings reveal that participants commonly experience feelings of frustration and isolation. Many reported inconsistent policies and accommodations across institutions and described how N95-related limitations negatively influenced clinical exposure and specialty choice. Trainees expressed concern over being forced to choose between religious identity and career aspirations. Discussion: This study is the first national exploration of challenges related to religious beards in medical education. Marcel D'Eon (Lead Author), Latika Nirula (Co Author), Greg Turner (Co Author), Qian Wu (Co Author)

Methods: We followed Arksey & O'Malley (2005). Inclusion criteria: 1) studies on any educational intervention(s) that 2) addressed improvement of teaching meaning there must have been a specific needs assessment of participants 3) within HPE and 4) published 2013-2023.

Results: From the 1,766 articles initially found, we identified 25 articles for data extraction. Most interventions targeted teaching skills (14), many feedback (8), and most remediation studies addressed professionalism (5 of 6). Interventions included feedback exclusively; coaching; simulation; and interactive workshops. Few papers cited theories/frameworks to guide interventions. Studies reported changed teaching practices with satisfaction and learning.

Discussion: Only 25 articles selected suggest that improvement and remediation in HPE may be under-practiced, described, and evaluated. The preoccupation with unprofessionalism in the remediation studies contrasts with improvement studies where teaching skills and feedback were addressed. Many of the interventions were not clearly described making replication or further study unlikely. The absence of theories or frameworks reveals a weakness in HPE improvement and remediation practices. Intense, longitudinal programs recommended in the literature were not universally used.

Shannon Wong (Lead Author), Laura Nimmon (Co Author)

Methods: Our methodology was qualitative interpretive description. We conducted 10 semi-structured interviews with Internal Medicine residents at one institution. Data collection and analysis proceeded iteratively. During the final stages of analysis, we were sensitized to the concept of professional identity formation, which highlighted how critical moments of transition stimulated different ways of being and becoming.

Results: Residents described identifying with the role of a curer at the start of their training. This identity was shaken and confronted when there was a change in patient care that moved away from curative intent. Residents emphasized the role of supportive relationships during these periods of transition, which helped them feel validated and understood. In the context of these supportive relationships, residents were able to embrace a renewed identity as healer, thus invoking new meaning into their role as health care providers.

Discussion: A transition in patient care away from curative intent is an emotionally powerful catalyst for resident professional identity formation. Supporting residents through these difficult transitions has the potential to widen how residents perceive themselves as physicians.

Emily Quick (Lead Author), Catherine Patocka (Co Author)

Methods: We analyzed a purposive sample of 137 EPAs completed by 28 emergency medicine faculty for residents in the University of Calgary Department of Emergency Medicine between August 2019 and October 2024. Using a critical realist and hermeneutic stance, framework analysis guided mapping to our pattern system of feedback.

Results: On average, 10 pattern representations were present in each EPA, with 16 distinct pattern representations identified. All EPAs contained at least six pattern representations reflecting mandatory ePortfolio fields. Additional pattern representations, in descending frequency, included judgement, output of performance, purpose of performance, specificity, discussion, results, function, culture, recursion, and actual action. Narrative content was concentrated in referent and meta pattern domains, with limited representation of the feedback response domain. Most EPAs followed a consistent structure: describing the case (100%), what the learner did (85%), and whether performance was satisfactory or unsatisfactory.

Discussion: Overall, EPAs demonstrated regular and consistent pattern syntax, emphasizing assessment-focused content. Patterns aligned with processing and response-central to formative feedback- were underrepresented.

Kate Lim (Lead Author)

Methods: Medical learners worldwide participated in the inaugural Orbis Global Hackathon (December 2024). Anonymous online surveys were administered pre- (n=36) and post-event (n=26), structured around the Knowledge, Attitudes, and Practices (KAP) framework. Knowledge was assessed using six multiple-choice pediatric ophthalmology questions from retired University of Toronto exams. Attitudes and practices were measured on 5-point Likert scales. Fisher's exact tests compared item-level differences; aggregate knowledge change was tested using paired Student's t-test (α=0.05).

Results: No individual knowledge item showed a statistically significant difference; however, aggregate analysis revealed a significant 9.3 percentage point increase in correct responses (t(5)=6.43, p=0.001). Learners' comfort with pediatric eye disorders improved, with "somewhat comfortable" rising from 35.5% to 52.2%. Attitudinal gains were notable, with over 85% expressing strong intention to incorporate pediatric ophthalmology into future practice compared to moderate baseline enthusiasm.

Discussion: Hackathon participation enhanced learners' pediatric ophthalmology knowledge, confidence, and professional attitudes. Hackathons may serve as scalable, interactive tools to supplement curricula in underrepresented subspecialties. These findings support further exploration of hackathons in medical training, including evaluation of long-term retention and career influence.

Zoë Chabot (Lead Author)

Methods: Peer-reviewed studies published from January 2015 to January 2025 examining the implementation of gamification strategies in robotic surgery simulation training were included in the scoping review. Studies assessing resident engagement and skill development outcomes were included. A descriptive analysis was conducted, focusing on gamification elements such as competitive leaderboards, team-based challenges, and structured performance metrics. Patterns in implementation approaches and training outcomes were identified and synthesized.

Results: A total of 29 studies were included in the scoping review. Gamification strategies were associated with notable improvements in resident engagement, reflected by extended practice time and increased simulator utilization, with increases in practice time of 9.7-fold improvement in some cohorts. Participation rates showed clear enhancement from 33% to 53% during intervention periods, and performance metrics consistently improved throughout gamified training sessions. Effective implementation strategies often included team-based competition frameworks, expert demonstration videos with synchronized hand position guidance, and standardized scoring rubrics. Qualitative assessments identified primary motivators as competitive elements and recognition systems, while barriers to participation and sustained engagement included limited clinical time availability and simulator accessibility.

Discussion: This scoping review maps existing evidence supporting the effectiveness of gamification in enhancing resident engagement and skill acquisition in robotic surgery simulation training. Implementation success appears most robust when incorporating structured competition, clear performance metrics, and institutional support mechanisms. While sustainability remains a challenge, with some studies noting decreased effectiveness in extended intervention periods, the overall evidence supports gamification as a valuable adjunct to traditional surgical training methodologies. Future directions include developing sustainable engagement models and establishing standardized metrics for skill assessment across institutions.

Methods: The World Federation for Medical Education (WFME), in collaboration with the International Academy for Continuing Professional Development Accreditation (IACPDA), has developed a CPD Recognition Programme. Haifa Akremi (Lead Author), Laura Leclair (Co Author), Marie-France Deschênes (Co Author), Bernard Charlin (Co Author), Vincent Jobin (Co Author), Véronique Castonguay (Co Author), Nicolas Fernandez (Co Author)

Methods: We conducted a dialogic, interpretive qualitative study across 14 sessions with eight members (emergency physicians, LbC specialists, and medical-education researchers) to codesign pulmonary embolism and aortic dissection LbC modules. Data was collected through "hot" and "cold" focus groups followed designing sessions. Transcripts were thematically analyzed using an Input-Mediator-Output (IMO) lens focused on role attribution, maintenance interventions, and decision-making process.

Results: Three themes characterized the process: (1) Resolving a conceptual tension between encyclopedic teaching and uncertainty-driven clinical reasoning was pivotal to progress; (2) Directive leadership by a clinically and pedagogically dual-trained scientific director aligned LbC principles with emergency-care realities and accelerated production; (3) Punctuated time flow-after a slow, exploratory start, work shifted to rapid, efficient cycles, enabling completion of two modules.

Discussion: Effective LbC co-design hinges on bridging experiential clinical knowledge with instructional coherence. Multidisciplinary teams thrive when roles are clearly defined and leadership is decisive, enabling them to transform uncertainty in practice into productive ambiguity for learners. These findings provide actionable guidance for continuing professional development units seeking to scale LbC: pair content experts with LbC pedagogues, normalize conceptual tension early, and design sessions that support rapid decision-making cycles.

Amber Strachan (Lead Author), Deborah Ocholi (Co Author), Deandra Romain (Co Author), Janelle Olughor (Co Author), Princess Olawale (Co Author), Hallie Green (Co Author), Onye Nnorom (Co Author)

Methods: Participants were recruited through convenience and snowball sampling. Julianah Oguntala (Lead Author), Casey Schapel (Co Author), Meeta Patel (Co Author), Seema Marwaha (Co Author), Reena Pattani (Co Author), Justin Lam (Co Author)

Methods: Community partners were intentionally consulted at each stage of the writing process, from brainstorming to draft review. Each script was co-written and reviewed by a diverse group of faculty, medical students, and residents, including individuals from equity-deserving groups represented in the videos. A follow-up form was also distributed to reviewers for feedback on clarity, effectiveness, cultural appropriateness, and alignment with lived experiences. Reviewers were also invited to identify scripts that perpetuated or inadequately addressed biases or stereotypes. Responses were shared with the steering committee and script authors to guide revisions.

Results: Survey themes included the importance of diverse consultation, script clarity, and educational value. Feedback indicated that the scripts appropriately reflected learner experiences, including those often left undisclosed due to fear of repercussion. One script was critiqued for oversimplifying a complex conflict resolution scenario. The scripts were thoroughly revised based on this broad consultative feedback to ensure they accurately portray and teach how to respond to learner mistreatment.

Discussion: Broad, iterative consultation ensures video scripts on learner mistreatment reflect authentic experiences and avoid reinforcing inequities or stereotypes. Co-creation supports epistemic justice and produces accessible, effective curricular tools for faculty development.

Priya Chopra (Lead Author), Suzanne Cook (Co Author), Dorothy Bakker (Co Author)

Methods: Curriculum development began four years prior to the school's launch. Initial engagement with community members through surveys and in-person sessions established the TMU SoM's foundational 5 Pillars and 7 Values. Guided by these principles and aligned with competencies from the Medical Council of Canada and CanMEDS roles (notably Advocate and Communicator), the course framework was developed. An iterative process of re-engagement ensured community knowledge was incorporated into learning objectives, course themes, cases, and a cornerstone Community Advocacy Project (CAP). A "glocal" (global issues with local impacts) framework was also co-emphasized to reflect the region's diverse and migratory populations.

Results: The resulting CGH course is being implemented in September 2025 with the inaugural UGME cohort. The pedagogy combines large-group active learning delivered by both faculty and community with the experiential CAP experience. The course integrates principles of health promotion, disease prevention, knowledge translation, and determinants of health.

Discussion: This course represents an innovative model of curriculum development rooted in genuine community engagement and project-based experiential learning. Moving beyond traditional didactic teaching, this approach can serve as a framework for other medical schools seeking to fulfill a social accountability mandate.

Jocelyn Bonti-Ankomah (Lead Author), Xavier-Philippe Legare (Co Author), Genevieve Gauthier (Co Author), Shellyza Sajwani (Co Author), Randilynne Urslak (Co Author), Salmaan Kanji (Co Author), Christine Landry (Co Author)

Methods: Four virtual focus groups were conducted across two rounds, using the nominal group technique. The focus groups went from creating a shared understanding of planetary health to proposing competencies through an iterative process across both rounds. Meetings were recorded and transcripts analyzed via inductive content analysis. Guidance from an Indigenous representative was also incorporated. Preliminary competencies were drafted, refined, and prioritized following feedback from focus group participants.

Results: Fifteen individuals (faculty, student and Indigenous representation) participated, representing 10 of the 11 Canadian pharmacy schools. Three themes were emphasized by the participants: applying knowledge in patient care, change management and advocacy, and equity and social determinants of health. Five final competencies were selected.

Discussion: These results show strong support for addressing planetary health in pharmacy education through equity, sustainability, and action. With five clear competencies to integrate within curriculum, this study offers an actionable way to prepare future pharmacists to navigate planetary health challenges and take on their role as environmentally responsible healthcare professionals.

Shania Sheth (Lead Author), Siddharth Seth (Co Author)

Methods: The program consisted of 60 undergraduate students. Students completed a pre-program and post-program survey that assessed their research knowledge and confidence on a Likert scale in 18 domains categorized as foundational research skills, understanding literature, conducting research, and communicating research. Statistical analysis was completed using Chi-square and Fisher's exact test.

Results: 48 students (80%) consented to participate in the survey. Domain specific analysis demonstrated a significant increase in the student's knowledge across all 18 domains (p<0.001). The largest improvements were seen in knowledge on thematic analysis, narrative literature reviews, and research communication. 19 participants reported securing a research position, 9 were in the process of securing a research position, and 11 were not interested in a research position.

Discussion: The ASPIRE Research Program significantly improved students' self-reported research knowledge and confidence across all measured domains. These results highlight the value of comprehensive research training programs to help reduce barriers for undergraduate students to access research opportunities.
